FOREWORD

The Motupore Conference for ICAD Practitioners was organised by the staff of the Conservation
Resource Centre, a unit of Papua New Guinea’s Department of Environment and Conservation. It was
funded by the United Nations Development Programme through the Global Environment Facility as
part of Papua New Guinea’s Biodiversity Conservation and Resource Management Programme.

This Programme was designed to meet three goals:

e Establish two pilot integrated conservation and Development projects to develop innovative
methodologies for the conservation of biodiversity

e Increase capacity within the Department of Environment and Conservation to deliver
conservation by providing training and infrastructure.

e Determine the feasibility of establishing a conservation trust fund for future funding of
conservation initiatives in PNG.

The development of strong working links with environmental NGOs in Papua New Guinea struggling
to develop sustainable models of ‘integrated conservation and development’ has been a hallmark of the
Programme. It was in this context that the Conservation Resource Centre initiated the 1995 Meeting of
Integrated Conservation and Development Projects in Madang. That meeting saw many valuable
presentations, but was precluded, by time constraints, from allowing as much exchange of experiences
as the field practitioners would have liked.

Accordingly, the Motupore Conference was planned specifically to meet that need. Participants were
enabled to pause and reflect on the effectiveness of our different strategies, and to sharpen the tools
used for implementing the ICAD process. From the very first day this tool-sharpening was effected by
enthusiastic debate and discussion by all participants in plenary sessions lasting well past midnight, and
spreading out from the formal conference room to the sea-wall, the mangroves and the beach at
Motupore.

Because the participants had personally struggled with problems in the field, it was a sharing of real-
life experiences and real-life strategies to tackle problems. As well, there was a conviction amongst the
practitioners that the issue of combining conservation with real development was too urgent to allow
any false pride about making presentations ‘academic’ or about covering up difficulties faced in the
field. The need and desire amongst everyone to learn from each other, to share their experiences, was
evident from the outset, and over five days never ebbed.

This collection of presentations from the Conference does not attempt to capture the full range of

discussion of that crowded five days, but is in itself offered as a contribution to ongoing sharing
between practitioners.

Felix Kinbag Mike Parsons
A/National Project Manager Chief Technical Adviser

UNDP/DEC Biodiversity Conservation and Resource Management Programme.

ACKNOWLEDGEMENTS

The editors of the second ICAD Conference papers and proceedings, wish to thank the contributors to
this volume, not only for their readiness to offer their work to help others, but also for their co-
operation in preparing the work for publication; a matter sometimes rendered difficult by distance and
communication problems.

The staff of the Conservation Resource Centre (Mike Parsons, Felix Kinbag, Danny Charmilou, John
Lakoro, Thelma Manuqwadi and Simon Saulei) would also like to record their appreciation of the
University of Papua New Guinea’s Motupore Island Research Department Staff, especially Thomas
Maniwavie, Sai Gwae, Joseph Aitsi, Dede Damafo and Caspar Dako, for taking much of their time in
transporting and helping participants, and for the general hospitality they offered. We would like to
thank Dr Simon Saulei for his work in convening the conference and in chairing, and Wep Kanawi for
his opening chairing of the Conference. Thanks also go to the SHRM catering staff who spent the
whole five days on the island catering for the participants’ three meals a day. Finally, we are grateful
to UNDP/DEC for sponsoring the conference and to the participants, whose attendance, lively debate
and stimulating contributions made the conference such a success.

EpITORS’ NOTE

The Motupore Conference of ICAD Practitioners was not arranged as an academic exercise. It was
driven by the pressing need within the ICAD community for real interaction and exchange of ideas
between those working in the field, attempting to develop new ways of interweaving conservation and
development, while simultaneously wrestling with exactly what conservation and development might
mean in the Papua New Guinean context.

To some extent, the papers in this volume reflect the field-based nature of the conference, and the
knitting together of vastly differing approaches and ideas into one fabric. Papers cover a wide range of
topics, and use a wide range of approaches, from rigorously academic to broadly speculative, and from
philosophical to very pragmatic. The addresses of organisations involved in the Conference have been
included both in the list of participants and with each paper, in the hope that this will contribute to
furthering the cross-fertilisation of ideas which has already proved of so much value. Addresses are in
Papua New Guinea unless stated otherwise.

Throughout, there are many references to the Papua New Guinean cultural context, for example, to
“bigmen’ or ‘cargo’. It will be understood that words or phrases such as these are used in their local
sense, and the editors have confidence that readers of these conference papers will not need these to be
‘translated’. In the same way, the editors have felt no hesitation in including short phrases, and in one
instance an entire questionnaire in tok pisin, since this is very much the lingua franca of Papua New
Guinea.

INTRODUCTION :
INTEGRATED CONSERVATION AND DEVELOPMENT (ICAD) —
CREATING APPROPRIATE INCENTIVES FOR BIODIVERSITY CONSERVATION
IN PAPUA NEW GUINEA

Simon Saulei,
Conservation Resource Centre,
P.O. Box 165, Waigani, NCD, Papua New Guinea,

The Integrated Conservation and Development (or ICAD) process for conservation has been widely
discussed over the past four to five years since being implemented in Papua New Guinea. The ICAD
practitioners gathered at the Motupore conference were asked to share their experiences, both successes _
and failures, relating to the implementation of their respective ICAD projects. The main objective was
to develop approaches that could assist in resolving some of the persistent problems associated with the
implementation and management of conservation programmes in PNG. Invited speakers to the
conference were given the task of addressing specific issues and/or sharing their experiences in
conceiving, implementing and managing ICAD or similar conservation projects in both formal and
formal discussions. At the end of the five-day conference, recommendations were drawn up, and are
included in this report. It is hoped that these recommendations will be taken up for consideration by
those who are sponsoring, managing and implementing ICAD projects, including the Department of
Environment and Conservation. The conference raised more questions than answers and was very
provocative. It was an open meeting to allow a wider audience to contribute to both the oral and
written presentations together with lively discussions.

The conference was organised to address a number of fundamental issues, which have long been
central to the problems of planning, implementing and managing the long-term sustainability of ICAD
projects in Papua New Guinea. These issues are as follows:

1. Alternative Economic Incentives for Integrated Conservation and Development (ICAD)
rather than Logging for ‘Development’

In addressing this issue, one question we need to consider is ‘What strategies are available to combat
non-sustainable logging?’ This is because what you see is what there is. The fact is that the economic
incentive possibilities we have available cannot compete on a one-to-one basis against what the loggers
are offering. We urgently need to address the following questions in order for us to really appreciate
what we conservationists are up against: So what do we do now? What are the current status and
continuing weaknesses of sustainable timber-cutting for villages? What eco-timber and community
forestry or portable sawmill projects are operating in areas of conservation interest? What markets are
they tapping into and how? How are these operations guaranteeing adequate returns to local
communities? How are these operations guaranteeing sustainable use of resources and the conservation
of natural habitats and their biological diversity?

What other alternative economic incentives under ICAD than Logging could be implemented in ICAD
projects? What are the possibilities of initiating the followings:

® Eco-Tourism?

e Nuts and other Non-Timber Forest Products?, and

e@ Butterfly Farming?

2. Western Concepts of Conservation versus Traditional Taboos and Christian Theology

We need to address the issues relating to I[CADs and churches or conservation and Christian theology.
One of the most important rules of successful rural development is to utilise pre-existing institutions,
rather than creating new ones. One of the most widespread village institutions is that of the village
church. How can we work conservation and the ICAD process into the church teachings, and yet how
do we avoid the ICAD process being viewed as another ‘religion’?

The need to incorporate traditional views into conservation initiatives has to be thoroughly assessed as
this may be a method of ensuring that conservation is an approach to traditional and Christian practices.

Villagers used to achieve wildlife conservation and habitat protection indirectly, through masalai areas
and other traditional beliefs. People may have left alone forests or reefs because of fears of ancestor
spirits. None of this really is a conservation practice. It achieves the same result, but for a different
reason. Thus: do we use the traditional reasons and try to reinforce them? Is it even possible to
harness these traditional beliefs of spirits today? Or should we teach people about the modern-day
important reasons for conserving habitats and wildlife?

There is a need to make the concept of ‘conservation’ relevant to local communities as this will greatly
assist in ensuring that the community accepts and fully participates in planning, implementing and
managing ICAD projects as their own.

3. Cargo Cult Mentality and Conservation Strategies

Most PNG communities are used to getting handouts from the government, companies and politicians,
which only confirm to the local communities the cargo cult mentality concept. Many have experienced
material cargo from projects. Everyone wants conservationists to play the same game too, and deliver
the cargo. If we don’t, they say they aren’t interested. What do we do to fight against the cargo-cult
mentality? Or do we give in and deliver cargo too?

Providing gifts oftentimes builds dependency and a ‘hand-out mentality’. This eventually destroys the
concepts of self-reliance and pride. Thus, giving things to communities and providing help without a
contribution-in-kind can be considered bad. Yet, isn’t reciprocal gift-giving the very essence of
Melanesian relationship and trust building? Aren’t relationships and trust the basis of working
effectively with communities? So what do we do: do we avoid gift-giving and try to build community
self-reliance? Or do we help the community and provide gifts to develop the kind of relationship that is
so important for long-term conservation initiatives? What is right and what is wrong?

4. Regulation of Protected Areas

We encourage local communities to make their own laws to manage their wildlife. But why would any
villager want to enforce laws against their own wantoks, thereby causing unhappiness and community
imbalances that people usually take pains in avoiding? Enforcement is a hard thing to carry out in the
villages by the villagers themselves. In that context, what can we do to increase people’s motivation to
enforce wildlife rules and regulations and general protected areas?

Conservationists want to see communities establish protected areas as evidence of their conservation
initiative. Yet, if communities establish protected areas at the beginning, is it really their initiative, or
is it that of the conservationists? How do we resolve the dilemma of establishing protected areas early
or as a final step in our conservation approaches?

In establishing and regulating protected areas, what options are there, that are not only available, but
feasible, in light of the unique situations we have in Papua New Guinea?

5. Land Ownership and Community Participation in Conservation

Prior to independence, people did not understand the multiple-values of their land, forests and reefs as
much as they do today. That is good. But it also means that today, as soon as a resource is seen to
have economic value to the resource owners, disputes arise, and people start contesting ownership.
ICAD processes are typically seen as offering potential cargo and benefits. How do we deal with the
issue of land disputes in determining protected area boundaries, or in determining resource use on
different pieces of land and reefs? How can we resolve land disputes in ICAD areas?

Remote communities tend to control large areas of undisturbed habitats, exactly the size of habitat that
conservationists are interested in helping people protect. Yet, low levels of education, literacy and

SO

.
a
a

often poor, sometimes distorted perception of the outside world also characterize remote areas. How
on earth do we actually educate such people about conservation and development?

When we are talking ‘conservation’ to local communities, they are demanding ‘development’. How do

we describe conservation in a way that it not only makes sense, but also motivates them (the villagers)
to actively embrace conservation?

Experience of ICAD projects from overseas may assist us in assessing the common problems and/or
issues that such overseas communities had in implementing ICAD projects. We can learn from how
they manage to overcome difficulties and sustain their conservation programmes/projects.

Conservation is for the common good. Yet, what really motivates people are individual, not communal

rewards. How do we motivate individuals, and at the same time, achieve results that are for community
benefit?

Who do you hire? How do you choose them? What do you pay them? And how does hiring a few
allow the community-as-a-whole to feel involved and be part of the process? The fact is, ICAD
activities will only help a few in the short-term; how can the community be convinced that this is worth
supporting, when many people will receive few or no short-term benefits?

We often say that we want to hear or know the communities’ point-of-view and address their needs.
Yet, if we explore villagers’ needs and biggest problems, ‘conservation’ usually doesn’t even make the
top 20 list. So now what do we do? If we address their needs, we won’t be doing conservation. Yet, if
we offer them the conservation program we were hired to implement, this means that we really didn’t
listen to the community and hear their needs. How do we resolve this conflict?

6. ICAD Project Management and Implementation

Since the inception of ICAD as a process for conservation, problems have arisen both in the
management and implementation of the programs/projects. What are these and is there any way out of
them? Are the problems associated with individuals, groups, communities or are they institutional in
nature? Why is this so? Are there any problems between the sponsors of the projects/programs, the
managers and implementers? Are such problems associated with differing agendas/interests of the
concerned parties? How are local communities getting involved in such projects? Continuity of
programs/projects depends on a number of factors: funding, land security and manpower as well as the

political will. Are these factors associated with any of the projects/programs previously or currently
being implemented?

OPENING ADDRESS
BY THE
SECRETARY OF THE DEPARTMENT OF ENVIRONMENT AND CONSERVATION

Presented by Kembi Watoka
A/Deputy Secretary
Department of Environment & Conservation

On behalf of the Department of Environment and Conservation, I welcome you all to this important

Conference. This meeting is the second ICAD meeting sponsored by the UNDP/DEC Biodiversity
Conservation and Resource Management Programme.

The first one was held in 1995, and its proceedings as published, have become a widely distributed and
much appreciated resource for ICAD practitioners in Papua New Guinea, the Pacific region and around
the world. The proceedings of the second have the potential to be even more valuable.

In the First ICAD Conference, we discussed what we were all doing and how we were going about
doing it. In this conference we are going to go further, and we are going to dig deeper into our
collective experience. We intend to explore issues that you, as ICAD practitioners, are confronted with
in your day to day work, but which cannot be readily resolved. They are ‘big picture’ problem issues.
They are common to many, if not all ICAD areas. They are also the ones suggested by yourselves as

points that should be discussed at the conclusion of the last Conference in the lead up to the present
one.

In the last Workshop the question of ‘How does conservation link to the government?’ was raised.
Perhaps lying beneath this question was the reality that customary landowners have the say about how
their land is to be managed, and so what is the role of government? However the answer to this
question, as reported in the proceedings, did not address the customary land ownership issue. Instead it
strongly urged the NGOs, who are the backbone of integrated conservation and development in this
country, not to ignore the administrative system of local Council Wards, district government and
provincial government and to involve them as much as possible in the ICAD planning and
implementation and management process.

In 1997 this has become even more urgent with the implementation of Organic Law, which will see
conservation become a provincial responsibility, and therefore, a district level responsibility. This has
the potential for strengthening or weakening our efforts, depending on whether we work together co-
operatively, or independently, as competitors in conservation, with each of us looking after our own
conservation territory rather than the interests of the environment that we are mandated to protect.

This Environment does not have these borders, and does not discriminate between government and
non-government, national NGOs or clan-based organisation. In the same way it is clear that,
government should openly and fully acknowledge the leading role played by NGOs on the ground and
in the front line, in the struggle to obtain our common conservation objectives.

I wish to draw your attention to the first public address on Conservation Policy made by the Minister
for Environment and Conservation, the Honorable Robert Sakias. In this address to the nation, the
Minister made it clear that he wishes to be guided by NGOs, many of whom are represented here today
and who are involved in implementing our conservation projects, as well as in the conservation policy.
He has issued invitation publicly and directly to you, the conservation NGOs, to take a senior role as
partners in Policy-making, as his personal advisers on how to overcome the major obstacles to
conservation in Papua New Guinea. He issued the invitation, and I would like to endorse this
invitation. I would like to see this Workshop address the challenges he raised, and suggest and build a
means by which the Minister can receive this advice and this guidance on a regular, co-ordinated and
comprehensive basis.

And in this and in all your other endeavours I wish you every success.

Thank you.

ARE ECONOMIC INCENTIVES FOR ICADS
CONDUCIVE FOR CONSERVATION
IN

PAPUA NEW GUINEA?

Simon M Saulei and John Genolagani*
Conservation Resource Centre, P.O. Box 165, Waigani, NCD.
* Department of Environment and Conservation, P.O. Box 6601, Boroko, NCD.

INTRODUCTION

Around the world, attention has been focused on the rapid pace at which global environmental
degradation has threatened to shape profoundly the quality and future course of life on earth. The loss
of biological diversity, an indicator of the richness of the world’s ecosystems, is among the most
critical of these changes; both individual species and their habitats are in fast decline (Barbier, 1987;
World Bank, 1992; Conservation International, 1997).

The ecological zones in which biodiversity is the richest are also often areas in which some of the
world’s poorest people live. These people depend on the natural resources around them for production
and extractive purposes, such as tilling the soils and harvesting forest products. Thus, environmental
sustainability issues not only are relevant to the ‘haves’ of the world, but are of immediate relevance to
poor people dependent on the viability of the natural resource base. Environmental degradation is not
an issue that only the wealthy people have the luxury of addressing. It is an issue related to the failing
livelihoods, fuel scarcity, longer and harder working days, deteriorating health, hunger, and thirst of
most of the world’s people (World Commission on Environment and Development and the
Commission for the Future, 1991; Reid, 1995).

Integrated conservation and development projects (ICADs) offer a new approach, for addressing some
of the core issues related to environmental degradation. ICADs attempt to create conditions for the
poor to invest in long-term, sustainable exploitation strategies so that they can avoid depleting their
resources for survival (Brown and Wyckoff-Baird, 1992; Sekhran, 1996). This approach addresses the
issue of conservation of the resources as well as the livelihoods of those who live in and depend on the
environments. If properly implemented, it is likely to be an effective means of conserving biodiversity.
The approach is quite different to the conventional one in which barriers are erected around protected
areas and where poachers and encroachers are punished as the best means of conserving biodiversity
throughout the developing countries. The effectiveness of such an approach is sometimes doubtful for
two crucial reasons. Firstly, it is becoming apparent that unless the people who are most directly
impacted by conservation projects perceive that such projects serve their economic and cultural
interests. It is unlikely that any approach based on barriers and punishment will prevent unsustainable
resource utilisation in the protected areas over the long term (Environmental Defenders Office, 1994).
Secondly, where the land and its resources, which are owned by traditional customary landholders (not
by the State) are to be protected or conserved, agreements with the landholders have to be reached in
order for the land to be given up for such purposes.

It is commonly perceived now that ICADs represent a range of innovative approaches which resource
owners and users can relate to more positively and which may increase the probability of promoting
successful conservation of biodiversity (Brown and Wyckoff-Baird, 1992). Furthermore, ICADs offer
alternatives for economic and social developments, which do not deplete the very resources on which
human populations depend for survival. Successful ICADs may offer a means of balancing the needs
of the local people, the environment, and future generations.

In PNG the desire for economic development, and to some extent, the rural poverty are the primary
factors contributing to over-exploitation of natural resources and encroachment on to protected areas.
Integrated conservation and development (or ICAD) projects seek to:

e improve the quality of life of people living in areas rich in biodiversity, and

® promote the conservation and management of such areas.

The underlying assumption is that by increasing the options for local communities to manage their
resources for the benefit of current and future generations, better conservation will result. ICADs
provide local communities with economically and ecologically sound alternatives, the establishment
and strengthening of institutional capacities, improved information and an enabling policy
environment. It should be noted at the outset that ICADs are not cure-all and may not be the most
appropriate response in all situations (e.g., they are generally not appropriate when the resources are
critically endangered).

ICADs then, represent a new methodology and philosophy and, as such, are still very much in an
experimental phase. As a result, special flexibility is required of planners who might prefer to limit
uncertainty and to anticipate all possible outcomes. ICADs are probably more complex than standard
development projects, since the possible alterations that can arise from combining development with
conservation activities are just beginning to be understood. This reality is important to appreciate, yet
one should not be daunted by it. The most critical aspects of any proposed ICAD to comprehend are
the risks that are involved and the levels of uncertainty that exist. With this in mind, any new ICAD
project should be monitored as a test case, so that over time a body of analysis becomes available to
guide project design and decision making. In this way, the ICADs that are designed and implemented
today will be able to inform future conservation and development planning.

In a layman’s perception, the subject of economics deals with demand and supply of goods and
services, costs, prices and profits of national commodities in trade both in-country and abroad. It is our
view that this conventional economic practice is undergoing re-definition in relation to (environmental)
resource economics and needs to be reflected for the future of a ‘conservation industry’ in PNG.

In this paper we will be addressing the issue of ICAD incentives as to whether or not they are
conducive to conservation efforts in Papua New Guinea. To begin with, we would like to look at the
different agendas from at least three different stakeholders; local, national and global and these are
addressed at both national and local levels.

PAPUA NEW GUINEA'S NATIONAL AGENDAS

To discuss PNG’s conservation economics entails a more comprehensive coverage, which is beyond
the scope of this. paper. For the purpose of this conference however, some broad issues, which are
directly related to the broad principles of economics in terms of demand and supply need to be placed
in a better perspective. Since PNG is one of the main stakeholders in biodiversity conservation due to
its very high biodiversity status, we will be focusing our discussions on the question of ‘who demands
biodiversity conservation, that the ICAD process needs to address’ (see Genolagani and Saulei, 1997).

We consider that in PNG there are two types of agendas: national and global, which are generally being
influenced at both the national and local levels. Such agendas are thus discussed below.

National Level

_ At the national level, we can diagnose two important sources of influences, which in the end will
determine which way the government makes decisions; these are the national and international agendas
{see Sekhran & Miller, 1994). In so far as the national agendas are concerned, they are basically
addressing the issues of national aspirations for socio-economic developments, which are aimed at
generating revenues for enabling or facilitating mechanisms of providing goods and services, including
the development of infrastructure, and investments into sustainable development of natural resources.
In the process of planning and implementing natural resource development strategies, conservation is
more often than not being given low priority; the government’s objective is to look at both micro and
macroeconomics of development strategies (see Avei, 1996). The final desired outcome is to change
from a subsistence economy to a market-driven economy. Where does this place conservation
intervention, particularly insofar as biodiversity resources are concerned, in the economic development
that is an integral element of the much desired ‘social development’ for our people?

In implementing this revenue-generated objective from both subsistence and market driven economies,
the government faces a major stumbling block. The government has to borrow external capital to
initiate its development programs, and therefore it has to comply with the conditionalities of such
external borrowings. Our foremost experience is illustrated by the Structural Adjustment Program,
ARE ECONOMIC INCENTIVES FOR ICADS
CONDUCIVE FOR CONSERVATION
IN
PAPUA NEW GUINEA?

Simon M Saulei and John Genolagani*
Conservation Resource Centre, P.O. Box 165, Waigani, NCD.
* Department of Environment and Conservation, P.O. Box 6601, Boroko, NCD.

INTRODUCTION

degradation has threatened to shape profoundly the quality and future course of life on earth. The loss

and extractive purposes, such as tilling the soils and harvesting forest products. Thus, environmental

poor people dependent 6n the viability of the natural resource base. Environmental degradation is not
an issue that only the wealthy people have the luxury of addressing. It is an issue related to the failing
livelihoods, fuel scarcity, longer and harder working days, deteriorating health, hunger, and thirst of
most of the world’s people (World Commission on Environment and Development and the
Commission for the Future, 1991; Reid, 1995).

Integrated conservation and development projects (CADs) offer a new approach. for addressing some

resources for survival (Brown and Wyckoff-Baird, 1992; Sekhran, 1996). This approach addresses the

environments. If properly implemented, it is likely to be an effective means of conserving biodiversity.
The approach is quite different to the conventional one in which barriers are erected around protected
areas and where poachers and encroachers are punished as the best means of conserving biodiversity
throughout the developing countries. The effectiveness of such an approach is sometimes doubtful for
two crucial reasons. Firstly, it is becoming apparent that unless the people who are most directly
impacted by conservation projects perceive that such projects serve their economic and cultural
interests. It is unlikely that any approach based on barriers and punishment will prevent unsustainable
resource utilisation in the protected areas over the long term (Environmental Defenders Office, 1994).
Secondly, where the land and its resources, which are owned by traditional customary landholders (not
by the State) are to be protected or conserved, agreements with the landholders have to be reached in
order for the land to be given up for such purposes.

It is commonly perceived now that ICADs represent a range of innovative approaches which resource
owners and users can relate to more positively and which may increase the probability of promoting
successful conservation of biodiversity (Brown and Wyckoff-Baird, 1992). Furthermore, ICADs offer
alternatives for economic and social developments, which do not deplete the very resources on which
human populations depend for survival. Successful ICADs may offer a means of balancing the needs
of the local people, the environment, and future generations.

In PNG the desire for economic development, and to some extent, the rural poverty are the primary
factors contributing to over-exploitation of natural resources and encroachment on to protected areas.
Integrated conservation and development (or ICAD) projects seek to:

e improve the quality of life of people living in areas rich in biodiversity, and

e promote the conservation and management of such areas.

with particular reference to the forestry sector. In most instances, the conditionalities are such that
conservation agendas are included, and this makes it difficult for the government to entertain given its
development objectives. The cessation of Government funding of the Public Investment Programme
component of the UNDP GEF-ICAD Project since 1996 is an example — this has hampered the
development of macro-economic intitiatives. The overall ‘development’ projects within the ICAD
programs is another clear demonstration of this, where micro-economic enterprises are being pushed
for by the program proponents, both by the NGOs and DEC. Further, where conservation agendas are
included in the conditions of the borrowed capital, more often, the requirement is for micro-economic
enhancement, not for macro-economic initiatives. Thus, the state of affairs is that we have a conflict of
prioritisation; the national government faces a dilemma between the two influential agendas.

Local Levels

At the local levels, each community is defined by a societal hierarchy, with an individual as the
primary basis in identifying ‘hierarchical needs’. From each individual to the community levels there
are expressions of varied requirements, but on the whole these can be assessed as being the basic needs
and services where materials and finance, particularly income, are essential. Needs and services
include school fees for education, housing, health, water supplies, clothing, infrastructure (eg. roads,
bridges, wharves, airstrips) and transportation, that must be delivered by developers including the
ICADs in this context.

Global/Donors’ Agendas

The introduction of the concept of ‘sustainable development’ at the 1972 World Conference on Human
Environment and its translation at the 1992 Rio UNCED is a testimony to the modern ‘world order’.
The vested interests by the industrialised countries for the survival of their peoples through resource
exploitation in developing countries is a major imperative. Similarly the aspirations of developing
countries to attain industrialisation and or agricultural self-sufficiency is an opposing imperative. The
point of meeting of these imperatives has been manifested in a number of major global and regional
conventions primarily on the trade of national commodities. This broad context includes the GATT
and, closer to us, the development of the APEC and the Melanesian Spearhead Group. All of these
must contain conservation benefits. What are the implications of these developments for conservation
in PNG?

The Government under its function to protect our national sovereignty and interest has seen fit to take
on obligations to various international commitments and undertakings, especially the outcomes of the
Rio UNCED. The ones of interest at present are actions called for in the Agenda 21 and meeting
obligations of the Convention on Biological Diversity (CBD), the UN Framework Convention on
Climate Change (UNFCCC), and the Montreal Protocols under the Vienna Convention.

In short PNG is bound to preserve as much as possible intact large biodiversity holdings including its
forests under national protection. Their function being for such areas in PNG to continue supplying of
essential ecological goods (e.g. food, fibre, materials etc) and services (e.g. clean air, quality water and
natural environments for human inspiration) which should replenish degraded environments for a
sustainable global welfare.

The argument centres on the payment for the ‘opportunity costs’ to PNG in terms of socio-economic
development. This includes research and technology, direct international funding in terms of grants
and loans and technical assistance in various forms. The latter may include using the NGOs
(international and national alliances) and the private sector (conservation grants in project areas). Since
1992 most of the grant-funding has been through the Global Environmental Facility (GEF) - an
‘environmental fund’ controlled by the industrial countries because they contribute to the fund. In
addition the three executing agencies (UNEP, UNDP, World Bank) of the GEF similarly impose
conditions of their procedures on the recipients including PNG through the national Government. The
burden of implementing these conditions are difficult as they are filtered down to the provincial
governmental systems and the local communities, to supply the ‘conservation’ needed to meet national
obligations! The question that ICADs needs to address is whether the conditions, the processes and
procedures by various support programs is what the landowners or the resource owners want?

While it is noted that micro-enterprises are the current trend of development activities undertaken by
ICADs the mentioned conventions provide opportunities for export of our ecological goods and

services. The ones in this regard include the ‘green’ timber by portable sawmills and other reduced-
impact-logging operations, bioprospecting and carbon sequestration, which are covered by the CBD
and UNFCCC. Are these economic incentives necessarily contrary to the current development projects
in the ICADs, which are cottage industries?

In the same manner we queried whether the development of macro-economic enterprises such as the
mineral mining, oil and gas extractions, relevant fisheries, forestry and agriculture operations are
applicable to the ICADs? Or is the ‘shying-away’ from the macro-scale development projects dictated
by outsiders and not the ‘silent’ resource owners?

The current mode of the ‘D’ or ‘development’ in ICADs suggests that, by virtue of outside vested
interests, the global agenda is for biodiversity-rich countries, including PNG, to focus on micro-
economic development projects and activities for the ‘conservation industry’ by the ICADs. For
further elaboration on this point, see Sekhran (1996). This would not be what the national government
is trying to attain, with its emphasis on macro-economics.

WHAT?’S THE FUTURE?

The future of conservation in PNG will depend very much on how these needs and wants at both the
national and local levels are met and sustained. Further, PNG wants to protect its rights over its
biodiversity - through the Intellectual Property Rights legislation. As such the issues of both macro
and micro economic developments of its biodiversity needs to be adequately covered in any future
policy or legislative proposition or else the present approaches to conservation would not attain
fruition.

Are ICADs conducive to Conservation?

Empirical evidence at this stage of ICAD demonstration projects suggests that they (ICADs) may not
be conducive to biodiversity conservation. They have merely become academic and research oriented
data compilations and analysis, which are far removed in terms of providing the benefits that are
tangible to landowners and the communities at large. The international tied grants that provide the
seed resources both to the Government and the non-government sectors have a set agenda that limits
the level or the forms of resource exploitation that can occur. The resource owners at large are
persuaded to come to terms with longer periods of data gathering and analysis, while a quest for
“conservation conviction’ is being pursued with evangelical zeal. This is contrary to the beliefs of the
resource owners at large in wanting economic development, which promote higher tangible benefits
including income. Hence it is still unclear how far the ICAD approaches which are now being
demonstrated are in fact conducive to biodiversity conservation.
