wo

‘CONSERVATION AND DEVELOPMENT?’
OR PERHAPS
“CONSERVATION OR DEVELOPMENT’...
WORDS AND CONCEPTUAL LINKAGES USED IN SUSTAINABLE RESOURCE
MANAGEMENT

Larry Orsak
Bismarck-Ramu ICAD Project
C/- Conservation Resource Centre
P.O. Box 165, Waigani, NCD.

ABSTRACT

It is hard to promote conservation in Papua New Guinea, unless one understands how it relates to
‘development’ — the topic more often on the minds of Papua New Guineans. This essay uses word
twists to illustrate some connections between ‘conservation’ and ‘development,’ as well as discussing

what ‘conservation’ can entail and how it relates to the integrated conservation and development
(ICAD) process.

INTRODUCTION

What is conservation? What is development? And what aspects of those processes are unique to
Melanesia, versus being in common to all cultures? Such questions confront field practitioners, even
administrators, of ICAD (integrated conservation and development) projects throughout Papua New
Guinea (PNG). Those questions came out in many forms at the second ICAD Practitioners

Conference at Motupore Island. But they also appeared prominently at the first ICAD Practitioners
Conference in 1995... and at the 1993 CRI Workshop!

All this confusion highlights why concepts are so important, and why field experience, although
equally crucial, doesn't eliminate the need for all ICAD field practitioners to know the concepts behind
their work. Unfortunately, the concepts involved in an ICAD approach are complex and not quickly
learned. For example, ask ten people what ‘development’ is, and you'll probably get 10 different
answers. But it's time to make a stab at this educational need, particularly since the ICADs are
marching on. I will take a quick, rather simplistic approach in this paper. My goal is to lay a basic
framework that can be built upon by experience and further interactions and readings. I will do this by
playing around with all the ways we can link the words ‘conservation’ and ‘development’ together in a

phrase. In other words, through these word plays, I’ll explore how we can integrate ‘conservation’ and
‘development’ — in short, the ICAD process.

TWISTING THE WORDS CHANGES THE MEANING

Conservation for Development? Or Development for Conservation?

‘Conservation for development’ is quite different from ‘development for conservation’:
‘Conservation for development’: This describes the situation where
conservation activities are used as a tool to achieve a development objective.

‘Development for conservation’: This describes the situation where
development activities are the tool, and conservation is the goal.

We could accept one or the other of the above possibilities as an ICAD goal, but we cannot embrace
both. What is our goal in an ICAD project? Is it conservation? Or is it development?
Conservationists would probably say the goal is conservation, and thus embrace ‘development for
conservation.’ Villagers and rural development specialists, on the other hand, are more likely to have
‘development’ as their goal. To them, the accurate phrase is ‘conservation for development.’

Is each group correct in their assessment? Is it possible that both answers are correct? I think so. I
would argue that any conservationist who promotes ‘development for conservation’ really doesn’t
understand the relationship between the two processes. Why are we conserving natural resources,
anyway? One very important reason is so that we will have them for future generations, not just for
now. And what do we use natural resources for? Almost invariably, they are used not just to maintain
our lives, but to improve our lifestyles too. That ‘change for the better’ is another way of describing
the development process. Thus, conservation is for development. We’re conserving natural resources
so they can be used to achieve lasting development.

‘Conservation and Development’? ‘Conservation or Development’?

Or ‘Conservation for Development’?

‘Conservation and development’ suggests that we can pursue both processes at once. ‘Conservation or
development’ indicates that we can either do ‘conservation’ or we can do ‘development,’ but not both
simultaneously, because they are contradictory processes. On the other hand, accepting the phrase
‘conservation for development’ means that the whole reason why we’re doing conservation is to enable
us to have renewable natural resources available to sustain our development. Well, what is the right
answer? How are the two processes related to one another? And which phrase best describes the
ICAD process?

Villagers nearly always think that the issue is ‘conservation or development,’ which is why they reject
conservation. As Eddie Malaisa (Oro Conservation Project) once told me, villagers will always
choose development, and that can be frustrating for ICAD practitioners. But hold on. If / were given
such a choice — ‘conservation’ or ‘development,’ I would choose development too! That is the reality:
all of us will choose the development road, if we have to make a choice.

But there isn’t a choice, for reasons already given. If the customary resource owners of PNG do not
conserve what they have, then renewable natural resources will be depleted or destroyed entirely. Once
that happens, there is nothing left for tomorrow. Any development that those resources may have
brought to people will collapse, once the natural resources are used up, and the money that was earned
from those resources is gone. Without natural resources, how will people earn most of their money?
Without money, how can people pay for school fees? Clothes? How will people buy the food that
gives variety to their lives? How can people maintain roads and the PMV services without the money
that came from the use of their natural resources?

Natural resources are the fuel that runs the ‘development motor.’ As long as there is natural resource
fuel available, the ‘development motor’ keeps running. But if the fuel runs out, then the ‘development
motor’ shuts down and eventually rusts.

That little tok piksa illustrates the argument against using the phrase ‘conservation or development.’ It
also can be used to argue against the phrase ‘conservation and development,’ since what we’re
describing is a development outcome, which is not pursued alongside a conservation outcome. Instead,
conservation is an important tool needed to achieve development.

Thus, the most accurate way to describe the process seems to lie in the phrase ‘conservation for
development.’ Does that mean we should replace ICAD (conservation and development) with the
acronym ICFD (conservation for development) from now on? Not really, but only because we’ve
overlooked the ‘I’ — integrated. Integration means that ‘conservation’ and ‘development’ are somehow
linked, without specifying the nature of that linkage. Anyway, ICAD is an acronym that was coined by
Bruce Jefferies when he was in Papua New Guinea. Elsewhere in the world, the acronym ICDP
(integrated conservation-development) is usually used. Perhaps that is the most accurate acronym and
phrase to describe what we’re doing.

‘Development for Conservation for Sustainable Development?”:

A Better Way to Describe the [CAD Process?

If we choose the phrase ‘conservation for development’ over ‘development for conservation,’ then
we’ ll probably upset the conservationists, who are worried about ‘conservation,’ not ‘development’ as
the driving goal. Is there any way to combine the two philosophies in a phrase that fully describes
what we are doing, and reflects everyone’s goals?

12

Let’s think. The fact that most villagers are not concerned with long-term or sustainable development
needs to be considered. They will usually be driven by short-term development objectives. In many
cases, villagers are striving only for the superficial ‘trappings’ of development (e.g., roads). That is
why conservationists working in PNG are always pressured to provide short-term development
benefits. But they really have nothing to do with the long-term, sustainable development, which is the
ultimate goal of natural resource conservation, and is framed in the PNG constitution as a national goal
too.

Thus, ‘development’ fits into both the early stage of our process, as well as being the ultimate goal.
That being true, we need to take a broader view of the ICAD process. What is the full range of
objectives we are trying to accomplish? I submit this is the phrase that really describes the process:

‘short-term development incentives for conservation for long-term sustainable
development’

Do you have any problem with that?

‘Biodiversity Conservation for Development’? Or ‘Natural Resource Conservation for
Development’?

Those NGOs and other conservation bodies in PNG who receive money to implement ICAD projects
probably were told that the big goal is ‘to conserve biodiversity.’ ‘Biodiversity’ is short for “biological
diversity’ — in other words, all the living species, plants and animals, from protozoa on up to tree
kangaroos. Thus, when we talk about ‘conservation’ in ICAD projects, the donors expect that we’re
referring to ‘biodiversity conservation.’ They do not think that we are embracing non-wildlife
resources — fertile soil, clean water, or building materials — unless somehow the depletion of those
resources would hurt the biological diversity of the area.

When we speak about ‘conservation for development,’ and we have natural resources as the
conservation objective, then it makes sense from the village context and the normal PNG context.
Remember the argument I made as to why natural resources are critical for development? That outlines
the rationale for conservation natural resources in total.

However, if our conservation goal does not relate so much to natural resources in general, but is mainly
targeted at conserving biological diversity, are we accommodating the local context anymore? Are
villagers using tiny binatang right now to advance their development? Is anyone getting development
from the small lizards in their forests? Can they achieve development by selling the tiny sea slugs that
hide within their coral reefs?

Of course not! So: is it untruthful to tell people that biological diversity is for development, when the
fact is that the great, great bulk of biological diversity has no value at all in helping people achieve
economic development?

The answer is no: we are not promoting a giaman story when we say biological diversity conservation
is important for development. It is important, but in the longer term, and on more of a national level.
Those countries that conserve their biological diversity are protecting a much greater array of potential
drugs, chemicals, and other useful attributes contained within all those species, which some day might
be utilised in PNG’s development. Just think of all the useful things we get today from plants and
animals around us. After all, all our food crops originally came from wild species. If people had
destroyed all the wild rice and potatoes in the world centuries ago, today we would not have
commercial rice or potatoes to eat. A small binatang in the forest might have no apparent use today.
Yet, some day it may be the subject of research that finds a useful chemical, or whose study yields a
finding that helps improve people’s lives in a more indirect way. This is why we need all those wild
species, including the tiny insects. We must look after all of them — biological diversity in total ~
because we never know what species may be useful in the future, and which may not. That’s why the
conservation of biological diversity is important for long-term, sustainable development. Countries
that lose their biological diversity will have fewer options available in the future to maintain their
development, compared to nations which have wisely utilised their biological diversity, and conserved
it such that no species were allowed to go extinct.

13

Of course, this argument will probably be difficult to get across to villagers, especially those with
relatively little formal education. Nevertheless, biological diversity conservation is important for
people’s long-term development, so it is important for villagers throughout PNG. When we struggle
for biodiversity conservation, we aren’t overlooking the people’s needs. Quite the opposite: we have
the people’s long-term needs at heart, considering what they will require in the future, as well as what
they need now.

Another Consideration for Describing the ICAD Process: Local Values Versus Global Values
Any ICAD practitioner who works for an international conservation group gets their pay cheque not
because those international conservation organisations have an overriding concern with Papua New
Guineans conserving their natural resources, or even because they want Papua New Guineans to
harness their biological diversity for the country’s development. More, and more, they are interested in
seeing PNG’s forests, reefs, and the resident biodiversity conserved, because of the global values.
PNG’s species could help the world in numerous ways, as they are doing right now. They could spawn
a generation of new medicines that could cure diseases throughout the world, or provide our children
and grandchildren with oxygen, while absorbing the carbon dioxide produced by the world’s factories
and vehicles. Papua New Guineans everywhere gain something from the global value of biodiversity,
whether they live in the city or the most remote village. We all breathe air that comes from trees.
Whether we have water to drink is dependent upon the weather, which in turn, is dependent (as is
becoming increasingly clear) on how people everywhere in the world are managing their environment.
Papua New Guineans benefit from how other people conserve natural resources and biological
diversity, just as Japanese, Germans, Australians and Americans are gaining from the global values
secured in PNG’s biological diversity. That's the big picture the international conservation groups
are increasingly seeing as being fundamental. That is why overseas organisations, including the
United Nations, are willing to support ICAD projects and processes in PNG.

Undoubtedly this argument will be another one that is hard to ‘sell’ to villagers. Nevertheless, it can be
mentioned that villagers benefited from wild bananas when they bred more tasty cultivars from those
wild ancestors. They benefit from foreign forests every time they take a breath. They benefits from
Kiribati’s (another Pacific Island country) salt water when they catch a tuna. These are not potential
benefits — they are real ones! Papua New Guineans already benefit extensively from global
biodiversity and natural resource values.

Taking all this into account, perhaps we should revise our definition of the ICAD process and its goals
a final time. What about this acronym — STDIFCFLTSDAPGVAOU - which stands for:

“short-term development incentives for conservation for long-term
sustainable development and the protection of global values for all of us”

Well, that acronym will be hard to pronounce, and not terribly sexy to use in our project proposals.
But the phrase certainly embraces more of what we do, when we carry out an ICAD process.

CONCLUSIONS

ICAD projects integrate ‘conservation’ and ‘development.’ The reason why most of us here in PNG
think ‘conservation’ is worth doing, is because we see it as a way to help rural people achieve
development. In the short-term, natural resources are fuel for people’s ‘development engine.’ In the
long-term, that natural resource fuel will depend a lot on how much biological diversity remains
present. If natural resources run out, then the ‘fuel’ for the ‘development motor’ stops, and the
development collapses. When that happens, people will be poorer than they are now. They not only
will have no development, but they will have fewer natural resources too! Indeed, they will have less
than their tumbuna (ancestors) did!

If people in PNG really want sustainable development, then there is no choice but to start conserving
renewable natural resources, including PNG’s remarkable biological diversity. | Conservation is
essential if people desire development that will last. That, more than anything else, demonstrates why
we in PNG are implementing ICAD projects. Thus, it is a very important concept for ICAD field
practitioners to absorb, and to pass on to the customary resource owners with whom they interact.

14

‘ CCN’ Sia ait

If the relationship of natural resources with conservation with development is not transferred to, and
embraced by the customary landowners, then we can be assured that ICAD activities will never lead to
a lasting conservation outcome, much less any sustainable development for the village communities.
Instead, resource owners will constantly demand this or that ‘cargo’ in exchange for carrying out
conservation activities. Those conservation activities will immediately stop when the ‘cargo’ finishes.

On the other hand, if people come to see for themselves that conserving their resources is essential for
their own development and their clan’s future well being, then many problems will solve themselves.
The ‘cargo’ demands and ‘conservation blackmail’ will stop and the ICAD process can succeed.

In harnessing the local values of biological diversity, the ICAD process hopefully will secure that
biological diversity for its global values too. If the ICAD processes developed by Papua New
Guineans for Papua New Guineans can succeed at protecting these global values, there is no doubt that
more funding will flow into PNG to assist customary resource owners develop ICAD processes.

That’s the reason why every ICAD practitioner needs to know enough about the concepts, that they can
explain the difference between ‘conservation or development’ versus ‘conservation for development,’
as well as all the other semantic possibilities and acronyms — including STDIFCFLTSDAPGVAOU!

Acknowledgements

Attendees at the September 1997 ICAD conference provided considerable food for thought with their
general discussions and questions, which led me to write this essay. Special thanks to Dr Simon Saulei
and Mr Mike Parsons for stimulating some unifying thoughts presented in this discussion.

15

Is CONSERVATION THE ANSWER?

Barry Lalley
National Volunteer Service
P.O. Box 4073, Boroko, NCD.

INTRODUCTION

There is a growing concern about the environmental destruction taking place in PNG and the speed at
which the natural resources are being depleted.

Expatriates who are genuinely concerned with the welfare of the PNG people and their environment have
targeted conservation awareness and education as essential to combat this destruction. These expatriates
have seen the depletion of resources in their own countries and are alarmed at the possibilities, which exist
in PNG. In those countries, which have depleted their resources a conservation ethos is taking place.

However, to many Papua New Guineans the idea of depleting resources is often hard to comprehend. The
resources have always been there, they are there now and they will be there in the future. That’s how it’s
always been. To many the idea of conservation may be quite alien.

The question is should a conservation ethos be built in PNG? Can it be built? And how should such an
effort be undertaken? More importantly is the idea of conservation (like so many others) a western import
which only stands to confuse Papua New Guineans rather than empowering them? Are there indigenous
ways in which this issue can better be addressed than embarking on a ‘conservation’ campaign?

BACK GROUND

Dr. Larry Orsak, the former Director of the Christensen Research Institute (CRI) in Madang has published
an essay entitled Twenty Arguments for NOT Using Natural Resources Sustainably and the Weaknesses
With These Arguments: Villager Perspectives in Papua New Guinea.

In the essay Dr. Orsak draws on his experience and those of others who when talking with villagers are
given reasons why they (villagers) are not or should not use their resources sustainably. Dr. Orsak lists
each argument, why, he feels, the argument exists, how the argument ignores reality and a possible counter
argument.

In the Bismark/Ramu Integrated Conservation & Development (ICAD) project, a team of community
development workers has been assembled to work in the communities. Before each entry into the field
these community workers attend a 4 day briefing. The workers are highly motivated, have a good grasp of
development issues and the issues connected with conservation.

In November, prior to their entry into the communities, the training staff at CRI role-played the arguments
put forth in Dr. Orsak’s essay. Three things struck me throughout the session: 1) the community workers
did NOT respond the way Dr. Orsak did in his counter arguments 2) they were not as comfortable as they
usually are when we conduct sessions in which we do role plays and 3) they were unsure of themselves.

When the community workers were queried about number 2 & 3 above they claimed the questions were
difficult to address because there was no context to them. So much more was needed. It struck me, that
because Papua New Guineans see the world in terms of relationships, of course it would be difficult to
address these questions. Was a culture clash taking place? Certainly the western mind can simply
extrapolate a statement, study it and respond. Though the PNG mind could (which the community workers
did) it didn’t really make sense to them.

16

That evening, I was involved in a conversation with two other expatriates involved with the ICAD process.
One of my co-workers was sharing a story about a tribe of people in South America. During the course of
the story he mentioned how western scientists working with the tribe discovered the tribe-categorized
things quite differently from themselves. When my co-worker cited some examples of the things the tribe
put in the same category it struck me that the tribe placed these-things in the same category because of the
relationship between the items. This was quite different to the western mind, which placed the items in
scientific categories.

| connected this story with what had occurred with our community development workers earlier in the day.
They said it was difficult for them to answer the questions because they were “out of context”. Where was
the relationship? Who was involved? Why was that particular argument raised? It couldn’t have been said
on its own. What was the story? How was it said?

Later that night I thought about the days’ events. I began to question if conservation was what we should
be talking about. Again it’s a term and concept brought in by the west — or was it? Perhaps we could get a
conservation result without ever having to talk about conservation. Perhaps there were Papua New
Guineans methods, customs and/or beliefs, which we haven’t tapped in regard to our conservation goals in
the Bismark-Ramu ICAD.

The next evening I discussed these thoughts with Dr. Orsak. It was decided I would hold discussions with
some Papua New Guineans about these ideas and see where it all led — if anywhere.

DISCUSSIONS AND DISCLAIMER

The following section entitled “Discussions Summary” is just that — a summary of discussions I had with
Papua New Guineans. These discussions were not done in any scientific nor systematic manner nor was
the intent anything other than getting views of the topics referred to above.

In all, I held discussions with 15 people (6 women, 9 men). They ranged in age from early twenties to late
sixties. Their formal education ranged from university graduate to no formal schooling. Each discussion
was held separately with each person though wantoks and family invariably came and went during the
storying. Ten provinces were represented.

I took no notes during the actual discussions and very few after. I have used the most imprecise terms such
as most, some, several and few. In reference to Dr. Orsak’s essay, some people read it, while others I read
it to.

Basically the discussions could be broken down in two parts. The first was the concept of conservation,
traditional ways of looking after the environment and how villagers (today) could be assisted in looking
after their resources in a sustainable manner. The second part dealt with Dr. Orsak’s essay.

The Discussions Summary
Almost everyone felt their tumbunas looked after the bush. Everyone one talked at length about the
telationship Papua New Guineans have with the environment.

Basically it was felt that their tumbunas did not see themselves outside of nature. They were part of it.
Thus the community was made up of people (living & dead) the ground, the water, and the bush — hence
the spirits referred to as masalai. These are very real to Papua New Guineans.

Several people stressed we white people had no idea how Papua New Guineans related to the bush.

Masalai are spirits of the bush. They would let it be known if they weren’t happy or if things weren’t
Straight.

17

Given the above I questioned what had happened to that relationship with the masalai now. With the mass
destruction of the bush, ground and water something must have changed. People obviously don’t seem too
worried about the masalai. Every person said this wasn’t true. As a matter of fact this was a big issue
throughout PNG. Not only were people (Men) selling their bush and ground to outsiders, but they were
disturbing the masalai, which was causing havoc socially and spiritually.

In areas being logged, mined, fished it seems that when something ‘bad’ occurs invariably it has to do with
the masalai being upset — and then the argument being again about who’s responsible. In this case who
allowed the foreigners to enter and bugger up the resources. Apparently these are serious issues and come
up constantly.

Though the younger more formally educated try to downplay this, they don’t appear to be getting through.
In addition they have difficulty convincing even themselves, such is the strength of the belief in masalai.

When I mentioned an argument, which Dr. Orsak puts forth, that faim bipo their tumbunas did not have a
real conservation ethic, but places were ‘conserved’ by default (i.e. Malaria, masalai etc.) Most respondents
couldn’t understand why that mattered. To them that was looking out for the bush. They (the people)
weren’t looking out for the bush, the masalai were.

Interestingly, they again went back to their premise of people just being part of the whole scheme of things.
Two asked, ‘why do you white people always think we (man/meri) have to look after the bush? The bush
can look out for itself’. These two and a few others said, ‘We don’t look after the bush — the bush looks
after us.’ The oldest respondent said he was sure I had no idea what that meant. His last comment was
‘It’s not us (man/meri) and the bush! We aren’t separate! We’re one’.

I asked the respondents to try to explain how they could let the current devastation of their environment
take place given what I had just been told. They were each rather quick to let it be known many people
were upset with what was happening. Just because an area is being logged, for instance, - doesn’t mean
everyone in that area supports it. The respondents were in agreement in their feelings most of the people
didn’t support logging- especially the women. They cautioned me — don’t assume everyone supports the
destruction that is occurring. To the respondents this would be a terribly wrong assumption on my part.

Dr. Orsak’s Essay
The essay, of course, stimulated long discussions. The responses were not as varied as one would think.

Some people were more passionate in their feelings, but fundamentally their thoughts were quite similar.

The majority had three immediate responses: 1) Who is the target audience?; 2) Not many women were
consulted; 3) It’s too long. Almost all appreciated the efforts of a white man trying to assist.

It was also felt by many that the arguments were too western. In other words a reason is put forward and
then there is a response to that reason. The majority felt uncomfortable with this.

They (like the community development workers) needed it in a context. One said there is simply no way
for Papua New Guineans to really get a handle on a proper response unless more is known.

Several questioned if these were really statements by Papua New Guineans. Many said these kinds of
feelings were not how the majority of Papua New Guineans felt, especially women. Several people were

shamed at such statements.

When addressing whom this paper was meant for half said it should be for white people or highly formally
educated Papua New Guineans. The feeling was that the paper could have a reverse effect in that maybe
some Papua New Guineans who didn’t have these feelings now would. In other words the paper would put

‘wrong ideas’ in people’s heads.

Quite a few respondents felt the responses were too long and detailed. Again they went back to the target
group. They felt if the target group was the village people one didn’t have to go in too much detail. By

18

going into detail two things could occur: 1) the villagers could be confused, and 2) they would get ‘wrong
ideas’.

One interesting response was if these types of papers are given to Papua New Guineans, it could be
dangerous. People get a hold of a paper like this, and try to understand it. They don’t really get a handle
on it, but it doesn’t stop them from going to the village and giving out ‘wrong’ information.

One person also said the possible responses were too logical and would only make sense to the western
mind.

Several people simply felt it was too economic.

Most women and many men felt the paper was addressing a small group of Papua New Guineans. It was
felt the statements in the essay were not indicative of how most Papua New Guineans felt. They felt it
would be better to address the majority of Papua New Guineans.

When asked how they saw this being done they said by talking about culture, masalai and God. These are
the village arguments against the type of people who made the statements in the essay paper.
CONCLUSIONS

The discussions lead me to believe we must be careful in our conservation approach. I believe there are
ways we can achieve a conservation outcome without necessarily talking about it in western terms. Iam

convinced this has to do with relationships Papua New Guineans have with their environment.

It is important we acknowledge these relationships, understand that there is a logic and to work from within
that framework.

Regarding Dr. Orsak’s essay: a strategy needs to be formed as to who will be targeted and how they will be
approached. The.essence of the essay must be tailored for each audience, it needs to be turned into Tok
Pisin (and Hiri Motu) and it should be more concise.

Reference

Orsak, L. 1997. Twenty arguments for NOT using Natural Resources Sustainably and the Weaknesses
with these arguments: Villager perspectives in Papua New Guinea. Christensen Research
Institute, Madang

19

ECO-TOURISM: AN ALTERNATIVE ECONOMIC INCENTIVE
FOR INTEGRATED CONSERVATION AND DEVELOPMENT IN PNG

John Kambowa
Manager, Planning and Development
Tourism Promotion Authority
P.O. Box 1291, Port Moresby, NCD.

Eco-Tourism can become an alternate economic incentive for integrated conservation and development
programs in Papua New Guinea.

If eco-tourism is nature-related and environmentally sensitive, friendly, and sound then Papua New Guinea
is a natural choice with its natural resources. PNG has perhaps a unique advantage in the global arena
elevated by its unique and varied biodiversity stock that is of superior quality and of global prominence.

The Lakekamu Basin is one to note as stated in the 1996 Issue of the Biodiversity Conservation Network;
the area contains ‘over 200 bird species, 49 of that are endemic, and 84 mammal species, 15 of which are
endemic to PNG’.

To mention just a few, the province of Oro is known for the existence of the Birdwing Butterfly which is
the largest in the world, while the East Sepik is known for having the Scott’s Tree Kangaroo and Giant
Glider. The Milne Bay area has sea turtle nesting sites, dugong, giant clams, black coral, and whales.
There are Leather Back Turtle nesting sites in the Lababia area of Morobe Province, and across the country
are the many species of our Birds of Paradise, our National Emblem.

If we take into account all biodiversity stock held today within our pristine terrestrial and marine
environments, if we think of all the known and the unknown species with which we are blessed by mother
nature, we can realise the significance of such an abundance of treasures of nature, an abundance which has
almost disappeared in the industrialised world today. Of course, this very precious environment in PNG
forms the valued basis for the development of eco-tourism enterprises as an alternate revenue incentive for
every equity holder. The opportunities of eco-tourism development initiatives it offers are in abundance
and plentiful.

There could be more than 400,000 species of biodiversity in PNG, representing 5% of the world’s
biodiversity in less than 1% of the land area, according to a country study on biological diversity
commissioned by the UN Environmental Program, and published in 1994. This suggests the breadth and
length of opportunities in tourism to the world and especially nature lovers. In parallel, an American eco-
tourism wholesale representative during a familiarisation visit to PNG in 1994, who visited Kantobo, Wasi
Falls, Tari, Wagu in Upper Sepik, Karowari, and Madang has expressed in her report: ‘I feel I can now tell
people what to expect. That PNG is the last paradise left on earth. Everyone should see it to help preserve
it. Eco-tourists will go wild to know they are part of a grass roots tourist destination development. | will
have to qualify each client to know that they are right for PNG and vice versa’.

In PNG, eco-tourism activities in some form had existed before the use of the buzzword ‘Eco-Tourism’
became common practice. Most activities had been in areas where the Department of Environment and
Conservation had assisted in the process of setting apart some 37 listed conservation areas covering PNG.
Many tourists had traveled to these sites, as they still do today. Mt. Wilhelm in the Simbu Province
receives many tourists every year, as do the Variarata National Park in Central Province or the Balek listed
area in the Madang Province. These can be one part of the eco-tourism product in the wider picture. The
others could be ones currently being developed in Wildlife Management Areas (WMA) with assistance
from various integrated conservation programs sponsored by various environmental organisations like Mt.
Crater, Lake Kamu, or the Lababia in the Morobe province. Likewise many other components of the eco-
tourism activities can be brought into the spectrum. While also appreciating some success stories of self

20

help community projects like the Kunabau Women’s environmental program which recently received a
K3000 PATA Grant Award in an effort to conserve their environment for tourism in the Korinigl area of
Simbu Province which has a product associated with guesthouse development.

We all can now agree that truly PNG has the natural ingredients to develop competitive eco-tourism
products. Given these vast opportunities, many eco-tourism initiatives were undertaken in recent times
spread out in many rich resource areas of PNG. The players are various donor agencies, environmental
organisations, individual communities, individuals, and some private firms.

Some of these activities did not measure up to original expectations, some have been successful while some
are still in early stages. Most of these projects were driven by various motives. Some were driven by
conservation as the prime objective, some were driven by community peer group pressure for status, some
were done on a trial basis (test and see) without objectives, but very few were driven by market
opportunities and demand. Therefore, the success of varying projects depended pretty much on its original
motives, objectives and goals. For projects being driven by market opportunities, their success story is
translated into the bottom line — how much profit was derived at end of a fiscal year, and how much will
be the dividends declared to stakeholders. The success story in a market-driven scenario is the one that has
recovered or is prone to recover its investment returns over a period of time and realises a profit on top of
its principal investment.

The state of PNG encourages and promotes tourism development in PNG through its national policy and
Tourism Promotion Authority being sensitive to environment and culture which recognises the importance
of our very environment with minimal impact. Eco-tourism falls well within that guideline, and is one part
of the tourism industry as a whole. The cultural component is another in order to diversify general tourism
product and plant in country. Some benefits are to create meaningful participation among every
stakeholder of an eco-tourism activity. TPA encourages that an anchor product must be market-driven to
realise its viability and capability to feed spin-offs that can be widely recognised.

Profits derived each year must be fiscally transparent, and contribute to the overall cash economy of the
communities, thus resulting in an improved community standard of living. In other words, the tangible
cash benefits generated in a project is easily interpreted by the resource owners and agreed as meaningful.
The cash earnings could then be utilised on community improvements like water supplies, schools, or
dividend distribution, etc. Hence, the success of eco-tourism projects driven by market demand will be
sustained over a period of time.

Conservation programs may not be sustained if eco-tourism project developments do not equally emphasise

sustained economic viability. In a recent study program conducted by the Australian Institute of
Conservation, we had a number of speakers from various institutions who came and spoke about integrated
program opportunities in various conservation areas around the Pacific. Eco-tourism was one alternate
incentive, as widely discussed among many these days. One of the program speakers of WWF Pacific
Region stated: ‘Whatever happens to integrated projects in conservation areas, our prime objective is
conservation’. Of course this is true, but the statement implied all integrated conservation programs are
driven by conservation as the prime dictator, without regard to its viability, market assessment,
management, etc. This is something, similar to a bait, thrown in the water to a hungry fish, while the line
controlled by the man on the land manipulates the fish at his whim. So are the integrated project
developments in conservation areas. If this is the formula being adopted in integrated projects in PNG,
then there is the danger that all integrated conservation projects may not be sustained, and time will only
judge in the PNG context.

To have an eco-tourism project driven by market opportunities, one has to make a reasonable examination
into niche market areas in eco-tourism and accordingly develop a marketing strategy that is costed in an
annual work program.

Since TPA was established in 1993, TPA’s tourism marketing and promotional activities are more or less
broad-based to cater for all industry needs. The main focussed markets are Australia, Europe (Eastern),
North America (USA), and Asia (Japan).

21

i
i
i
i
ig

Most of the bona-fide travelers who arrive in PNG are attracted by a combination of products in PNG that
ultimately encompasses our virgin environment and cultural components. Evidently, most of this category
of people tends to see the real beauty of PNG by going into environmentally and culturally sensitive areas.

The visitor arrival into PNG are accounted by those who travel on business, holidays (FITs), visiting
friends and relatives, and others. Total arrivals in 1993 was 33,552, in 1994, 37,735, in 1995, 42,328 and
in 1996, 61,385, with the highest increase — of 45% — coming in 1996, when bona-fide visitors accounted
36% of the total visitor arrivals. A reasonable number of these arrivals into PNG had been to some eco-
tourism development sites.

Since we recognise the potential of eco-tourism, and cultural tourism development in PNG, TPA, in an
effort to strengthen community participation at the village community level, is currently developing a guide
manual titled ‘PNG Village Guest House Development Guide’. This will be a means to target markets, and
set a standard of quality products. The manual will be ready for distribution in November 1997.

Concurrently, TPA has also facilitated an Eco-tourism Lodge in Enga Province called “ Kumul Lodge” as a
case study among others while developing the tourism guide. The lodge is completed and is trading now.
It’s located at the foot of Mt. Hagen, right at the border of the Western Highlands Province and a fine
model for PNG because it was initiated, built, operated, and owned by the Akilaka Tribe of Enga.

To conclude; the TPA firmly believes that properly assessed eco-tourism project developments in any
integrated conservation programs in PNG can become a major source of revenue with many related spin-off
activities for communities. We mean projects must complement conservation initiatives with equal
emphasis, effort, and resources allocated to enable sustenance of the product. Ultimately, the project we
envisage must be market driven. In order to achieve an optimal result for all stakeholders, the TPA urges
every participant in any eco-tourism development to allow other activities that would provide sound basis
of a development plan that encompass natural resource inventory, product market assessment, product
marketing, training, conflict resolution, and environmental impact reviews. Reluctance to address one or
some of these components can cause deficiencies. Adrian Lipscomb, a travel writer and tourism industry
Consultant from Australia published a paper, ‘Village Based Tourism in the Solomon Islands:
Impediments and Impacts’, which concluded with following analogy: A chair has four legs. If some legs
are lengthened without modifying all four equally, the chair will become lopsided and unstable. So it is
with tourism industry. The four legs can be equated to the four main interrelated activities or needs of a
developing tourism industry. These are the product development, marketing, infrastructure development,
and training. If one or more of activities is ignored or inadequately addressed the tourism industry as a
whole cannot develop to its full potential — the chair will be lopsided, unstable and less functional.

22

TOURISM AS AN ECONOMIC INCENTIVE FOR CONSERVATION

Mike Parsons
Chief Technical Adviser
UNDP/DEC Biodiversity Conservation
and Resource Management Programme
P.O. Box 165, Waigani, NCD.

INTRODUCTION

While ICAD groups in Papua New Guinea are slowly increasing their interest in tourism as an incentive for
conservation, and for Conservation Areas in the South Pacific, supported by SPREP’s South Pacific
Biodiversity Conservation Programme, ‘ecotourism’ is the income-generating activity which attracts the

greatest interest. Ecotourism is defined here as nature-based tourism which directly assists in conserving
natural resources.

INDIRECT TOURISM

Before discussing the tourism option for conservation, there is a need to point out that indirect tourism,
selling tourism services without hosting tourists, exists as one of the preferred options for many indigenous
peoples in Australia and elsewhere. Two examples are (i) the wholesaling and retailing of art and craft and
licensing of designs to merchandisers and (ii) equity investment in tourist accommodation. In Australia the
vast majority of Aboriginal art and craft is sold to visiting overseas tourists, particularly from German-
speaking Europe and North America, but increasingly from Asia. They buy this art and craft from retail
outlets in cities, who get it'from the communities. The communities do not meet any tourists. But they get
the tourists’ money. The largest involvement of Aboriginal communities in tourism is in indirect tourism,

through the sale of art and craft. Similarly, some communities have invested money in hotel
accommodation, but few actually work in these hotels.

DIRECT TOURISM

Tourism is about selling a range of services to visitors. In direct tourism, the services are sold directly to

the tourist. What services are tourists most happy to pay for? This is a crucial question for communities to
consider when planning to become involved in tourism.

There are five main services: accommodation, food and drink, transport, an ‘entertaining’ or ‘learning’
experience, and souvenirs or mementos. While sometimes visitors to museums and other places are willing
to pay an entry fee, access fees — ‘paying to go through a gate or door’ — are low on the tourist preference
list. This is particularly the case when it is the gate-fee is paid to simply gain access to an area of land.

On the other hand, ‘gate-keeping’ is a preferred option for many landholders who are happy to earn a living
from resource-rent. In Samoa, many beaches are accessed through payment to a gate-house staffed by
landholders, though there are very few if any services provided when one arrives. In Australia, gate-fees
apply to Kakadu and Uluru National Parks, where Aboriginal owners receive a share of the receipts. Uluru
is owned by Aboriginal people who lease it to the National Park Service for an annual rental of $150 000,
but legal title was only granted conditional upon lease-back. In Kenya, small local private tour operators
pay Masai in the Masai Mara Reserve per-head fees for use of campsites on their land, as well as daily
entrance fees. In Namibia, operators have cut down on campsite fees by not having campsites — but a
drive-in, drive-out operation, that is environmentally friendly, but less lucrative to the local Himba tribe

who receive the per-head fee. On the other hand, groups such as Skeleton Coast Safaris encourage
Production of local craft (International Resources Group, 1992)

In Fiji, Tavoro Forest Park and Reserve, a protected rainforest on Taveuni Island, began by charging an

23

entry fee. According to Young, this local community-initiated, and community-managed ‘ecotourism
project on their land...is economically, ecologically and organisationally a success’. (Young, 1992) Why?

Tavoro’s success can be traced to:

1. Piggy-backing on existing tourist infrastructure and tourist demand.

Taveuni, a relatively small island, was an existing tourist destination, with the best diving in the country.
In 1991 when the Park was opened tourists paid US$8000 in nine months to enter. This was because
tourists were already on the island, staying at resorts, and looking for something else to do. All the costs of
getting people to choose to holiday at Taveuni, flying them to Taveuni and accommodating them at
Taveuni were borne by somebody else, not the Tavoro landholders. They didn’t have to entice people to
visit Taveuni — they were already coming to visit. They simply had to get them to visit Tavoro.

2. Community familiarity with tourists

Tourists had been visiting the area for decades. This had prepared people for an influx of visitors. But it
also altered them to negative impacts. They structured the tourist venture so that few visitors enter the
village, but simply visit sites away from where people live or work. The villagers can work as guides, and

women produce and sell handicrafts.

3. Trusted, Skilled Leadership
The community believed in the tourist project as an alternative to logging, because they trusted the people

who gave them that message. These included, from within the clan, a priest and the priest’s father, a
respected elder with skills in bookkeeping, and from outside the clan, but related to clan members, an
educated, young environmentalist whose Taveuni village had chosen to log. The Tavoro clan withdrew
from the logging agreement on the basis that tourists would pay money to visit. They believed this because
they believed the people who told them. The people who told them this story could bet on it happening
because of the level of existing tourist demand and infrastructure — see above.

4. Aid Money to Build Tourist Service Infrastructure
It was realised that capital was needed to supply services for tourists for walking tracks, picnic areas, a
visitor centre and signage. The New Zealand Government gave AUS$60 000.

Even greater levels of inputs can be seen in the Koroyanitu venture in western Viti Levu, Fiji. This is one
of the ventures being assisted by the South Pacific Biodiversity Conservation Project run by SPREP. At
the last Technical Management Advisory Group meeting, the Chief Technical Adviser reported that:

The tourism activities at Koroyanitu commenced with the development of a lodge at Abaca
village, funded by NZODA. With financial assistance from Jale Baba, the community bought a
pick-up truck to transport tourists to and from the lodge. The New Zealand defense forces
provided a helicopter to transport building materials to Batilamu Camp, on the cliff edge. Track
construction work has been undertaken by local people, with the assistance of volunteers from an
Australian NGO, Involvement Volunteers. The Fiji Co-operative Support Association has assisted
with the legalities of setting up the co-operative structures, and with auditing the books.
Academic staff of the University of the South Pacific have assisted by identifying species for use
in the development of interpretive materials. All of these activities have been initiated or
supported by the management structure established by SPBCP (SPREP SPBCP, 1997)

Who are the Eco-Tourists?
Ecotourists are not necessarily a single market segment, but many ecotours are oriented towards the

adventure travel and educational travel markets, while espousing an ethical component related to support
for indigenous cultures and local conservation initiatives (see Fig. 1 below).

How Do I Find Them?
It is more a matter of how do they find you? Where do they look?

In travel brochures, or travel guide books — are you included?

From newspaper or magazine reviews, in-flight and other travel magazines — have you featured?

At travel agents offices when booking a holiday — do the agents know about you? If yes, how did they
hear about you? Would they recommend you? If yes, why?

From nature conservation groups and societies — have you made yourself known to them?
Through tour wholesalers — how did they hear about you?

From tour brochures at airports, hotels, or tourist bureaus — have you printed and distributed any?
Have they run out? Are they up to date?

Trying to locate this market is not a simple affair, particularly if you are an isolated rural enterprise in
PNG! As you can see from the above examples, most such enterprises in the Pacific have depended upon
outside help, at least in the start-up phase, often quite substantial help. Where help is minimal, there is an
ability to piggy-back on an existing high level of tourist infrastructure and tourist traffic.

_ Samoan Ecotours.

Since 1989, the Western Samoa Visitors Bureau (WSVB) and the Division of Environment and
Conservation (DEC) and its predecessor have been promoting the notion of eco-tourism in Western Samoa.

Bamford’s report (1991) on environmental tourism in Western Samoa in 1991, recommended that the
Western Samoan Government demonstrate how eco-tourism could contribute to the management of
conservation areas. The following year the Western Samoa Tourism Development Plan 1992-2001
‘highlighted the need to integrate tourism with national environmental management goals in a manner that
allows for planned development without resulting in resource depletion’ (Sandford, 1993). | Sandford’s
1993 Visitor Services Plan discussed the nature of the eco-tourism market and nominated a couple of eco-
tours in the context of the negotiation of local area conservation trusts - the latter notion promoted by
Pearsall and Whistler (1991), though with a somewhat checkered history at Tafua Peninsula.

{n 1993, funds were obtained from the New Zealand Government overseas development aid program

i

(NZODA) by the WSVB to develop and implement a National Eco-tourism Strategy Plan. The author was
commissioned to undertake this task. Initially this was achieved through designing a pilot programme to:
e test the level of interest in the market
e provide a role model for other operations
® provide training opportunities for Western Samoa tour guides and
generally indicate the problems and potential for specialised tours.

In its submission to the NZODA, the WSVB defined eco-tourism as that kind of tourism, which is:
e based upon relatively undisturbed natural areas,

® non-damaging, non-degrading, low impact and small-scale,

e a direct contributor to the continued protection and management of the areas used, and

® subject to an adequate and appropriate management regime.

Sandford (1993) declared ‘eco-tourism is a special form of nature-based tourism and is based upon four

principles:

e@ Small scale, low impact and small numbers. This means minimal development and infrastructure.

e The motivation of the tourist to see natural areas and to learn about them.

e A commitment by landowners and tour developers/investors to manage sites for conservation
purposes.

e The economic benefits gained from eco-tours are used to support conservation and development’.

This model of eco-tourism can thus be conveniently summarised in terms of method, motivation and
management. ,

The means or method employed by the eco-tour operator to access and utilise the touristic resource is
explicitly chosen on the basis of best minimising negative and enhancing positive social, cultural and
environmental impacts.

The general motivation of eco-tour operators are expected to reflect that of their clients; a deeply felt
professional and private ethical commitment to ecologically sustainable, social and culturally responsible
tourism. This commitment underpins the specific motivation behind the tour design by the operator, and
the purchasing choice of the customer. Thus the expert, in-depth tour interpretation offered, and the
expected attitude and behaviour of one’s fellow eco-tourists during the tour is part of the overall tour
product on sale.

Most importantly, eco-tours overtly aim to assist in the management of the environmental resource that
provides the basis of the tour experience, rather than simply using it as a commercial or recreational
opportunity. This may mean for example, that part of the tour cost funds a resource management plan or
organisation involved in resource management, or purchasers directly participate in hands-on resource
management activities as part of the tour itself.

But in all the above definitions, it is still obviously quite possible, in the Samoan context, for eco-tourism to
provide minimal income and employment to Samoans, as it is silent on the questions of ownership and
control. Indeed, in Western Samoa, there was a foreign-owned and -operated surfing safari business, a
foreign-owned and -operated water sports business and one foreign-owned rural area resort claiming to be
‘trekking experts’. Thus, it was felt that Samoan ownership and control of eco-tour operations is added to
the above definition, with the specific objectives of the WSVB Eco-tourism Program being:

" To assist villages in developing ecologically sustainable and commercially viable alternatives to the
exploitation of rainforest timber and coastal reef life.

= To assist the conservation, environmental education and cultural revitalisation programs of Samoan
villages and their organisations such as the national environmental NGO, O Le Siosiomaga and the
island-based conservation society Fa’aaso Savaii

# To help fund field research into the maintenance of biodiversity, and the preservation of representative

26

ecosystems and of rare and endangered species by staff of the Department of Environment and
Conservation.

The role of the Western Samoa Visitors Bureau was to identify potentially viable opportunities for eco-
tours, to promote these opportunities to those most appropriately placed to act as eco-tour operators and to
market the new ventures to the public. In doing so, the Bureau works closely with the Department of

Environment and Conservation. The prospective proponents, are village communities, private sector tour
operators and Samoan non-Government conservation organisations.

The strategy followed these steps:

Identifying key areas where biodiversity conservation was under threat

Ascertaining potential eco-tourism resources in those areas

Consulting the village leaders regarding their interest in having the WSVB and the Department of
Lands, Survey and Environment visit them to talk about tourism as an alternative income source.

Conducting a full tourism awareness programme in each village community observing the full
customary Samoan protocols for hosting guests.

5. Conceptualising eco-tourism products with communities
6. Assisting communities in developing these products
7. Trialling these products.

In a separate parallel strategy, a move was made to identify possible local operators for a national eco-tour
venture. This would solve the problem, of internal transport, by supplying the tourists to the village
enterprises by ‘eco-bus’. The role of this venture was thus to link the village-based enterprises into an

overall eco-tour programme. This idea became the very successful company, Eco-tour Samoa, winner of
the 1997 Tourism Council of the South Pacific Eco-tourism Award.

In its operations Eco-tour Samoa found that it had to provide the following:

Culture (cultural interpretation - enhancing indigenous cultures)
Adventure (challenging oneself - soft or hard adventure but safe)
Nature (assisting biodiversity conservation)

Education (travel-learn experiences - guests & villagers & operators)
Development (participating in sustainable development initiatives)

® ® 6 @ 6

But while satisfying the above definition of eco-tourism, it also found that its tours needed to be value for
money. They needed to contribute to local social/environmental issues. They needed to be informative and

professional. They needed to complement local sustainable tourism ventures. For all this, they also needed
to be well organised.

Eco-tour enterprises need to satisfy six disparate needs: to satisfy the niche traveller; to influence travel
industry members; to co-opt governments and regional tourist organisations; to pursue equitable and
sustainable human development; to assist researchers, and; to help find solutions. This is already a
demanding task. But in addition, eco-tourism enterprises must meet the requirements of local governments,
special interest groups, academic researchers, local community groups and conventional tourists.

THE ECONOMIC POTENTIAL OF NON-TIMBER FOREST PRODUCTS
FOR ICAD PROCESSES IN PAPUA NEW Guinea!

Simon M. Saulei & John A. Aruga*
Biology Department, University of Papua New Guinea,
* Department of Environment & Conservation, P.O.Box 6601, Boroko, NCD.

SUMMARY

Forest resources have always been and will continue to play an important role in the lives of millions of
people, whether it is in Papua New Guinea or elsewhere into the future. Like all ecosystems, forests are
complex, dynamic entities in which an impact on one or a few components is likely to have ramifications
throughout the system. Today, forests are seen as potential renewable resources to be used as export
commodity, raw materials for industries and as a means for developing infrastructure, creating
employment and small business opportunities over a short period of time, particularly in isolated and
yemote areas of the country. Much of the current utilization of the forests is basically for timber, while the
Non-Timber Forest Products (NTFP) are being ignored. This paper presents a review of the status of
NTFP and their future potential in Papua New Guinea. In particular, their potential in generating
additional foreign exchange earnings and revenues for the Government and the local people or resource
owners. There is however, more to be done in respect to research and development of these products as
well as their promotion at both national and international markets. Fundamental changes in attitudes of
both the government and traditional landowners towards forests as a ‘ free resource’ for ‘cashing in’ is
required, if the forests and their multiplicity of products are to be conserved and developed sustainably into
the future. Thus, forests must be considered as an important renewable resource, more valuable in the
long term as sources for cultural values and identity, timber, wildlife, food, medicine, etc. Further, it is
vital that some ecologically, socially and economically viable management systems be researched and
developed for implementation so as to protect, utilize and conserve the forests for future prosperity of the
country and its people.

INTRODUCTION

Throughout history, forests have been valued for their multiplicity of products and benefits that they
provide, both for subsistence and trade: food, medicines, spices, resins, gums, latexes, wildlife, fuelwood
and of course, timber and other wood products. However, today, from a development standpoint, forests
essentially have been seen as a source of just one product: wood.

This perspective has often resulted in intensive shortsighted use of wood resources to the detriment and
even destruction of the rest of the forest ecosystem. Particularly hard hit have been the forest dwellers; the
resource owners, who depend heavily on the forest resources that were being depleted and who often
shared little in the benefits derived from the extraction of timber.

More recently, however, the belief that forest are of value only for wood production in a macro-economic
context has gradually been modified in face of overwhelming evidence to the contrary, combined with a
growing concern over providing sustainable benefits at both national and local levels. It is now apparent
that forests provide a whole range of other products and benefits, most of which have long been known and
utilized by the local people, and of which many are still essential to their survival.

The challenge we face today is to better assess and quantify the values of these products and transform the
use of as many of them as are commercially, socially and ecologically viable from one of subsistence to
one of development; i.e., to bring them into the mainstream for forest products sub-sector planning and

‘Part of an original paper published.in the Commonwealth Forestry Review 1994

29

policy making alongside the already well established timber products of national and international
commerce. At the same time ensuring a lasting benefit to local people. These efforts must form an integral
part of a comprehensive and realistic approach to realising the full potential of forestry for conservation and
sustainable development.

In Papua New Guinea the forest resources have had, and will still continue to play a vital role in sustaining
the livelihood of its four million people, one way or another. For the most part, 85% of the population will
continue to depend very much on the forests for their supplies of various products, which are directly or
indirectly derived from plants and animals that exist in the forests.

The country is fortunate in having over 70% of its total land area still covered by forests of one kind or
another (Saulei, 1990). From the tidal mangrove and lowland rain forests of the coastal plains to the mossy
or subalpine forests of the highlands (Paijmans, 1976). The forests and other vegetation types in the
country support some of the most unique and diverse flora and fauna in the world (Saulei, 1991). Although
the forests are biologically diverse with profusion of both animals and plants, the underlying soils, in
general, are relatively poor as almost all the nutrients are bounded up in the living biomass. Once the forest
cover is removed through traditional shifting and commercial cultivation, logging, mining and other
infrastructural development, rapid loss of nutrients may arise through erosion and leaching. These
processes may effectively transform the once luxuriant forest into wasteland if such areas are not
rehabilitated or able to recover naturally (Saulei, et al., 1992).

As the country’s economic activities expand and the population continues to increase, an even greater
pressure will be exerted on the forests and other natural resources. To date, forests are seen as a ‘free
resource’ which must be utilized to generate more revenue for national development and for offsetting
foreign debts, provide raw materials for the industries, create employment and small business opportunities
(Saulei, 1991). However, a fundamental change in attitudes by both the Government and the customary
resource owners throughout the country should be the starting point for any substantial reforms for
conservation and sustainable development of the forest resources. This is because these resources cannot
continue to be seen as another resource to be ‘cashed in’, but instead must be considered as being
renewable. Thus, if properly managed, the forests would continue to supply timber, wildlife, food, ete.
both for the present and into the future. Economically, socially and ecologically accepted systems of
managing the forests have to be devised for sustained development of the forest resources. Sound
reforestation, managed natural forests and rehabilitation programmes have to be developed and
implemented in those areas already degraded. These are tangible steps that must be taken. Further, the
establishment of protected areas such as parks and reserves and the development of non-destructive timber
harvesting techniques need to be encouraged and developed for implementation in order to sustain the
forest resources throughout the country, before it’s too late.

The utilization of non-timber forest products (NTFP) in the country has a very large and promising
potential for the future and therefore, need urgent assessment and evaluation to reduce the current heavy
dependence on timber as the only major economic commodity that can be extracted from the forests.

In this paper, a review of the past and current status of non-timber forest products, their future potential and
development, the impacts of their extraction on the socio-economic environment of local communities, the
problems of their management, and research and development requirements in the country are presented.

TRADITIONAL UTILIZATION OF NON-TIMBER FOREST PRODUCTS

The forest resources in Papua New Guinea have for many centuries, formed an integral part of the life of
the rural communities. It is the source of supply for a host of products: materials for house construction,
canoe making, cooking utensils, clothing, fuel, medicine, sorcery and adornment. Even today, these very
resources are still very important and have formed part of the subsistence economy of many rural
communities throughout the country. For instance, betel nut (Areca catechu. L.) has become one of the
major income earning commodities for many rural villagers, especially along the coast. Likewise, a myriad
of non-timber products such as edible nuts, fruits, vegetables, roots and wildlife have also supplemented the

30

economy of the villagers. For example, Powell (1976) has recorded the many uses of non-timber forest
products that the forests have provided to the people. Other similar recordings of these uses and many
others can be seen in some of the following references: Peekel (1983), Holdsworth (1977), French (1986),
Haberle (1991), Woodley (1991), and Saulei & Aruga (1994).

The history of early uses of the forests and their multiplicity of products can be dated many centuries ago
from archaeological evidences of human habitation. In Papua New Guinea, it is acknowledged that man
has been in the highlands for at least 11,000 years, in marginal areas of the highlands for some 26,000 years
and along the coastal plains some 50,000 years ago (Bulmer, 1966, White et al., 1970; White, 1972;
Golson, 1981). The first arrivals were mostly hunters and gatherers who became agriculturalists only
recently. Today the majority of the people are subsistence agriculturalists; growing mainly food and tree
crops that are of value for their traditional uses and practices. Powell (1976) listed some 251 species of
food plants in the country, of which 157 species (63%) were gathered from the forests, savannas and
grasslands, another 51 species (20%) were both cultivated and harvested from the wild and 43 (17%)
comprised those that were always being cultivated. Presented below are brief summaries of these more
commonly used food plants and others in the country:

(i) Tubers/corms

Staple foods in Papua New Guinea are predominantly starch, comprising tubers such as yams (Dioscorea
spp.), sweet potatoes (/pomoea batatas (L.) Lamk.), cassava (Manihot esculenta Pohl.); corms such as taro
(Colocasia esculenta (L.) Schott) and the related Araceae (Cyrtosperma chamissonis (Schott) Merr.) as
well as the recently introduced Xanthosoma sagittifolia (L.) Schott. In addition, a non-tuber/corm plant:
sago (Metroxylon sagu Rottb.) is also being utilized as a staple food source in many coastal areas, most
prominently in Gulf and Sepik Provinces. Some of the recorded tubers and corms used as staple food in
the country are given below (Table 1).

(ii) Fruits

According to Powell (1976), 48 species of plants served as fruits in Papua New Guinea. With increased
interest in ethnobotanical studies this figure will be altered in the future. Breadfruit (Artocarpus altilis
(Park.)Fosb.) is an important fruit tree which is widely used in the coastal areas as supplementary food
source. Others include Syzyggium malaccensis L., S. aqueum (Burm.f.) Alston., Paratocarpus venenosa
(Zoll. & Mor.) Becc., Berckella spp., Diospyros spp., Garcinia spp., Gnetum gnemon L., Magnifera minor
B1., Morinda citrifolia L., Spondias dulcis Forst. and Pangium edule Reinw. In addition, there are number
of recently introduced fruit plants such as Mangifera indica L., Psidium spp., and Cucumis spp., which are

31

now cultivated and utilized by the local people. Many species undoubtedly still remain unidentified in
some remote areas of the country.

(iii) Nuts

There are number of nut tree species being used as supplementary food source in both the coastal and
highlands areas of the country. Some are important to villagers in specific areas and in many cases these
are individually owned in the forest with seeds often being transplanted around the villages and in gardens.
Canarium indicum L; and C.solomonense B.L.Burtt. are widespread along the coasts and the major islands
forming an important seasonal food supplement in these areas. Other nuts of importance are collected from
Cocos nucifera L; Terminalia catappa L., T. kaernbachii Warb., T. coplandii El\m., Coode and T.
solomonesis Exell.. Some of the nuts, which are commonly used as seasonal supplementary foods in the

country are presented in Table 2 below.
