(iv) Vegetables

Many of the food supplements mentioned above are usually prepared with a combination of vegetables.
Powell (1976) for example, listed 26 species of plants as supplementary vegetables, of which the most
commonly used are: Gnetum gnemon, Amarathus spp., Colocasia esculenta, Setaria plamifolia (Koenig)
Stapt., Cucumis spp., and ferns (Asplenium, Athyrium, Ctenitis, Cyathea and Dryopteris).

(v) Wildlife

In Papua New Guinea, wildlife plays an important role in the villagers’ everyday life. Traditionally,
various forms of wildlife have special symbolic significance to the people. The legends are full of
characters depicting animals and birds. Different clans have special relationship with particular form of
wildlife, which serves as their totems.

For many communities living in the rural areas, the different birds (cassowaries, pigeons, parrots, scrub-
fowls) and animals (wild pigs, wallabies, cuscuses, bandicoots) that they hunt, form an important part of
their diet. These wildlife provided the villagers much of their protein requirements which would otherwise
be absent in their daily diets. Hides and feathers of many of these animals are extensively used for clothing
and decoration and are also highly valued in traditional exchanges.

A number of these forms of wildlife have special ceremonial significance. For example, cassowaries are
highly priced in the Highlands for bride price exchanges, traditional compensation payments as well as for
singsing decorations. By present day standards, a live cassowary is valued at about K2,500 in the
Highlands. With the advent of modern monetary economy, wildlife has become another important source
of cash income for many rural villagers.

In its various forms, wildlife has provided Papua New Guinea with a great deal of importance in its cultural
identity and dietary requirements. From the utilitarian point of view, its conservation makes good practical
sense. However, for most villagers the value of wildlife extends further: it is something that gives life its
identity, richness and diversity.

(vi) Medicinal Plants

Throughout the country, almost all cases of sicknesses, injuries and deaths are, one way or another,
attributed to supernatural powers. Even minor accidents, cuts, bruises and general body pains are
considered being inflicted by outside forces. Glick (1972) distinguished between ailments, which are
socially insignificant and are usually treated in a simple, practical way, and illnesses, which are much more
serious, requiring explanation in terms of sorcery and witchcraft. This demands responses, which involve
the use of rituals, and magic for which both plants and animals from the forests are used.

Plants used for healing ailments or illnesses have played an important role in the lives of the villagers for
many centuries. Treatments of ailments and illness are quite varied, ranging from treatment of cuts and
wounds, insect and snake bites, skin diseases and sores, general body pains and swellings, ear and eye
infections, coughs and colds, respiratory complaints, dysentery and diarrhoea, aids to birth control, fertility,
ritual and magic. The use of medicinal plants in the country is well documented (Floyd, 1954; Stopp, 1963;
Panoff, 1970; Straatmans, 1971; Womersley, 1973; Powell, 1976; Holdsworth, 1977, Peekel, 1983;
Woodley, 1991).

A REVIEW OF THE NON-TIMBER PRODUCTS INDUSTRY
The non-timber forest products commonly referred to as ‘Minor Forest Product Industry’ in Papua New
Guinea started in the late 1700s and is still being continued to the present. In the early days the industry

was carried out in a small scale and involved such products as bark, rattan and sales of butterflies and
orchids. Presented below is a brief review of the early status of this industry.

33

|
|
q
i

(i) Copal Gum

Copal gum is an alcohol soluble resin obtained by tapping the bark of Kauri trees (Agathis spp.). In the
country, the Kauri resources are found in natural stands in both East and West Sepik Provinces with about
two dozen stands containing an estimated 200,000 trees (Zieck, 1975a; 1977). Other stands have also been

located in the Central, Morobe, upper Madang and New Britain Provinces.

The Copal industry initially started in the Western part of New Guinea (Irian Jaya) in the early 1900s and
later introduced to West Sepik Province.

The Kauri resource is also found in other parts of the country, particularly along the river valleys of
mainland and the islands of Milne Bay, Ambunti - April/Salome area (East Sepik Province), while the
most extensive stands of Vatica is found in the Western Province. The Kiunga area has been estimated to
have a density of about 5 Vatica trees/ha (Zieck, 1975b). While in Milne Bay Province, especially around
Tagula Island (Pere Plains) it is estimated to contain 400,000 trees, averaging between 15 — 17 trees/ha
(Zieck, 1978). From official statistics, export of Vatica papuan Dyer, from Tagula Island started in 1887.
Over a 50 year period from 1925 -1975 a total export via Samarai amounted to 2, 383 tonnes (or 47.64
tonnes p.a.) were exported with a value of between US$260 -$5,209/annum (Zieck, 1975b).

(ii) Massoy Bark

Massoy bark (Crptocarya spp.) trade in South East Asia started as early as 1603 in Indonesia with the
establishment of the Dutch East India Company. In the early years of the Dutch East India Company the
massoy bark trade, especially in West New Guinea (Irian Jaya) yielded commensurate profits with average
price of $4360.00 per tonne (Zieck, 1973). Papua New Guinea did not have an export trade in massoy
bark in the early years of the 1900s, except for the border areas of West Sepik Province.

Massoy was exported to Germany during the years of former German Administration of German New
Guinea. However, export figures could not be traced. Likewise, some exports of massoy took place,
especially via Samarai during the former British Administration of British New Guinea (Lane-Poole, 1925),

but again there were no formal records.

In 1970 a quantity of some 500 kg of massoy bark was shipped from Port Moresby to Hamburg for
flavoring liqueurs (Zieck, 1973). Further, a third of a ton was shipped to Singapore in 1972. There is a
market demand in Asia for massoy bark and although the export of massoy bark has continued for a
number of years, no accurate records are available. Massoy bark resources are widely distributed in the
following areas: East and West Sepik Provinces, Kiunga and Bosavi (Western Province), Kikori, Mt.
Karimui, Aseki, Kokoda, Afore, Baniara, Ramu and the Finnisterre Range areas.

(ili) Tannins
Traditionally, colour in terms of body decoration and painting effects had some prestige in most local
communities throughout the country. Tannins were extracted from barks of trees, roots or leaves for body
decoration or personal effects. Such commodities like red clay and extracts from mangroves played
important roles. For many coastal villagers, mangroves provided the body decoration ingredients, while in
the highlands red clays, which are mixed with Bixa are used. Rhizophora spp. are the best producers of
tannins, while appreciable amounts could be extracted from Ceriopsis, Bruguiera, Lumintzera and

Zylocarpus.

The tannin industry in the country was established at Aird Hills, Gulf Province in 1954 (Zieck, 1970).
Chopped barks of mangroves were treated by continuous counter-current extraction with cold then warm
water to produce an extract called ‘cutch. The tannin industry lasted only for three years after which the

factory was closed due to:

PEV (polythene vinyl) market competition in fishing net preservation market;
Mangrove tannins were unsuitable as an ingredient for adhesives,

Mangrove tannin had the tendency to produce impart red colour to the finished leather; and,
High fixation had the tendency to produce ‘ empty’ leather products.

34

Presently, tannins are used mainly for the production of local tapa cloths, especially in the Northern and
Morobe Provinces.

iv) Rattan

Papua New Guinea has approximately sixty species of rattan (Calamus spp.). There has been irregular
exports in small quantities in the late 1900s (Zieck, 1976). However, the industry has boomed in the last
five years, especially in the form of raw materials. The export market has picked up recently largely due to
export bans on raw rattans from Indonesia, Malaysia and Philippines. A large number of processing plants
have been set up in the country and presently the export value of rattan averages about US$2.5 million per
year. The potential for export of rattan is very bright, at present the prices range from US$750 per metric
tonne (PNG Forest Research Institute, 1990). However, to maintain future production schedules, the need
to harvest the commodity on a sustained basis requires future resource inventories, research and
development, otherwise the same experiences faced by the neighboring South East Asian countries may be
repeated.

Currently, there are numerous rattan exporting companies scattered throughout the country. Although no
accurate figures are available, it may number some fifty companies and the potential for the rattan industry
to be expanded into a cottage industry seems very promising.

Papua New Guinea has a vast rattan resource, especially in the Sepik and Gulf Provinces with appreciable
volumes found in Madang, Morobe, Northern, West New Britain, Manus and Milne Bay Provinces.

The rattan industry was established in the 1970s with the view to export raw materials as well as furniture.
Although accurate figures are not available, there are a number of furniture manufacturing industries in the
country, which presently cater for the local markets.

With the increase in demand for rattan, the Forest Research Institute is currently engaged in research
activities aimed at estimating the extent of the resource, monitoring and promoting of the rattan industry
and studying the regeneration potential of rattan in logged-over areas.

(v) Sandalwood

Sandalwood (Santalum magregorii F. Muell.) export started in the early 1900s. However, there are no
accurate records of the exports even though the resource is quite widespread (Zieck, 1970). According to
the Department of Forests records, the export value for this resource fluctuated from year to year. In 1978
the export value of sandalwood was equivalent to about $A11,000 for 6.80 tonnes and in 1984 the value of
export exceeded some $A45,000 (Department of Forests, 1986 — cited in Paul, 1990). Sandalwood is
continuously being exported, but accurate figures of the volume exported are not readily available.

THE CURRENT STATUS AND FUTURE DEVELOPMENT OF NON-TIMBER FOREST
PRODUCTS INDUSTRY

The non-timber forest products industry in the country seemed to have simmered down after the 1970s and
this is, perhaps, due to the departure of some expatriate officers and the absence of qualified national
officers to continue the industry. Although a number of individuals, especially expatriates, continued to
export small quantities of such products such as Copal, Massoy, Sandalwood and Rattan up to the late
1970s, the industry was still not well established. The only non-timber forest product, which has become a
big industry in the country in recent years, is the rattan cane.

(i) Rattan

Raw rattan processed and sold to countries overseas has up to now become an annual foreign revenue
earner; about K2.5 million (US$2.7 million) was generated, for example in 1989 (PNG Forest Research
Institute, 1990). Although the industry is still in its infancy, the potential for the product to become
established as an income generator for rural villagers has not been fully realised. Presently, there are about
fifty registered rattan exporting companies in the country which earned between US$350 - $750 per metric
tonne of rattan export (PNG Forest Research Institute, 1990).

35

(ii) Insect Ranching
Being located in the tropics, the country has one of the richest insect fauna in the Asia-Pacific region. Man
has utilized the by-products from activities of insects for many centuries, notably honey from bees and dyes
from various insects such as cochineal. Insects also provide a nutritious food source in various parts of the
world. In many parts of the country for instance, sago weevils are treated as delicacies.

Insect farming or ranching in the country has not being well developed, except for butterfly farming.
According to Collins and Morris (1985) the first example of successful farming of butterflies and other
insects today is a very lucrative business. A recent estimate of US$20-30 million has been taken as a
conservative earning. According to Mercer (1989), export trade from Taiwan alone is worth US$20-30
million. Collins and Morris (1985) also considered a figure of US$100 million per year as not excessive.

Insect export business in PNG has been limited to a few overseas entrepreneurs since the turn of nineteenth
century. This industry however, provided very little income to the local insect collectors. Clerfas (1979 —
cited in Mercer, 1989) gave an example of a villager being paid twenty cents ($0.20) for a butterfly which
later was sold for US$250 overseas.

It is not possible at this stage to give an accurate account on the prices of insects exported from the country
as prices in the overseas markets. Presently, the Insect Farming and Trading Agency (IFTA) pays
approximately K2 per pair for the common green birdwing butterfly (Ornithoptera poseidon) and K3.50 for
the Bougainville birdwing (O. priamus urivillianus). Other insects have prices ranging from K20 per insect
depending on their rarity.

In the first three years of operation by IFTA (1978-1981), 4, 000 shipments of butterflies and other insects
were received from local collectors and farmers with payments amounting to US$180,000. Collins and
Morris (1985) estimated that in 1983 the annual trade in butterflies amounted to US$110,000. Today this
figure is estimated to be about US$250,000 per annum (Mercer, 1989).

There is still some reluctance on the part of many people to be involved in insect farming as many still
regard it as a supplementary source of income. In 1983 a diligent farmer could earn an annual income of
US$1,200. Today, this figure would be about US$3,000 per annum, which is quite comparable with
earnings from coffee and other small holder farmers in the agricultural sector.

(iii) Orchids

The orchids of Papua New Guinea have over the years received wide literature coverage dating back to the
nineteenth century. According to Schlechter (1914) the first orchids to appear in Europe from the country
was from German New Guinea in 1823. These orchids identified as Oberonia gladiata A.Rich., Carteretia
paniculata A.Rich. and Denfrobium marcrophyllum A.Rich. were collected from New Ireland. By 1900s
resulted in the recording of 1,102 orchid species new to science.

The orchids of Papua New Guinea have since being subject to numerous investigations manifesting the
country’s richness in orchid flora. Schlechter (1914, 1982) wrote ‘there is consequently in New Guinea a
richness in orchids which is not even approximately reached in any of the neighboring territories of similar
latitude, nor indeed in any other country of island complex of similar size in the world’. °

Presently, about 2751 species of orchids, representing 134 genera have been recorded for the country
(O’ Byrne, 1992).

Though the total number is impressive, there are undoubtedly many more species yet to be discovered.
Export market in orchids have become a big business in Papua New Guinea however, most have been taken
out of the country illegally, mostly by private collectors. The situation remains the same with private
collectors paying up to $A10.00 per plant. The industry involved are the National Botanical Gardens,
Private collectors and small farms (e.g. Amos Orchids in Lae). Orchid farming is a big industry in many
countries and in Japan, the industry earned more than 4.4 billion Yen in 1987 (Suto, 1988).

36

The potential in PNG is here, but requires further encouragement, research and development.

Many orchids have been taken out of the country despite the fact that some genera have been listed under
CITES as protected species. The slipper orchids of the genus Papliopedilum are one such example. In
order to address this problem, the Department of Environment and Conservation since 1990 imposed a ban
on the export of wild orchids from the country. The main objective of this ban is not to deny the world
access to the country’s orchid genetic resources, but to allow the country and its people, time to take the
initiative to research, develop and conserve its own natural resources. This is in view of the fact that
information on the distribution, abundance and biology of the country’s 3000 orchid species is far from
complete.

The ban had drawn criticisms from overseas collectors, who argued that the ban is a negative attitude by the
Government, especially when these plants are continuously being destroyed by logging, mining and other
infrastructural developments. Others still argue that the world market already has access to cultivated PNG
orchids, therefore there is no need for such a ban, nor a need for an orchid industry in the country.
Realising the fact that world trade in orchids is a thriving business, the attitude taken by overseas collectors
is being short-sighted. For example, Japan alone turned 13 million orchids which generated some 4.4
billion yen in 1986 (Suto, 1988).

Most orchids taken out of the country by collectors are purchased from local collectors who may be paid as
low as low as $A2.00 per plant whereas the real value could be ten times more. An article in the September
1984 edition of Orchidian advertised sales of 23 PNG orchid species at prices ranging from $A7.30 to
$A14.50 including 25% postage and handling costs by a collector in Edinburgh, United Kingdom
(Howcroft, Pers. Comm.). These orchids were collected in 1900s. Most people support the view of
Howcroft (1990) who wrote ‘the issue of orchids being destroyed by logging or other destructive operations
should not be seen as an excuse for plants to be collected by overseas collectors nor the reason for
abolishing ban on orchid export’.

One way of solving the problem of orchid exploitation by overseas collectors is to establish an orchid
industry based on artificial propagation in various places in the country have failed. However, it can only
be proven viable through research and development which up to now has been non-existent, except for
hybridization.

(iv) Mushroom

Many varieties of edible mushrooms thrive in the moist, humid tropical forests of Papua New Guinea.
Most are gathered from the forests and utilized as supplementary food source. World trade in mushrooms
is a thriving enterprise, especially in Japan, where shiitake (Lentinus edodes) alone had a production value
of 42.5 billion Yen (US$28 million) in 1987 (Hara, 1988). Shiitake mushrooms occur naturally in South
East Asia, Australia and Papua New Guinea.

The cultivated trials of shiitake in Papua New Guinea were initiated in 1974 at Bulolo, Morobe Province.
Initial trials showed that daily temperatures were too high to produce high quality shiitake, though the
yields of the mushrooms were quite promising. The trials were than transferred to Mendi, Southern
Highlands Province, where conditions were more favorable for shiitake cultivation. The industry initially
thrived in Mendi with sales of dried shiitake being sold in Port Moresby for US$10 per kilogram.

In 1983 a FAO mushroom expert was engaged by the Government to examine the possibility of growing
shiitake commercially in Papua New Guinea. This report suggested that shiitake cultivation in the country
could be ideally suitable for economically depressed areas of the highlands. On the basis of this
recommendation, several small farms were established in the Southern Highlands. However, lack of funds
has hindered further expansion of these farms for setting up shiitake on a commercial basis. The potential
for shiitake production in the country for local consumption still remains high and only requires further
funding and commitments, both from the Government and local farmers.

Other common mushrooms, especially Glamerella spp., Tuber spp., Canthrelus spp. and Boletus spp.,
which is scarce in Japan, could also be farmed in the country. According to Hara (1988), Japan imported

37

dried shiitake from China, South Korea and Taiwan totaling 893 tonnes at a value of 1.5 billion Yen
(US$10.36 million) in 1982. There is a large world trade in dried shiitake mushrooms and demands are
starting to exceed supplies (Arentz, 1983). The country could have a thriving mushroom industry provided
that research and development of the industry is done to investigate its commercial viability.

(v) Sago

Sago (Metroxylon sagu) is an important staple food source, which is found in all parts of the country, but
has an extensive development in the Gulf and Sepik Provinces. Chemical analysis of sago indicated that it
comprised mainly starch and water with small amounts of protein and other microelements (Calcium —
10mg, Phosphorus — 12.5 mg, Iron — 1.5 mg per 100g of edible portion — Barrau, 1959). Average yields of
sago have been recorded at between 110-136 kg per trunk from near-flowering palm and up to 400 kg from
palm that are naturally sterile (Barrau, 1959). Powell (1976) has recorded the art of sago making and
preparation. Sago is a multi-purpose palm. Its leaves are used for thatching house roofs and walls, fronds
as wall claddings, midribs are woven for fish traps and the pith offer excellent substrate for mushrooms and
sago grubs.

(vi) Seeds

Although seeds of different native plants have been utilized for centuries for both traditional and
commercial purposes, little attention has been directed to them, except for those that have become
commercialized with established markets, such as cocoa, coffee, coconut and cardamon The National
Forestry Authority (formerly the Department of Forests) through the Forest Research Institute (FRI), has
been conducting seed collections and research in collaboration with CSIRO (Australian Tree Seed Centre)
over the last thirty years into the use of various native timber species seeds for use in forestry plantations.
For more information about such collections see Turnbull et al. (1983) and Gunn et al. (1989). To date,
such collaborative research efforts have resulted in the high demand for seeds of a number of these timber
species for use in forestry plantations both within the country and abroad. In particular, the seeds of Acacia
mangium Willd., Eucalyptus deglupta Bl., Araucaria cunninghaii Ait., A. hunsteinii K.Sch. and
Terminaliaa brassii Exell.. In addition, there is a growing demand for palm seeds. However, the extent of
commercializing the sales of many different native plant species seeds has yet to be established.

AN OVERVIEW OF SOCIO-ECONOMIC IMPACTS OF FORESTRY
DEVELOPMENT

In many less developed areas of Papua New Guinea, local forests represent the only major source of
income which can be exploited to obtain greater access to the cash economy and to the goods and services
such as those available in economically active parts of the country. Local communities often welcome
forest operations which bring immediate cash payments from royalties, access to roads, transport and
community facilities as well as full-time or occasional wage employment, opportunities for expansion of
small business enterprises or sales of garden produce and NTFP to company employees (Saulei, 1992). For
these villagers, forest developments may also mean increased government and economic services and
easier access to major centres. Under such circumstances, villagers have downplayed or disregarded
cultural or subsistence uses of the forest resources as they consider immediate financial benefits as being
more important than traditional values, beliefs and practices. These situations all came about because there
were no alternatives or the discussions of the impacts and possible benefits being offered to them (i.e., the
villagers).

The developments of forest resources in the country is a complex process involving landowners, local
enterprises, public servants, local and national politicians and foreign logging companies. Each set of
factors in this process has its own goals and perceived benefits or disbenefits, which will result from the
project or from the way in which the project is being implemented. It is therefore not surprising to find
that conflicts and tensions arise between groups or individuals that may have very different views on how
the local forest resources should be exploited at all. One of the prominent features that come out very
clearly from these conflicts is the question of ownership. In Papua New Guinea, the various village clans
traditionally own over 97% of the total land area. Most foreign investors see this land tenure system as
being problematic, creating uncertainties about the long term resources and land securities for development.

38

Today, the impact of forest development in the social lives of the villagers has become more profound.
Less time is spent on subsistence activities such as fishing and gardening, as the villagers are content with
goods such as tinned fish, rice and other items provided from the trade stores. Alcohol consumption among
men is one of the major causes of social problems, while gambling is affecting rural lives, especially among
women. Increases in the number of unmarried young women becoming pregnant and cases of adultery and
broken marriages are also becoming more regular. Other inherent social problems include:

a. Loss or disregard of traditional values, practices and beliefs;

b. Communal based social relationships and activities being altered to individual household or immediate
family basis

c. Less community involvement in maintenance of services for schools, aid posts and church activities;

d. Socio-cultural disruption as a result of cross-cultural differences between the local people and those
employees/employers who come into the project sites;

e. Increases in sexually transmitted diseases;

f. Renewed disputes among clansmen over land ownership titles when economic benefits become more
profound; and ,

g. Less concern about environmental protection and traditional ways of conserving the forest and
wildlife.

In many parts of the country the situation has changed, especially in those areas that have been subjected to
previous logging operations. Initially the villagers would welcome the royalty and other financial benefits
for buying trucks or starting businesses. However, many are becoming more and more aware of the
consequences of development. They have now seen and realised what unplanned and unmanaged logging
operations could do to their land and traditional forest products. Many did not fully understand just what
the environmental and other consequences would be until logging had actually commenced (O’Collins,
1990). They only began to realise that the attraction of financial compensation had blinded them from
seeing other costs. Many have seen their food trees, wildlife, cultural sites, building and firewood
resources vanishing, or are unable to have access to medicinal plants (De’Ath, 1980). Only then have they
realised for the first time, the consequences of forest exploitation without proper planning and
management.

In order to assess the potential or actual impact of forest operations in a particular community or network of
communities, it is essential that the different social values placed on forest resources by the landowners are
understood by government policy-makers, forest managers, developers and other decision makers. It may
also be true that local decision-makers may not be sure of the long-term implications of the project. On the
other hand, they are eager to gain direct financial benefits, which will come from commercial logging.

At the same time they may be concerned about the future and the impact which these new developments
will have on their families, local communities and the environment. Likewise, the confusion may be
compounded by the fact that they may not be sure of what will happen after logging operations have
ceased. It is only through open dialogue with the local landowners as well as through the implementation
of properly drawn up operational and environmental plans together with systematic monitoring that these
impacts will be minimised and benefits being equally shared by both the developer and landowners.

MANAGEMENT ISSUES ASSOCIATEDWITH NON-TIMBER FOREST
PRODUCTS (NTFP) DEVELOPMENT

The task of forest managers is to maintain or increase the productivity of forest resources, while at the same
time protecting them from over-exploitation with the aim of providing essential products and services and

39

simultaneously allowing for the needs of the rural people (Lamb, 1977). With respect to NTFP, the
challenge therefore becomes one of assisting ‘development’, while at the same time promoting the
continued and even increased utilisation of these products on a sustainable basis.

Ironically, in some cases increased awareness and understanding about the importance and the economic
potential of NTFP has been directly linked to the processes that have resulted in their destruction. As
cultures and communities move towards a marketing economy, the subsistence use of NTFP tends to
decline. Unfortunately, this decrease has generally not been paralleled by an increase in the management

of selected NTFP as part of the development process. As a consequence, traditional knowledge about these
products can be lost. Far too often the true value of NTFP to the community and the nation is only

understood when the sources have disappeared through lack of appropriate planning, management or
narrow-vision development for short economic gains.

Hence, the first step towards improved management of NTFP is policy re-orientation. For the most part,
NTFP constitute a neglected resource due to ignorance which must cease. In formulating land-use plans,
forest policies and the evaluation of development projects, the utilization and impact on NTFP resources
and their potential role in the rural and wider economy should be considered routinely without exception.
This will require changes in attitudes supported by policy and legislative changes to ensure that NTFP as
well as timber resources are equitably valued, conserved and developed on a sustainable basis.

One of the fundamental dilemmas to sustained development and management of NTFP resources is the
apparent lack of knowledge about the resources themselves, the demand for and the commercial value.
Ideally, the compilation of a sound management plan requires knowledge and understanding of the
complete life cycle of the species concerned, their interactions with other species, as well as an
understanding of their present role and future potential in both local and national economic development.
Unfortunately, little or nothing is known about the inter-relationship between the majority of NTFP species
and their surrounding environment. Likewise, the basic qualitative and quantitative information
requirement for estimating present and future productivity as well as their demands are often lacking.

The management of NTFP in their natural habitats or in plantations is essential if their full economic
potential is to be realised. This is important as it can provide local benefits and offer another source of
resource to be exploited, apart from timber. Although timber extraction would be lower, this could be
offset by the extraction of NTFP resources on a continuous basis and also would yield the benefits of
greater ecological stability inherent in less intensive timber extraction processes.

Vesting management control of forests to owners would not only minimise the problems associated with
land ownership and conflict of use (Saulei, 1992), but would also renew the incentive for conservation and
sustainable management of the resource once present, but now weakened by external threats to the resource
base. This local control is also justified on an ethical ground, as this is a recognition and protection of the
owners’ traditional rights of use and to their land and resources that sustain their way of life.

For the non-timber forest products to have long term future and be of benefit to the local people, it must
have an environmentally, economically and socially sound management policies and regulatory controls.
The current policy and legislative control measures which are embodied in the National Constitution and in
both the Forest and Environment & Conservation Acts are aimed at wise management and utilization of the
resources. However, the present trends of resource developments are not conducive to the set principles
and thus need to be properly scrutinized by the responsible Government Departments/Agencies; namely the
Department of Environment & Conservation and the National Forest Authority. In respect to the
management and conservation of flora and fauna, the Government through the Department of Environment
& Conservation has adopted the conservation and management policy, which controls the following:

a) commercial, trading and export of birds of paradise, crocodiles, butterflies, orchids and other flora and
fauna considered endemic or endangered;

b) over hunting or collection of wildlife and plants;

40

c) interference of villagers’ rights to wildlife and plants by traders, collectors, researchers and visitors;
d) destruction of unique habitats; and,

e) establishment of National and Provincial Parks as well as locally owned and controlled reserves,
wildlife management areas and sanctuaries.

RESEARCH AND DEVELOPMENT OF NON-TIMBER FOREST PRODUCTS (NTFP)

In many tropical countries, the export value of NTFP far exceeds some of the major export commodities.
This situation may reflect the attention that such countries have given to research and development of these
products as another source of their export commodities.

In Papua New Guinea, research and development of the NTFP was and still is being conducted by the
Minor Forest Products section of the PNG Forest Research Institute, Department of Forests. This section
has conducted research into the utilization of rattans, copal gum, massoy bark and Vatica resin until late
1970s. However, since then, very little has been done to extend research and development and the
promotion of these products. The potential contribution of NTFP to national development remains very
promising, but that a lot has yet to be done.

The above constraints to effective NTFP management, therefore suggest that research into NTFP should be
increased, especially in respect to their abundance, distribution, variation, ecology, reproductive biology,
traditional and new methods of propagation, cultivation and use, identification of market and non-market
values (also see Paul, 1990). Because of the nature, use and importance of NTFP, which are so closely
linked to local ecological, economical and socio-cultural conditions, most research will need to be location
specific. Case studies should identify the various uses of NTFP, the extent of these uses and how they are
changing. Such studies should also identify who uses NTFP and which groups are most dependent on them
for their subsistence and income-generating benefits. The potential of new products should also be
considered. To be more effective, such research must be interdisciplinary, long-term and participatory,
especially in regard to the resource owners, the Government and the industry.

An understanding of marketable and non-marketable values is also important. Unfortunately, there is often
no commercial yardstick for measuring the value of many of these NTFP used in the domestic economy of
rural communities.

Research into the improvement of NTFP, either by increasing the population of the desirable species or by
improving the yield, must be fostered. Within the natural forest, increased populations can be produced by
ensuring that the micro-environmental requirements of the desired species are enhanced through the
application of appropriate silvicultural treatment such as pruning and liberation thinning of both the desired
and undesired species. Further, productivity may also be improved by selection and breeding in established
orchards, plantations or natural forests, but this can only be attained through long term research on the
desirable characteristics of the selected species with ample financial support (Saulei, 1990).

BOTANICAL PROSPECTING AND CARBON OFFSET CONCESSIONS
IN FOREST MANAGEMENT AGREEMENT AREAS

Mike Parsons, Chief Technical Adviser
Simon Saulei, Tropical Forest Ecologist
Conservation Resource Centre
P.O. Box 165, Waigani, NCD.

INTRODUCTION

Papua New Guinea has come to a stage where it has to address the issues of both development and
conservation. In considering economic and social developments it has to take into account the limited
resources it wants to utilize, the impacts of developing such resources on the environment and their
sustainability, the population growth and its needs in the future. We have to devise appropriate
methods of how to utilise the same resources for a multiplicity of products and benefits.

Natural forests offer a multiplicity of products that are either currently being extracted or have yet to be
realised. Thus, apart from developing the forests just for timber, we can use the same forests for
extracting other non-timber products such as for food, building materials, medicine and also for
offsetting carbon or other greenhouse gases emitted into the atmosphere.

In this presentation we would like to discuss the issues of using the forest under current or future
concessions for a combination of end products:

e timber, carbon offset and bioprospecting; or

e carbon offset, reduced impact logging and bioprospecting; or for,

e carbon offset and bioprospecting alone.

Prior to discussing these two latter possibilities, we would like to provide some background
information for our suggestion that such options could be carried out under the current Forest
Management Agreement (FMA) arrangements of the National Forestry Act.

Background

Forestry in Papua New Guinea is governed by the Forestry Act (1979) Revised (1991) and as Amended
(1993). In this Act the Forest Authority is given the responsibility or mandate to “manage, develop and
protect the Nation’s forest resources and environment in such a way as to conserve and renew them
as an asset for succeeding generations’.

Therefore, under this Act, the Forest Authority is the main Authority for issuing permits, license and
authority to any person or bodies registered as Forest Industry Participant to harvest, sale, export or
process any forest products for commercial purposes. The export permits for plant materials or
specimens other than trees for education/research purposes are issued by FRI. The export of trees is
controlled by the NFA.

Forest Management Agreements (FMAs) and Timber Authorities (TAs) of the Papua New Guinea
Forest Authority (PNGFA) gives the management rights for ‘forest produce’ to the State.

Under the Forestry Act ‘Forest Produce’ is defined as:
Growing or dead trees, whether-standing or fallen; and
Any part of or any product of such trees; and
Any other vegetable growth, whether alive or dead.
Forest industry operations means harvesting, sale, export or processing of trees or other forest

produce for commercial purposes, but does not mean the traditional use of trees or other forest
produce.

45

i
i
i
i
i
f

While the section of the Act dealing specifically with FMAs omits references to ‘forest produce’ and
deal explicitly with timber rights, Section 60 of the Act sets out ‘Rights under Forest Management
Agreement’ as follows:

Where the State enters into a Forest Management Agreement... The exclusive right of
cutting and removing timber from the area covered by the Agreement and of planting,
growing and managing timber on that area vests in the Authority and in persons claiming
under the Authority.

Section 87 sees timber authorities being given ‘for the purpose of the removal of trees on areas
designated for agriculture or other land use by the relevant authorities; or road-line clearing, outside the
area covered by a Forest Management Agreement; or for the purpose of harvesting of other forest
produce to an amount specified in the timber authority’ (our emphasis).

It is in the sections dealing with enforcement and regulations that ‘forest produce’ and the related
‘forest industry operations’ take centre stage. A sample of these are as follows, (our emphasis):

122. OFFENCES
(1) A person, who carries out forest industry operations except under and in accordance
with a timber permit or timber authority, is guilty of an offence.

(zj) the regulation of transfer pricing in relation to forest produce and timber.

Thus it is very clear that under the Act, FMAs and TAs include management rights over forest produce.
However the PNGFA’s commercial utilisation of these rights is currently limited to logging
concessions under the FMAs and TAs of the new Forestry Act, 1991 (Sect.58 and 87, PNG Forest
Authority, 1991).

There are opportunities, however, for commercial concessions to be let by the PNGFA for non-logging
activities, encompassed in the broad definition of forest produce and forest industry operations under
the Act. Botanical prospecting and carbon offset trading are two such non-logging examples.
Biological prospecting is the search for new ‘wonder drug’ medicines from ‘forest produce’ by the
world’s pharmaceutical companies. Carbon offset trading is selling the forest for its ability to reduce
carbon dioxide emissions to overseas companies. Both of these activities are multi-million kina
industries involving companies equal or greater in capitalisation than those currently involved in
logging in the country. Widening the use of ‘forest produce’ rights obtained under FMAs and TAs to
the issuing of concession agreements for botanical prospecting and carbon offsets would have the effect
of:

® Widening the options for a financial return to landowners on the utilisation of their forests.

e Broadening the commercial utilisation of rights by the PNGFA obtained under FMAs

® Encouraging a more rational, efficient, balanced and holistic approach to forest management by the
PNGA.

BOTANICAL PROSPECTING

World-wide sales of western medicines exceed US$200 billion. In 1996 sales by the fifteen largest
pharmaceutical companies were between US$5 to US$13 billion. Many of these companies are now
looking to secure rights to potentially valuable naturally occurring medicinal compounds. The areas
where one is most likely to find these medicines are in exceptionally high biodiversity rainforests, such
as those possessed by Papua New Guinea.

47

Before the 1992 Biodiversity Convention, pharmaceutical companies pirated the biodiversity of
developing countries. For example, Eli Lilley derived two medicines from the rosy periwinkle of
Madagascar earning them US$100 million a year, but Madagascar got virtually nothing. The rosy
periwinkle was endemic to Madagascar, now the major source is Texas — Eli Lilley ‘pirated’ it, and
grows it there for itself.

Similarly the entire adult population of the plant producing the anti-cancer drug maytansine was
harvested by US botanical prospectors in Kenya, some 27,215 kg for the American drug development
program. Kenya did not benefit from this harvest.

Under the 1992 Biodiversity Convention, to which PNG is a party, each country’s sovereignty over its
biodiversity is now recognised. Each country agrees to enact its own legislation for bioprospecting
under fair terms. In 1991, the huge American pharmaceutical company Merck, signed a bioprospecting
agreement with Costa Rica worth US$1,135,000 to that country.

In Suriname, Bristol-Myers Squibb has a negotiated bioprospecting agreement, involving the
Saramakaner tribe, Conservation International, and the US National Institute of Health.

Countries such as the Philippines and Indonesia are now enacting their own bioprospecting legislation.

Papua New Guinea has no bioprospecting legislation or national policy, although this is required under
the Biodiversity Convention it has signed. But bioprospecting proposals are currently being submitted.
According to sources at Griffith University, the Queensland Pharmaceutical Research Institute has
proposed a AUS$100 000 agreement for botanical prospecting in Papua New Guinea, with a Swedish
pharmaceutical company, whose annual sales exceed US$5 billion. In this proposal, Papua New
Guinea, however, is said to receive only 0.3% of net sales of any drug discovered. Other research is
also being conducted. The American Smithsonian Institution has just completed a preliminary
ethnobotanical survey in two areas of PNG in cooperation with environmental NGOs.

The PNGA could negotiate botanical prospecting agreements under existing and future FMAs and
TAs, which may ensure proper returns to landowners and to the nation for forest produce containing
active medicinal compounds. [t would also enable adequate protection for the resource and promote
sustainable harvesting. Four billion US dollars (US$4 billion) is currently being spent in
biotechnology, and is expected to reach US$50 billion by the year 2000. If PNG manages its very high
biodiversity properly through the conservation of forests, which is the role of the PNGFA, it should
attract a large proportion of this investment.

The National Botanic Gardens in Lae, which are under the Forest Research Institute, and a division
within the PNGFA, may play a key role in facilitating botanical prospecting agreements. NGOs such as
the Christensen Research Institute and Conservation International and the Universities could also
provide scientific and technical facilities.

The PNGFA will need to liaise with the Department of Environment and Conservation, as it is the line
agency for the Biodiversity Convention, on the drafting and passing of botanical prospecting
legislation. Such legislation will need to cover marine as well as terrestrial environments. However,
some ambiguities remain in the legislation for the protection of Papua New Guinea terrestrial flora.

The Department of Environment & Conservation regulates flora export, import and re-exporting by
issuing permits through the International Trade (Fauna and Flora) Act (Convention on
International Trade in Endangered Species of Wild Fauna and Flora, known as CITES), but only
for those that are listed in the CITES list. The Department carries out this function through the
Management Authority and Scientific Authority, which are defined in the Act as follows:

Management Authority means:

e the person, body or body of persons designated by the State to be the Management Authority for
PNG in accordance with Article IX of the Convention; or

e in relation to any particular fauna or flora to which this Act applies, where two or more
Management Authority specified by the State as the relevant Management Authority in respect of
the fauna or flora.

48

Scientific Authority means:

e the person, body or body of persons designated by the State to be the Scientific Authority for PNG
in accordance with Article [X of the Convention; or

e in relation to any particular fauna or flora to which this Act applies where two or more Scientific
Authority have been so designated — the Scientific Authority specified by the State as the relevant
Scientific Authority in respect of that fauna or flora.

There is no policy and legislation for regulating the sale, exchange and distribution of Plant Genetic
Materials, including cultivars or landraces either within the country and abroad. In addition there is no
Intellectual Property Rights legislations for the Plant Genetic Resources (PGR).

The law that governs the protection of flora (Forestry) in the country does not allow any wild plant
materials to be collected from the forest/bush and taken out of the country. The Living plant materials
have to be seed raised or grown in cultivation for second generation before they can be taken out.

In addition, under the National Parks Act, Subsection (3) part (d) the Director of Parks may, in the
performance of his functions, deemed all plants and animals inhabiting a given National Park area to
be under protection. In other conservation areas, such as the Wildlife Management Area (WMA), it
only deals with the management of the fauna not the flora. However, if in the WMA the owners want
to utilize the flora or any forest produce from the WMA for commercial purposes then the Forestry Act
applies. Such that the owners have to be registered as Forest Industry Participants and issued license to
harvest, process and sell their flora regulated by the Forest Authority.

CARBON OFFSETS

According to the World Bank, carbon offset trading will soon create tens of billions of dollars of
income for developing countries. Carbon offset trading is an activity whereby companies invest in
projects in developing countries that reduce carbon gas emissions (that cause the ‘greenhouse effect’)
and in return obtain “carbon credits” which they could use to trade off against the emissions they
produce in their own countries. At the moment there is no mandatory requirement for such companies
to reduce emissions. This may happen after the Kyoto Conference in December this year (1997).
There is no doubt that in the near future companies will be forced by law to reduce their emissions.
Therefore, companies are seeking to demonstrate that carbon offset trading can work, as this will make
compliance with these laws cheaper.

Reduced impact logging, afforestation and forest conservation are major ways of securing sequestration
of carbon which can then be traded, for example, to an American electricity-generating company.

Two examples of carbon swap forestry projects are the Mercado Project in Bolivia and Rio Bravo
Project in Belize.

The US$8.78 million Mercado Project involves expanding the boundaries of the Mercado National
Park and assisting the development of reduced impact logging, and other alternative sustainable income
generating activities. US$7 million funding for the project has been sourced through the American
Electric Power company. The project estimates it could offset as much as 14.5 million tonnes of carbon
over thirty years. The company is being assisted by a local Bolivian environmental NGO and The
Nature Conservancy.

The US$2.6 million Rio Bravo Programme in Belize involves five United States private electric power
companies and The Nature Conservancy. The project will rescue a 14 000 ha parcel of land from
unsustainable agricultural clearance for biodiversity conservation. It also includes a sustainable
logging project. The estimated carbon savings are 5.2 million tonnes over the next forty years.

The Costa Rican Government has gone ahead making its own deals with private landholders for carbon
savings, packaging them up and selling them overseas. It recently sold US$2 million worth of carbon

savings at US$10 a tonne. On this basis, the Bolivian project would be worth US$52 million.

In early 1995, the UNDP/DEC Biodiversity Conservation and Resource Management Programme

undertook the first comprehensive attempt to develop a carbon offset investment prospectus for a PNG
Forest Management initiative. The prospectus was part of a sustainable forestry project associated with
an integrated conservation and development project in Lak, New Ireland Province. Unfortunately the
project did not go ahead because industrial logging had taken away the resource base needed for the
sustainable forestry project.

The Lak project would have saved around three and a half million tonnes of carbon.

While it did not proceed, the Lak initiative was a valuable experience in this new field. Papua New
Guinea, to this day is still the only country in the Asia-Pacific region to have “in-development’ status
from the US Government for a carbon offset forestry conservation proposal. As such, Papua New
Guinea is now the country best placed in the whole region to establish a carbon swap forestry

conservation project with the USIJI.

The PNGFA could negotiate carbon offset agreements as part of its management rights over its FMAs
as well as liaising with the Department of Environment & Conservation as this is the lead agency in the
Climate Change Convention.

CONCLUSION

Botanical prospecting and carbon offsets could

e Be undertaken under existing FMAs and TAs as complementary to logging, increasing returns to

landowners and PNGFA, and keeping all parties honest.

e Financially assist logging companies to engage in sustainable practices.
e Provide better overall forest management
e Provide more options to landowners for forest use

e See the PNGFA defining biodiversity conservation as a commercial activity rather than ‘locking

up’ of a resource.

e Enable PNGFA to provide an alternative commercial use for FMAs considered being unviable.

e Provide the best possible commercial returns for the forest being managed - rather than being

dependent upon a single commercial use. Logging may be a less valuable use of forest produce
than botanical prospecting or forms of carbon offset, e.g. afforestation.

Goals for establishing Bioprospecting projects in PNG could include

1. To prevent loss of traditional knowledge of plant/animal-derived medicinal properties

2. To provide research opportunities for PNG-based scientists and students that emphasis the benefits
of intact forest ecosystems and the indigenous knowledge of medicinal plant/animal resources.
The ethnobotanical and Zoological research may in turn provide opportunities for sustainable

economic development in PNG

3. Working with PNG-based institute, NGOs or Government agency that promote the conservation
and/or sustainable use of forest resources

50

REDUCED IMPACT LOGGING

Wesley Watt
Pacific Heritage Foundation,
P.O. Box 546 Rabaul,
East New Britain

INTRODUCTION

In PNG the common logging practices in the large forest concession areas are the so-called Selective
Logging and the Clear-Felling logging practices. Selective Logging invariably means selecting all those
trees of commercial value, together with ‘selecting’ anything else that gets in the way. Clear-felling
normally only applies to operations such as Jant outside Madang, although there are a number of suspicious
forestry/agriculture developments in which clear-felling is a necessary prerequisite.

Each concession area may be 100,000 hectares or more of forest land. Obviously common in all these
operations are the inevitable impact to the environment from the use of large machinery for harvesting,
hauling and handling of the logs in various stages of timber extraction, (Louman and Nicholls, 1994),
together with unnecessary damage cased by untrained, inexperienced or simply destructive operators. All
of this can result in a substantial amount of earth movement, especially in rugged areas to achieve the
correct road gradient according to logging roads specification. This leaves a lot of bare earth, which is at
risk of erosion.

In East New Britain there are many examples of these kinds of operations in concession areas not far from
our base at Ulatawa Plantation. The regeneration from the logged over areas comprises vines, fast growing
secondary herbaceous species, most of which are of poor quality (Saulei and Beehler, 1993). The
remaining trees left from the harvest which are usually partly damaged and may die some time later. Vigus
(1994) reported that loss of remaining trees could continue up to 10 years after harvesting, with losses as
high as 79% of all trees over 10cm. dbh. The few larger trees left behind are usually of poorer quality of
species not well known in the markets. Many reports have discussed this subject but the question is where
is the limit and what do we do?

REDUCED IMPACT LOGGING

Reduced impact logging is a broad topic to discuss as it can be debated not only from the biological, but
also from the social, political, economic and cultural point of view. I do not intend to discuss the topic
subject to any of the above areas today. Instead I would like to present our experiences of the environment
as a good focus for debate.

While there is a big debate about what is the right balance to reduce the impact of logging, hectares of
forest areas are being harvested by large logging companies who are basically interested only in how much
profit they can make from the timber.

The focus appears to be on Eco-Forestry operations as a way to reduce logging damage, and I will discuss
the experiences of the Pacific Heritage Foundation.

Eco-Forestry Sawmilling Projects

In East New Britain and other areas Pacific Heritage Foundation has helped landowners to try and reduce
large-scale logging through the community based ecoforestry sawmilling project in various communities in
the Bainings. While the projects are owned by the landowners within a community, Pacific Heritage
Foundation assists in selecting equipment, providing technical support and managerial skills, training and
marketing services to the landowners. (Henderson, 1996.)

51

SND 7M EOL U RINSE NERceateNanerreecone eet eee eT
The important thing about these projects is that adequate awareness and education programs are done at the
community level about the wealth in the natural resources and the potential danger to their environment and
their livelihood from large-scale operations. The projects also provide the opportunity for communities to
participate in the practical example of alternative means of doing things.

When working with the community we have noted the importance of a two-way dialogue in which those
who try to educate are also willing to listen to better understand the situation in the communities as they
can vary from one community to another.

We usually act on requests from interested individuals or groups from the community to begin any of our
projects.

From our experiences, awareness and education are much more effective if they can be supported by
practical examples. Reduced impact logging by ecoforestry portable sawmills is not necessarily the
solution to high impact logging, but they tie in with educating people. This presents a better (hopefully)
options can be developed to address the impacts on the biodiverstiy of both flora and fauna.

Equipment
The basic equipment in these small-scale ecoforestry projects is a portable sawmill (Lewisaw mill), a
chainsaw and a winch. There has been very little use of heavy machinery in these projects.

There are different makes of portable sawmills that are available in PNG and the final purchase should be
selected according to the situation in each area. The availability of parts in the country is an important area
to consider when making selections. We have selected Lewis sawmills because they are light and can be
easily moved around the forest solely by manpower. It should also be noted that there is a dealer which is
centrally located in Lae.

All the manpower to operate the sawmills comes from the community.

Training

The initial training requirements are organised by PHF. Training covers basically two aspects, the
technology and the managerial skills. The wokabaot sawmill has simple technology, which enables simple
training before the communities can operate the sawmills without supervision. It may be necessary at times
to bring in specialists from outside to do some specialist training. For instance, a chainsaw specialist who
will teach safety, maintenance and directional felling techniques if such skills are not available in the
organisation. At other times training is carried out in the existing sawmill projects in which a number of
people from one community get practical training from existing sawmill projects in another community. In
this way the people get a hands-on training and the training period can be effectively short. In the Wide
Bay Area for example, prior to the setting up of the Merai sawmill project, a team of five men was sent to
the Mu community sawmill project which had been operating for two years. The five-member team spent
two weeks working on the sawmill project together with the regular Mu sawmill operators learning how to
operate the sawmill and the chainsaw correctly as well as learning some simple book-keeping skills. After
this training they also get some training during the setting up of the sawmill and get follow up monitoring.

Forest Management training on the other hand, can be a long-term process as it deals with the social,
economic, political and cultural values of the communities involved. These can vary from one community
to another. It is important to note that the community needs to agree on all decisions. One simple example
comes from one of the Wide Bay Projects: the community developed a system in which the people decided
not to collect royalty payments for logs cut on their land, instead divided the logs so that half of the logs
goes to the sawmill project and the other half to the owner of the trees. In this case, everyone is happy and
the project carries on.

The obvious concern is how practical is the silvicultural technique when the aim is to try and harvest the

forest resources in a sustainable way. Although there are researches being carried out on the growth rates
and the natural regeneration of the trees after logging, there are still a lot of unknowns about the cutting

52

cycle that would be used as the basis for sustainable harvest of the forest. Of course the size of the forest
‘resource is one factor. The basic rules that are applied in our projects are simple.

The number of trees that are allowed to be felled in one spot at one time depend on the stand density of one
particular area and the species composition in the area.

The size of the area disturbed by harvesting is kept to a minimum however, the safe limits are an issue of
debate. The regeneration of different species responds differently to different intensity of light on the forest
floor. Kamarere for instance, likes plenty of light so it should have a different silvicultural treatment to the
other species.

One skill which we are investigating further is the tending of selected species in the re-growth phase, the
removal of weed competition, and the thinning of undesirable species. Vigus (1994) has demonstrated the
effectiveness of these techniques, but one obvious problem is: who pays for the labour?

These simple guidelines are practical in the communities if their attitude is right. To reduce the area of
disturbance the good skills in directional felling techniques help determine whether the impact of the
operation will be contained within a reduced area.

It is also important to leave some adult trees of the species felled to maintain biodiversity in the immediate
vicinity. In theory the trees left standing will provide seedlings for the next crop. Also it is the older trees
which provide nesting sites for various bird and other species. Many communities in heavily logged areas
reported the relative scarcity of kokomos after logging; reflecting the destruction of nesting trees.

To minimise risk on soil erosion, the harvesting operations should be confined to flat areas and on areas
with slopes less than 30 degrees.

Marketing

Apart from the local markets for sawn timber, the timber yards in towns will usually take all the lumber
that are sawn. Quality of course is important. PHF plays a leading role in finding the markets for the small
sawmilling projects.

IS REDUCED IMPACT LOGGING PRACTICAL?

In our experience with one of the first eco-forestry sawmill projects in the Bainings of East New Britain
Province, the project was able to demonstrate to an extent, that reduced impact logging can be practical.
Our Permanent Sample Plots in a harvested area showed that after three years there is a very satisfactory
species composition of natural regeneration.

This highlights the people’s general understanding and appreciation of what is expected from these
operations as a result of close monitoring.

Constraints

There are admittedly a number of constraints in the community Eco-forestry sawmill projects that cannot
be over-looked, such as easy access to the forest resources and to the markets, loan repayments,
technological barriers, land tenure, markets, book keeping skills, and forestry skill in the community.

Access to the forest

In most cases in PNG the cash crops are nearest the village, followed by food gardens, then the natural
forest. Sometimes the distances to the forest can be at least 1000 m from the road or access from the beach.
After a while the long distances for carrying timber (on their shoulders) can be a problem and is a
discouraging factor to the project.

53

Transport
Transportation costs, either by canoe, boat or road are quite expensive and can bite a lot into the profits of
the project. Local markets should always be surveyed as a fall back.

Loan repayment

The tendency to harvest the easily accessible areas first is a common practice because of easy access and
the pressure to repay loan on the equipment quickly. It makes it difficult to adhere to the code of logging
practices and of course, the implementation of appropriate silvicultural measures.

Technology

Some knowledge of technology is important to keep the mill operating at all times. Spare parts can be.a
problem if one has to order from overseas. A good supply of spare parts held at PHF headquarters is very
helpful to the small sawmill owners.

Land Tenure

It is important to get the land tenure and land access issues dealt with in the early stage of the project. As
this will greatly affect the way the project runs. Incorporated land groups in an area should be investigated
further.

Markets

Wholesale and retail commercial markets are very strict on quality. Second grade timber may end up not
being sold or if sold, for a very poor price. Some markets are very selective in terms of species, which does
not help with the silvicultural treatment to be applied in the forest.

The local markets can normally absorb the second grade sawn timber, with the added benefit of
contributing to improvements in village housing.

Book-keeping skills
Simple book keeping skills are important and training should begin at the earliest stages of each project.

Forestry skills
Some forestry skills are important to have in the community and training in the necessary skills is essential.

CONCLUSION

What do you think is the most important issue now regarding reduced impact logging? Is the community
based ecoforestry sawmill project the way for reducing impact logging should be achieved? Or that our
efforts should be to educate the people of Papua New Guinea to have the right attitude towards
development in the conservation of our environment?

on the Consultative Meeting on Eco—Timber’, 9-10. Unpublished Monograph, Pacific Heritage

Ir No ICAD ECONOMIC INCENTIVE CAN COMPETE AGAINST THE SHORT-TERM
BENEFITS FROM NON-SUSTAINABLE LOGGING, WHAT Do WE Do Now?

Larry Orsak
Bismarck-Ramu ICAD Project
C/- Conservation Resource Centre
P.O. Box 165. Waigani, NCD

ABSTRACT

More ICAD projects are finding out that entering a competition to provide greater, short-term benefits
to landowners, compared to alternatives such as industrial logging, is doomed to failure. Nearly all
the economic incentives being offered today, in the context of current markets and transportation
infrastructure, have limited income-generating potential. Communities are likely to abandon many of
these economic opportunities once they see for themselves how few benefits come from all the required
work. Thus, economic incentives for ICAD areas mainly buy time. This is only a viable strategy if
the time gained is utilised to build longer lasting community values, perceptions, and convictions that
their long-term well being requires that they utilise their natural resources sustainably.

A FORMER BELIEVER IN THE POWER OF ICAD ECONOMIC INCENTIVES

I was one of the blind believers. When I began training people to do butterfly farming extension work
in 1990, I not only had an illusion, but promoted it too: I automatically assumed that butterfly farming
could be sufficiently lucrative that it would cause communities to say ‘no’ to non-sustainable industrial
logging. “Give the people beetles, and they will turn down logging royalties,’ I thought.

It’s not true. Virtually all the economic incentives we promote give smaller short-term benefits, but
overall, bigger long-term rewards. Yet, there is a gut urge in all of us to accept the short-term over the
long-term, the quick money over the long-term investment. That’s why it is so difficult to argue the
value of the long-term to village communities in PNG — or anywhere else, for that matter. And that’s
why people choose the foreign logging schemes.

In the current social environment, I have come to believe that those portable sawmills, butterfly farms,
orchid gardens, and eco-tourism lodges are, at best, nothing more than a time-buyer. Max Henderson
(Pacific Heritage Foundation) has often said this about his organisation’s establishment of wokabaut
somil operations for communities that otherwise might choose non-sustainable industrial logging.
None of these activities are likely to buy more than a few years time. In fact, I suspect that if the
foreign loggers come back to the wokabaut somil communities in 5-10 years, they will have easy
pickings when they offer ‘quick money’. By that time, the villagers will be disappointed that we
‘didn’t bring in the expected cargo.’ They may actually be angry about us and the deception they think
we put on them. ‘You go!’ some communities may say. ‘We'll bring in people who can really help us
develop.’ Enter the big bulldozers, the yellow brick roads through the forest, and the K50 notes.

Some Facts About the Economic Incentives We Are Promoting

There are very few non-timber forest products that can earn good money for very many communities
in PNG. Even utilising the timber itself in a small-scale way, presents all kinds of cost-inefficiencies
which ultimately lower profit. But even assuming that the activities are lucrative — witness tapa cloth
making in Collingwood Bay — there will always be members of the community who do not or can not
participate in that economic activity. Those marginalised people could easy become the ones who
lobby to bring in a logging operation. The fact is that everyone wants to participate in lucrative
economic activities, yet no single economic activity can embrace everyone in the community...
anywhere in the world.

In addition, those with a drive to make money will try to earn cash in more than one way. A fact we

55

keep hiding is that most communities could still carry out the economic incentives we offer, while
continuing to destroy habitat and resources. For example, they could leave a few hectares of land
unlogged, and set up an ecotourism operation inside the tiny remaining patch. Few tourists will notice.
It’s a creative solution, but hardly allows for the protection of Papua New Guinea’s biological diversity
wealth. Not only that, surrounding an ecotourism operation with non-sustainable industrial logging
indisputably diminishes future development options for the community. Yet, in the short-term, short-
sighted view, it appears that non-sustainable industrial logging, ecotourism, butterfly farming and the
like can go hand-in-hand. Thus, people will try it.

Another fact of life that we overlook is that some of our economic incentives are not really dependent
on having a lot of ‘big bush’ habitat in the area. For example, villagers could raise the Priamus
Birdwing Butterfly (Ornithoptera priamus poseidon) which fetches about K1 each, even after they had
logged their “big bush,’ as long as there are trees in the area. | Collingwood Bay people plant the
mulberry trees for tapa cloth making in their gardens; that tree does not grow in the ‘big bush’ habitats.
And so it goes.

Finally, we have to accept an aspect of human nature — no one works if they don’t have to, and getting
more reward for less work is attractive to every human being on earth. The fact is that no matter how
lucrative portable sawmilling is for a community, it requires a lot of work. Contrast that with the
perception of ‘money for no work’ that logging royalties offer. Most communities get engaged in
wokabaut somiling with little idea of how much work and small-business capacity it all involves.
They learn soon enough. Small wonder that most wokabaut somils in Papua New Guinea are no longer
walking about, or even functional!

A Confession Needed From ICAD Practitioners

All this paints a very gloomy picture. Yet, I remain optimistic because I hope I see where the
solutions lie. My personal redemption started when I made a personal confession: we can no longer
base our ICAD programmes on economic rat-races. We have no hope of winning such races; it is
crazy to even try and enter them. A preface statement of this ICAD conference was: “That fact is that
the economic incentive possibilities we have available cannot compete on a one-to-one basis against
what the loggers are offering.” To that, we ask: “So now what do we do?” I think the answer should
be, “We need to stop pretending that we can offer bigger short-term incentives, because we can’t.”
Such a response would be a very big confession on the part of ICAD practitioners, so there will
certainly be resistance to stating it. Yet, once said, this confession begins the process of finding
solutions!

ICAD practitioners may feel that such a confession would be suicidal, leading the communities they’ ve
worked with, to reject the ICAD approach. Yet, there is an indisputable fact that we keep overlooking:
some communities in Papua New Guinea have turned down industrial logging without ever being
offered an ICAD alternative. Is it because these communities are non-Melanesian in their outlook?
Are they just plain strange? Whatever the reason, we need to study the underlying basis for these
decisions, and figure out if we can replicate them with other communities in PNG. Clearly, for some
reason, certain rare communities have come to understand why non-sustainable, environmentally
destructive logging is not the best option for their future, no matter how much quick money is offered.

A LIGHT AT THE END OF THE TUNNEL?

Let’s accept that the economic incentives we are offering to ICAD communities are only buying us a
few year’s time. How do we use that time effectively? What should we be doing that can put different
perceptions and values into the community when our borrowed time runs out? What can we do that
will produce a situation, several years down the line, where the communities realise that they aren’t
going to make much money with what we offer them, yet they still believe that they are still following
the best pathway to long-term development?

There are 2 objectives we should direct substantial time and effort to achieve, during the several years
“borrowed time’ we gain from the ICAD economic incentives:

56

(1) Build a deep relationship of trust between the ICAD facilitators and the communities with
which we work. If people trust us, they tend to listen more to us (of course, if our advice is not
sound, they’ ll stop trusting and stop listening!). They will be more likely to believe us when we
say, ‘the long-term benefits that your big bush can provide are important for your life!? The
problem is, too many people have as a goal ‘build trust’ without much idea of what mechanisms
will or will not lead to the development of that elusive ‘trust’ goal.

Facilitate a clear understanding by the villagers themselves, of (a) the balance of the long-
term costs and benefits, and (b) what costs and benefits come from pursuing different
activities to utilise natural resources. Another word for this process is ‘education.’ The
education needs to be far more intensive and detailed than the current ‘awareness’ activities most
ICAD projects engage in.

Bubbling with trust and armed with information, | am convinced that few villagers would accept non-
sustainable logging. These beliefs can become incorporated into the local culture, such that they will
continue to strongly influence decisions made on behalf of the people’s resources, in the same way as
‘clan’ and ‘land’ convictions deeply affect decision-making today.

But all this takes time. We have bought only a few years time with our butterfly farms, our wokabaut
somils, and our artefact and bilum sales projects. Will we wisely use the short time we have? It all
depends upon whether ICAD project staff has a clear idea of how they can achieve these deep levels of
trust and education in the communities. It depends upon whether the ICAD field practitioners
understand the concept of education in general, and what specific processes bring people through the
full educational process.

Does your ICAD project have a plan for carrying out this essential education in the next few years?
