CARBON OFFSET OR GREENHOUSE GAS MITIGATION:
AN ADDITIONAL OR ALTERNATIVE ECONOMIC INCENTIVE
FOR ICADs IN PAPUA NEW GUINEA!

Simon M. Saulei
Conservation Resource Centre
P.O. Box 165, Waigani, NCD.

ABSTRACT
Current approaches to financing biodiversity conservation or forest resource management in Papua New Guinea do

not appear to be progressing well as expected due to a number of reasons, many of which are too familiar to us.
The most obvious of these are the types of land tenure systems inherent in the country and the dilemma of satisfying
the immediate financial and material needs of the landowners.

One possible means where funds could be made available for the purposes of biodiversity conservation and forest
management is through this innovative carbon offset or greenhouse gas (GHG) mitigation advocated under the
United Nations Framework Convention Climate Change (UNFCCC) and sea level rise.

In this paper the concept of carbon offset and how this can be applied in PNG to fund biodiversity conservation and
forest management strategies are discussed.

INTRODUCTION

| One of the first questions that people asked when they hear one talks about carbon offset is ‘what is it (carbon offset
or Greenhouse gas (GHG) mitigation), and how can we be involved in it?’

Carbon offset or greenhouse gas (GHG) mitigation project is an investment for reducing both the current and future
_ greenhouse gas emissions to the atmosphere or increasing the estimated biological uptake of greenhouse gases from the
atmosphere.

The GHG mitigation project is basically any form of modifications to the ‘conventional’ energy supply sources,
energy utilisation techniques, and land use patterns that have the greatest capacity to serve as a GHG project
opportunities. For instance, generating electricity from non-polluting sources, such as wind or solar energy,
supplies a GHG mitigation service. Such an electric generating facility is a potential project because it produces
both electricity and avoids carbon dioxide emissions in contrast to fossil fuel alternatives and their associated
emissions. Increasing the efficiency of capturing, transporting, generating and utilising conventional energy
provides a form of greenhouse gas mitigation by accomplishing more economic production with the same, or lower
overall GHG emissions.

Papua New Guinea has the potential to harness this GHG mitigation or carbon offset opportunity through application of
a Joint Implementation (JI) or Activities Implemented Jointly (AIJ) emerging model, being developed mainly in eastern
Europe and central America.

The implementation of such projects in PNG would reduce the country’s overall current and future greenhouse gas
emissions. Ultimately, the quantified emission improvements could then be credited to the investor, effectively
counterbalancing, or offsetting, penalised gas emissions within the investor’s country.

This type of an emission trading system is currently in a pilot phase. Under it, however, governments, corporations or
even individual citizens may develop investment opportunities. Industrial countries or companies would purchase
offset services through mechanisms that have yet to be finalised, but which are presently being experimented on with a
growing scale. Such investment opportunities must conform to a host country’s social,. cultural and economic
development priorities. Industrial concerns, whose ability to reduce their own greenhouse gas emissions is constrained
because they face high marginal costs in modifying their industries, constitutes the primary market for these
investments.

‘ Paper presented at the 3“ Climate Change and Sea Level Rise Meeting, Noumea, 18-22 August, 1997.
59

because they face high marginal costs in modifying their industries, constitutes the primary market for these
investments.

There are, however, already opportunities to develop projects for marketing and review within several governmental
and private sectors pilot programs. For example, at the end of July this year, there were already some 41 officially
‘accepted’ international emission reduction projects (including planned and ongoing initiatives - Anon, 1997), as well
as several other well-known programs. This figure is likely to rise dramatically over the coming months and years.
Despite continued uncertainty, this is fast becoming a new area for direct foreign investment.

STATUS OF CARBON AND OTHER GASES EMISSIONS IN PAPUA NEW GUINEA

PNG has a relatively small source of GHG emissions, which is currently arising from the change in land-use patterns.
According to the World Resource Report (1994), the land-use change in 1991 accounted for 29 million tons of carbon
dioxide emissions, while energy and industrial-related emissions amounted to only 2.2 million tons. Methane
emissions are estimated at around 10,000 tons per annum from a combination of both solid waste disposal and
livestock. Hydro-energy provides the mainstay of the country’s energy requirements, and the potential for expansion is
quite enormous. Increased export-oriented exploitation of the country’s oil and gas reserves, however, could increase
energy-related emissions over time, and the same could be said about the growing transportation sector.

CARBON OFFSET FOR FOREST CONSERVATION AND MANAGEMENT OPTION

One of the major constraints to the initiation and maintenance of the processes of conservation and also to
implementing sustainable forest management programmes in Papua New Guinea is that of capital. For PNG, the
most viable immediate investment opportunities which will make available the necessary funds required to finance such
projects is through carbon offset. This can be obtained through a number of ways, such as by altering agricultural and
forestry land use activities and patterns. Papua New Guinea accounts for over 90% of globally negligible GHG
emissions. With over 100,000 hectares of forest annually affected by shifting cultivation and a further 14 million
hectares of forest presently available to industrial logging interests, the scope for positive impact is substantial (World
Resources Institute, 1994). For biodiversity conservation and forest resource management advocates, these carbon
offset or GHG policy developments may offer new financial resources for the implementation of both conservation and
management activities. For forest management interventions aimed at carbon sequestration there are five distinct areas
that PNG can enter into:

i. Reducing the rate of deforestation.

ii. Increasing the net area of forests through afforestation.

ili. Increasing the stocks of carbon within existing Forests.

iv. Substitution of wood fuels for fossil fuels.

V. Increasing the efficiency of wood use (an increase in the efficiency of downstream processing can be

calculated as generating an offset, in that it lowers the amount of raw material required per unit output).

All these areas have potential application to Joint Implementation or JI type investments in PNG and elsewhere.
However, they are conditional upon an accurate and assessable GHG quantification. Although, areas 4 and 5 are not
directly addressed here, they do have application in PNG. JI investment could, for instance, make it possible to
generate the electricity requirements of timber processing mills from wood residue rather than diesel (because it
involves the direct displacement of an energy equivalent of fossil fuels, offset calculations for using biomass fuels are
relatively straightforward). JI investment could also be targeted at increasing the efficiency of PNG’s mills, in terms of
lowering wood waste. Such interventions have already been the focus of a number of successful JI projects.

However, the issues of reforestation, preservation and forest management are more encouraging to the current potential
for JI in PNG. These carbon sequestration methods have come to be broadly accepted as being less ecologically
intrusive than massive tree farm monocultures. More recent research has focused on the carbon implications of
community agro-forestry, rehabilitating of degraded forestlands, preserving threatened areas, and employing low.
impact logging techniques.

From the perspective of biodiversity conservation, the use of GHG payments to reduce the damage from commercial
logging or pay for outright preservation of threatened areas is socially cost-effective. Both methods generate substantial
positive externalities through the maintenance of species habitats. Less directly, the implementation of agro-forestry
schemes and even plantation development, if properly designed, can ease pressures on standing primary forests.

60
REST CONSERVATION

ne interest in forest conservation and sustainable management as a carbon offset method stems from the fact that the
orld’s remaining primary tropical forests still represent huge banks of sequestered carbon. The protection of forest
al otherwise would be degraded or destroyed therefore presents an opportunity to immediately impact carbon flows.
eforesiation activities have a substantially slower impact. The avoidance and mitigation of carbon releases from these
anks provides the quickest, forestry-based, opportunity to lower the accumulation of carbon dioxide into the
mosphere. Forest preservation also generates substantial positive externalities beyond carbon flows, a fact that has
ot escaped the notice of conservation advocacy groups such as The Nature Conservancy (TNC). In effect, the use of
HG transfers to help preserve threatened areas is a 1990s update of the “debt for nature’ concept from the 1980s
tuart and Sekhran, 1996).

rest conservation for carbon sequestration purposes takes two forms: direct and indirect. Direct interventions
essentially require the ‘locking up’ of land resources into preserves. Indirect interventions comprise a wider range of
ossibilities, including increasing agricultural productivity, thus lowering the need for slash and burn cropping, the
development of agro-forestry to meet fuelwood needs, and the improvement of tree harvesting techniques, or Reduced

Impact Logging.

Avoiding deforestation is, however, a complex and controversial endeavour as this infringes directly into the hearts of a
particular region’s poverty, resource ownership, population and economic growth determinants. Often, the pressure on
standing forests stems from government policies. As such, the potential use of offset fund programs which directly
interfere with such policies, is interpretable as an unacceptably patrimonial use of funds and power, and an affront
against a nation’s sovereignty. This is one of the aspects of ‘debt for nature’ swaps which limited the effectiveness of
that tool. As such, there appears to be a bias among potential offset investors against the idea of ‘locking up’ resources,
along the lines of many ‘debt for nature’ initiatives, unless safeguards are established to provide surety and the project
has been endorsed by both local communities and the host country government.

Projecting a reasonable baseline of greenhouse gas emissions, which would otherwise occur without financial
intervention, is difficult for conservation offsets. Since forest degradation has such a wide range of causes, including
shifting agriculture, permanent land clearance, and industrial timber extraction, proponents of preservation or
management offsets must establish:

1. the carbon content of the forest;

2. the area of standing forest ultimately protected due to the financial intervention; and

3. the area of forest (and carbon content) which would have been lost in the absence of protective measures. These
are all matters of interpretation, and subject to countervailing calculations.

Despite these difficulties, direct forest conservation and/or management activities form an integral part of several robust
carbon offset projects and proposals and should not be dismissed as unworkable. Rather, conservation can be an
important component of projects with multiple environmental and developmental agendas. Due to the immediate
greenhouse gas benefits, which are available from conservation, its inclusion can dramatically increase the GHG cost-
competitiveness of an investment package. The Lak initiative in southern New Ireland, PNG attempted exactly this by
developing a prospectus, which emphasised a local development initiative in association with forest protection (though
the Lak project made initially minimal claims in regard to the overall greenhouse gas benefits from conservation). This
framework is quite similar to the basic outlines of several other successful USIJI projects, such as the US$2.6 million
Rio Bravo Conservation Management Program in Belize and the US$8.78 million Mercado National Park project in
Bolivia.

The Rio Bravo project involves five US private electric power companies, and The Nature Conservancy; an
international US based NGO group. The project is based on a 14000 ha parcel of land saved from unsustainable
agriculture clearance for biodiversity conservation. It also includes a sustainable logging project. On the other hand,
the Mercado project involves the expanding of the Mercado National Park boundaries threatened by poaching, logging
and unplanned agriculture, providing technical assistance to low-impact logging firms, establishing commercial orchid
farms, supporting an eco-tourism program and assisting local landowners in alternative sustainable income generating
activities. The US$7 million funding came from the American Electric Power Company.

Reduced Impact Logging (RIL)

As an alternative to protecting areas from industrial logging, there is a growing movement to use carbon offset finance
to implement more sensitive forest extraction techniques, particularly in the tropics. It is recognised that uncontrolled
logging causes substantial release of greenhouse gases from the decaying biomass damaged during the log extraction
process. A literature review of damage estimates shows conventional industrial tropical logging operations cause up to

61

70% damage to residual tress in logging coupes. A reduction in such damage improves the potential for the forest to
regenerate without intervention.

Reduce impact logging (which is similar to the newly adopted ‘Logging Code of Practice’) is an attractive forestry
offset option because approximately half of the eventual greenhouse gains are realised over the first several years. On
the other hand, biodiversity values are maintained, fire risks are lowered, and the integrity of the topsoil is maintained.
However, a fundamental feature of the reduce impact logging offset method is that forests continue to provide
economic potential through the continued production of timber resources.

RIL driven carbon offsets are complex, but somewhat simpler than those for preservation. It is necessary to establish
test plots in comparable conventionally logged areas and in the RIL areas in order to make a comparison. Sampling
methodologies for this type of work have been developed and improved over the past several years. Once a coefficient
is established for the differential, the overall greenhouse gas savings may be calculated by multiplying the coefficient
by the overall area affected by the type of logging. Given that a fundamental benefit of RIL is that the regenerative
capacity of the post-logged forest is enhanced, it is necessary to maintain some type of scientific verification in the test
areas for a number of years. RIL techniques, as practised in a Sabah, Malaysia Pilot Program include the following:
Operational plans drawn up in 1:5000 Map

Pre-selection and marking of harvestable timber

Pre-harvest cutting of climber vines

Slope and riparian restrictions on logging

Restrictions on logging during periods of bad weather

Lowering the impact of access roads through planning

Use of directional felling techniques

Planning of skid trails

No use of bulldozer blades on skid trails

These components are not unique or original, in fact they now being implemented as the ‘Logging Code of Practice in
PNG. However, they have rarely been utilised in past on a commercial logging scale. In comparison to afforestation
programs, RIL is advantageous from the perspective of the offset investor in that a very large percentage of a carbon
dioxide savings come immediately, rather than being distributed over 30, 60 or 100 years. This lessens the risk of
project failure for investors.

The project implementer requires substantial capacity and commitment to effectively apply RIL techniques. These
techniques are cost intensive, and require intensive training to re-skill labour. Their implementation must be closely
monitored for compliance. Though RIL, by improving forest regenerative capacity, increases long term forest resource
values, over the lifetime of the average concession, such positive effects will likely create little downstream monetary
benefit. Given that most company harvest rights are for less than 60-100 years (the assumed regeneration period untila
viable second harvest is ready), financial cost/benefit equations tend to mitigate against the use of RIL; the incremental
costs generally cannot be recouped in current timber markets. Nevertheless, this is slowly changing due to increasing
consumer pressure for the sustainable use of forests, but at present there is a fundamental need to identify financing
options to cover the incremental costs for these techniques.

It should be noted that RIL timber should be able to meet the most stringent guidelines for sustainably produced timber.
This is a secondary output from a RIL carbon dioxide offset project. The value of this should not be underestimated,
especially as the timber industry gears itself to deal with more stringent production guidelines under the ITTO 2000 and
other initiatives.

Plantations
As noted above, the option for using monoculture plantations to sequester atmospheric carbon has been analysed under
a variety of different scenarios. The lack of comprehensive emissions reduction policies has meant that there are n0
economic drivers to implement ‘carbon farm’ plantations. There are no examples of carbon-dedicated plantations, OF
even commercial forest plantations with carbon offset components built into them. This will change as emission taxes
or permits become more stringent and forest managers with extensive plantation experience, design investment options
with greenhouse sequestration components built into them.

Future plantation developments may be difficult to classify within the Activity Implementation Jointly context becaus®
such investments are responses to global market forces for wood fibre. However, the economic challenges o
plantations, which do not incur positive cash flows for at least 5-10 or even more years after inception, has inhibite

62
investment. Additionally, high capital costs and delayed returns tend to favour the establishment of high-yield species
monocultures, short rotations, and minimal cost management policies, all of which can be environmentally problematic.
Risky locales, without track records for investment, are also negatively weighted in such a financial calculations. If
there are investors interested in claiming the GHG capacity of plantations, the overall cost of borrowing capital might
be lowered. If this is the case, commercial forestry plantations might be able to overcome these anti-environment

biases.

Activity Implementation Jointly (AIJ) investments can, theoretically, make lower growth areas financially viable, make
it possible to choose longer rotations, or to use a wider range of species than eucalyptus, pine and teak. These species
alone account for 85% of all plantation investments in the tropics. Since the overall investments in an AIJ plantation
could be quite large and equity driven, it is likely that the AIJ participant might become a joint venture partner in such a
project, claiming a portion of downstream revenue as well as GHG benefits flowing to them.

Forest Rehabilitation/Regeneration

The utilisation of enrichment planting techniques (or ‘regeneration naturally’ Vigus 1994) may be appropriate in areas
affected by industrial logging. A substantial amount of scientific research has been undertaken into various silvicultural
prescriptions for forest rehabilitation and reforestation. In high impact commercial logging operations, failure to apply
some type of treatment ensures that only low value pioneer species will regenerate in the affected areas, decreasing
overall carbon content while increasing the future risks of forest fire and agricultural conversion. But traditional
financial cost /benefit calculations weigh heavily against this type of reforestation. In the absence of legislation and
enforcement, most concessionaires are unlikely to undertake such efforts effectively, even if they have the technical
capacity to do so. Thus, rehabilitation would appear to be an excellent area for Joint Implementation investment.

PROBLEMS

The above discussions indicate that there are number of possibilities for PNG to be involved in GHG Joint
Implementation investment projects. However, in order to initiate this, there are numerous problems that have to be
sorted out and these include:

1. Lack of baseline data regarding different forestry interventions and their projected impact on greenhouse
gas releases to the atmosphere.

Only with adequate baseline data can project developers formulate accurate investment prospectus. While it may seem
daunting to develop such databases, there are shortcuts, which can be utilised. It should be noted that the Netherlands JI
initiative has a research component which scientifically models ongoing projects for their GHG project potential, a
system which is likely to be partially copied by Germany and Japan. Forestry authorities should insist that any forestry
based project sponsored donor agencies should always include a GHG calculation component, even if done quite
basically for gross comparison purposes. The information could then be computerised into a database for future project
proposals, which may seek to develop carbon offset investment components.

2. Lack of government awareness about the potential for JI leading to new investments

Although, PNG government endorsed the Lak project, the awareness of carbon offset investment potential was rather
non-existent. As stated earlier, for any project to be entered into JI or AIJ it has to be endorsed by the host country.
Thus serious considerations be given to formulating standardised methods which can expedite project approval
processes. A regional approach to AIJ mechanisms and the project development process would be appropriately
discussed in this meeting.

3. The rather late entry of the region's industrial countries to look at JI/AIJ programmes in the region means
that greater long-term demand for carbon offset investment options may still be required from the US and
European countries.

4. Carbon offset investments are not cargo, they represent an emerging project investment paradigm which
values long-term sustainability.

While some carbon-offset projects are developmentally oriented, and are not likely to pay a return, others are
economically profitable as well as environmentally beneficial. Accessing these new pools of capital requires work, co-
operation and commitment. Several years of experience has shown that it is difficult to fully develop an investment
prospectus, let alone a marketing strategy, in situations where the participants’ commitment to a potential project is

tentative or non-committal.

63

CONCLUSIONS

Papua New Guinea can become a beneficiary of carbon offset investments for biodiversity conservation and forest
management. However, since there are virtually no baseline data in existence, the first activity that should be looked
into is in the area of establishing such baseline data as these will be used for assessing or calculating the offsetting costs.

A CRITIQUE OF
CHRISTENDOM’S RELATION TO THE LAND
WITH
DIRECTION TOWARDS A CHRISTIAN LAND ETHIC.

Zawia Taiye
Lutheran Chaplain
Papua New Guinea University of Technology
Private Mail Bag, Lae, Morobe Province.

INTRODUCTION

Christendom has recently ~ and with some justification — been called to task for its neglect and even
exploitation of the land. Writers such as Lynn White and Carl Amery have even laid the blame of the
contemporary ecological crisis at Christianity’s doorstep, citing both the modern notion of progress as the
by product of the Judeo-Christian view of history, as well as an extreme anthropocentrism which places
humanity at the centre of creation, driving a wedge between humanity and nature.

While Christianity’s implicit notion of progress has convinced humanity, especially in the West, that it
must manipulate the land for its own ends, its anthropocentrism has provided the justification that it has the
divine right to do so. Though overstated, such criticism must be taken seriously and weighed against the
biblical witness describing God’s intended relationship between humanity and land.

According to the Old Testament, the land remains God’s possession, even as God has entrusted its use to
humanity as a gift. Yet the use of the land is also to reflect the intentions of its creator. Even the New
Testament, with its marked eschatological outlook, emphasises that the gifts of creation, including the land,
are not to be misused while waiting for the new creation.

The Christian call for responsible care for the land is not only a matter of self interest, since humanity is
dependent on the land for survival, but also a matter of faith in the God who is the creator of both humanity
and the land. While examples may be cited where Christianity has either encouraged exploitation of the
land or stood by silently while it has occurred, a proper understanding of the land will give a responsible
perspective for the well-being of both humanity and creation.

A CRITIQUE OF CHRISTENDOM’S RELATION TO THE LAND WITH DIRECTION
TOWARDS LAND ETHIC

One of the so-called unknown words of Jesus reads: ‘The world is a bridge. Pass over it. But build not
your dwelling there.’' This remark stems from a very old tradition and provides in a nutshell the problem
of Christendom’s relation to the land. The land is used, as a means but not always appreciated for its own
intrinsic value. Since the goal of the Christian does not lie in worldly enjoyment but in the attainment of
the hereafter, the land has often been neglected and sometimes treated with downright disrespect. The
latter was especially true for the monastic movement, beginning with the desert fathers up to Bernhard of
Clairvaus, with the possible exception of Francis of Assisi. They either neglected the land and its beauty
by moving into the inhospitable desert or they saw it simply as something to be domesticated. They cleared
the forests and used the logs to vault their celestial fortifications into which they fled to escape the vanity of
the world. This is the point at which the criticism of Christendom’s relation to the land commences.

' Joachim Jereias, The unknown sayings of Jesus, trans. Reginald H Fuller
(London: S.P.C.K., 1957), 99.

CRITICISM OF CHRISTENDOM’S RELATION TO THE LAND

On December 26, 1996, at the meeting of the American Association for the Advancement of Science, Lynn
White Jr. presented a paper, which sees in Christianity the main culprit for our present ecological crisis. He
begins with the truism that ‘both modern technology and modern science are distinctively occidental.’
White does not find it coincidental that modern science, which has largely been produced by descendants of
the peasants of northern Europe is distinctively ruthless toward nature. Yet this exploitative attitude
towards nature did not appear until approximately AD 830. White sees this documented in Frankish
calendars which show people coercing the world around them: ‘plowing, harvesting, chopping trees,
butchering pigs. Man and nature are two things, and man is master.’* While in antiquity every tree, spring,
stream, and hill had its own guardian spirit, Christianity destroyed pagan animism. This enabled the
followers of Christianity to exploit nature in a mood of indifference to the feelings of natural objects. No
longer did the spirits need to be placated or to be thought of as being in charge of a particular situation, they
simply were declared non-existent. White sees this victory of Christianity over paganism as the greatest

psychic revolution in the history of our culture.

Though many claim today that we live in a post-Christian age, our daily habits of action ‘are dominated by
an implicit faith in perpetual progress which was unknown either to Greco-Roman antiquity or to the
Orient. It is rooted in, and is indefensible apart from Judeo-Christian teleology.”* Even Marxism and Islam
stem from this new way of thinking. White calls Christianity, especially in its Western form, the most
anthropocentric religion the world has seen. In absolute contrast to ancient paganism and Asia’s religions,
perhaps with the exception of Zoroastranism, it established a dualism of humanity and nature and insisted

that it is God’s will that humanity exploits nature for its proper ends.

White, however, observes that the Greek East, ‘a highly civilised realm of equal Christian devotion, seems
to have produced no marked technological innovation after the late 7" century.” He sees a decisive
difference between the Greek saint who contemplates and the Western saint who acts. Thus the Christian
conquest of nature would more easily emerge in Western world. Even in the West he notes Francis of
Assisi as the big exception. ‘Francis tried to depose man from his monarchy over creation and set up a
democracy of all God’s creatures.° Why is it then, we must ask, that Eastern Christendom did not
contribute as vigorously to the exploitation of nature as did the Western world and why is it, we must
further ask, that in the West we have the exception of Francis of Assisi?

Could it be that the Eastern inhibition towards nature is an aspect of the prevalence of the Greek spirit? If
this were the case, there is still Francis of Assisi who certainly was not influenced by Greek ontological
thinking. Lynn White refers to Francis’ view of nature and humanity which ‘rested on a unique sort of pan-
psychism of all things animate and inanimate, designed for the glorification of their transcendent creator.”
As aremedy White refers to the spirituality of the American Indians and to Judaism. Perhaps the latter has

more validity, as we will try to show later.

Carl Amery has made a similar claim in his book The End of Providence. The Merciless Consequences of
Christendom. Sensitized by the first report to the Club of Rome, Amery sees the deepest root of the total
victory of humanity as being influenced by Christendom in the Old Testament concept of the covenant.
Not only a special day of creation was attributed to humanity, but humanity was also made in the image
and likeness of God. Thus a chasm was created between humanity and everything else:
Who would still possibly doubt this absolute and total superiority is confronted with God’s
command. It is the explicit command of total dominion. Humanity is called to fill the earth, to
dominate it. Magic ideas are not connected with this. This means, it is totally up to nations;

Lynn White, Jr., The historical Roots of our Eschatological Crisis, ‘(1967), in David and Eileen Spring,
eds, Ecology and Religion in History (New York: Harper Torchbooks, 1974), p.19.

resource, flowers, herbs provide the arsenal, which is humanity’s disposition, they are a terrain for
hunting and the field for harvest.

When the Puritans came to North America they re-discovered the Old Testament. Their goal had been
America, the new Promised Land. When the new exodus occurred there were not Philistines in the land,
just Indians, perceived as uselessly wandering around. Since they evidently did not know what to do with
the land, they had no right to claim it as their own. The earth which the American farmer occupied was
God’s own country and nobody else’s. In contrast to the European peasant with his century old community
traditions, who was wedded to the inherited land, the American farmer treated the farm and the land as a
strict business enterprise. ‘If the farm did not yield sufficiently, one either turned to a different occupation
or one sought new land in the West, where it was practically laying there for nothing.” Altogether the
economic activities obeyed the same laws of mobility and yield, and free enterprise was an economic
reality in the US from the very beginning.

Carl Amery may have overstated his point. Yet back in 1965 Harvey Cox in his book The Secular City
noted the spirit of secularisation in the Judeo-Christian faith, since there occurred a disenchantment of
nature, a desacralization of politics and deconsecration of values. He even calls the creation accounts in
Genesis ‘a form of ‘atheistic propaganda.’ It is designed to teach the Hebrews that the magical vision, by
which nature is seen as a semi-divine force, has no basis in fact. Yahweh, the creator, whose being is
centred outside the natural process, who calls it into existence and names its parts allows man to perceive
nature itself in a matter-of-fact way.’'® This has led mature secular humanity to tend nature and to make
use of it, to assume the responsibility assigned to the first human being, Adam. Not nature, created by God,
but the city, created by humanity, became the medium of religion. Yet is Christianity justified in
abandoning nature even if God did not disclose himself finally through nature, for example, through the
burning bush, but in a human being, in Jesus of Nazareth?

THE BIBLICAL ACCOUNT OF THE LAND

The OLD TESTAMENT Hebrew knew primarily two words most commonly used for the land, ‘adamah
and ha’aretz. ‘Adamah which is related to Adam and to dam, meaning blood, is primarily the red earth, the
fertile soil, the arable land, and the cultured land. Ha’aretz, however, can be used for earth in a cosmic way
in contrast to the sky or the sea, or simply as a particular territory, region, or country. Quite often it is a
generic term, at times used for the dry land and even synonymous with dust. That the Lord God formed
man of dust from the ground (‘adamah) is one of the classical insights of Old Testament anthropology.'!

There is an intimate connection between Adam (first used as a generic term for humanity and the proper
name for Adam) and ‘adamah (earth). Humans are made of dust. ‘You are made of dust, and to dust you
shall return’. (Gen 3:19), we hear again toward the end of the story of the fall. Humans and nature form a
unity. Humans are taken from land and go back to nature. We also hear prior to the creation of humanity
that ‘there was no man to till the ground’. (Gen 2:5). Similarly we hear after the creation that the Lord God
‘took the man and put him in the garden of Eden to till and keep it’. (Gen 2:15). ‘The land belongs to man,
but man also belongs to his land. It has been entrusted by God to his care; man must control it, keep it
good, enhance it, and help it produce; the relationship is to be one of friendly partnership. Man was created
from the soil, lives in communion with it and is destined to rest in its bosom.’ ””

Carl Amery, Das Ende der Vorsehung die gnadenlosen folgen des Christentums (Reinbek bei Hamburg:
Rowohlt, 1972) p. 16.

67

For the semi-nomadic people of the patriarchal time, the land had a special meaning since they did not own
the ground even to bury their dead. Therefore special mention is made, that Abraham finally purchased
land from the Hittites ‘as a possession for a burying place’ (Gen 23:20) so that he could bury his wife
Sarah. Before that Abraham had received the promise of the Lord that He would give him and his
descendants the land of his sojourning ‘ all the land of Canaan, for an everlasting possession’ (Gen 17:8).

The promise of the land stands at the very beginning of Israel’s history. Abraham left his country and his
relatives for the land that God would show him (Gen 12:1). While Abraham received only a token
possession of the land, the fulfillment of the promise came at a later stage when the exodus from Egypt
finally led to the occupation of the Promised Land. [According to the Old Testament scholars the once
separate Sinai and exodus traditions have been welded together to form a unity]. The covenant executed at
Mount Sinai does not extend only to the people but also to the land. As we can glean from the Book of
Judges Israel’s revolts against God not only threatened their integrity as a people, but also their territorial
boundaries. The prophets even pointed to a clear connection between Israel’s sinful ways and their being
led into exile. One can even say that the land is a central, if not a central theme, of the whole testament.

Since the land is the Lord’s gift to his people it must be treated with reverence and must serve the common
good. Knowing of the precariousness of the semi-arid environment the Israelites were cautious in their
actions towards the land. Only in the supreme case of eschatological punishment did the land and its
destruction form part of this total punishment (Jer 7:16, 20 and Rev 7:1-4). Yet this destruction is reserved
for God alone and not allowed to humanity. The Israelites are admonished that in case of war against a city
they ‘shall not destroy its trees by wielding axe against them; for you may eat of them, but you shall not cut
them down’ (Dtn 20:19). Only trees that do not bear fruit may be cut down and used to build siege works

against a city so that it falls.

The land is a gift of the Lord who wants his people to live in this land according to his commandments. If
they do otherwise he will punish them. Especially the prosperity of the land, its fertility and the much-
needed rain stood as a symbol of peace and security. The Lord pledged to protect his whole creation and
all of humanity. Therefore we hear in the covenant with Noah: ‘While the earth remains, seed time and

harvest, cold and heat, summer and winter, day and night’.

The land becomes an avenue for justice towards the weak and the aliens. Thus gleanings were to be left for
the poor after every harvest. During the sabbatical year the land should be left to fallow and its produce
should be left to fallow and its whole produce should be left for the poor and for the wild animals. Every
fifty years in the Jubilee year — this was at least the idea — each family should recover its inheritance. This
was to be a year of massive land reforms so that no one could acquire huge amounts of property.
Especially in the time of monarchy, land grabbing and land accumulation was singled out as one of the
chief evils. Even the kings were in this crime. Yet ‘woe to those who join house to house, who add field to
field, until there is no more room, and you are made to dwell alone in the midst of the land’ (Isa 5:8).
Anyone who removes his neighbour’s landmark shall be cursed (Dtn 27:17). ‘Land was seen in Israel as
the chief symbol and instrument of independence, freedom and dignity; lack of land was one of the greatest
evils. Since laws did not protect sufficiently hired labourers; they had the right to receive their salary at the

end of the working day, and little else. ‘'°

In the NEW TESTAMENT, the land does not receive the same prominence as in the Old Testament. Many
important themes simply have not been picked up in the New Testament since there was no need caused
through a specific situation. While ‘the seal of the covenant between Yahweh and Israel was a gift of the
land’, the New Testament community was looking towards the consummation of the kingdom of God and a
new creation.’ Jesus and the New Testament writers looked at the land, its fruits and seasons, not as a
promise to be inherited but mainly as a teaching medium. It is clear from the New Testament that God

'S So, rightly, Walter Brueggemann, The Land: Place as Gift, Promise, and Challenge in Biblical Faith

(Philadelphia: Fortress, 1977), p.3.
'* Joseph C Hough, Jr., ‘The care of the Earth: The Moral Basis for Land Conservation,’ in Quarterly

Review | (Summer 1981), p.18.

68

cares for his creation. He feeds the birds of the air and adorns the grass of the fields with lilies (Matt
6:28ff). Not a single sparrow is forgotten before God (Luke 12:6).

Since the Christians are freed from the ethics, particularly of the old Israel, they are no longer attached to a
particular piece of land, which they have received.'? For instance, the members of the Jerusalem
community ‘sold their possessions and goods and distributed them to all, as any had need’ (Acts 2:45). If
they wanted to hold on to some of the proceeds from selling their land, they would be punished by death
(Acts 5:1-11).

This was nothing really new because they are already in Judaism, in Qamran community, there were full
members who were expected to turn over all their property to the community and there were others, less
fully integrated into the community, who supposed to turn over part of their wages.

Yet the Jerusalem community of Christians seemed to have been fairly unique in their oblivion to earthly
goods at that time. Their great benefactor, Paul, had promised to bail them out when their assets were used
up (Gal 2:10) and he tried to raise money for their support in Galatia, Achaia, and Macedonia. [He asked
the Corinthians to save from their individual earnings so he could collect their contributions when he
visited them and take them to Jerusalem (1 Cor 16:2). He did not want to tax them but simply gather the
gifts, given ‘not reluctantly or under compulsion, for God loves a cheerful giver’ (2 Cor 9:7).] He was sure
that God would not bless people who were so attached to their possession that they could not share.

Since ‘the form of this world is passing away’ the Christians should not hold on to anything but deal with
earthly goods in such a manner that they are not attached to them (1 Cor 7:29-31). This Christian's freedom
of clinging to things was already prefigured in Jesus’ Sermon on the Mount where he says: ‘Therefore I
tell you, do not be anxious about your life, what you shall eat or what you shall drink, nor about your body,
what you shall put on. Is not life more than food, and the body more than clothing? Look at the birds of
the air, they neither sow nor reap nor gather into their barns, and yet your heavenly Father feeds them’
(Matt 6:25f.).

Yet this freedom should not be understood as a carefree attitude, but as both an opportunity and obligation
to live and work according to the spirit of God. Christians should live in such a way that they become
inheritors of the kingdom. ‘According to his promise we wait for new heavens and a new earth in which
righteousness dwells’ (2 Pet 3:13). This means that the land still remained a promise, something to which
the Christians were looking as an inheritance. Since the Israelites had already experienced the tenuousness
of the Promised Land and were looking forward to the establishment of the new Davidic kingdom, the
Christians sought even less to attain the earthy goal. Their eschatological outlook led to the spiritualisation
of the land promise. Considering the injustice on this earth, they were looking forward to the redemption
‘as sons of God’ (Rom 8:19). Seeing how much the creation was abused they were not so optimistic to
believe that the earth on which they presently lived would be turned into a new paradise. They knew ‘that
the whole creation has been groaning in travail’ (Rom 8:22) and needed to be freed from ‘the bondage to
decay’ (Rom 8:21). God himself has to intervene, not only to endow the redeemed with new, immortal
bodies, but also to bring this old, misused and fallen creation to the initially intended splendour and
perfection.

Though Christians longed for these new conditions, they do not live in the new world. They still live on
this earth, marred by imperfection and human sinfulness. They live in their new-found freedom as children
of God and respect, treasure, and enjoy the earth as their temporary living quarters. God has also clearly
commanded that the earth, the sea, and the trees are not to be harmed until the final consummation, when
this earth will perish and a new earth will emerge (Rev 7:2f.).

When we pause for one moment to reflect on this rather sobering conclusion, what can we learn for a
Christian land ethic?

For the following, cf. Robert M Grant, Early Christianity and Society. Seven Studies (New Y ork: Harper,
1977), p. 100ff., in his comments on private Property.

69

COMMENTS CONCERNING A CHRISTIAN LAND ETHIC

Aldo Leopold writes in his Sandy County Almanac:
A land ethic, then, reflects the existence of an ecological conscience, and this in turn reflects a

conviction of individual responsibility for the health of the land. Health is the capacity of the land
for self-renewal. Conservation is our effort to understand and preserve this capacity.’

If there arises any ecological consciousness from the Judeo-Christian tradition it cannot come from the
premise that there is a divine quality within nature. The land clearly belongs to the created order in the
same way as humanity has been created by God. Once the world was created, it was designated good and

entrusted to human care.

It serves as habitation until the coming of the kingdom and is part of God’s order of preservation of the
human race. In order to ensure happiness and the prospect of survival ‘until he comes’ it should be enjoyed
and must also be treated well. The condition of self-renewal, which Aldo Leopold also stipulates, should
be a natural goal for the human care of the earth. The attribute ‘good’ which God accords to the creation
according to the priestly creation account could also serve as ‘a powerful motive for all sorts of action to
control and limit the exploitation and pollution’ of the created world.'”

An ‘improvement’ or ‘development’ of the land would be outright hypocrisy unless it keeps in mind that
the creation is good already and that it has been created in an ordered and structured way. The interference
with the land, though vital for human survival and the development of culture, must realise that it interferes
with an organic kind. The ruthless exploitation of the land and its natural resources destroys the natural

harmony and usually also the beauty of the land.

But humanity can also live in a symbolic communion with the land without destroying it. This is shown,
for instance, by a farmer who does not just use the land but loves it. Love for the land, however, can either
be kindled by romanticism or by compassion. While romanticism provides a powerful stimulus for earth
care, it overlooks the fact that we do not yet live in a new world to come. Our land is still marked by
earthquakes and fires, and by the larger devouring the smaller. The paradisiacal time of peace when
humanity and the meat-eating animals supposedly were both vegetarians has long since disappeared. By
contrast, a compassionate love towards the land is not necessarily idealistic. Yet it must face the question
how it could be sustained. Simply to assume that humanity should extend its love to nature overlooks the
point that for us humans the land is not a fellow sufferer who understands.'® Humanity must shoulder its
responsibilities alone. The land is an object entrusted to our care. Only in metaphor but not in reality can

we dialogue with nature.

Even Albert Schweitzer’s ethics of reverence for life can engender love for the land only in a very limited
sense. His starting point: ‘I am life which wills to live, in the midst of life which wills to live’ endeavours
to show that I live in a context of life and therefore should accord all life the same reverence as J accord to
my own life.'? We must agree with Albert Schweitzer that we should not make a sharp distinction between
higher and lower forms of life. No life should be destroyed if such destruction can be avoided. This does
not only apply to animal life but to plant life as well. Even picking a flower without necessity is a sin
against life. Yet we live in a fallen world that continuously changes and demands that we reconsider our
values and priorities. Though all killing is evil, some of it is necessary to preserve one form of life against

Oxford, 1949), p.221.

and Eileen Spring,eds., op.cit. p.74.

in the other. Also cf. the Orthodox understanding of an icon.

70
another form. Yet which life should be eliminated and for what reasons so that other life can flourish? In a
fallen world and in the competitiveness it entails decisions, which must be made to militate against loving
every kind of life.

Similarly to a husband and wife loving and cherishing each other, we can only love the land if we
remember that it is a gift which has been entrusted to us so that we can enjoy it and take good care of it.
The failure to conserve the land is not only a misuse of God’s gift but a sin, not only against humans and
animals, but against God. The gift character would also render absolute private rights to land problematic.

Yet do our statements not conflict with observations of Lynn White and Carl Amery that the Judeo-
Christian tradition has neglected the land? If nothing else our perusal of the biblical sources should have
shown that ‘the Bible gives no support to those who would exploit the earth’s resources at the cost of
destroying any species of life.” Yet the New Testament is also a source book for American public policy.
Furthermore, as Lynn White himself admits, Christianity had been around for many centuries before the
modern technological era emerged with its large-scale onslaught on nature. It is therefore questionable
whether there is a direct connection between biblical faith and modern science. As the Islamic mediation
of modern science indicates, it might very well be that the intellectual roots for modern science are found in
the Greek spirit.

We must, however, admit that the modern notion of progress is inexplicable without the Judeo-Christian
belief in a historic progression from the creation of nature to the present course of history and to its
consummation. But the modern idea of progress alienated itself from its Christian foundation. “Though
maintaining a linear view of history, it deprived history of its God-promised goal. Consequently, the
progressiveness of history became an end it itself. At the same time, we promoted ourselves from the
position of God-alienated and God-endowed actors in history to the position of deified agents of history.’”!
Our present situation of environmental exploitation and human deprivation is to a large measure
attributable to this shift in our basic self-understanding.

It would be unfair if we were to blame secularisation alone for the neglect of the land. Christendom too has
been remarkably silent in the process of exploiting nature. The inherent spiritualisation of the land in the
Judeo-Christian tradition was often misunderstood as a license to regard the earthly and bodily dimension
of life as a necessary evil. It could be either abused through ruthless domestication or neglected without
appreciating God’s creation.

Yet the weightiest problem arose when utopian dreams completely moved aside the spiritualistic
dimensions of the Judeo-Christian hope and sought to establish the new world here on earth. It is indeed
part of the Christian stance to proleptically anticipate the envisioned promise already here on earth, as a
sign that one is aligned with the promised goal. Yet disaster arises once imperfect humanity starts out to
realise perfection. Only exertions and ever-greater use of force result from the vain attempts to cover up
one’s blunders. [Instead of issuing a strong eschatological caveat that only God can truly bring about a new
world, calvinistic and pietistic groups rush to the fore in realising the dreams of the pilgrim fathers. The
questionableness of the whole endeavour has long become evident and the new world does not look that
different from the old one from which one had attempted to escape. Yet theologians, such as Friedrich
Gogarten and Harvey Cox hailed the secularisation process as the dawn of a new age. Even today a
theological anthropology can be written by a renowned theologian observing that there is an increasing
anthropocentrism in the development of modern Christian theology which has its legitimate reason in the
fact that Christian theology is related to the salvation of humanity. Then he proceeds to develop his
anthropology without ever considering the life-mediating and life-sustaining cosmos. ]

*°So, rightly, Richard H Hiers, ‘Ecology, Biblical Theology, and Methodology: Biblical Perspectives on the

The Christian pre-occupation with salvation above and beyond this world in a new world to come quite
often has led to a neglect of the created order. This tendency was intensified through the Protestant
reformation. While Luther and Calvin both had a good measure of natural appreciation of nature, their
followers with few exceptions only continued the emphasis on salvation of the individual. Therefore, there
emerged an acosmic personalism, which climaxed in the so-called neo-Reformation theology of Karl Barth
and Rudolf Bultmann.” Only recently has theology rediscovered that salvation has a cosmic dimension
and that history cannot be thought of without the land on which it is executed. Having been so pre-
occupied with the inner self; we have relinquished our responsibility towards the earth. ‘We have forgotten
that our God rejoiced in his creation and declared it to be good.’ In conclusion I would like to summarise a
Christian land ethic noting the following points:

Statements of Faith and Fact

e The earth is an orderly, interdependent system in which plants and animals live, adapted to their
environment over thousands of years.

e The earth is characterised by diversity, richness and a variety of plants, animals, environs, all of which
were declared by God as being good (Gen. 1:31).

e Humans are part of the earth but do not own it since it is a gift from God entrusted to their care (Gen.
1:26; 2:15; Psalm 24:1-2).

e The original innocence of the garden has long vanished; human lives and the land are marred by decay,
selfishness, and strife.

Ethical Statements

e Humans cannot assume that they have an absolute right to the land.

e The land, belonging to the created order, is finite and, has a limited capacity for improvement and
carrying burdens; therefore, just limits must be placed on expansion.

e Jn most places we cannot live on the land without modifying it so that it provides necessary shelter and
nourishment. These necessary cultural interferences with the land should be weighed in several respects:
Do the changes enhance or impair our general well being? Do they unnecessarily inflict harm on the
created order? How do they affect the well being of future generations?

e Since our lifetime is shorter than that of the land and since successive generations are always
depending on the preceding ones, the land should be kept in trust for future generation.

e All life, including our own, is sustained by the land. Therefore, human life cannot have an absolute

DOES CONSERVATION DENOTE DEVELOPMENT?

Elizabeth Tongne
East New Britain Sosel Ekson Komiti
P.O. Box 780, Rabaul, East New Britain Province.

SUMMARY

Conservation and especially developmental issues can raise a considerable amount of interest and
various interpretations among people as to the value of Conservation and Development. In this paper I
will present the Conservation International/East New Britain Sosel Ekson Komiti (CI/ENBSEK)
programme, which was initiated by the landowners themselves. The paper is an account of my own
experiences from the point of view of a traditional landowner and as a co-ordinator of the Wide Bay
Programme. This is not an academic paper. Firstly, I will look at conservation, and secondly, I will
look at development as meaning: 'to bring out the full possibilities of. Finally, my paper will look at
whether Conservation is Development or not.

INTRODUCTION

The presentation in this paper will focus on the challenges and experiences we have encountered while
working with the landowners of the Wide Bay Programme. It will mainly focus on our own and the
landowners’ views as we have struggled together to address the issues of conservation and
development. My presentation is influenced by the types of communities we have been working with.
I have tried to consider Development and Conservation from different angles as this will allow us the
flexibility to facilitate the community’s search for the road to development, which will meet their
specific needs and at the same time allow them to benefit from their resources without compromising
their future. I have tried to choose from the dictionary definitions which suit our situation and which I
felt are most appropriate.

Terms used in this presentation:

Conservation: | The controlled use and systematic protection of natural resources
(The American Heritage School Dictionary)

Denote: Equal or sign of.

Development: __ Is the bringing out of the full possibility of (people and their
natural resources). (Longman Dictionary of Contemporary English)

FUNCTIONS OF CONSERVATION

What are the needs of conservation?

Every living and non living things whether human or natural needs sustainability to continually provide
for each other within the system or cycle of nature. To fulfil man’s satisfaction resources within the
system of nature have to be used in order to provide better quality to life. For the chain of life to
continually provide for man, there must be an understanding of life by man such as:

e Whoam]asa human being,

e What life meant to me,

e@ What life is all about.

So firstly man has to understand himself/herself as a human being and critically decide when, how,
why and where to use his/her resources to enable his/her sustainability. For continuous survival and
satisfaction or fulfillment of life, resources must be used and sustained to provide for future changes.

What are potential disadvantages of conservation?
e Conservation is sometimes understood as a complete ban from any activities by man to satisfy his
basic needs. It would mean that his/her freedom in using natural resources could be very limited.

e In some cases communities are forced to create venues for conservation which do not really mean
anything within the context of the community’s way of life.

e Conservation could be used as a model by those wanting to meet other goals without caring
whether communities fully understand and can benefit from their resources. In the end resources
could be developed but without fully satisfying those immediately concerned.

DEVELOPMENT

Development is understood differently by different people at different levels. For many, development
means a change of life style from the past to the present. Good roads, proper transport, access to basic
services and much more. To clarify more on this let us specifically iook at the types of development. I
will be talking about two, integral human development, and money development, or ‘cargo’
development

This refers to the holistic development of human beings. This is the type of development that takes
place within individuals physically, mentally, spiritually and emotionally. Development must start

within human beings in order to achieve the type of change, which is sustainable both socially and
environmentally.
