WILDLIFE MANAGEMENT AREAS IN BROADER PERSPECTIVE

Stephen Babo
Department of Environment & Conservation
P.O.Box 6601, Boroko, NCD

INTRODUCTION

Wildlife Management Area (WMA) is Papua New Guinea’s home grown concept to Protected Area
Systems for conserving wildlife in the country. WMaAs are protected areas established under the Fauna
(Protection and Control) Act to help village people conserve or look after the wildlife on their land. In
most cases, WMAs can only be declared at the request of the landowners, but the declaration does not
in any way affect the ownership of the land.

There have been a few cases where WMAs have been declared because of their scientific importance;
in which case landowners have been talked into establishing the area asa WMA. Two examples of this
are the Crater Mountain and Lake Kutubu WMAs.

THE WILDLIFE MANAGEMENT AREA SYSTEM

Wildlife Management Areas are traditionally-owned areas in which the landowners form a
management committee to oversee the area, and define rules for the protection, management and
controlled exploitation of the fauna. The establishment and management of WMAs involve
consultation and participation of the community or communities concerned, before the Minister grants
approval and the rules are gazetted. The rules may provide for hunting licenses, license fees, and
royalties to be paid for any animals taken.

Legislation

The Fauna (Protection and Control) Act 1966 and its amendments, provide for the establishment of
Wildlife Management Areas, in most cases, on traditional land with day-to-day management provided
by the resource owners, in conjunction with the Department of Environment and Conservation (DEC).

The Department mostly responds to requests from resource owners for the establishment of WMAs.
The resource owners then establish systems for managing the area, monitor the environment, charge for
services as appropriate and enforce management rules negotiated for each area. The formal
establishment of WMAs lends government authority to these landowner managed areas. The WMA
concept has been the major conservation area strategy currently used in PNG.

Purpose

Resource owners appear to have a variety of motives for establishing WMAs, the main one being the
basic land right/ownership issues. Most WMA arise from the desire of a community to consolidate
their ownership over a parcel of land and regulate the use of that land by ‘outsiders.’ These outsiders
may be visiting hunters or squatters. WMAs are susceptible to change in status if the resource owners
are so inclined. No clear strategy exists for dealing with this eventuality.

Biodiversity Conservation

Biodiversity conservation in PNG requires large conservation areas where the integrity of the system
can be protected. Areas need to be in selected locations that are species rich and/or high in endemism.
These areas need to be managed for conservation on a long-term basis. Since they are established
primarily to meet a variety of resource owner needs, the WMAs are often of inappropriate size and
location from a biodiversity conservation point of view. In reality, WMAs serve less of a protection
function than is perceived. However, the potential does exist for WMAs to provide valuable inputs to
biodiversity conservation provided they are established with that perspective in mind and managed

(supported) for that purpose.

719

WMaAs, then, play an important role in meeting certain social and conservation needs felt by resource
owners, but can only be regarded as one of a number of possible contributors to achieving biodiversity
conservation.

Selection of Wildlife Management Areas

There is no statutory requirement for assessing a site’s appropriateness as a WMA under the Fauna
(Protection and Control) Act as long as landowners see a need for management or conservation in their
areas. Further, there appears to have been no systematic effort by DEC to develop a network of related
or linked Fauna protected areas.

Existing WMA

Currently there are twenty-eight areas declared under the Fauna Act as WMAs and about a hundred
more are being proposed by the landowners. The existing WMAs are declared on an ad hoc basis
mostly according to the landowners’ request.

Apart from Maza and Tonda, most of the other WMAs are very small by international standards and as
such, they are unlikely to conserve their present flora and fauna in the long term especially if
surrounding lands with their forests are cleared and there are no opportunities for species migration.

CONCLUSION

To conclude, WMA has been widely seen and accepted by landowners as both a means of re-enforcing
their rights over their land as well as protecting wildlife on the land. However, recently with more and
more development taking place resulting in more forests being cleared, landowners are beginning to
look at establishing WMA to protect some of their forests. I strongly believe that WMAs, if refined,
can also be used for biodiversity conservation. After all, in terms of size, it has been shown at Tonda
(590,00ha) and Maza (18,230ha) that large areas of land can be designated and declared as Wildlife
Management Areas to cater for biodiversity conservation. This is of course provided they are given
enough funding and attention by DEC (i.e., DEC has more to do than what they are doing at present).

80

PRIVATE CONTRACTUAL AGREEMENTS FOR CONSERVATION INITIATIVES

Brian D. Brunton
Greenpeace Pacific,
P.O. Box 136, Gerehu, NCD.

INTRODUCTION

This paper starts from a heresy: the proposition that conservation and development are matters of business.
For the purposes we intend, (using private law to conserve land) ‘development’ must mean business and
commerce. This is so because private law is about private property, business, contracts, etc. It is not about |
conservation, the warm and furry and the wet and slippery. Landholders want the good things in life; they |
want money, which is made in business. In Papua New Guinea, with its largely undeveloped regulatory
economic framework, business and commerce are in essence matters of private law. It is implicit in this
paper that conservation initiatives under the public law options in Papua New Guinea of the Conservation
Areas Act, National Parks Act, Fauna Protection and Control Act are so problematic due to systemic
mismanagement of the legislation that they cannot be relied on to bring ‘development’. Forget
conservation for the time being. The answer to ‘development’ lies in private law agreements. One simply
cannot do business within the framework of any legislation that is administered by DEC. Private law, the
law of agreements, protects and enforces agreements, not only between the parties who intend to be bound
by the agreements and who freely enter into the agreements, but also from interfering third parties who
wrongfully try to disrupt business or contracts....and somewhere down the end here the lawyer drafts into
the agreements a series of clauses that conserve and look after the furry, the feathered and the wet and
slippery as a condition of making money. The bottom line is that if you want to protect a conservation area
it is better to go directly to a national Court judge and ask for a contract to be enforced, than it is to ask a
DEC officer to enforce the provisions of the legislation administered by that department.

OUTLINE OF THE ARGUMENT

Basic rule

Under Papua New Guinea law there are only two ways of acquiring property rights. The first is by consent:
agreement, or contract. The second is by compulsory acquisition. In essence this means that only the
State, using procedures set out by Act of Parliament can force anybody to give up their property. Indeed,
property rights are protected by the Constitution. The private property may not be compulsorily acquired
without ‘fair compensation’. For general purposes ‘fair compensation’ will mean ‘market value’. In fact,
the State seldomly uses its powers of compulsory acquisition. In the context of customary land,
compulsorily acquisition is traumatic, political and even expensive.

All customary land is private property

97% of the land in Papua New Guinea is owned privately, with a status of title that is vested in traditional
groups in perpetuity (forever and ever). The Constitution and various Acts of Parliament recognise custom
as being part of the legal system of this country. Traditionally owned land is recognised as such by the law.
The courts will enforce trespasses against customary land in the same way as they will enforce trespasses
against alienated land. Traditional ownership has always included a power to exclude trespassers, and
asserted rights of exclusive possession. These are the hallmarks of title. In this respect, although there
have been no decided cases on the subject, fishing-ground, reefs and areas of the sea are private property.

Customary land may not be alienated privately

Generally, the title to customary land, and interests in customary land may only pass according to custom,
and customary procedures must be followed in transferring interests in customary land from group to
group, or group to person (aboriginal inhabitant). This creates issues of certainty of title as there is no
mechanism for registering customary title at present (the issue is politically sensitive). An Act of

81

Parliament (the Lands Act) stops interests in customary land being alienated to persons who are not
aboriginal inhabitants. Such transactions must be done through the State, either state purchase, state
leasing, or lease-leaseback arrangements.

Trees and fixtures are part of the land

The common law recognised the ownership of anything attached to the land as being with the title holder.
Buildings, machinery attached to buildings, crops, trees etc are part of the land in modern law. [Hence you
must be careful to properly survey any alienated land before building on it. If you put the building on
someone else’s land, it could be costly mistake. ]

Promises, agreement, contracts

Where two or more people agree that they intend to enter into a formal legal relationship, and they
exchange promises, and value in money or money’s worth is promised in exchange, or in fact is exchanged
(called by lawyers: consideration), the law will say that a contract exists. Contracts are binding and will be

enforced by the courts.

Covenants (promises)-that-run-with-the-land, and those that don’t.

The law has always distinguished between two types of promises or agreements that are associated with
land. While the law (Lands Act) stops a customary owner from entering into a contract which sells an
interest in land [a covenant that runs with the land], the Land Act does not stop a contract for an activity
that goes on top of the land. For example if customary land holders want to sell gravel rights to a builder
they cannot enter into an agreement which passes any rights to the gravel while it sits at the bottom of the
river. But they can give a license to the builder to enter onto the land with a front-end loader as their agent,
dig up the gravel for them and put it into piles on the banks of the river. The piles of gravel can then be
sold, in the same way the villagers can sell a pile of coconuts. In that situation, the sale of the gravel dug
up and dumped on top of the land does not involve a covenant that runs with the land. It is a straight
forward sale of goods. So the way around the Land Act is to avoid covenants that run with the land.

Agreements involving Papua New Guinea

Papua New Guinea villagers are bound in modern law by the contracts they enter into, in the same way as
any other person. (‘Customary agreements’ such as bride price, are not strictly matters of modern contract
law, they follow the rules of the particular custom of the group involved, although do not be surprised if
Village Courts and other customary adjudicators adopt modern contract rules when applying them).
Certainly, the courts will enforce an agreement in proper legal form, expressly stating that it intends to take
the parties out of customary relationships. If the contract is breached the defaulting party will be liable for
damages and, in financial terms, will have to compensate the plaintiff for the fair value of the lost bargain.
The courts may decide that damage alone is not a sufficient remedy, and order that the defendant carry out
the terms of the contract. Orders for specific performance, as they are known, apply against the person of
the defaulting party. If they refuse to carry out the order of the court to deliver up the promise they have
made, they are in contempt of the court, and they risk a jail sentence until the contempt is purged. The only
difficulty with Papua New Guinean villagers is in their identity. It is a protracted and costly exercise to
make an inventory of a land-owning entity, and to pursue it through the courts. Clans and lineages are in
law unincorporated associations; if an unincorporated association enters into a contract then each member
of the association is jointly and severally liable for the debt incurred. Redress in the courts can be sought
against each or every one of the members of the association. Such actions have been made a lot easier with
the enactment of the Land Groups Incorporation Act. An incorporated land group can sue and be sued in
its own name in relation to the contracts if enters into.

While it is true that written agreements between parties may make litigation easier, the full benefit of a
written agreement is not in its ultimate enforceability by the courts, but in its function as a guide to the
future behavior of the parties to the agreement. Contracts are plans: ‘we agree to conduct our relationships
in the manner we now put into writing; hopefully we will never disagree about how this relationship is to
operate in practice, but if we do, and we have to go to arbitration or (heaven forbid, court) then this is what

we intended. ..etc, etc.’

82
Agreements to conserve and develop

The ‘development’ side of conservation and development may well be nothing more than a business
arrangement. It could be cast in terms of pure business or commercial arrangements or a mixture of
commercial and social arrangements. An agreement not to use land, to set it aside may not in itself be
commercial or for a business purpose, but there is no reason why agreements to set aside land for
conservation purposes cannot be conditions of a wider agreement to do business. Providing the agreement
does not run with the land (is not about transferring the title or ownership of an interest in customary land),
or is not a restraint of trade (to be explained later), the law will uphold agreements involving Papua New
Guinean villagers or their lawfully incorporated bodies. The law will uphold such agreements if the subject
matter is a promise to do something on the land, or not to do something on the land, and consideration for
money paid or services rendered etc., land be used in such a way as is described in the contract. For
example, a technical assistance agreement may promise that one party will provide technical assistance
(goods and services) to develop a particular business venture, on a condition that the other party pays
certain fees or other expenses and does not sign a Forest Management Agreement with the Papua New
Guinea Forest Authority during the duration of the agreement. It would be possible to draw up a contract
that agreed to provide goods and services to an incorporated land group providing they promised to set
aside a designated part of their land for conservation purposes, and the regulation of that conservation area
could be made a condition of the contract.

Unlawful restraints on trade

The law of agreements hated monopolies and unreasonable restraints on trade. To attempt to tie up large
areas of forests over long periods of time may be considered an unreasonable restraint. But it would not be
too difficult to draft around these rules and give protection and exclusivity in proportion to the other
benefits of the contract.

Conspiracy to induce a breach of a contract

The law of torts (civil wrongs) creates a civil action against any person who, as a third party, wrongfully
disrupts the business of other parties, or tries to induce a breach in an existing contract. If an NGO comes
between a logging company and a land-owning company and seeks to disrupt the business arrangements,
the NGO risks an action against it. The law protects contracts and contracting parties from the unlawful
interference in their private business and their agreements. The law does not always have to help the forces
of darkness. It can be turned around and used by the forces of light. Losses caused by unlawful
interferences in the contracts and businesses of others are compensatable with damages, and the defendants
(loggers or Forest Authority) have injunctive orders against them. These laws can be used to defend
agreements to conserve and develop.

83

PROTECTED AREA ESTABLISHMENT: THE FIRST OR THE LAST STEP IN AN ICAD
PROCESS?

Larry Orsak
Bismarck-Ramu ICAD Initiative
C/- Conservation Resource Centre
P.O. Box 165, Waigani, NCD.

ABSTRACT

Timing is all-important when implementing integrated conservation and development (ICAD) projects.
For example, when should we implement ‘protected areas’ — early in the ICAD process, or as a last
step? This essay weighs some costs and benefits of establishing formal protected areas at different
stages of an ICAD process. This is judged against the option of doing away with formal establishment
altogether, relying instead on an informally designated, community-enforced protected area. The
current tendency to try and establish government-sanctioned protected areas early in an ICAD process
can lead fundamental problems which only surface later. ‘Early establishment’ seems mostly driven by
donor pressures, or represents an artefact of developed country strategies, automatically adopted by
Papua New Guinea.

INTRODUCTION: ‘TIMING’ AS A CONSIDERATION IN THE IMPLEMENTATION OF
ICAD ACTIVITY

The integrated conservation and development (ICAD) process in Papua New Guinea is still evolving.
We're still debating whether or not certain events and activities should be part of the process. The
ICAD process is incredibly complex (Wells & Brandon, 1993) so it makes sense that we should be
analysing and evaluating rather deeply. It is not just ‘whether’ something should be part of an ICAD
process; ‘when’ is an equally important consideration. An example is the introduction of economic
incentives in an ICAD process:

e when introduced early, such incentives represent an external motivator that can be viewed more
as a bribe, than as a reward.

e when appearing later, after the community has motivated itself towards ICAD objectives, the
very same incentives tend to reinforce people’s conservation behaviour, and can boost local support
for the conservation outcomes of the ICAD initiative.

This issue of ‘timing’ is equally important when we consider the establishment of formal protected
areas within an ICAD area of interest.

The first step in Papua New Guinea’s ICAD process has usually been to follow the results of the 1992
Conservation Need Assessment (Alcorn, 1993). That assessment pinpointed regions of exceptional
biodiversity. All ICAD sites in Papua New Guinea today, not surprisingly, cover “biodiversity hot
spot’ areas to some extent.

Following this ‘biological criteria assessment’ step — which sometimes is done informally -- the ICAD
project implementors interact with people of the area. During that process, which can last some time,
the establishment of a formal protected area usually finds itself on the agenda rather quickly. These
days, the protected area vehicle is normally considered to be either a Wildlife Management Area or a
Conservation Area (although no Conservation Areas have yet been gazetted). Protected area
establishment thus becomes an early objective in the ICAD process, rather than a late goal... which, of
course, fits the way protected areas have been established in Australia, the United States, and other
countries.

84

But is this really the right sequence of steps for Papua New Guinea? Let’s explore!

PROTECTED AREA ESTABLISHMENT AS AN EARLY STEP IN AN ICAD PROCESS

The Benefits

A Rallying Point for Action: A protected area is, theoretically, something to rally around, because it
represents a tangible accomplishment. Once an established Wildlife Management Area exists, for
example, it then can become a symbol that the community can support and work towards maintaining.
A protected area can develop into a kind of status symbol.

Creates A Community Institution and Benchmark That Can Help Sustain the Linked Activities:
A pathway to achieving the sustainability of any process is to create individual conviction within a
community. When those convictions begin to be transferred across generations, through teachings and
in-common observations and experiences, then the convictions start becoming part of local culture — a
fundamental basis of social sustainability of an ICAD area.

In the context of ‘building convictions’, a protected area can serve, in effect, as a community or clan
totem. The longer that ‘totem’ remains, the more likely it becomes part of the local culture, and thus
stimulates a mechanism by which it will be sustained as a symbol. Many long-established protected
areas (Gahavisuka Provincial Park near Goroka; Varirata National Park; Tondo Wildlife Management
Area) may represent examples of this process of institutionalisation. In other words, they stand as a
piece of land that will be used in a specified way, and this decision is no longer really questioned by
the customary landowners.

A little-known example, which strongly indicates the same process, is the Crater Lake National Park.
This is a small protected area owned and gazetted by the government, located near Afore on the
Managalase Plateau (Oro Province). It was gazetted and surveyed around the time of Independence.
~The local landowners, until recently, adhered to the boundaries and did not violate the integrity of the
area. This is especially interesting since there were no development benefits, such as tourism or any
other tangible rewards that flowed to the customary landowners or surrounding villages by leaving the
land alone. Nevertheless, they looked after the area.

Within the last two years, a village councillor made a garden within the boundaries. Local landowners ©
immediately reported this to the National Parks officer in Popondetta (John Gore); in the face of
opposition, the counsellor retreated. That incident indicates that this small National Park has

essentially become a local institution. The fact that this protected area was established before or soon

or Independence may be significant in understanding why there is so much respect for a kind of land

use that was largely imposed by an outside body. Since the lake protected by the park has traditional

spiritual value, one might argue that the park’s land use designation is locally driven, albeit indirectly.

Yet, note that other government land in the area, which has remained undeveloped, and does not have

anywhere near the same spiritual status, also largely has its integrity respected by the Afore people.

A Means of Keeping the Government’s Other Hands Off? Once the Department of Environment
& Conservation agrees upon a specific land use for an area, there is some legal indication that this
decision cannot be pre-empted or overridden by other government bodies. This seems to apply more
to the Forest Authority (which is ‘developing’ a resource whose ownership is vested with traditional
holders), and less so to the Department of Minerals & Petroleum (which develops a resource whose
ownership is constitutionally vested with the State). The example of Crater Mountain Wildlife
Management Area (Eastern Highlands Province) being encroached by mineral exploration suggests
that the ‘stamp of approval’ offered by the Department of Environment & Conservation may, in fact,
be ignored by other government departments. Thus, formal gazetting and establishment of a protected
area is a government ‘stamp of approval’ that today carries uncertain power.

Evidence That Biodiversity Conservation Has Been Achieved: The conservation of PNG’s
biological diversity is PNG government obligation, taken on when Papua New Guinea became a

85

signatory of the international Convention on Biological Diversity. The conservation of biological
diversity, in fact, is considered the primary conservation objective for an ICAD. People are much
more uneasy that biodiversity conservation is being achieved without some sort of protected area being

present.

A High Profile Benchmark for Donors: All donors want to see some sign of progress in an ICAD,
and all ICADs have an ICAD facilitator that is searching for funds, be it an NGO or a government
body. What better symbol of conservation achievement than a gazetted, formal ‘protected area’?
Because so many ICAD initiatives are chronically short of funding, this reason alone is sufficient
impetus to motivate the ICAD facilitator to get a protected area established... the sooner the better!

The Problems

Every action has an equal and opposite reaction! There are benefits accrued by making protected area
establishment as an early goal. Achieving this early in the ICAD process, however, also creates

problems:

You Can’t Shortcut Community Consensus-Building; Consensus Will Probably Require More
Time Than It Used To! Before Independence, customary landowners certainly knew a lot less about
(a) the economic value of their nature resources, whether on the reefs or in the forests; and (b) the
options available to convert that resource wealth into cash wealth. Even in somewhat remote areas,
those realisations have grown with time. That knowledge has empowered customary landowners to the
extent that they are less likely to agree to any one outsider’s arguments or the government’s
sentiments, with regard to how they should use their land or reefs. In the old days, a wildlife officer
could share his viewpoints on the value of establishing a Wildlife Management Area, and the
community would quickly buy into that idea, perhaps superficially, but still strongly enough that a
protected area could be formally gazetted. Back then, the land use alternatives weren’t as conspicuous
or as available. Nowadays, there is likely to be a diversity of opinions within the community on what
to do with the resources. Consensus will be harder to reach. Yet, community consensus remains
crucial, if the goal is to have some sustainability in any decision made by that community.

When protected area establishment is slotted early in an ICAD process, it ‘hurries up’ a process that
today is taking more time than it did 50 years ago — the process of community consensus-building.
Speeding up this process seems artificial; after all, how could the community possibly come to rally
around the idea of a ‘protected area’ so quickly, considering the reality of today’s rural communities
and their increasing diversity of opinion and education? It doesn’t make sense. In that context, what
really happens when a protected area is formally established early in an ICAD process? Probably,
the community has not reached full consensus. They probably are vor all in agreement that a piece of
their land should be set aside in a way that precludes ‘flashy,’ yet non sustainable, land and resource
uses, such as commercial, large-scale logging). Thus, support for the protected area in that community
is likely to be shallow. This superficiality should frighten ICAD practitioners. Yet, the problem may
not surface until ideas for an alternative use for the resources attracts the community’s attention, takes

hold, and starts growing.

Helps Create A Foundation for Potential ‘Conservation Blackmail’: Wildlife Management Areas
that were established years ago, never had an implementing non governmental organisation (NGO)
‘facilitator’ associated with them. These early Wildlife Management Areas also tended to derive more
from community-based motivation (or the initiative of a bigman), rather than being driven by outside-

origin motivation.

Before the days of PNG-ICADs, there was far less talk about linking economic development with
protected area establishment. Instead, the benefits which came from Wildlife Management Area
establishment, tended to be nothing more than a continuation of those benefits already in place — the
harvesting of megapode eggs, for example, in West New Britain; or the hunting of wallabies and deer

in Tonda Wildlife Management Area.

Today we operate within the ICAD environment. Economic and other development incentives take a
prominent role in our ICAD planning, and are discussed alongside protected area establishment. We

86

have seen how this emphasis on economic benefits frequently places ICAD practitioners in an
uncomfortable competition with commercial loggers; the abandoned Lak ICAD was the most extreme
example (McCallum & Sekhran, 1997). From a local community’s standpoint, this outside
competition for their resources (competition between the outside conservation agencies versus the
commercial loggers) is ideal: ‘Who is going to offer us the most for our resources, the ICAD people or
the loggers?’ they may ask.

If a protected area is established early in an ICAD process, it represents both a commitment and an
investment by the ICAD agency (be it government or NGO) into the community. The problem is, this
investment — if it occurs early in the first years of a community interaction process — takes place before
there is real trust between the community and the ICAD agency and any evidence (i.e., the presence of
conservation conviction) of a lasting commitment by the community. At that early stage, the
community could not possibly be wholly and strongly convinced that the ICAD agency really has the
community’s best interests at heart.

If we are operating in an environment where trust is not total, and commitment is not deep, a lot of
investment by the ICAD agency creates an imbalance. This, in turn, creates the potential for
‘conservation blackmail.’ Many of us have seen this expressed in the following form: ‘If you don’t
start doing more things for the community, we will go to loggers and cut down all the trees in that
WMA.” Such confrontations are harder to deal with when there is a formal protected area established,
and even harder still when the ICAD facilitator has established infrastructure in the community — too
much investment that one hates to waste! This was one of the valuable lessons learned through the
now-defunct Lak ICAD project in southern New Ireland.

Formal Boundaries Can Create Suspicion: When there is formal gazettal of a protected area, clear
boundaries must be established. When this occurs in the early years of the ICAD agency’s interactions
with local communities, a number of problems can surface:

e Restriction of land use ‘rights’: protected area gazettal clearly marks off an area which will
receive a specified land use (in the case of a Wildlife Management Area), which in turn, will clearly
restrict other forms of land use. Land use is such an important decision, and the freedom of land
use is a paramount consideration. Traditionally, land use changes either came about suddenly and
violently through fighting, or came from years of slow transition. In that context, it’s hard to
believe that any strong community designation of land use for a protected area could appear after
only a few years of community interactions with the ICAD facilitating body.

e Finality of the Decision: Gazettal creates a black-and-white documentation which can fuel land
and resource disputes, particularly if the overall protected area covers land of more than one clan
or family line.

e Exaggeration of Misunderstandings: it may create suspicion as to why the ICAD agency wanted
to mark off boundaries: Nowadays, the topic of land registration and the World Bank is very likely
to come into the picture. Virtually any rural community that has access to radio knows of these
issues. This creates greater suspicion that any outsider who seems interested in people’s land or
resources, may be interested for reasons that would not meet with the community’s approval — such
as wanting to take away their land.

The Community Is Tempted To Relinquish Its Responsibility for Enforcement: The original
source of local requests to establish many Wildlife Management Areas has been to try and ‘lock up’
the land and resources so that neighbouring clans/tribes cannot encroach or poach, as might have been
the case (or the threat) before WMA establishment. This was true for the megapode egg sites in West
New Britain (King, 1989) and also to some extent for Crater Mountain, where the local people feared
the continued encroachment of neighbouring Simbu people.

Communities often face situations where growing competition for resources in the proposed protected
area exists; one party — the ‘true’ landowners -- hopes that establishing a protected area will solve the
problem. How? A common assumption is that once the government gives its ‘okay’ to the protected
area by gazetting it, the government will/should take over some responsibility for enforcing laws

87

related to the protected area, and help the customary owners keep encroachers out. Operating under
that assumption, landowners would be tempted to ‘let down their guard’ and keep hoping that the
government will take over. During that time, encroachments could increase and squatters even move
into the area.

In reality, the government does not take on these responsibilities, and the customary resource owners
are left holding the bag. However, if the resource owners have already let down their guard in
enforcing the desired uses of their protected area, it may be an uphill battle to re-establish the
motivation and conviction to carry out this enforcement. The fact is that no one wants to be
confrontational to others, yet enforcing a protected area’s rules absolutely requires some confrontation.
Thus, the integrity of the protected area could be permanently destroyed, because formally designating
the protected area led to the customary owners losing their motivation to enforce the designated uses
for that site.

Offers Hope for Claiming Title to Land and Resources: It makes sense that a community that does
not clearly hold title to land, will try any means to establish ownership. Boundary setting and
government approval for protected areas represents a kind of ‘stamp of approval’ which can be
promoted as a stamp of approval for ownership. In the course of formally establishing a protected
area, it makes sense for one group to hide from the ICAD facilitators any other claimants to the land.
In return, it is all-too-appealing for the ICAD facilitator to ignore the possibility of land disputes, and
accept the community’s word at face value. Clearly, this creates a situation that can later explode and
do great damage to the ICAD initiative.

Creates A Basis for Disputes by Increasing the Perceived Value of the Resource: People may
overlap in their use of resources, but don’t fight over those resources until they are perceived to have
great value. The simple process of formally gazetting and boundary-marking a piece of land as a
protected area shows an investment in the resource. This, in turn, indicates that the resource is of
greater value than people thought before. All this is apparent to neighboring landowners. Thus,
formal surveying and gazettal of protected areas is likely to create disputes, rather than diminish them,
which then drains scarce resources (especially a local community’s time and energy) away from the
ICAD process.

Underlying Causes of the Problems Derived from ‘Early Establishment’

The problems that can come from formally designating protected areas too early in the ICAD process

have these underlying causes:

e Outside imposition: gazettal represents a certain amount of outside (albeit passive) imposition on
a community’s land use; very, very rarely do we have an ICAD situation where the idea for gazettal
has totally derived from the landowners themselves.

e Insufficient Discussion Time: early in the ICAD process, there simply has not been enough time
to discuss these issues to the point that they generate a deep, widespread community consensus on
how that land or reef, targeted for a protected area, should be managed. Thus, the formal
establishment of a protected area early on in the ICAD process may ‘hurry up’ too much a natural
community process of reaching the same goals, but more solidly.

e Dependency on Outsiders: without a long education and community facilitation process taking

place first, speeding up protected area establishment sets a trend of outside ideas for action
dominating over community-generated ideas. That reduces the chances of internally derived
motivation for ICAD activities taking place within the community, which can eliminate a crucial
element for social sustainability of those [CAD actions.

88

PROTECTED AREA ESTABLISHMENT AS A LATE STEP IN THE ICAD PROCESS

Benefits Accruing from ‘Early Establishment’ of Protected Areas: Are They Restricted to the
‘Early Establishment’ Option?

Even if there are many problems that could result when we force early establishment of formal
protected areas, that does not rule it out as the favoured option. We need to evaluate if the benefits
accrued (as previously outlined) are significant and do not extend to alternative strategies — e.g.,
establishing protected areas /ate in the ICAD process, or even doing away completely with formal
designation.

In this section, I will review each of the ‘benefits from early establishment’ in the context of whether
those benefits are restricted to this early strategy alone.

A Rallying Point for Action and a Community Institution? The Kau Wildlife Area (Madang
Province) and the Collingwood Bay ICAD area (Oro Province) are excellent examples of community-
designated, not government-designated, protected areas. Moreover, in the case of the Kau Wildlife
Area, we see the start of a trend whereby the protected area symbol is being integrating into the local
culture -- it has now been embraced by 2 generations, and has infected some members of the third.
Collingwood Bay too has no government-designated ICAD protected area. Instead, the nature
resources of a very large area are being used as usual — mostly sustainably — and some Maisin people
are rallying around ICAD more as a way of life, than a project that is focused on a particular piece of
land. From these two examples, it would appear that it is not essential to designate a protected area
early in the ICAD process in order to utilise the protected area as a rallying point for action and as a
community institution.

As A Means of Keeping Other Hands of the Government Off: Indisputably, the hand of
government is weak in PNG. In fact, it is growing weaker, as witnessed every time mineral resource
owners demand a greater share of the royalty pot. In the case of Kau Wildlife Area, the resource
owners already repelled a strong logging offer that included strong-arm lobbying by provincial forestry
staff. The Maisin people of Collingwood Bay had their land designated as a potential forestry area, but
rebelled to the point that the Forest Authority gave in, writing a letter stating that the government
would not solicit any more potential logging companies. Again, the community’s motivation towards
repelling non-sustainable resource use seems very important in determining the ultimate use of the land
and its resources. This local sentiment ultimately seems more effective than the legal advantages
gained by having a formally designated protected area. Thus, the verdict is that this oft-stated benefit
of gazetting protected areas is a bit dubious.

A High Profile Benchmark for Donors: This source of pressure for getting a protected area
designated will continue. Early gazetting of a protected area is the best way to show accomplishments
to donors. At the same time, arguments against early gazetting might be accepted by a donor if
alternative, equally convincing performance measures of progress were offered in substitution.

This, then, may be the biggest benefit that is achieved when we put protected area establishment as an
early step in the ICAD process, and is not so much available at a later stage. To that, we have to add
the importance of ‘tradition.’ The fact is, the ‘early establishment’ strategy is conventional —
traditionally, when government has established protected areas, there was not a lot of community
interaction that had to take place. The land was purchased, the protected area established. Any ICAD
field practitioner working in PNG knows that community consensus building takes time. Yet,
somehow this experience hasn’t been compared against why we try to hurry up protected area
designation.

Harnessing the Benefits of the Late Establishment Option:
Why Not Trust Villagers To Come Up With the ‘Protected Area’ Idea
On Their Own?

Early designation of a protected area in an ICAD process almost certainly means that outsiders (usually
staff of the government or NGO body that is facilitating the ICAD process) brought in the protected

89

area idea. The idea almost certainly was not generated from within the community as a means of
sustainably using their natural resources. That this process of outside imposition continues virtually
unnoticed, almost implies that communities wouldn’t or shouldn’t come up with the ideas themselves.

This is wrong. Indeed, if the community itself came up with the idea of establishing a protected area,
this would garner greater community support, and better chances for sustaining the decision through
appropriate conservation action. Why? Because the mere fact that the idea came from the community,
rather than the ICAD facilitating agency, shows that there is community motivation for the ICAD
process itself, rather than community motivation for ICAD ‘cargo. ’ Surely, as ICAD practitioners, we
would prefer that situation.

We role-played this strategy once with the Community Development Team that interacts with local
communities occurring within the Bismarck-Ramu ICAD area of interest. The team members played
the villagers, while I played the role of ICAD facilitating agency representative, who came into the
village. The Community Development Team asked questions concerning resource use and
conservation, and we talked about their experiences of using resources in the past, versus today, and
how growing populations made traditional hunting methods less sustainable. The team members did
not know at the time where this discussion was leading (they are trained as community interactors, not
as conservationists); the team members simply considered the information presented to them. As they
came to see the relationship of increased human population and greater efficiency at utilising nature
resources and the depletion of those resources, guess what? Someone brought up the idea of setting
aside a place where the animals and resources could recover and would be left alone. I did not give

them this idea; they came up with it themselves!

I have faith that there are many smart people in the villages, and given a body of information and
knowledge, they will see for themselves the value of a protected area. Yet, this community facilitation
process often takes time. It cannot be done effectively in a few meetings or a few months. It is likely
to take a couple of years, before the community as a whole sees a protected area as a useful tool for
sustainable resource use, and seeks such an establishment.

CONCLUSIONS

Each ICAD area must be taken on a case-by-case basis, as to when the formal establishment of a
protected area should occur. The most important ‘lesson learned’ about ICADs is that they much
incorporate a flexible process within an overall framework (Wells & Brandon, 1992).

The timing of any ICAD activities within the overall ICAD process is a good place to measure how
flexible an ICAD project is. Hence, this is an area that we can evaluate a project’s flexibility to assess
its overall chances of success. Yet, while timing must be flexible, it must be purposely considered and
planned. Activities should not be solely scheduled into the ICAD process because that is when they
‘just happened.’ Weighing the pros and cons of scheduling such ICAD activities as ‘economic
incentives’ and ‘protected area establishment’ at different times in the ICAD process is an important
capacity-building exercise, and an important decision in-and-of-itself.

Warnings that the timing of protected area establishment may be wrong, include situations where:

e The main driving force for protected area designation is from outside (e.g., donor support).
That is, protected area establishment is largely being used as a benchmark of achievement

for the outside world.

e The idea for a formal protected area originally came from the ICAD facilitating body,
instead of being a community-derived idea, or a proposal that was offered to the
community only after the community reached an impasse.

e interactions between the community and the ICAD facilitating organisation (NGO or

government body) have not gone on long enough, or intensively enough. The measure of
‘long enough’ is to evaluate if periods of friction or misunderstanding between that agency

90

and members of the local community still occur. Protected area designation can create
new areas of friction, particularly if some suspicion of the ICAD facilitator by the
community remains.

If protected areas are gazetted too early, the achievement may be an illusion. An ICAD programme
that can claim an established protected area looks successful. However, this may be masking problems
that will later threaten the integrity of that area. The underlying purpose of formal protected area
designation should be to ensure the long-term conservation integrity of that area. However, if the
timing is not right, then this integrity will be threatened by other factors. Formal gazettal may, in fact,
create problems in maintaining the area’s integrity, rather than contribute to sustainable conservation
of the resource.

There is plenty of precedent in PNG to show that a protected area does not have to be government-
sanctioned and government-formalised, in order to exist and be sustained. A protected area is still
considered ‘formally designated’ if the community alone makes that decision, and makes it firmly. All
the customary masalai areas and other tambu areas are examples of community-formalised land use
designations, which were maintained in a sustainable manner through custom. Probably this kind of
situation applies mostly to small land areas, or areas held, without dispute, by one family line.
However, the conditions under which such traditional protected areas can be sustained, bear further
exploration.

If the reason for gazetting a protected area is to gain funding or support from donors, which then feeds
into the ICAD facilitator, we must seriously question if this in fact, should be the proper main
motivator for protected area establishment.

In the Bismarck-Ramu ICAD initiative, an unusual approach is being tested: government gazettal of
any protected area is being left until late in the process. Initially, we have identified a region of interest
in which communities are contacted. In this area, Community Development Team members will walk
and talk and visit villages. Through that process of interaction, team members do not to talk about
protected areas and do not draw boundaries. Total emphasis is being placed on community
interactions and discussions of community problems. An animation process found so effective for
rural development projects is the community interaction technique (Hope & Timmel, 1984). The
overall process -is to uplift what the community’s real problems are, and when possible, link
conservation concepts and sustainable development mechanisms with a third goal: to increase the
community’s self-reliance in solving their basic problems, instead of relying so much on ICAD
facilitating bodies or anyone else.

These interactions seem to have little to do with the objectives we hold as conservationists. Yet, in
time, the issue of conservation will be brought up for discussion by the community itself, not by us.
And in time, we believe that the importance of protected areas will become a revelation to community
members themselves, if they truly become committed to sustainable resource use. That is when it is
time to think about gazetted protected areas.

What if the communities never come up with these ideas and sentiments about using their natural
resources sustainably? We’d argue, that is not a problem! Why then carry out the ICAD process with
any community that is not truly committed to sustainable resource use? After all, ICAD project
resources are scarce; scarcity mandates that we work with the best possible candidate communities!

“KAMPANI BILONG ENVIROMEN’:
COMMUNITY MOTIVATION FOR BIODIVERSITY CONSERVATION
IN THE BISMARCK-RAMU ICAD AREA.

Flip Van Helden!
Prins Hendriklaan 16
3972 EV Driebergen-R
The Netherlands.

INTRODUCTION

In a short presentation to the Department of Environment and Conservation in June, I described ICAD
projects as a ‘deal’ or ‘transaction’ rather than as a traditional aid project. The reason for this view was that
ICAD projects are different from other aid projects in that the local communities with whom the project
interacts are not in the first place the intended beneficiaries of the project. Something called ‘biodiversity’ is.
In the context of PNG land tenure ICAD projects are primarily aimed at securing conservation from and by
local communities, and only secondarily at bringing development éo local communities.

By demanding conservation from local communities the project team imposes a cost on people who, under
the terms of the ICAD deal, are expected to stop logging, hunting and making gardens in the protected area.
In return, to offset this cost, the project offers some form of ‘development’. If local communities don’t like
the offer, or find a better offer elsewhere, there is no deal and the project objective of conserving biodiversity
is not achieved. I stated that the relationship of ICAD projects to local communities is therefore not
necessarily different from that of logging or mining companies. All of these organisations aim to use other
people’s resources and enter into negotiations with local communities to achieve their aims (Van-Helden
1997).

ICAD projects as partner

In the Bismarck-Ramu area we are actually trying to get out of such a negotiating position and away from the
idea that there is a ‘conservation for development’ transaction. Mike Parsons suggests that the Bismarck-
Ramu project has moved towards an ‘Industrial Relations Model’ where the project assists people in their
attempts to harness conservation and development, much like labour unions assist employees in the
negotiations with their employers. Instead of the project being the provider of services, development and
conservation, and the opponent at the negotiating table, the project attempts to support and assist communities
in coming to grips with the economic and environmental changes around them. By adopting a participatory
mode through its intensive Community Entry Approach the Bismarck-Ramu project is trying to interest
people and communities in looking at natural resource and development issues in a broader way. At the same
time the project is forced to take a serious look at what local people regard as their dominant needs and wants.

This view of the ICAD process throws up questions relating to the fundamental tension between the project’s
conservation agenda and its emphasis on the participation of communities. Immediately obvious is the
discrepancy between the objectives of the project and its funding agencies on one hand and the need to put
local people’s needs first on the other. The Global Environment Facility (GEF) clearly stipulates that its
funds are to be used for biodiversity conservation, but people in the Bismarck-Ramu area talk about health
and education, about roads and bridges, about money in their pockets, not about conservation. In taking a
participatory pitch the project runs the risk of being pushed further and further away from the mandate given

1 Associate researcher at the PNG National Research Institute, and working on the Social Feasibility Study of the
DEC/UNDP Bismarck-Ramu ICAD project. The author would like to thank Mr. Philip Holzknecht and Dr. Colin Filer, as
well as the Community Development Workers of Bismarck-Ramu project for their comments on earlier drafts and
presentations of this paper. All views expressed, however, remain solely those of the author.
