:

by its funding agencies.”

This tension between the objectives of the project and the needs and wants of local communities can be
brought back to the fundamental issues of:

e How the project and local people each perceive the notion of ‘conservation’ and the assumptions that
underpin these perceptions and,

e How the personnel of the Biodiversity Program on one hand and people in the Bismarck-Ramu
communities on the other set their priorities in terms of the changes they would like to see take place.

In this paper I want to look more closely at the conservation concept as it is used by international
organisations on one hand and the rural communities of the Bismarck-Ramu on the other. In order to do this
I will first shortly mention the assumptions underpinning biodiversity conservation in the ‘western’ sense*
followed by a discussion of some traditional notions of the relationship between man and nature in the
Bismarck-Ramu and the possible motives of communities for conservation. I will end with a discussion of
future options for the project.

BIODIVERSITY CONSERVATION VERSUS TRADITIONAL VIEWS ON NATURE

The stated goal of the Bismarck-Ramu ICAD project is to achieve biodiversity conservation in PNG. In
practice the implementation of this objective is thought to take the form of a large protected area cutting
across a number of different ecological and vegetation systems with a minimum size of 80,000 to 100,000
hectares (Beehler 1993; 10 & 77). This area is to be kept free from human intervention through logging,
gardening or hunting in order to maintain the integrity of its ecological systems. This is a restrictive form of
conservation, which allows for very limited use of natural resources in the protected area only as little is
known about the impact of one element of the eco-system on other elements. This western view of
biodiversity conservation presupposes firstly, that nature is vulnerable and that man has to take responsibility
for its well-being, and secondly, that natural resources with a very limited scope for direct human usage still
fulfil an important role and therefore still have value. This view of conservation, however, is fundamentally
different from the way in which many traditional PNG societies look at nature and the relationship between
man and nature.

Much like the western world until only recently, rural people in the Bismarck-Ramu area and other parts of
PNG have not had to seriously look at the long-term implications of their economic behaviour. The generally
small populations with their low-impact technologies either did not profoundly affect the ecosystem on which
they were dependent or were able to adapt due to the relatively slow rate of environmental transformation.
People in the Bismarck-Ramu, like elsewhere in PNG and in many other parts of the world, generally have a
utilitarian view towards their environment. They are more concerned with the immediate yields of their
gardening and hunting and increasingly with their ability to earn cash than with questions of future
sustainability. In addition they are predominantly concerned with the welfare of the small group of clan

2 This can lead to outright conflicts of interest between the cause of biodiversity conservation and the needs of local
people. Whereas population growth in a slash and burn agricultural system is probably the most immediate threat to
biodiversity in the Bismarck-Ramu area, local communities regard improved medical services and a larger clan
population as their primary concern. The present high infant mortality actually limits the pressure on natural resources
and a project component aimed at improving community health and reducing infant and maternal mortality would
therefore directly undermine the main goal of the project. What comes first: biodiversity or people’s health? If we say the
latter, would the GEF still agree to fund it? And, if the project is a partner to local people in their endeavours to manage
resources, should we then also help them in their negotiations with a logging company? If so, would this approach fit in
the mandate of the Biodiversity Program?
3 When I speak about a ‘western sense’ I denote the way in which the large international organisations regard
conservation and development. These views are mainly driven by western governments, NGO’s and pressure groups and
to a much lesser extent by people and organisations from developing countries. I do not imply that people from western
countries think in one way and non-western people in another. The notions discussed here run across linguistic, cultural,
rural-urban and institutional boundaries.

94

members and relatives to which they belong rather than with the well-being of elusive entities as ‘the nation’
or ‘humanity’.

This utilitarian view of nature implies that land and resources not used in some form or other by the local
community have no value to the local community. This is a fundamental problem with regard to biodiversity
conservation, as the notion of setting land aside for the simple sake of nature preservation or biodiversity
conservation is difficult to conceive for local people. Why would one give up a large part of one’s resources
if these resources were not going to be used by anybody? The suggestion that these resources have to be kept
for future use projects the benefits of conservation on some vague future generation for which people living
in the present do not feel directly responsible. Obviously even the future use of these resources has to be very
limited if one wishes to preserve the resource into perpetuity. Conservationists in PNG rarely comment upon
the implicit conflict in the idea of ‘conservation for later use’.

Traditional conservation practices?

Although certain actions of traditional communities in PNG have an environmentally beneficial impact these
are usually not the result of a conscious effort to preserve resources for future use.” They are more the
unintended consequence of labour and risk minimisation strategies (Bulmer 1982). An example of the
environmentally beneficial consequence of labour minimising behaviour arises when people prefer to cut
secondary regrowth for the making of gardens rather than primary forest, the latter being much harder to cut.
But the resulting reduction of human pressure on the ecosystem is not what drives the decision to do so and
should not be regarded as a proof for the existence of a conservation ethic in the western sense (Allen 1988).
By the same token the introduction of a labour-saving chainsaw credit and maintenance program would be a
much-appreciated development in the Bismarck-Ramu area.

Traditional religious restrictions on resource use may also have had a beneficial impact on the environment.
Many rural societies had religious restrictions with regard to the type of game that could be taken, the time
when certain game was allowed to be hunted, and the place where it could be hunted and gathered. Some
rituals are thought to have regulated the periodic staging of pig festivals to reduce the pressure on scarce
resources (Rappaport 1968). Important to emphasise however, is that these magico-religious restrictions were
not aimed at specifically preserving the forest and its resources, but were aimed at safeguarding the well
being of individuals and groups through spiritual means. The environmentally beneficial impact of these
systems was a side effect, not the aim of the exercise.

A similar argument can be made with regard to ownership and use rights. Restrictions on communal and
individual access to resources are common throughout PNG and the Bismarck-Ramu area alike and extend to
garden and hunting land, pandanus orchards, fruit and nut trees, display trees for birds of paradise, fishing
locations, crocodile swamps and sago stands. These measures are effectively aimed at guaranteeing a
continued access to natural resources for a specific individual or group but have again little to do with a
traditional conservation ethic.

The fact that these traditional practices are not defined as conservation practices does not mean we should
discard them as irrelevant. On the contrary these practices may provide the point of entry for discussion of
conservation from a ‘western’ point of view. In Collingwood Bay for example, the initial reasons for refusing
the logging companies was the fear of the destruction of tambu places. Once that decision had been made the
traditional idea of looking after these places could be reinforced and adapted to include notions of
environmental conservation.

4 In this respect PNG peoples are not different fromi other peoples across the world. Conservation awareness in western
countries is a phenomenon of the last few decades after centuries of rampant environmental destruction. It is also one that
is still mainly restricted to the higher educated middle classes which can afford to talk about.

5 There are exceptions with regard to the preservation of certain specific species like freshwater fish and prawns (Orsak
1997), the case of bats in Crater mountain which were only hunted every couple of years or the singling out of male game
in order not to affect future population growth (Personal Communication John Ericho).

95

OS OO ee en On

Can man look after nature?
Apart from the utilitarian view of natural resources referred to above and the fact that the conscious

management of the environment was very rarely an issue in traditional societies it does not come naturally to
local communities to take responsibility for the well-being of nature. In the Bismarck-Ramu like elsewhere in
PNG, people traditionally regard the forest and its game as belonging to the forest spirits, the masalai. The
masalai are feared, and capricious spirits which may cause harm to man and which either have to be appeased
or tricked into surrendering the game that lives in their forest. The Jimi for example, traditionally feared the
spirits of the forest in the Bismarck fall as well as the ‘wild men’ that were thought to live in the Ramu valley.
The masalai were thought to inhabit certain remote areas in the forest, known as ples nogut in Tok Pisin,
which are often characterised by large standing stones and were not accessible to man.

The spirits may abduct humans and pigs, but can also be kept away by lighting a fire under certain trees and
other ritual. Especially the Kip-Kange or Kondol-Kange, a malevolent spirit, was known to abduct and kill
people. The Kip-Kange roamed the Bismarck fall where it approaches people in the shape of a man and
entices them to come along. People go ‘spak’, drunk, or ‘/ong-long’, crazy, and when they wake up find
themselves in a place where they did not intend to go. When the Kip-Kange kills it does so by swelling the
limbs of its victim until they burst. The victim will see the blood run and will die, but will not feel any pain.®

Many communities refer to the wildlife of the forest as ‘the pigs of the masalai’, as opposed to the ‘pigs of
man’.’ Traditionally people had to take care not to be too successful in their hunting lest they would anger
the masalai, which could lead the hunter and: his household to misfortune, disease and possibly death.
Misfortune could be averted by killing a pig or chicken for the spirits after a successful hunt. This may have
served as an internal restriction on hunting. The definition of ‘too successful’, however, was not defined by
the community or some religious authority, but depended more on the future fortune or misfortune of the
hunter. If something went wrong in his household, it could always - in hindsight - be attributed to a breach of
rules vis-a-vis the spirits of the forest, or in other cases, the ancestors. Often the glasman, the local magic
man, would be involved in determining the nature of the breach and the procedures, including the possible
killing of pigs and chicken in the sacred grove of the sub-clan, to redress the situation.

In a similar vein custom on the Ramu River dictates that the hunters cut the belly of their game and leave the
intestines and blood behind to thank the spirits of the forest fot the game that they have been allowed to take.
When a wild pig is killed, the ears and tail are left behind for the spirits. When the game has been taken back
to the village and cooked in the stone oven it is divided between the different families and the visitors. In
some cases the hunter may then beat the kundu drum and sing songs to thank the forest for the meat. In
Yawetama village on the Ramu River, it was seen how after a successful hunt two extra plates of food were
placed on a shelf near the fireplace. One was for the ‘people of the forest’, the masalai, the other for ‘the
people of the village’, the ancestors (Personal communication Boehner Pangasa and James Magado).

These practices seem to suggest a dichotomy between the ‘social world’ of the village with its people and
their ancestors and the ‘natural world’ with its spirits. This dichotomy implies a worldview in which the
spirits of the forest own and look after the forest and its game, while the ancestors guard and take care of the
village. The forest is regarded as something alien, something which needs to be kept at a distance by clearing
space for houses and gardens. Only when something is taken, or hunted in the forest do the masalai need to
be propitiated to surrender the game or resources needed by man. Under all circumstances, however, man is
an intruder and a coloniser who has to watch his step and who has to work hard to carve his place out of the
endless surrounding forest.* It is man that is vulnerable, not nature.

6 Healy records the similar spirit names ‘kondle tanji’ for Narak and ‘kibi tanje’ for the Kandawo and records that men
die suddenly after having their internal organs eaten away by this spirit (1973:106-107). See also Jenny Hughes on spirit

beliefs in Chimbu (1988).
7 This is a common notion in the Highlands, which is also mentioned in the descriptions of Maring hunting by Healy

(1973, 1977), Clarke for the Bomagai-Angoiang in the Simbai valley (1971), Rappaport for the Tsembaga Maring (1968),

Bulmer on the Kalam of the Kaironk valley (1982) and Dwyer on the Etoro (1982).
8 People in the Bismarck-Ramu speak with respect of the strength and endeavour of those men that venture further into

the bush and cut new gardens in primary growth.

96

Bulmer suggests that the idea of conservation is fundamentally contradictory to this traditional worldview,
‘almost an impertinence’, as not man or his ancestors, but the spirits are in control of the bush. If the masalai
are the owners of the forest and its game, than a declining yield from hunting does not necessarily lead to the
conclusion that the game is diminishing due to over hunting. It may simply mean that the spirits are no
longer being propitiated properly by the younger generations. Traditional beliefs may thus on one hand have
reduced the pressure of man on his environment, but may also lead to a situation where the causal
relationships between human action and environmental degradation are obfuscated by competing
explanations of sorcery and the wrath of ancestors or masalai (Bulmer 1982; 69)?

A large number of these traditional beliefs are now disappearing under the impact of education, the churches
and people’s experiences in other parts of PNG where they witness the consequences of plantations, mines
and forestry concessions. The disappearance of these traditional beliefs has both positive and negative
consequences for the environment. On one hand it removes the previously existing restraints on hunting,
gardening and the cutting of trees, on the other it opens the doors to scientific explanations of ecological
interdependency and environmental decline with remedies focusing on human behaviour and responsibilities.

In the light of the above discussion it is probably fair to say that, although there are possibly a number of
exceptions with regard to specific scarce resources, there is not much evidence in the Bismarck-Ramu of a
traditional conservation ethic which prescribes the systematic management and preservation of environmental
resources, There never was much need to do so due to the relative abundance of the environment and the fact
that the notion of ‘looking after’ the environment does not come easy. Setting land aside for the sake of
biodiversity preservation is probably slightly ridiculous in the eyes of utility-oriented local people as it is not
clear who is to use the local resource and what for; future generations, ‘humanity-as-a-whole’ and the
‘intrinsic’ or ‘unknown’ value of nature not being easy notions to identify oneself with."°

FUTURE WORK IN THE BISMARCK-RAMU

The conclusion that biodiversity conservation ideals per se are not likely to exist in the Bismarck-Ramu
leaves three possible responses open to the project:

Firstly, the project could simply conclude that because people in the Bismarck-Ramu have no direct interest
in biodiversity conservation there is therefore no real opportunity for conservation in that area. Given the
extraordinary power that landowners have in PNG, especially the future sustainability of a conservation area
established for the ‘wrong reasons’ would be doubtful. This conclusion is potentially a valid one, which
would not only apply to the Bismarck-Ramu but possibly to all of PNG with the exception of those land
groups that opt for conservation on their own account. This conclusion seems supported by a number of
experiences in other ICAD projects, and implies that PNG should no longer be a target for uninvited
biodiversity conservation efforts.

9 It is sometimes suggested that environmental awareness and a conservation ethic should be built on these types of
traditional beliefs. These types of beliefs could play a role in the case of protected areas aimed at certain culturally
important animals like the cassowary in the Kaironk area where the Kalam people regard this bird as classificatory kin, or
in the case of totemic ancestors like certain turtle in Gulf province. In most cases, however, these beliefs are based on
complex mystical explanations which are increasingly alien to the younger, more educated and travelled generations,
incompatible to Christian doctrine and often in direct competition with the modern creed of market participation,
economic efficiency and income maximisation. Even if attempted, it would as Bulmer states: “require a culturally very
well-informed extension officer to meaningfully relate selected factors of traditional explanation to modern
environmental resource management” (1982; 69). Especially linking the abstract idea of “biodiversity”, a blanket term for
all living plants and creatures in the environment, to traditional stories may prove very hard. Biodiversity education may
actually be better approached with notions emphasising man’s custodianship over God’s creation and his daily
dependency on resources and services derived from nature.

10 In addition not using land and resources weakens one’s ownership to the land. In many cases the use of land and the
bestowal of use rights on other parties reaffirms the ownership of its owners over the land (Giddings 1984). On the Ramu
River this means that clangroups keep relatives living in remote parts of their territory as spiman, to make sure that no
strangers settle on their land.

97

Secondly, the project can respond by stating that people in the Bismarck-Ramu do not possess the right frame
of mind and conviction for genuine biodiversity conservation and therefore need more and _ better
conservation education in order to build this conviction. Once local communities accept ‘western’ notions of
biodiversity conservation, resource scarcity and the need to look after nature, they may be prepared to bear
the responsibilities imposed on them as some of the last custodians of world biodiversity. This view by-
passes the fact that people in the Bismarck-Ramu have real and more immediate needs than biodiversity
conservation for the sake of humanity and future generations. Expecting presently living people to forget
about their poverty, the existing malnutrition, the lack of employment opportunities and the high number of
women that die in child-birth in order to embrace an abstract western notion of biodiversity conservation is
not realistic. This option suggests that the project should focus on environmental education, until such time
that people in the Bismarck-Ramu start to see conservation as an essential part of any strategy for future
development and until they can afford to think about other, more long-term issues than immediate survival."
It is obvious that such a waiting period may lead to the depletion of natural resources in the Bismarck-Ramu

area.

The third option lies in identifying those groups of people that have an interest in conservation in order to
achieve other objectives that can be supported by the project and its resources. There where there is a
juncture between the needs and wants of local people and the conservation objectives of the project there is
room to look at the establishment of a protected area or wildlife management regime. The project team will,
however, have to accept that people may want conservation for different reasons than the team, the GEF or
the international community. If the project does accept that people have a different motivation to set aside
land for conservation purposes and to bear the costs that are associated with refraining from a number of
resource uses, there may be room for co-operation between the project and local communities.

The main question for the project then is which set of motivations and expectations held by local
communities is acceptable to the project and allow for the establishment of a conservation regime as a by-
product of the fulfilment of existing needs. A few examples of possible reasons why people in Bismarck-
Ramu could be interested in conservation:

Material development as motivation for conservation

The main pre-occupation of people in the Bismarck-Ramu area lies with the improvement of their material
well being and their access to basic health and education services. The most common way of achieving a
better lifestyle is thought to be through access to income generating activities and better services. In most
cases this is primarily expressed as a demand for infrastructure that allows people to grow and market coffee
and earn their own money and facilitates access to government services. It may also allow for companies to
develop the local natural resources. The main drive for people to participate in the Bismarck-Ramu project
thus stems from the hope that the project can assist in achieving some of these communal and individual

‘development’ aims.

In the Jimi valley expressions as: ‘Mipela nidim wanpela kampani bilong enviromen’ show how material well
being through a ‘kampani’ or ‘bisnis’ and conservation are conceptually linked in one. This strategic use of
language is not different from attempts by the Bismarck-Ramu project to package conservation in such a way
that it becomes palatable to local people: ‘Conservation or Economic Development? No! Conservation for
Economic Development!’ (Orsak 1997). Another example is the preoccupation with conservation as
professed in the unsuccessful political campaign of John Opo. His interest in conservation was one that was
mainly driven by the promise of an improvement of the local living standards and incomes through
conservation. Opo obviously did not promise to look after birds, he endeavoured to look after people and

himself.

A project approach that couples material benefits to conservation is not different from the one tried in Lak. In
Lak, however, the expectations of the local communities were so narrowly defined, and the time preference

11 Jt is interesting to note that two well-known conservation initiatives in PNG, namely of the people of Collingwood
Bay and that of the Didipa clan in Madang were initiated by people who had seen the consequences of development on
plantations and around the mines in other parts of PNG. Both relatively well educated groups initially rejected logging
proposals on their own accord without the support of, or alternatives offered by outside agencies.

98
for material benefits had become so high, that the project failed to offer a sufficiently large and readily
available package of incentives to dislodge the already established timber company. The community based
eco-timber projects started by the Pacific Heritage Foundation, Village Development Trust, the European
Union and the German GTZ also play on people’s material needs and aspirations by providing people with
immediate income earning activities, thereby aiming to reduce the incentive to invite a logging company in.

Another possibility along these lines is to simply pay people to conserve a part of their forest. These so-
called ‘conservation easements’ are increasingly common in many developed countries and in the case of
simply setting land aside have the great advantage of being relatively easy to administer and control. The
adoption of such a conservation mechanism would form an interesting dilemma for the Bismarck-Ramu
ICAD project which emphasises self-reliance rather than dependency in its development activities. It would
run the risk of turning the project into a ‘deal’ as discussed in the opening pages of this paper.

Territoriality as motivation for conservation

Although being an alien notion in traditional PNG culture where the masalai look after the bush, the western
concept of ‘environmental conservation’ is readily accepted when translated into pidgin during community
development work in the Bismarck-Ramu. This somewhat unexpected result is a product of the translation of
the word ‘conservation’ into ‘/ukautim bus na graun na wara’ in Tok Pisin. Looking after bush, land and
water, is something that many landowners regard themselves as already doing. The difference with the
western idea of environmental stewardship is that local people seem to interpret it mainly in a territorial sense
as maintaining their traditional claims to the land and its resources (see footnote 10). Land and resources are
passed on by the ancestors and have to be defended against other humans and in turn passed on the next
generations. It does not necessarily mean you can not cut the trees that stand upon the land or kill the last
cassowary as well.

In this context ‘conservation’ is much more related to preserving a spot to which one and one’s people
belong, through which one identifies one-self, in which one buries ones dead and in which one finds security.
It does not necessarily have to say anything about the extent to which that spot is endowed with natural
resources, let alone about its level of biodiversity. For example, the traditional landowners in the Ramu
valley, do not seem to mind so much that the Goura pigeon is becoming locally extinct, they mind that their
Goura are taken by the Jimi settlers. It is a matter of territoriality and ownership, not conservation. They
themselves, for example, rapidly depleted the crocodile over the last fifteen years for the sale of hides. The
virtual disappearance of this animal is not usually a point of concern other than that people express a mild
regret at the foreclosure of this source of cash.

Communities motivated by territorial aspects, like the Ramu Valley people, who are worried by the arrival of
large numbers of Jimi settlers, may wish to use the idea of a National Park as a buffer between themselves
and the Jimi settlers. This is a tricky situation, as the delineation of a Protected Area would not necessarily
reduce hunting and gardening pressure and as the Government’s ability to police such areas is very limited.
The gradual establishment of boundaries and ownership, however, could be a first step towards more
intensive management in the future, but could also generate a raft of new problems associated with land
group incorporation and the definition of customary land boundaries.

The preservation of a lifestyle and identity as motivation for conservation

Although communities under pressure from outside migrants may reduce the idea of conservation to a
territorial aspect by wishing to keep the available resources for themselves, some may also widen the concept
of conservation to include notions of group identity, culture, language and history. These are not usually
included in western notions of conservation. Interesting in this respect is the story of the Didipa clan which
conserved an area of land close to Madang since 1964 and when discussing options for ‘development’
decided on the establishment of a museum in which traditional stories, knowledge and technology could be
stored. A similar response is visible amongst the Sepu clans on the Ramu River. They describe themselves
as a tree surrounded by the ‘bushfire’ of development with the logging and oil companies in the south and
east and the Ramu nickel-cobalt mine and an increasing number of highlands settlers to the north.

The Sepu people are simply afraid of the rapid changes that are taking place around them and do not wish to

99

engage a logging company. They present their interest in the old idea of a national park on the Ramu River in
terms of ‘showing’ their children how they lived, what they ate, where they came from, what their roots were.
For them ‘conservation’ acquires the notion of preserving their identity and their style of life, a notion which
is much wider than the biodiversity conservation aimed at by the Bismarck-Ramu project but not
incompatible with it.’

CONCLUSIONS

From the above it is clear that the notions of man’s relationship with nature as used by the international and
PNG environmental constituency are not the same as those used by the people who own the biodiversity.

In ‘western’ eyes nature is vulnerable and its maintenance the responsibility of mankind. In many PNG
communities nature is seen as powerful and all-surrounding, and as lying outside the social world and the
responsibilities of man. Conservation follows logically from the first view, it is incompatible with the second.
The value of nature for local communities lies with the extent to which local people can make use of
resources to meet immediate needs. Biodiversity conservationists claim that its value lies in its benefits to
mankind and the biosphere as a whole and in potential, but presently unknown, future uses. The first view
implies a need to use resources to access their inherent value for the local group, the second the need to leave
them alone for the well being of mankind and future generations. ‘Western’ ideas of biodiversity
conservation are restrictive and traditionally lie close to the idea of preservation of resources by restricting
their use to non-destructive uses only. Local notions of conservation may be much wider, emphasising a
continued use of resources and may imply cultural revival, a passing on of traditions and language and,
possibly, the preservation of part of the bush to show future generations what life was like in the recent past.

With regard to future work in the Bismarck-Ramu ICAD project, one of the main things that the ICAD
project team will therefore have to take into account is that professed conservation ideals will always be
linked to other collective and individual aspirations, needs and concerns. In the minds of local people not
only material development may be linked to conservation; other considerations of a more service-oriented,
territorial, or cultural nature that may play a role as well in the embrace of ‘conservation’ as an idea. There
are, however, no groups where biodiversity conservation for the sake of the preservation of global species and
plant diversity is an ideal and objective in itself. People have a fundamentally different understanding of their
relationship with nature, have too many other pressing needs and wants and are certainly not prepared to
simply shoulder the cost for global environmental concerns.

The project will therefore have to start work by building a relationship with communities and by identifying
issues and activities that are valued by communities and that may have conservation as a side effect. Given
the high diversity of communities in PNG, their different cultures and histories it is fair to conclude that there
is no blueprint for ICAD projects. Every project will have to establish a link with communities on the basis
of people’s own priorities and perceptions. The more overlapping ways one can find to make conservation
benefit local people the more chance there is to achieve conservation in rural PNG. The time and credibility
bought through such an arrangement can be used for conservation education. It will, however, always remain
the case that the project enters into a difficult and fluid situation characteristic of many landowner agreements
in PNG. Needs, wants and values change, and any agreement remains open to competition from both insiders
and outsiders pursuing other resource uses.

COMMUNITY PARTICIPATION ISSUES IN THE
CONSERVATION OF THE MARINE ENVIRONMENT.

William Asigau
Conservation Melanesia
P.O. Box 344, University, NCD.

INTRODUCTION

PNG is an island country, surrounded by water; the biogeography is influenced by the oceanic location of
the island of New Guinea. The ideal location of the island of New Guinea places PNG in a rich and
biodiverse environment. The few studies done in the marine environment have revealed PNG to be very
rich in biological life. Terrestrial studies have revealed that the island of New Guinea is a ‘centre of
evolution’, which means that there is much speciation occurring in the biotic environment. While the fauna
and flora of PNG is limited in the higher taxon it is very rich in genera and species diversity. PNG is
therefore an important site for the conservation of fauna and flora.

Most conservation programmes in PNG exist on land, while only a few exist in the marine environment.
The few conservation areas that have marine components are not properly managed and suffer from lack of
adequate funding.

The need for conservation in the marine environment is pressing to save the rich natural habitat itself, and
also because in certain seasons the local people are very dependent on the environment as a food source.

It is important for communities to participate in marine conservation programmes because:

e they own the resources
e they use the resources, and
e they are part and parcel of the system that is needed for conservation.

The ignorance of landowner concerns by developers has resulted in project stoppages and civil unrest. It is
therefore important to involve landowners in the development of conservation projects. This paper aims to
discuss some community participation issues that may be important in the development of marine
conservation programmes.

ISSUES IN MARINE CONSERVATION IN PNG

Marine Tenure and Land Ownership

To be able to develop marine conservation projects we need to have an understanding of marine tenure.
Customary marine tenure in PNG is yet to be studied, but from unpublished literature, it is obvious that
marine tenure varies from province to province. In Manus province, for example, studies have shown that
ownership can be isolated to coral heads, while preliminary studies in Madang lagoon have indicated
ownership and usage of certain parts of the lagoon and reefs is seasonal. This meant that one group can use
certain parts of the lagoon and reefs in the first part of the year and another claims jurisdiction in the second
part of the year. Demarcations by village ownership are common in the Central province. Common
(village) boundaries are more usual.

An understanding of the marine tenure patterns will help in the conservation of marine habitat and marine
organisms.

102
Incentive Packages

My experience working with local communities has revealed that people are more attentive if there are
incentive packages. For example, a wage is offered for a villager to have an active role in the project. In
most cases the incentives are for individual gain rather than for communal gain. Participation is induced by
individual or family incentive packages, which are offered to solicit support or action for common good.
The absence of incentives often lead to non or partial participation and in many instances, participation
does not last. Incentives are necessary but their usage should be carefully screened.

Education and Awareness

Unlike the terrestrial environment most things in the sea are not easily seen, and are therefore not
appreciated, often you have to dive into the sea or someone has to bring it up to you to see and appreciate.
The assumption that if you live on the coast, you would know much about the sea is not always true. While
many people live by the sea, they do not know what is in the sea therefore education and awareness
programmes are a must for the communities to truly appreciate the sea and participate in marine
conservation programmes.

In the same way, it is often true that the destruction in the sea is not readily recognisable e.g., degradation
of the reef may not be easily seen until it is too late. A more educated and aware community will ensure
active participation of community members in marine conservation projects.

Involvement or Exploitation?

How should marine conservation be approached in this country? Is there the need to conserve? Are our
marine environments under threat? PNG is not industrialised therefore the levels of pollution isn’t that
much however there is much interest shown by people from other than within the country to conserve and
manage marine resources. Therefore the emphasis to conserve is not coming within but from outside. If
this is the case then the outsider’s agents will seek support from local communities for conservation of
marine environment. Interests other than within the local communities are going to be the driving forces.
Therefore the outside agents are going to seek support from local people. Will the processes used in
soliciting support be exploiting, hence marginalizing the community, or empowering them to make sound
decisions regarding their resources? Are they (the outside agents) going to be giving aid to get that support
or are they going to let the local people own the processes? There are already signs of community
exploitation in the guise of marine conservation. The major beneficiaries are other than the local
communities.

Intrusion by Squatters

In many parts of PNG especially areas close to the urban areas; squatters are becoming a problem due to
their indiscriminate collecting habits in the intertidal areas. The experience in the Hiri East is that people
(squatters) come in their busloads to collect in the intertidal areas and are known to have caused much
damage to the area.

The cutting of mangrove wood for firewood is also a concern. The practice is becoming common. People
other than traditional landowners have to use resources (marine) which they never used before. This is of
concern to local traditional owners. How will these people be controlled in the establishment of marine
conservation areas? Many are already claiming rights to the resources because no one is questioning their
prolonged use of these areas.

Transparent Role of Outside Agents.

A possible conflict between landowners and outside agents could arise from the non-transparent role of
outside agents helping in the development of marine conservation programmes in PNG. The intentions of
outside agents must be clearly understood by communities if meaningful participation is to prevail.
Community participation must not be advanced for gains other than that of marine conservation.

Understanding power structures within communities.

A common fault in developing projects is the assumption that all members of the community are equal. A
closer look at the community will reveal power structures within communities, which can then be exploited
for the benefit of the project. Often people within the community are engaged in the projects to ensure

103

there is community participation. However if the power structure is abused and/or selection of local project
staff is done in a manner which interferes with the power structures within the community, this could have
a negative effect on the development of the project.

Family rivalry

Often there is family rivalry, clan rivalry and village rivalry within the area of interest therefore selection of
project staff to ensure community participation must not be seen as increasing the chances or giving
favorable condition to one group of people to over come the other members of the community. When this
happens you will find rival groups working against the project. Community dynamics must be understood
so that they may be exploited to benefit the project.

Traditional Roles

The traditional roles relevant to marine conservation should be identified. It is evident within coastal
communities that certain people were seen to have authority over some fisheries resources, reefs or an
activity related to the sea. The traditional roles should be explored and utilized wherever necessary because
the recognition of traditional roles will reinstate the authority of the traditional leaders and give them status
hence getting their support and the community's support for marine conservation.

Government and Traditional Leadership.

It is common practice in Central province to ignore traditional leaders and to work with the government
established local government councilors. However there are conflicts with the modern practice therefore
these should be resolved before the communities are engaged in the project.

Use of Interest Groups / Core Group.
It is our experience in East Hiri that not every member of the community is going to be interested in marine
conservation. In fact most people would appreciate the concepts but would expect others to take the
leading role in implementing the ideas.

Many village leaders/elders are also preoccupied with other village matters, e.g.: church duties, village
court duties, etc therefore give some time but not enough to ensure the conservation ideas are developed
and implemented in the areas of interest. Therefore it is always the group of people that have an interest in
the marine environment who are willing to take the leading role. Often the lack of such a group in the
community can be an obstacle for developing marine conservation programmes. We have therefore looked
at the establishment of fishermen's associations as a means to have an interest group to take the lead role in
mobilising community to support marine conservation programmes.

Neighboring Communities

Meaningful participation is sometimes disrupted by the lack of co-operation from neighboring
communities. When a neighboring community finds itself missing out it can cause problem for its neighbor
leading to non co-operation or participation of communities.

The lack of appropriate legislation.

An obvious feature is the lack of appropriate legislation for the conservation of marine resources. While it
is apparent in the Forestry Act that community participation is ensured by law the same cannot be said for
marine conservation because of the lack of an appropriate legislation for marine conservation.

The current laws, which could be used, are weak in this area therefore lack the ability to ensure there is
participation of communities in marine conservation programmes.

CONCLUSION
I have tried to raise some issues, which may not necessarily be restricted to the conservation of marine
resources. Communities, whether living by the sea or in the highlands, must be involved and have an

active role in the management of marine conservation areas. If the call for conservation of marine
environment is from sources other than within the communities, the communities must be empowered to

104

participate meaningfully. They must not be mere fronts to attract funds for marine conservation projects
because this is marginalizing and exploiting communities rather than involving them as equal partners in
development of the marine conservation area.

The developers or the outside agents involved in marine conservation must be transparent in their actions to
develop trust within the local communities. The idea of incentives is good but it should be used as a
positive way to solicit support for marine conservation and that it doesn’t become the reason for
community: involvement. The above analysis was based on information from the following areas:
Paramana village marine conservation project, Central Province; Cartret marine rehabilitation project,
North Solomon Province; Maza Wildlife Management area, Daru, Western Province; N’Drolowa Wildlife
Management Area, Manus; Crown Island, Madang Province; Hiri East Coastal Zone Management, Central
Province, and Long Island Wildlife Management Area, Madang Province.

105

CONSERVATION AND COMMUNITY: PARTICIPATION IN CONSERVATION

Samuel Krimbu
Wau Ecology Institute
P.O.Box 77, Wau, Morobe Province.

INTRODUCTION

Conservation, like any form of development activities in the country, will always encounter the issue of
the resident or indigenous community settlement within and on the periphery of the project site. Some
of the important issues which need to be addressed, which I will be discussing include: the concept of
conservation and how the people must be aware of the constitution; the land ownership issue; the
community understanding and standard of living in terms of exposure to development; and the
community participation in other community organisations and activities currently practiced.

CONSERVATION AND ITS CONSTITUTIONAL MEANING TO LOCAL COMMUNITIES

The National Constitution of Papua New Guinea has its: fourth Directive Principal that ‘Papua New
Guinea’s natural resources and environment should be conserved and used for the collective benefit of
all and should be replenished for future generations’. The message in this paragraph is clear, but after
twenty-two years of independence I believe that most of the rural and urban population are still
ignorant of what the constitution says and the meaning behind this phrase. They still do not relate
conservation projects as being part of our country’s constitution. It is best to inform the landowners as
part of the awareness that what conservation is doing is really part of our constitution, it means taking a
copy of the constitution and having someone in the village read it to the community, and that they will
be part of this development process. As much of the awareness that has been carried out, people are
beginning to grasp the concept of conservation. Most of the literature that we, the educated, read
contains the constitutional text relating to conservation, but I believe the awareness material that we
produce does not inform the people on this. Therefore, letting them know through pidgin or simple
English of our country’s constitution will add some basis for our attempts in promoting conservation in
- the country.

LAND BOUNDARIES AND LAND OWNERSHIP

Specific customary land demarcation according to the indigenous communities as in comparison to the
administrative or modern democratic standards is a problem in terms of technical descriptions of on-
the-spot measurement for mapping identification. Most boundaries are passed on from oral discussions
and may indicate mountains and rivers etc., as physical boundaries. Therefore conservation and
development projects like any form of development in the country are usually hindered by the process
of identifying land titles within the community. Genealogy surveys can be carried out to indicate
landownership through family ties and generations however, as mentioned the demarcation lacks a
technical description and may prove a long process or lead to disputes when identifying land
boundaries, some usually ending up in court. Conflicts usually arise when dealing with boundaries as
claims do overlap and ownership within the overlapping areas need to be clearly identified. One of the
options for which this process can easily be resolved is by the meeting of clan leaders as major
boundaries are clan boundaries and the identification of families as families follow in the clan
ownership broken down to family titles within clan boundary areas. The current monetary system is, I
believe, having an influence on the clan ownership with the involvement of family landownership
overriding clan ownership over a stake of land. Therefore, in order to ensure a smooth running of the
project families now need to be identified as landowners and the dealings for the projects must ensure
that the family participation in the project site will cut down major clan disputes that are rife in the
country today. The process will then involve family-to-family understanding on landownership and
joint participation in development projects.

106

COMMUNITY EXPOSURE TO DEVELOPMENT

Papua New Guinea is a country that is developing, with various levels of development in all parts of
the country. By saying this, I am looking at the basic services provided such as infrastructure
development, commercial activities, basic humanitarian services being provided both by the
government and non-government sectors in the country. In relation to the trend of development in the
country, most of the conservation projects now taking place are in remote areas of the country, yet the
level of the people’s exposure to development varies from each other. Comparing the two project sites
we at Wau Ecology Institute have, the Kuper Range in Wau and the Shrader Range in Simbai, Madang
Province, Wau has had the exposure of development through the impact of logging and mining,
therefore the people already have the mentality of cash handouts which is hard to erase. The only
alternative is to create an atmosphere of self reliance with the people doing their own projects to earn
their own income, instead of waiting for the handout deliveries, which the mining companies are
known for in terms of occupation fees, land compensation, royalties, etc. Timber companies in the past
under the TRP had given landowners large sums of money to have the trees logged and that was it,
with no participation in development. On the other hand, the Shrader Range people in Simbai have not
been exposed to such development as in Wau and are trying to get involved in activities that will bring
them some form of income. The area is still accessed only by air, infrastructure and services provided
are still poor, and the people’s standard of living is still very low. Such measures can be summarized
by indicators such as iron roof houses, vehicles owned by landowners, business ventures, which is still
minimal. The only similarity that exists between the two project sites is that the number of educated
landowners is very low. Only one out of twenty has made it to high school and the rest, in fact most
return to their villages with no further education. Therefore, when bringing in the idea of conservation,
we have to be wary of the peoples’ understanding of development. Areas of less or low development
will differ from areas of high development and the good thing is to enquire through the established
government offices on the landowners’ participation in development and how they have fared from this
exposure so that we may not be giving them high expectations or we may be giving them less to their
expectations. Their understanding of the development activities and the conservation projects that is
being carried out will be a good step towards ensuring the success of the project.

COMMUNITY PARTICIPATION IN COMMUNITY ORGANISATIONS

In every community that we have worked with there is the problem of the distribution of incentives for
the participating community, where certain factions of both the participating and non-participating
groups in the community bring forth their disagreements on certain aspect of the services provided by
the projects. Therefore one aspect of the community that I see that can be effective is that a fair
disbursement of services and activities can be made through established organisations in the
community. Every community in the country participates in government and church organized
activities and it is helpful to know what type of organisations the community is affiliated to. This is
vital in terms of channeling information and services to various ethnic groups as well as various
communities in one ethnic group. In terms of the Local Level Governments (LLG) now being set up,
certain areas have been under the council, and other areas have been under the community government.
This is all coming together under this LLG. Therefore, each respective community where conservation
projects will be taking place will be under certain wards and it is best to put a submission on the project
activities to the council office on what the project is and what the project will be doing. The council
can be one area of providing information as well as solving disputes and it is also good to have
government recognition at the community level through participation with the LLG. This may also be
mentioned for church groups, which are more concerned about the spiritual needs of the people and can
be of assistance when bringing communities together.

107

THE WIDE BAY PROGRAMME: A VILLAGE—BASED CONSERVATION AND
DEVELOPMENT PROGRAMME

Linda Passingan
East New Britain Sosel Ekson Komiti
P.O. Box 780, Rabaul, East New Britain Province

INTRODUCTION

East New Britain Sosel Ekson Komiti (or ENBSEK) is a PNG privately funded non-government
organization dedicated to promoting sustainable development at the grassroots in PNG. EENBSEK’s
objectives are:

(1) To build, link and support a network of grassroots volunteers and development organizations;

(2) To create learning opportunities at the grassroots through programs that provide awareness, build
knowledge and transfer skill related to development;

(3) To provide technical support and advocacy services to the people served by the organization in
development-related areas of concern; and

(4) To educate and influence decision makers at a Provincial and National level on key development
issues.

Based in Rabaul, ENBSEK conducts various awareness programs regarding relevant issues in East New
Britain Province. These programs include Family Problems, Women and Violence, Environment and
Conservation Awareness, AIDS Awareness, and Drugs and Alcohol.

The forests of New Guinea constitute one of the great expanses of tropical rain forest remaining on Earth,
and are a global priority for biodiversity conservation. Approximately half of this forest (34 million
hectares) is contained within the nation of Papua New Guinea and the numerous large and small islands to
the north and east, including rich in diversity and endemic species found within a wide range of habitats
including montane cloud forests,.lowland rain forests, wetlands, mangroves, and thousands of kilometers of
the planet’s most pristine and biologically diverse coral reefs. Given its pristine environments, there is a
rare opportunity in PNG to demonstrate an environmentally sound model of economic development.

PNG’s biologically rich forests are threatened by ever-increasing commercial logging, and pressures from
population growth continue to result in even greater forest conservation to gardens in a country where most
people live a primarily subsistence lifestyle. To this day, most Papua New Guineans (85%) are rural-based.
These indigenous cultures depend on the forests for food, medicine, building and decorative materials, and
spiritual needs that the forests provide.

However, Papua New Guineans are increasingly moving into the cash economy. They now are willing to
risk losing habitats and resources that have sustained them and their ancestors for centuries in return for
cash used to pay for school fees, outboard motors, and may other material goods and services. The result
has been an increasing trend over the past decade of landowners selling their forests to overseas logging
companies.

While few would object to PNG wanting to exploit its forest products, most logging in PNG is not
sustainable from a conservation standpoint. Timber exports have dramatically increased ‘over the past four
years — by nearly 300% — and a country that was once considered one of the last major tropical forest
wilderness areas is now becoming one of the world’s threatened hotspots for biodiversity conservation.
Furthermore, little consideration has been given to protect ecologically critical tracts of PNG rain forest.
Although the PNG government has recently enacted several positive reforms in the forestry sector, these
have had minimal impact on current logging operations, and they do not adequately address the need for

108

protected forest areas. Indeed, PNG is one of the last large tropical nations without an adequate network of
protected areas.

Commercial logging is not the only threat to biodiversity conservation; there are also many documented
cases of social, cultural and economic injustices enacted on villagers by some timber and washing water
having spoiled, reefs damaged, and logging roads causing erosion, even resulting in houses collapsing
down hillsides.

Presently, landowners see logging as the only means to development — financing local infrastructure
development and providing cash for services and material goods. The government either usually cannot or
does not provide villagers with these services, which leaves the mining and logging industry as the only
hope villagers see for achieving local development.

Nearly all of PNG’s land area (97%) and much of its coastal zone come under a legally recognised system
of customary resource tenure, where Papua New Guineans exercise resource-use rights based on ancestral
claims to areas. Most of PNG’s land thus belongs to its people, and may not be sold but only leased or
otherwise used according to the desires of its landowners. This means that village conservation and
development projects must primarily target village communities if they are to be if they are to be
successful. At the same time, these projects must confront and attempt to reform government policies and
commercial operations that have negative impacts on the environmentally sound use of natural resources.

BACKGROUND TO THE WIDE BAY PROGRAMME

Forest destruction in the Islands Region of PNG (Manus, New Ireland, and New Britain Provinces) is more
severe that in any other region of the country. No other region has experienced as much logging. Virtually
all of the region’s land territory is either already logged or under an existing logging contract. From a
biodiversity perspective, the threat is especially severe because of the abundance of endemic species
harbored within this region, and the widespread disruption of life in village communities.

The goal of the program described by this document is to implement activities to cease or prevent major
logging operations within ecologically critical forest areas of the region, beginning with East New Britain,
and to replace them with alternative environmentally sound. Furthermore, the long-term goal of this
program is to promote these forest areas and adjacent habitats (such as coral reefs) as legally designated
conservation areas, providing a successful model of how to integrate conservation and village development
of PNG.

The emphasis of the approach of this conservation area program will be on landowner training and
empowerment. As the legal stewards of their natural resources, their support for the Program is essential.
Only if they feel ownership over the conservation area establishment process will the Program be
successful. Each project element of this Program is thus designed to provide maximum participation and
leadership by landowners.

East New Britain

New Britain is the second largest tropical island in the tropical South Pacific after New Guinea, with a
terrestrial area of 36,520 km’. ENB, occupying 15,500 km? of land, includes both volcanic and limestone
karst geological formations. Two large mountain ranges occupy central portions of the island—the Baining
Ranges on the Gazelle Peninsula, and the Nakanais to the west. There are extensive Eucalyptus deglupta
forests in much of the lowlands (although these have been excessively logged), Agathis forest above 900
m, and stands of large kauris (Nothofagus) are found above 1500 m. Coral reefs surround most of the
Province’s coastline, but are not well-developed in the east, near Rabaul.

Although still mostly forested, ENB has been one of the most heavily developed provinces of PNG, and is
the site of intensive agriculture and logging. The forests that have not yet been converted for these
purposes are known to be rich in plant species, though botanical studies have not yet fully documented this.

Nonetheless, botanists believe the island to be rich in diversity, and that the high altitude forests on the
Nakanai and Baining ranges are exceptionally rich and unique in their flora.

The terrestrial fauna of New Britain is numerous with many species found nowhere else in the world. Two
hundred species of birds are found on New Britain, of which 13 species and a further 19 subspecies have
distributions restricted to the island. An additional 36 species and subspecies of birds that occur on New
Britain are only found with the Bismarck archipelago. Ornithological experts on the New Guinea region
are confident that future surveys will turn up new species records for the island. Thirty-seven species of
native terrestrial mammals inhabit New Britain, four of which are endemic, including two species of giant
rat, a group of mammals only found in the Solomons and Bismarck archipelagoes. There are at least 54
native reptiles and 15 amphibians on New Britain, including 6 species of sea turtle that use much of the
islands coastal areas for nesting. 60% of New Britain’s amphibians are endemic, and future surveys will
undoubtedly add new species to the island’s herpetofauna.

Many of New Britain’s vertebrate species require primary forests for survival, including endemic species
like the New Britain buzzard, the New Britain bronze-wing and the New Britain Redheaded honeyeater.
This is also the case for many amphibians, reptiles, and mammals that would inevitably be prone to
extinction without sufficient habitat designated in forest reserves. Similarly, coastal environments that
harbor a great diversity of reef invertebrates, fishes, and dugong, are highly endangered by erosion and
salutation caused by logging. In terms of protecting the unique and varied wildlife of New Britain, there is
a strong imperative to establish large forest reserves that protect prime habitats and watersheds.

Endowed with rich volcanic soils and one of the South Pacific’s best natural harbors (Rabaul), East New
Britain is the site of some of PNG’s greatest agricultural activity. Many plantations occupy much of the
eastern Gazelle peninsula, producing coconuts, cacao, fruits, vegetables, and other plantation crops. ENB
is the country’s primary producer of copra, and it also supplies much of its cocoa and timber.

Approximately 186,000 people live in ENB, and speak 13 local languages in addition to Tok Pisin (PNG’s
lingua franca) and English. Rabaul and its nearby area have the Province’s densest human population. The
two main ethnic groups in ENB are the Bainings and the Tolais, although many immigrants from the
highlands and overseas make ENB their home. The Tolais invaded ENB from nearby New Ireland several
hundred years ago. Their occupation pushed the existing inhabitants — Baining and Sulka people — into
the mountain regions. Most of ENB’s inhabitants (70%) live in villages, and lead a largely subsistence
lifestyle. As in most of PNG, village services, (eg, health, education) are grossly inadequate, and there is a
strong local reliance on what the sea and forests can provide.

Administration of the Province is by both national and local governments. The former Provincial
Government of ENB was proud of its administrative abilities in comparison to other PNG provinces, and it
is hoped that this effective administration will continue in the newly established local government.

OBJECTIVES OF THE WIDE BAY PROJECT

The overall project goal is to help build the capacity of the local NGO, East New Britain Sosel Ekson
Komiti (ENBSEK), and targeted villagers to establish forest conservation areas while also earning money
from businesses that are environmentally sound and support rain forest preservation.

Estimated population that would be benefited by the project
Based on the most recent government census, the estimated human population within the project areas is
54,341.

Expected Effects of the Project.

The expected effects of the project are:

e Increased economic benefits from the sustainable harvest of forest projects for targeted villagers in
ENB Province;

e The creation of village-based forest conservation areas; and

110

e Improved capacity in ENBSEK and targeted village communities in sustainably managing natural
resources and in creating environmentally and economically viable businesses.

Program Sites

The conservation Program will initially target three regions of ENB: the Pondo-Inland Bainings region, the
Southeast Gazelle Peninsula, and the Cape Orford Peninsula. Each of these regions was selected because it
was identified as a national priority for biodiversity conservation by the Conservation Needs Assessment,
and because it contains the only extensive tracts of unlogged forest remaining in the Province.

The approximate area for each of the proposed conservation areas is:

Pondo-Inland Bainings 1,350 km
Southeast Gazelle 1.300 km
Cape Orford-East Nakanais 2,500 km

The degree of success of Program activities within these regions will determine the potential for program
replication in other areas of the Islands Region.

OBJECTIVE 1: To increase landowner awareness within target areas on issues in environment,
conservation and development so that they can make informed decisions about use and management of
their natural resources.

OBJECTIVE 2: Build the capacity of villagers participating in this project to identify and define
community-wide aspirations in conservation and development, and to define and implement a land-use
management plan.

OBJECTIVE 3: Identify and implement conservation-based development projects with participating
villagers, to include training in small business establishment and management.

OBJECTIVE 4: Ensure project support by key officials from local, province and national governments.

OBJECTIVE 5: Monitor plans for large-scale development proposals within and near to project areas to
ensure that these will not jeopardise the goal of the project.

OBJECTIVE 6: Design and implement a plan for biological and socio-economic monitoring and
evaluation.

OBJECTIVE 7: Promote village support and benefit from the Program through additional activities.

Village Awareness and Education

1. Volunteers recruited to assist project development from each area targeted under this proposal.

2. A training course for volunteers in issues of forest conservation, rural development and village rights.

3. A series of landowner workshops providing a forum for discussion of local and national issues in forest
conservation and village development.

4. Production of simple and cost-effective materials (photos, comic books, etc) designed to educate
landowners on conservation and development issues.

5. Completion of at least one rapid ecological survey of a target area.

Village Organization

1. Completed questionnaires from participating communities to include descriptions of issues of local
concern, land-use systems (such as gardening and hunting), population demographics, existing sources
of income, and local infrastructure (schools, clinic, etc.)

2. A series of village workshops discussing results of questionnaires and defining plans for management
of local natural resources.

111

Enterprise Development

1.

A written strategy for developing the capacity of ENBSEK and participating villagers to create and
manage small-scale businesses that promote forest conservation.

2. Implementation of this strategy

3. A survey and report on the abundance of galip nuts within targeted areas.

4. An economic analysis of the costs and benefits of different resource development options: galip nuts,
large-scale logging, and small-scale logging.

5. A marketing strategy for galip nuts, including an assessment of local and international market potential
with projected economic returns.

6. Business plans for setting-up galip nuts enterprises within targeted areas.

Other Outputs

1. Production of a provincial map indicating biodiversity priority area, logging concessions, village
locations, and other data useful for land-use planning. .

2. Meetings with key local and national government officials to explain project goal and activities.

3. A database on existing logging operations in ENB Province.

112

BEST STRATEGIES FOR COMMUNITY PARTICIPATION
IN COMMUNITY INITIATED CONSERVATION

Simon Passingan
East New Britain Sosel Ekson Komiti
P.O. Box 780, Rabaul, East New Britain Province.

INTRODUCTION

This presentation is the final and the summary of the three presentations given by the East New Britain
Sosel Ekson Komiti Inc. (ENBSEK) team. It is also the final presentation of one paper prepared by
ENBSEK but presented as three separate papers. These include the topics ‘Conservation Denotes
Development’ by Elizabeth Tongne and ‘Whose needs are we addressing when establishing
Conservation Areas?’ presented by Linda Passingan.

There are many strategies used in dissemination of information, skills and delivery of services to the
communities. Strategies are used by different people in different situations. This paper will look firstly
at how to enter the community, then look at approaches and finally look at steps and strategies used by
some experienced NGO practitioners. Focusing on community development, participatory approaches,
tools used in community development like PRA and briefly looking at attitudes/behaviour, sharing of
information and the type of methods used in community initiated conservation.

BACKGROUND

This presentation is based on real experiences with the Wide Bay Village based conservation
programme in the Pomio District of East New Britain Province. This is a joint venture programme
between Conservation International (CI) and East New Britain Sosel Ekson Komiti (Inc) and has been
in place since 1993.

Let us explore three fundamental scenarios before we look at the best strategies as used with the Wide
Bay Programme. In Linda’s paper, she has given us an account of why especially the Kair main clan of
Kampun programme site and Sulai main clan of Teimtop programme site, both sites within the Wide
Bay Programme decided to conserve their resources. The bottom line was sustainable development.
Sustainable — Development: using one’s skills and resources to improve one’s way of life and standard
of living without spoiling things for one’s children and future generations. However in the path of
sustainable development, there are problems and issues important for sustainable development of ones
resources. For example:
e Land and boundary disputes
Clan’s claims on land ownership due to maybe income-generating enterprises operating on
customary land. e.g. royalties paid re: logging operation.
e Availability of opportunity information
There is no proper community education, resulting in people taking the word development
wrongly, e.g. landowners may not really know the good and bad effects of logging
e Economic Division — suppression
Powerful individuals and clans get all the rights wrongly and suppressing the poor or the
weak.

Ladies and gentlemen, my question to this conference now is, do the landowners want conservation to
attain sustainable development when their harmonious way of living is being threatened by land
disputes, no proper community education/awareness regarding changes occurring and economic
suppression? Coupled with my first question is the big mistake that many of us make. That is, do we,
as change agents, community development workers or animators understand what the community’s
needs, or problems or wants are?

113

BEST STRATEGIES

Due to the time limit placed on my presentation, I would like to talk a little about the three important
scenarios as I work with the communities within the Wide Bay Programme. These are stages
happening inter-changingly when [ use a participatory approach with the landowners within the Wide
Bay Programme. For participation here, | encourage everybody to take part in his/her own way of
doing things and actually taking part in doing something. Whether they are members (silent/active) of
a certain group working towards decision management discussions and identifying issues, participation
may be self-mobilization or self-determination (voluntary) resulting in employment or in training
where participation may be active or passive.

How to enter the Community

In entering the community, we must remember that our beliefs and attitudes will influence the way we
can share, and that will influence the methods we use. But we must remember also that the methods
we use will influence how we can share which will in turn help to shape our behaviour and attitudes.
This is a continuing process which can best be shown by a diagram. !
