LANDOWNERSHIP AND COMMUNITY PARTICIPATION:
“THE MAISIN EXPERIENCE’

Carrol Poyap
Maisin Integrated Conservation
and Development Association (MICAD)
& Conservation Melanesia (CM )
P.O. Box 735, Boroko, NCD.

INTRODUCTION

Conservation Melanesia (CM) is assisting the Maisin community of the Collingwood Bay area in the Oro
Province, to facilitate the needs of the communities, whilst protecting biological biodiversity. It co-
ordinates technical assistance and expertise from individuals and organisations who have similar interests.
CM personnel also work in close consultation with the directors of the Maisin Integrated Conservation and
Development, (MICAD), to plan and develop the strategies with which the community wishes to achieve in
the future. ,

In this paper, I will try to discuss CM’s experiences with the Maisin community over the last two years,
what it has done to date, and how it plans to involve people in the next three years. As I have stated above,
CM’s role is really to facilitate the needs of the communities whilst protecting their biological biodiversity,
and at the same time coordinates technical assistance and expertise from individuals and organisations who
have similar interests in ICAD initiatives.

BACKGROUND

The Maisin people (speakers of the Maisin language) are spread over nine coastal villages of the
Collingwood Bay in the Oro Province of Papua New Guinea.

In late 1986 when one man from the area discovered documents relating to a multi-million kina logging
proposal by Sumitomo of Japan, he stormed into the provincial forest office and demanded that he be given
copies of all documents relating to the project. He was a principal landowner and all negotiations were
done without his or his clan's knowledge. Why did not he give his consent if his clan’s land was to be
involved? Although he objected to the project going ahead immediately, because certain important studies
had been overlooked, the provincial government and forest department turned a deaf ear. Things did not
seem too favorable for this landowner.

Something had to be done and done quickly because the provincial minister for forests and the provincial
forest officer were both Maisin men too and were land owners themselves. There was a battle between
ordinary citizen and the government, who had the power, and a multi-million kina multinational company
who had the money. There was only one option left: he had to exercise his right as a landowner to call for
further studies to be conducted to address the environmental impact of the proposal. The initial studies
failed to address demography, flora and fauna conservation, environmental impacts, and social impact.
There were no land demarcation reports to identify sacred sites and land ownership. The land owner
company too was limited by shares to only five individuals. There was something fishy about the whole
deal. When this was brought to the attention of the advocates, they threatened and abused him instead.
Little did the advocates know that this one man could put a halt to the project and cost them more than half
a million kina if they continued the way they were going.

116

After attempted intimidation and suppression of every effort he made to obtain a general understanding
from the resource owners, he had no other option left but to tell the forest department, the Oro provincial
government and the potential investor to look else where.

On the 17" March, 1987 the Post Courier carried an article titled ‘Don’t Push the Villagers, man has to
choose’, followed by ‘Leading the Wrong Way, drop this bold idea’ on the 9" of April, 1987. These two
articles stimulated one of the best debates ever both in the print and electronic media in PNG.

Also on 9" April 1987, the Post Courier published a news item, ‘Forests danger is greater than we thought,
scientists admit’, and a scientific report by Dr. Peter Kramer entitled ‘Mankind heading for Disaster’. This
report spelt out what poor rural communities stood to lose, how they failed to realise how important their
forests are and also how much the world would depend on them if they preserved their natural forests.

The Maisin community took advantage of this debate to see for themselves that they would rather protect
than to destroy their land for money which may not last forever.

As the Maisin solidarity grew from strength to strength to preserve their forest, the external threats
intensified which warranted a delegation of elders to visit the Forest Minister in Port Moresby in July 1994
to call upon the ministry to shelve any logging proposal for Collingwood Bay if Maisin land was involved.

During this time they published a statement ‘Collingwood Bay Group seeks alternative to timber tharvest’,
(Post Courier 6" April 1994) declaring their intention to protect their land in perpetuity against
unsustainable use. Having closed their doors to this monster called ‘development’, they realised the need
to be more organised. They needed assistance for an advocacy/business association to explore all other
options available in order to contain the growing pressure from within to shift from a traditional subsistence
to a cash economy. They must plan for their children’s future and establish sustainable small business
enterprises and permanently KEEP LOGGERS AND MINERS OUT.

With the technical assistance offered by Conservation Melanesia and Greenpeace Pacific, and keen elites
from their community, MICAD was established to oversee the Maisin people collective or individual
endeavours to transform their well-respected perception of ‘development’ into reality whilst maintaining
the integrity of their local environment.

THE MAISIN PERCEPTION OF DEVELOPMENT

Like anywhere else in this rapidly changing world, cash or money must be the total equivalent to the work
undertaken for development. However, the Maisin man does not see development to be quick cash in
exchange for destruction. He believes that development should mean a process allowing one to change the
standard of living from the current subsistence-dependent life, to one that will accommodate the cash
economy, adding on the supplements that one can acquire by exchanging cash for them. The Maisin as a
community wish only to be happy, have their children educated, have the best medical care, have access to
the most modern communication technology, while they retain their natural environment, preserve their
customs and culture and very importantly maintain their close links with societies that enable the Maisin to
survive against traditional enemies.

POLITICAL HIERARCHY

The Maisin society has a system of making decisions through the upper and lower chieftains (refer to
figures la and 1b below). There are two major clans, the Wo Ari Kawo and the Mera Ari Kawo, or simply
meaning the higher chieftains and the lower chieftains. They have a very strong tradition where all elders
are most respected by any Maisin individuals. With that notion, in any village meetings all chiefs and
elders are the ones who make any decision after long discussions and every one else acts upon it for the
betterment of the community.

Because of this respect for their elders, any younger Maisin person or women and children only take part in
the decision-making process at the family level. And so what the council of chiefs and elders agree upon
can be debated, but finally decisions made at this level must be carried out.

The Upper chiefs are people who settle disputes, make peace and maintain public relations both within and
outside their community while the lower chiefs are traditionally the warriors, hunters and gardeners. Many
things have changed but the decision-making process is highly respected, as this also has been recognised
as a very important aspect of maintaining their community’s unity and solidarity.

118

LAND OWNERSHIP AND TENURE

Having discussed the decision making system it will be very interesting to note that decisions regarding
land matters are dealt with in a totally different manner to that regarding communal well being.

This is when we ask: Who owns the land in the Maisin society? As in any traditional society, land is a
very important aspect of its existence and therefore it requires whole tribes or clans to participate in its
acquisition. It can be noted therefore in the Maisin society battles were the only form of acquiring land that
they own today. According to oral history those that own or hold traditional title to most land are the
warriors. However, that doesn’t mean that the peacemaker chief is landless. (Mera Ari Kawo)

Land is owned by the clan. Individuals or family units own only physical property such as gardens, fruit
trees, betel nuts, coconuts etc. Natural resources which are part of the land itself, such as water, mineral
forest and marine resources, are owned by the clan.

Any decision regarding the disposal of land and any natural resources on the land has to be made by all
elderly male members of each family of a clan. The Chief or clan elder may decide on his own as to who
should cultivate, hunt or gather on what portion of his clan’s land. However, he has no authority solely on
his own to dispose of land.

It is quite interesting that one clan may have portions and pieces of land spread out over a wide area. One
portion belonging to a clan may be in the middle of another clan’s land!

Because of these complexities relating to decisions over land use and the intermingled distribution of land,
it makes it all the easier for the Maisin clans to consult each other when one clan considers this monster,
called ‘development’, in their own perspective. The Maisin who are very much aware of the dangers
combined these two opportunities to venture into an ICAD programme.

COMMUNITY PARTICIPATION IN ICAD PROJECTS

Likewise, in conservation and sustainable development initiatives, all parties concerned are to come to an
agreement. Nonetheless, MICAD has sought advice from both clans to make decisions over utilisation of
their land and resources.

As a result of the importance of conserving their natural environment, they all decided to set aside their
land for the conservation activity and they are gradually moving forward with it. In such activities, it
involves a lot of discussions and decision-making on how to co-manage their land and resources that comes
through concessions.

Maisin is one of the most complex societies in PNG whereby their custom stands as a vital part of their
survival, so decision-making in conservation is not an easy task for MICAD and Conservation Melanesia.
(Both MICAD and CM are in the Maisin context non-members of the tribe therefore any decision to release
land to them or merely involve them in the affairs of their community must go under very critical scrutiny.)
MICAD boards of directors (BOD) are a point of contact where they represent their community members
by passing the message to them after the Board meetings. Whatever the community says is discussed in the
BOD for decision and action, planning the next move and so on.

WHAT IS HAPPENING NOW?
In recent years the Maisin have developed a number of activities to put into effect the sentiments of ‘The

Maisin Declaration’, (Post Courier 13 April 1995, p.29). Tapa cloth production and marketing has been
one of the first ventures promoted by MICAD board, with support from Greenpeace Pacific, since 1995.

119

CM had been assisting the community to review the use of their natural resources (community self-
assessments) and to draw up development plans for protecting their environment and using it for sustained
benefits. Field Teams have been formed in most villages to help develop plans and start community
projects. Two Peace Corps volunteers are now based at Uiaku to assist in business and community
development projects.

Following the ‘community self-assessment’ and successful trial business activities, there are now moves to
review the progress and develop plans for the coming year.

The MICAD board has also requested assistance in improving their planning and management skills after
conducting the community self-assessment.

The primary purpose of the self-assessment was to empower participants to identify conservation and
sustainable development opportunities in the lands owned by the Collingwood Bay Villages.

This kind of information was needed to help CM learn more about the area and focus its work objectively.
The information is also useful to MICAD officials as they deliberate on the kind of projects to initiate.

Conservation Melanesia used a participatory approach by using Participatory Rural Appraisal (PRA) tools
to collect data as an outsider to help communities identifying their own problems and solution to these
problems. The results of the self-assessment are what MICAD and CM are using to enhance the ICAD
concept in Papua New Guinea.

WHAT IS NEXT?

The Maisin continue to preserve their cultural values as they govern their 500,000 hectares of life-
supplying rainforest using traditional communal approaches. They are now looking at documenting
traditional tumbuna stories, creating tokples dictionaries and an encyclopedia for plants and animals. They
hope also to develop a formal curriculum that will incorporate traditional arts and skills with modern
academic approachs to enable the Maisin man to be accepted as a member of the wider community.

The Maisin’s effort to live like their ancestors while improving their quality of life is their greatest
challenge. It was out of their own initiative and their united efforts together with assistance from non-
government organisations they have have taken up this challenge; if they successfully avert the pressure to
clear cut their heritage, they will certainly go a very long way in their quest.

The questions that need input from ICAD practitioners:

e Do we really need to spend millions of kina on ICAD projects? (E.g. Lak Project)

e Should all ICAD Project organisations be uniform in their approaches and processes, when entering a
Community?

e Who should we involve in the ICAD initiatives in the communities? Everyone, the elders, influential
persons, Board members, etc.

120

DEVELOPING ECONOMIC INCENTIVES FOR CONSERVATION
THROUGH COMMUNITY PARTICIPATION

Gai Kula
Conservation International
P.O. Box 106, Waigani, NCD.

INTRODUCTION

For a long time we have claimed that economic incentives for conservation such as ecotourism, butterfly
farming, crocodile farming and etc ‘is a mode of ecodevelopment which represents a practical and effective
means of attaining social and economic improvement for all societies’. However, even we agree that such
claims are more often easily said than practiced. In many cases, ecodevelopment such as the above has led
to numerous problems rather than providing the substantial benefits that may have been intended. Some of
the most significant identifiable problems have been ecological damage and environmental degradation,
negative impact on local culture, and creation of local economic hardships.

BACKGROUND INFORMATION.

In recent years there has been an emphasis on planning and designing ecodevelopment projects, rather than
simply letting ecodevelopment activities happen based on market forces. There is widespread agreement
that ‘careful planning is necessary to avoid some of the negative side effects of ecodevelopment’. This
trend represents a substantial shift from promotion of a loose collection of activities to planned and
organized projects that try to manage and control how enterprises develop in a given area.

The emphasis of many of these projects has been to promote enterprise activities that will provide ‘funds
for conservation area management and generate income gains for local communities’. At the core of these
projects is the desire to maximize positive local socioeconomic and environmental impact and minimize
adverse impact. The 1980 World Conservation Strategy emphasized the importance of linking protected
area management with the economic activities of local communities (IUCN, 1980). The need to include
local people in park planning and management was adopted enthusiastically by conservationists and
protected area managers at the 1982 World Congress on National Parks (McNeely & Miller, 1984). This
Congress called for increased support for communities adjacent to parks, through measures such as
education, revenue sharing, participation in decision making, compatible development schemes near
protected areas, and, where compatible with the protected area’s objectives, access to resources. This
position was recently reiterated at the 1992 World Parks Congress in Venezuela.

A recent analysis of twenty-three projects attempting to link conservation and development found that
many of the projects had initiated nature tourism activities, but few of the benefits went to local people or
served to enhance protection of adjacent wildlands (Wells & Brandon, 1992). West and Brechin (1991), in
a review of over twenty-five cases, conclude that ‘there are only certain conditions...and planning actions
under which the positive economic development benefits will flow to local people’ and which can
‘minimize negative economic, social, and cultural impacts on resident people’. Similarly, another review
of ecotourism cases concludes that ‘a socially responsible and environmentally viable tourism cannot be
fostered without a dialogue constructed and controlled along indigenous needs and in indigenous terms’
(Johnson, 1990), West and Brechin (1991), and Wells and Brandon (1992) together contain descriptions of
over fifty programs.

Although these problems can be traced to many sources, there are several themes that emerge in the
literature which describe why ecotourism has not led to ecodevelopment. The first is an absence of
political will and commitment of governments to ‘mobilize the resources - human, financial, cultural, and

moral - to ensure the integration of ecological principles with economic development’ (Bunting et al.,
1991). Another is the fact that tourism is often promoted by large-scale interests from outside the area.
The result is that tourism is not structured to meet local needs and benefits often flow outside the area
(West & Brechin, 1991; Wells & Brandon, 1992). Finally, there is the lack of integration of local needs
and preferences into the planning process.

The Papua New Guinea Experience

Throughout this paper I have attempted to draw examples of areas that Conservation International (CI) is
working in. In PNG, CI is working closely with the Foundation for the Peoples of the South Pacific/PNG
(FSP/PNG), with the local communities in the Lakekamu Basin, one of PNG’s last intact expanses of
lowland rain forest sharing borders with Central and Morobe Provinces. CI is also working on the island
province of East New Britain, a region threatened by intensive forestry development and booming mineral
and oil exploitation with East New Britain Social Eksen Committee (ENBSEK). CI is also involved in
marine conservation program along the Aroma Coast and will be carrying out a major Marine RAP in the
Milne Bay Province.

ECONOMIC INCENTIVES FOR CONSERVATION

It is evident that there are many cases where a ‘project’ focus may not be appropriate or where providing
communities with economic benefits may not be the primary goal. For example, a tour operator may want
to work with a local community on changing or improving one aspect of a new existing tour route. Local
entrepreneurs may decide to set up viewing areas, food and craft sales, or lodges; park officials may want
to improve visitor education and lessen the impact of visitors on the environment. All of these are
appropriate activities in their own right. However, they represent discrete initiatives rather than the more
comprehensive activities targeting local people.

In PNG, developing economic incentives for conservation through community participation is still in its
infancy. This. means that we are yet to fully understand and provide examples on how social, biological
and economic conditions are all linked together to develop successful economic incentives, providing
benefits to local communities. This is a major objective and focus specifically with projects that are
designed to provide both conservation and development benefits to communities.

Discussion of Figure I below
Let me take you through a concept of developing a strategy for economic incentive that is designed to
provide both conservation and development benefits to communities through community participation.

Conservation International identifies its global biodiversity priority through the development of the Hotspot
Areas Maps where biodiversity is greatly threatened. These includes countries like Philippines, Indonesia
and Madaguscar; the Megadiversity Area Maps, which includes countries like Australia, Indonesia and
PNG; and the Wilderness Areas, where PNG is ranked high on the list. From producing these tools CI is
able to select countries where it can focus its attention.

At the national level for example the PNG Biodiversity Priority Map is used to select high biodiversity sites
where CI can work. In selecting these high biodiversity priority areas, CI is able to identify the
communities and the NGOs that it needs to work with, since the conservation purpose lies very much with
the communities concerned.

Threats to biodiversity and communities occur at two different levels. These are macro and the micro level.
Focusing especially on the micro level, which this paper focuses on, developing economic incentives
through community participation is particularly important in identifying the strengths and weaknesses of
the communities. It is also at this level that we start developing strategies for enterprises with the
communities.

Considerations for Developing Enterprise Strategies

In developing enterprise strategies ten specific issues critical to eliciting community-based participation for
conservation are identified here. These should be used as guidelines for developing strategies for economic
incentives for conservation through community participation.

e Local participation’s role; - communities decide what type of growth -communities exercising control
over their growth and development — communities involved in the identification, design,
implementation and evaluation of the development.

e Empowerment as a objective: - mobilize their own capacities, - be social actors rather than passive
subjects, - manage the resources, - make decisions, - control the activities that affect their lives.

e Local participation in the project cycle; - involve them in information gathering, - identify local
leaders, - what organizations exist, - what the community sees as key priorities, - what ideas,
expectations and concerns people have.

e Creating stakeholders; -contribute cash or labor to community projects, - can become individual
owners or groups of owners of enterprises.

e Linking benefits to conservation; which activities are a direct link between project objectives and local
benefits? Who won’t receive benefits under the activities being planned? What kind of long-term
investment will local people make in the activities? Who will control decision-making? Are there ways

123

activities can be structured to provide multiple benefits, such as employment for some, training for
others, and revenue sharing for the community?

Distributing benefits; Is it best to have a enterprise run by the community with some revenue sharing,
or is it best for individuals in the community to establish farms or lodges and restaurants individually?
Is there a local capacity to run the enterprise efficiently? Will some people be stuck long-term in
subservient positions? Will competition among individual owners erode group decision and
management? Is there the tourist demand to justify multiple investments by many people? What
organizational form is most appropriate, collective or individual? What uses of the resources are
destructive, which are sustainable and who in the community can affect these uses? If the benefits are
widely distributed, will everyone get so little that it is not worth their involvement? Conversely, if the
benefits are narrowly distributed, will that act as an incentive for others to participate or will it exclude
many people and lead to resentment and income inequality?

Involving community leaders; Formal leaders: Who are the local teachers, healers, agricultural
extension agents? Who is traditional authority? Who do you go to if you are sick? Does anyone get
agricultural credit? Who lends money? Has anyone worked a city and returned here? Who contacts
government for the community? Where do you buy things?

Identifying leaders; Different leadership strengths are often associated with different types of people.
For example, people who are willing to promote change and take risks are often younger, better
educated, and have contacts outside the area. People who give a project credibility are older,
respected, and greater social or economic positions. People who actually implement projects tend to
be oriented towards the community and middle-aged. It is also useful to bring people into project
planning who have leadership skills, but may not be regarded as leaders.

Identifying local organizations; Are there any religious groups that hold meeting? Is there a credit
society? Are there any recreational groups, such as a group that plays soccer or cricket or a theatre
group? Are people organized in any way by local economic activities, such as fisherman, farmers,
loggers, hunters, rattan gatherers, knitters, poultry or livestock raisers? What about collecting sap,
bark, or plants for medicine? Is there a women’s group or mothers’ group? Or a local development
society?

Monitoring and evaluating progress; Developing some key objectives and indicators for the activities
initiated can allow projects to measure the impact of their social and economic development activities,
and conservation objectives, so as to provide useful input for future planning. Importance of involving
stakeholders (i.e., intended beneficiaries) in order to give them a vested interest, and presumably
greater commitment to, the achievements of project goals. It is easy to measure achievements against
these kinds of objectives, particularly over short periods of time while projects are still in process, and
before more tangible benefits become apparent. Projects with a beneficiary orientation generally set
their goals in terms of readily measurable indices, such as income levels, numbers employed, crafts
sold. Improvement in these indices will be taken as representing project success. However, while
many of these measures are important, they are not in themselves sufficient. Nature tourism projects
with a participatory orientation will be interested in achieving similar goals to the beneficiary projects;
however, they are oriented more towards establishing a process leading to change which will be
sustainable beyond the life of the project.

Examples of encouraging community participation in developing economic incentives
1. FSP/PNG.

In 1996, the Research Conservation Foundation conducted a workshop on tourism in Madang. The
workshop was attended by FSP/PNG, the Village Development Trust/PNG, the Tourism Promotion
Authority and representatives of communities. As the result of this workshop, one of the representatives
from Lakekamu ICAD was able construct a guesthouse at Kakoro. The Kakoro guesthouse was opened on
22"' May 1997. The guesthouse is going to be advertised on CI’s web site. Continued evaluation
monitoring of the initiative should be encouraged if we are to learn lessons and understand the linkages
between social, biological and economic spheres.

124

2.ENBSEK

During a recent workshop in Rabaul, conducted by ENBSEK and attended by community volunteers, F SP
and Cl, the participants were able to develop a questionnaire to identify possible enterprises which could be
acceptable to the community (see Fig. 2 below). ESBSK believes that identifying existing enterprises is
essential and the community should select and plan on what activity is appropriate for them.

CONCLUSION

Why involve local people in ecotourism? There are lots of reasons, spanning moral, economic, and
environmental objectives. From an environmental and economic perspective, if local people are not
involved, it is likely that over time, the resources will be destroyed and the investment will be lost. From a
moral perspective, it is preferable that local people manage their own destiny rather than be buffeted by
outside forces. Perhaps one of the most significant responses to the problems of economic incentives is the
emerging participation of local communities in studying, discussing, and devising strategies to control or
capture control over the development decision-making process.

In fact, there is little experience in community based ecotourism planning and management. Although
there are relatively few examples of community managed ecotourism, one positive note is that there are
more numerous examples of communities jointly managing all kinds of things, from forests to irrigation
systems. Although ecotourism is often promoted by conservation groups, the groups with community
based experience often have their roots in community and rural development.

MAKING THE CONCEPT OF CONSERVATION RELEVANT TO LOCAL COMMUNITIES:
COMMUNITY VERSUS INDIVIDUAL INCENTIVES FOR CONSERVATION -
THE LAKEKAMU EXPERIENCES.

John Sengo
The Foundation of the Peoples of the South Pacific PNG Inc.
P.O. Box 1119, Boroko, NCD.

ABSTRACT

This paper addresses the question of how we can make the concept of conservation relevant to local
communities. Six approaches are discussed from Lakekamu Basin ICAD project experience: methods of
community entry, land-use management plan, environmental awareness and capacity building, profit
distribution, basic needs and services and active participation by local communities.

INTRODUCTION

‘Making the concept of conservation relevant to local communities’ can be rephrased in a question form as
‘how do we make the concept of conservation relevant to local communities?’ Here we go into looking at
methods that we can use to make conservation more relevant to local communities. So now one can ask
why somebody came up with the concept of Integrated Conservation and Development (ICAD). Was it not
to make conservation more relevant to local communities? I think yes, the ICAD concept should be seen as
a tool to make conservation more relevant to local communities in that it takes into consideration socio-
economic, cultural and environmental components of communities concerned.

Therefore my discussion in this paper will address Integrated Conservation and Development using the
concept of ‘Community Development’. I will briefly touch on community entry and then cover several
approaches that can be used to make conservation relevant to local communities, meaning it meets and
satisfies the basic needs and cultural aspirations of a community as well as protecting the environment.

The ICAD concept is in its experimental stage here in PNG and so has not been proven to be a way to
combat large-scale resource extraction processes such as logging and mining yet. It is probably education
and awareness that can enable conservation to combat large-scale enterprises when applied within the
ICAD concept. The following paragraphs, outline six approaches seen to be possible routes to making
conservation a relevant entity within local communities: Community Entry, Land-use Management Plan,
Environmental Awareness and Capacity Building, Profit Distribution, Basic Needs and Services and Active
Participation by Local Communities.

METHODS OF COMMUNITY ENTRY

Community entry methodologies are discussed in detail in Nadine Grant’s 1996 publication Community
Entry for ICAD Projects — the Participatory Way. | agree fully that this is the best way to approach a
community in order to make it a bottom-up approach in implementing ICAD Project goals and objectives.
Here I want to emphasize the need to use Papua New Guineans in initial community entry activities. I
think initial community entry should be done by Papua New Guineans (if within PNG) and preferably (or
where possible) by students or government officers like schoolteachers, community health workers,
community development workers or kiaps etc. The rationale behind this is that people already know that
the government worker works for a wage to serve them (the people) and so whatever they do will be taken
as part of this officer’s work for wages. One can argue that most of our local people have no faith in
government workers because of lack of basic services and what is termed ‘development’. My suggestion
here however, can be restricted only to data/information collection. This I believe could help to reduce

127

false expectations. We have been fighting false expectations created just from seeing a ‘white-skin.’ This
is not to say that this is bad, I am saying people tend to associate a white skin with ‘a lot of cash/money’ or
cargo. If we can start with fewer false expectations and still know more about communities we’re dealing
with, project progress may be a bit speedier.

In a way, the participatory approach (Grant, 1996) will enable a bottom-up approach once communities
participate more actively in planing and executing projects. Such an approach for entry into communities
to begin conservation and development work may contribute a great deal to making the concept of
conservation to filter into the community.

LAND-USE MANAGEMENT PLAN

Within an ICAD area, having a land-use plan is an important approach. This involves identifying and
mapping out land-use activities in the area. It can be done through various PRA techniques that have been
discussed in many papers and publications. In the Lakekamu Basin discussions are under way to establish
a ‘land-use plan’. Many community members expressed such concerns as, “what about our garden areas,
fishing grounds, hunting etc?’ Others say, ‘we would like to protect that area because it is one of our
sacred sites’. Others made their own rules as to how many fish one should catch and limiting fishing or
hunting to using a certain type of method and/or weapon(s). These things have brought to mind a
realization that the idea of land-use planning had long been part of our own cultures and daily activities.

During several village meetings I brought forward the idea of land-use planning and this was unanimously
accepted. We now have an ICAD committee that will take the lead in developing this land-use plan. The
committee is made up of the four representatives of each of the four ethnic groups and women from the two
women’s groups in Tekadu and Kakoro.

The rationale behind having this committee is that it will enable the local people to take an active part in
decision making and project planning. People will accept what is theirs especially that which they
themselves plan and develop.

The Lakekamu Basin as we see it will only be able to achieve conservation using the ICAD process if we
can also incorporate people’s other day-to-day use of the forest and rivers.

ENVIRONMENTAL AWARENESS AND CAPACITY BUILDING

While we embark on the mission to achieve conservation through small scale economic development,
making the community aware of reasons for such an approach is vital for long term consequences. People
have to be made aware of how these small-scale enterprises link up to conserving the natural resources.
The Lakekamu Basin, we feel will succeed if environmental awareness continues. Having field-based
officers is a plus and this alone has had a very significant impact.

Capacity Building is one other important step towards making conservation a relevant entity in the local
people’s lives. People need to be well aware of conservation issues and should be able to manage small
enterprise setups. Further, the aspect of informed decision making relies on this aspect. If people are
capable of sustaining small-scale development projects after the project’s life, conservation then becomes a
relevant inclusion into the communities, as it serves to bring in cash and other benefits that meet their
requirements.

PROFIT DISTRIBUTION
Conservation’s greatest threat is probably the ‘search for cash’. Several things put pressure on this need:

basic needs of education and health wish to improve living standards, need for store bought goods, food,
ceremonies and others (see Figure 1: Project Conceptual Model). Most of these things can be obtained if

128

there is a means to enable inflow of cash. However, the Lakekamu Basin has very few economic
opportunities. Existing economic opportunities are:

Gold Panning (Biaru, Kamea, Kurija), Betel nut sale (Kamea of Tekadu), Crocodile Skins (Kovio) and
various products from gardens such as peanuts. Transportation is difficult so most of the surplus garden
produce and betel nut could not be taken to the markets.

We noticed that because of very few economic opportunities people are hungry for new ideas but they want
big money if they cannot get it. This is evident in that, it is very difficult to get individuals or community to
work for collective benefits that will become tangible only after ten years down the road. Immediate
monetary benefits are what they want.

One of the many examples that had emphasized the need for proper profit distribution mechanisms due. to
our encouraging of individuals and /or small group ownership of enterprises is as told below.

The story goes like this... and there are several examples, I will talk about an example in Kakoro. We had
an opening ceremony for the guesthouse in Kakoro on the 22™4 of May 1997. People came from all over
Wau, Lae and the neighboring districts in the Morobe and Gulf Provinces. This guesthouse was built by a
clan of the Biaru group of people. On the day of the opening an issue long kept out of discussion surfaced.
The last speaker of the local community who came in from Wau talked about land compensation. This
made the Biaru people of Kakoro angry. They later demanded that within the ethnic group (Biaru
Language group) issues such as that of ‘clan ownership of land’ be addressed. Two days went by and still
there was no agreement reached. In fact the clans were divided. People present elected an elder as land
mediator who walked through the disputed area with assistants from Kakoro and Biaru people and marked
out the clan boundaries. This still did not solve the problem. There was still a lot of enmity within the
Biaru people among their clan groups. The guesthouse owning clan later told me, they want the project to
continue. Others later came to me and said all this is to make way for the project to progress smoothly.
They said they have not been cooperating much because of inter-clan disputes among themselves and that
this had to be sorted out. It was during my informal discussions that the real issue came to light: Profit
Distribution. ‘How can the whole community benefit from what is owned by one clan? The rest of us will
be spectators.’ ‘We want part of profits to be contributed to the community to cater for basic services.’
These and many other points were brought forward.

We propose to establish Incorporated Land Groups (ILGs) to enable equal profit distribution. We think if
the community can have proper organized units (groups) we call clan groups, with each member listed,
then distribution of profits will be made easier.

The point is that conservation-related enterprises can only be relevant to communities if profits from these
enterprises are distributed within the community in the form of supporting basic services or in improving
the general living standard. It is also Lakekamu Basin experience that project or enterprise ownership by
whole community will have a lot of difficulties and will not be able to get off the ground. We have
therefore encouraged either individual ownership or small clan group ownership of enterprises and with it
works on developing profit distribution mechanisms. This (equal profit distribution) however, will not
solve the land dispute within the ethnic groups and also between the four different ethnic groups. It can go
a long way in reducing enmity among ethnic groups and within ethnic groups and promote some level of
cooperation that can promote improving life-styles or living standards.

BASIC NEEDS AND SERVICES

The Lakekamu Basin Project field officers are ‘Community Development Workers.’ We feel that
conservation awareness is by now in pictorial form. This is to say that people have come to associate our
presence with conservation activities. Whatever we do in assisting the communities will be for
conservation. We help people in doing what they do so that they can conserve the Lakekamu Basin. One
could ask what if project officers leave after the project’s life? We hope that by then community elders and
young people will be aware of the need for conservation and how, as shown by these enterprise, improved
living standard and better social life is dependent on conservation.

So now we refrain from discussing conservation and focus on other community development activities.
Objective 4 is designed to address this (see Project Conceptual Model - Figure 1): We feel that we cannot
expect the local people to think about conserving the forest when it can bring them enough money to build
schools, roads, pay medical bills and other needs if they log it.

We should therefore assist to bring about these changes without a need for logging or mining. And so apart
from setting up infrastructure and training of local people to cater for Eco-tourism, much of our effort is put
into seeking assistance and organizing the community to be self-supportive. So we think that conservation
should be addressed on equal terms with basic services either simultaneously or after basic needs are
considered and addressed.

A Kurija elder once told me, ‘John yu toktok long lukautim bus graun na bai mi kisim moni we long baim
skul ; na kisim sik man/meri or pikinini igo long hausik? Yu gat tisa, dokta na marasin? Mi lukim planti
man/meri ol i katim timber na ol i gat mani na rot i go long lotu’ 1 told him I have none of those things but
I think I can find ways to bring about the means to address education and health problems and that it will
depend very much on what the community can do to support this.

We think that once the community feel or see changes in their life styles, they will be able to feel content
and consider conservation more seriously or in a different light to when they were striving only to meet
their basic needs. Thus by meeting those basic needs conservation may indirectly become a relevant entity
to the community.

ACTIVE PARTICIPATION BY LOCAL COMMUNITIES

I did mention this earlier but I’d like to discuss it separately to illustrate its importance in conservation. It
will be in the best interest of conservation if we consider active participation of local people. On several
village meetings this question came up. In Tedadu it was a two hour debate among local people to justify
the need to have one or two of them working with Resident Scientist. In Kakoro active involvement of a
local young man (Roehanny Yenip) had seen a quick progress in infrastructure development and
awareness. In Okavai involvement of one of the young men in baseline information collection had a lot of
impact in changing the opinion of big men and young spokespersons. These are some examples of good
things from community participation. By way of contrast, lack of active participation by local people
between August 1995 to August 1996 had seen very little progress in the Lakekamu project.

Thus I think, in a conservation project where the community is actively involved in its planning, and
execution, local needs, cultural and socio-economic aspects of concern from interacting communities can

be considered. In this way too, a community can accept an idea or a concept, which can then lead to
making it a relevant entity within a community. ,

CONCLUSION

In summary, conservation can be a lasting entity if it can have an impact on the way of thinking and
decision making of local people. This can be brought about if:

(a) Conservation approaches respect the existing traditional and cultural way of life of local people
(Method Entry & Land-use Management Plan).

(b) People accept what comes out of conservation related activities as their own (Active Participation by
local communities).

(c) People are educated enough to understand conservation issues within and without the country and able
to relate this to current trends in development (Environmental Awareness and Capacity Building).

131

(d) Conservation related enterprises are seen to be fair to all and meet the needs of individuals and
community as a whole and that general lifestyle improves (Profit Distribution and Basic Services).

So when the concept of conservation becomes part of people’s lives it becomes relevant to them
(Communities).

In conclusion making conservation relevant to local communities will enable a possible success of
conservation activities over large-scale resource extraction process. Each project will encounter different
cultural and socio-economic environments and so may see approaches in a different way. Generally
however, the Lakekamu experience presented here will contribute in a small way to developing a means
that will suit Papua New Guinea.

Grant, N. 1996. Community Entry for ICAD Projects - The Participatory Way. Department of

PROCESSES FOR EFFECTING COMMUNITY PARTICIPATION IN PROTECTED AREA
ESTABLISHMENT: A CASE STUDY OF THE CRATER MOUNTAIN WILDLIFE
MANAGEMENT AREA, PAPUA NEW GUINEA!

Arlyne Johnson
Scientific Program Director
Research and Conservation Foundation of Papua New Guinea
P.O. Box 2750, Boroko, NCD

INTRODUCTION

Community-based conservation which engages the participation of rural resource owners in sustainable
management of their natural resources is presently seen as one of the most promising methods for
protected area establishment. However, little systematic analysis of the methods and effectiveness of
this approach has been conducted (Brandon and Wells, 1992; Western and Wright, 1994). This paper
presents an analytical framework to assess the aspects of community participation which have been
used in the effort to establish the Crater Mountain Wildlife Management Area of Papua New Guinea.
The results of the Crater Mountain case are compared to other community-based conservation
initiatives. Lessons for conservation practitioners about the realistic use and constraints of community
participation for establishing protected areas, as experienced in Papua New Guinea, are drawn.

Definitions of participation and community-based conservation

Brown and Wyckoff-Baird (1992) describe the possible participation by communities in protected area
management as a continuum, which can range from ‘limited input in decision-making and control, to
extensive input into decision-making and ultimately stewardship of the resources.’ Over the last two
decades, the realisation that community participation was a significant variable in determining success
of rural development projects (Midgley, 1986; Oakley, 1991) has also influenced the field of natural
resource management in developing nations. Across the world, prior to 1970, protected areas were
managed by national governments who denied access to traditional resource users yet did not have the
capacity to effectively manage the natural resources or more importantly, enforce rules to conserve the
resource base (Wells and Brandon, 1992). Today, most efforts to manage protected areas by
developing nations give mention to the importance of community participation in protected area
management (Wells and Brandon, 1993) but the form and intensity of participation in each case varies
significantly (Oakley, 1991; Midgley, 1986; Wells and Brandon, 1992; Paul, 1987).

One widely-used definition which is used to describe ‘community participation’ in rural development
projects (Paul, 1987) states that it is ‘an active process by which beneficiary/client groups influence the
direction and execution of a development project with a view to enhancing their well being in terms of
income, personal growth, self reliance or other values they cherish.’ Cernea (1985 in Wells and
Brandon, 1992), describes local participation in protected area management ‘as empowering people to
mobilize their own capacities, be social actors rather than passive subjects, manage the resources, make
decisions, and control the activities that affect their lives’.

The ‘instruments’ used to promote this participation fall into two categories, agents of change and
institution building (Wells and Brandon, 1992). Agents of change are those individuals associated with
external agencies or within the communities whose presence catalyzes local involvement in the
development process (Paul, 1987). The strengthening of existing community institutions or the
development of community organisation is seen as a means which will provide for continuity of the
process that has been established (Midgley 1986; Murphy, 1994)

' A version of this paper, the basis of Ms Johnson’s presentation to the Conference, was published in
Colin Filer, ed., 1997, The Political Economy of Forest management in Papua New Guinea, National
research Institute and International Institute for Environment and Development, Port Moresby.

133

The world-wide trend from traditional state control to increased involvement of communities in the
management of conservation areas which border or are included in their lands has lead to the term,
‘community-based conservation’. Western and Wright (1994) described this is an approach that,
‘reverses top-down, center-driven conservation by focusing on the people who bear the costs of
conservation...community-based conservation includes natural resources or biodiversity protection by,
for and with the local community.’ Little (1994) adds that community-based conservation has two
outcomes. They are ‘the maintenance of habitats, the preservation of species, or the conservation of
certain critical resources and ... improvements of social and economic welfare.’ It is the additional
development outcome which distinguishes this approach from traditional protected area management.
Projects which are designed to link biodiversity conservation in protected areas with socioeconomic
development in adjacent communities are termed, ‘integrated conservation and development’ (ICDP or
ICAD) projects (Wells and Brandon, 1992; Brown and Wyckoff-Baird, 1992).

While attractive in theory, the actual mechanics of linking biodiversity conservation with rural
development as well as encouraging community participation in the process has proven to be elusive,
extremely challenging and yet to be realised in most cases which are characterised as community-based
conservation projects (Western and Wright, 1994; Wells and Brandon, 1992; Brown and Wyckoff-
Baird, 1992).

e It is not simply a matter of relying on traditional conservation beliefs of indigenous residents
within and near protected areas. Traditional practices alone are often not sufficient to sustain viable
populations of flora and fauna under the present day scenario of increased human population and
pressure on the natural resource base (Western and Wright, 1994; Brandon and Wells, 1992).

e Community participation alone cannot be idealised as a given solution to conservation challenges.
Midgley (1986) in a historical review of community participation reminds us that communities are
not homogeneous and that they, ‘suffer from conflicts, rivalries, and factionalism’. He states that ‘a
clearer understanding of these problems would allow a more realistic assessment of possibilities and
prepare workers more adequately for the problems they will face.’

e For many reasons, local participation is known to be time consuming and, in many cases, the
threats to conservation of biodiversity are often imminent and urgent (Little, 1994; Brandon and
Wells, 1992). The challenge of providing tangible community benefits which are derived from the
conservation of biodiversity often takes time. In the interim, community participation may be
limited.

Given these considerations, it cannot be assumed that most communities will sustainably manage

resources on their own (Little, 1994) nor that natural resources can be managed by the state through,

‘proclamation alone.’ (Bromley, 1994). The art of crafting the mechanisms which will provide for

biodiversity conservation and rural development through community participation is the challenge

facing today’s conservation practitioners. Bromley divided the challenge into three parts:

1. to create the means, ‘mechanisms’ for discussing, reviewing and assessing the values of
biodiversity conservation

2. to permit those values to be expressed in policies which incorporate incentives for conservation

3. to implement enforcement procedures to provide assurance that conservation actually results

‘A community-based conservation program with any hope of success will contain all three elements.’

The recognition of customary land tenure in Papua New Guinea provides an extremely unusual policy
environment where some degree of participation by rural communities is obligatory in the
establishment and management of a protected area. In a survey of 99 parks in 38 countries around the
world, only 21% were categorised with local people having legal title to all or part of the reserve (Zube
and Busch, 1990). As in all of PNG, traditional landowners retain guaranteed ownership of their land
and maintain an unprecedented level of control of the resources on their land. Successful conservation
in PNG, more than anywhere else, will rely on landowner management.

134

THE CRATER MOUNTAIN WILDLIFE MANAGEMENT AREA

Biological Significance

The Crater Mountain Wildlife Management Area encompasses approximately 2,700 square kilometers
of which 98% is covered by primary forest ranging from lowland rain forest of the Purari River (50
meters) on the Great Papuan Plain to montane cloud forest on the slopes of Crater Mountain (3100
meters) (Figure la). A diverse collection of flora and fauna indicate that the Crater biota is very
species rich and as such constitutes a natural resource of national and global importance (DEC 1993).

e

Solomon Sea

Guif of Papua

Port Moresh

Coral Sea

Figure 1a. Crater Mountain Wildlife Management Area in Eastern Highlands, Gulf and
Simbu Provinces, Papua New Guinea

National Legislation

Crater Mountain was gazetted as a national Wildlife Management Area on October 14, 1994. The
Fauna (Protection and Control) Act of 1976 provides for the establishment of Wildlife Management
Areas (WMAs) to be declared by the Department of Environment and Conservation (DEC) upon
request by the customary owners of the land. The landowners submit to the national government: a
legal description of the boundaries of the area to be gazetted as the WMA; a list of the clan leaders
who will sit on the local Wildlife Management Committees, and; the rules which the Management
Committee establishes for use of natural resources in the WMA. The boundaries, committees and laws
are reviewed by the DEC, and if not conflicting with any other national laws, are gazetted by the
parliament and recognized as the governing body and laws of the conservation area.

Despite the name, Wildlife Management Areas can be more accurately described as multiple-use areas
whose objectives are to encourage sustainable use of subsistence resources by customary landholders,
protect biodiversity, gain formal recognition of tenure and resource ownership, provide sustainable
opportunities for income generation, protect cultural values, and provide for scientific research and
educational opportunities. (Hedemark and Sekhran, 1995)

Given these objectives, PNG legislation does provide a fertile foundation for attaining the three
elements for successful community-based conservation as identified by Bromley (1994). Communities
can actually establish a protected area if they feel that the biodiversity they own has some value to
them if conserved. Through the management committees, communities can create the policies which
provide incentives to practice sustainable resource use and they can prosecute individuals who violate
the rules. Yet, given the heterogeneous nature of communities and the traditional lack of collaboration
among clans over a large geographic area in Papua New Guinea, how feasible is it that communities,

135

without further technical assistance, will be able to progress beyond establishment of small isolated
protected tracts? Will WMAs remain as ‘paper parks’, where gazettal takes place but no mechanisms
are put in place to realise the conservation goal? Despite the existence of favorable legislation, it has
been found throughout the world that most communities cannot be left alone with the expectation that
they will be able to ‘defend and conserve their resources in a sustainable fashion’ (Little 1994).

Community Profile

Crater Mountain Wildlife Management Area covers two language groups, Gimi and Pawaian. The
boundaries of the WMA overlap three political districts including Chimbu, Gulf and Eastern Highlands
Provinces. Approximately 3,000 Gimi occupy the northern half of the Wildlife Management Area and
are concentrated in an arc that runs through the villages of Herowana, Ubaigubi and Maimafu (see
Figure 1b). The only road access is to Ubaigubi on the northern boundary of the WMA with access to
the remainder of the communities by grass airstrips. The southern half of the WMA is inhabited by
approximately 600 Pawaians who have settled around an airstrip to create the village of Haia.

The Gimi villages of Crater are traditional Highland ‘Bigman’ societies. Each clan has one or more
‘Bigmen’ (chiefs) who maintain their position through their skill as politicians or fight leaders. The
Gimi are subsistence farmers who follow shifting agriculture or swidden farming patterns. The
Pawaians are semi-nomadic people whose primary subsistence is obtained through hunting and
gathering with limited short term cropping. Pawaian society is built around the immediate family.
They are true forest dwelling people, traveling around their land in small bands which usually number
less than twenty. Crater landowners are responsible for their own land, which may be days away from
the airstrip where they are settled. Use of the forest is restricted to the principal landowner and his
immediate family. Others require permission before they are allowed to cut, hunt or trespass. As with
any village violations, cases are reviewed by a village court composed of community representatives
who hear public testimony on the case, pass judgment and verify the fine.

Approximately 80% of the men and 50% of the women in the WMA speak fok pisin. Only 30% of the
WMaA residents are literate in tok pisin and less than 1% speak English (DEC, 1996). Government-
sponsored community schools have been present in Maimafu, Herowana and Haia for only the last two
to six years and may range from grades one to as high as grade six if teachers and funding are
available. There is a rudimentary health post in each village and at least one trade store which sells
basic supplies such as salt, rice, candles and canned fish. Outside of the research, ecotourism and
handicrafts enterprises, cash income is earned through sale of coffee or market hunting.

Threats to the Biodiversity of the Crater Mountain Wildlife Management Area

Land use impacts on biodiversity include sago and sweet potato garden plots and cutting for fuel wood
or local timber use. Subsistence and market hunting has already extirpated some game species in some
regions of the WMA. Cuscus and tree kangaroo populations are seriously depleted. Cassowary
populations are still strong, but the high rate of removal of cassowary chicks suggests a significant
population crash might occur in a few years with the death of the existing adult populations (Mack
pers. comm. 1995). An increasing human population in the region will continue to increase its impact
throughout the area. The delivery of community services (education and health) to rural areas in Papua
New Guinea is hampered by their remoteness and the corresponding lack of infrastructure required for
socioeconomic development. One of the few options seen for satisfying community cash needs and
infrastructure development in these areas is thought to be the selling of natural resources, minerals or
forests, to satisfy cash needs. Alternative forms of cash-generating activities will be needed to meet
cash needs.

Papua New Guinea national government policies and rules regarding resource extraction in national
protected areas could have significant impact on biodiversity in the area. Despite gazettal as a national
Wildlife Management Area, the southern lowlands of the Wildlife Management Area on the north side
of the Purari River boundary are included in the National Forest Authority designation of Turama
Extension Timber Permit TP2-12A, Forest Management Area (FMA) Block 3. Logging in this timber
permit area is scheduled to begin in the near future (National Forest Authority, 1996). The Department

northwest quarter of the WMA, exploration lease 1115, is currently held by MacMin Mining which is
presently conducting exploration for potentially significant gold deposits in the area (Post Courier,

Crater Mountain Integrated Conservation and Development Project

The Crater Mountain Integrated Conservation and Development (ICAD) initiative informally began in
1982 with the first attempts to establish a national protected area while addressing the socioeconomic
aspirations of the local landowners through the development of environmentally-sensitive enterprises.
Since its inception, the primary goal of the project has been the long-term conservation of biodiversity
in the Crater Mountain area, attempting to integrate conservation and development components to
achieve the product of a functional national Wildlife Management Area as described under the national
legislation. | The Wildlife Conservation Society (WCS), an international non-governmental
conservation organisation within the New York Zoological Society was the first conservation agency
to sponsor researchers and field workers in the project area, as early as 1975. WCS was also
instrumental in the establishment of the national non-governmental organization, the Research and
Conservation Foundation of Papua New Guinea (RCF) in 1986, which today serves as the lead agency
in the Crater Mountain project. Over the years, numerous national and international, governmental and
non-governmental agencies have provided financial and technical assistance to RCF and WCS in the
implementation of the project.

The effort to establish a national Wildlife Management Area had very informal beginnings which

developed from contact between expatriate scientists and the landowners in the area. It is important to

note that for over a decade, it was through these personal relationships that steps for gazettal of the

WMA and establishment of fledgling eco-enterprises of research and eco-tourism developed. It was

not until 1993, with the achievement of formal gazettal of the Gimi and Pawaian lands as a national

Wildlife Management Area that the ongoing activities took on the official title of a ‘project’ and the

following documented objectives (RCF and WCS, 1995):

e To increase the average annual per capita income of clans (landowning groups) from the
establishment of locally-owned research and ecotourism enterprises in the WMA

e To increase the level and range of understanding and skills of community residents who work in
the research and ecotourism enterprises in the WMA

e To increase the number of decisions and actions which integrate the results of enterprise,
biological and socio-economic monitoring programs ina WMA management plan

e To increase national involvement and human resource exchange within the WMA as teachers,
trainers and consultants working towards conserving natural resources in the WMA

The first two objectives had been informally in place since the first discussions with the Gimi tribe in
the 1970s. The last two objectives evolved in the 1990s with the realisation that the establishment of
environmentally-sound and sustainable businesses was not possible without a process for assessment
and for increased national involvement at all levels. Today, the emphasis of conservation efforts in the
Crater Mountain area is on building the capacity of local communities and their organisations to
assume the principal role of managing the operations in the Wildlife Management Area. As such, the
project is an ambitious effort to engage an extremely high level of community participation in
management of the conservation and development components of the Wildlife Management Area.

Agents of Change and Community Institutions in the Crater Mountain WMA

I. Field researchers

Expatriate scientists were among some of the first outsiders to spend extended periods of time with
WMA communities. Their concern for the unique biodiversity and cultures of the area was the impetus
for their ongoing informal dialogue with Crater communities about mechanisms of establishing and
operating the Wildlife Management Area and the associated eco-enterprises. Length of stay and
impact of field researchers has varied over the last two decades; some have stayed only months while
others developed long-term friendships with members of Crater communities, some returning for
intermittent periods of up to twenty years. Characteristics that have been common to most field
researchers has been a respect for the cultures of the WMA and an obvious admiration and enthusiasm

138

for the WMA’s unique natural resources. Most have been diligent workers who have enthusiastically
lived and labored in their field work alongside residents of the WMA who were their assistants, guides
and companions.

A significant example of these relationships is Australian photographer, David Gillison, who has
worked with the Gimi tribes since 1973. In the early years of his work, he returned for consecutive
seasons to his field site near Ubaigubi village where his original interest in the recording of ritual
theatre of the Gimi led him to the forest with Gimi men to document the displays of the birds of
paradise from which they said that the cultural theatre had evolved (Gillison, 1983). These
interactions solidified a strong mutual respect and commitment between David and community
members which gave rise to the first informal discussions with Gimi villages about the status of these
unique birds and the mutual concern for their decline as well as associated Gimi traditions.

A similar relationship between community and scientists evolved in the southern half of the WMA
where biologists Andrew Mack and Deb Wright came to work on Pawaian land near Haia in 1987. In
the process of building a research station and conducting five years of field work, they would
inevitably engage in ongoing dialogue with Pawaian community members about the uniqueness of
Crater’s natural resources and the options of land use which the Pawaians were considering. Along
with Gillison, Mack and Wright came numerous field assistants over the years who later came back to
do further studies of their own. In this way, a unique ‘family’ of scientists has evolved to live and
work with the Gimi and Pawaian on various studies of natural resources in their forests.

2. Field workers

Beginning in 1984, the project began to place resident and intermittent field workers as trainers in the
WMA. These trainers were both expatriate and national, from a variety of disciplines including
biologists, teachers, small business and rural community development backgrounds. The focus of their
work has been on providing technical assistance to village counterparts in business, community
development and WMA management. Initially, expatriates played a central role in field
implementation. As of 1995, all are in volunteer and advisory positions only, with national staff or
community counterparts directly responsible for field implementation. As with the scientists, most
resident field workers who have stayed for periods of two years or more have formed close friendships
with Crater communities.

Business trainers in the position of tourism lodge managers were first present in Ubaigubi from 1983-
1986. In 1990, the project began to utilise United States Peace Corps (USPC) Rural Community
Development volunteers as field trainers. The USPC program requires that the village provide bush
material housing for the volunteer in return for the technical assistance which they receive. The
volunteer is initiated with the development philosophy that his/her role is to train and support village
counterparts and not to lead and do work for the community that it does not want to do for itself. Since
1990, seven USPC couples have served in WMA communities and expatriate volunteers are still
working with the project in three of the five WMA villages.

In 1993, the WCS placed the first resident field coordinators and scientists on the project staff in the
WMA. While the USPC volunteers continued to work with small business and community service
committees, the field coordinators were assigned to strengthen and assist the clan leaders who sat on
the newly-formed WMA management committees. The coordinators also live in village housing and
spend much time in community meetings as well as on the trails of the WMA. As the first field
coordinator, Jamie James became known and admired by the communities for his capacity to briskly
patrol the rugged country between all five WMA villages. James was followed by two national
biologists, John Ericho and Robert Bino who have been equally well received and respected as mentors
to Crater communities.

3. Community Committees / Institutions and Village Coordinators

Community committees are composed of clan representatives whose selection and responsibilities vary
depending on the function of the committee. The institution which governs the operation of the WMA
is the management committee in each community. It is responsible for composing the laws of the
WMA, enforcing the rules and assessing land use practices. The institution which governs operation of
the Handicrafts Businesses in Herowana and Ubaigubi is called the Handicrafts Business Committee.

139

The institution which governs operations of the Research and/or Ecotourism Business in each
community is also the management committee, the same body which oversees general WMA
operations. In some cases, community workers, called Village Coordinators or Station Managers, have
been selected from or by the committees to carry out specified duties of the committee. These
individuals are often community leaders who have originally volunteered for a leadership role or have
previously worked as field researchers at the site.

Methods for assessing processes and outcomes of community participation in the Crater
Mountain Wildlife Management Area

The intent of this paper is to describe briefly the processes that have been used for engaging
community participation and the resulting extent of participation which has been achieved in the Crater
Mountain ICAD project from its informal inception in 1982 until the present. To assist in this
assessment, an analytical framework was developed (Table 1). It is modeled after Shripton (1989 in
Oakley, 1991) who used the method to assess the community participation aspects of health care and
nutrition projects. The framework identifies seven indicators (Table 2) which represent aspects of
community participation which must be present to some degree in order to establish a functional
protected area which is actually managed by the landowner communities. With the exception of
training, which is an indicator of external input, it is felt that all indicators of community participation
must approach level five if operation of the WMA is to be sustained over the long-term without
continued external inputs.

The framework is used specifically to assess and rank the presence and extent of community
participation over time in two aspects of the operation of the Wildlife Management Area over the
history of the Crater Mountain project. These two aspects are community involvement in:

e WMA Development: management of the Wildlife Management Area (the processes of
establishing the WMA and its operating policies and procedures as related to natural resource use)
and

e Eco-enterprise Development: management of the eco-enterprises, including handicrafts, research
and ecotourism, in the Wildlife Management Area (the processes for starting and maintaining
economic enterprises)

These two areas were selected for analysis as they represent the change over time of community

participation in the ‘C’ (conservation) and the ‘D’ (development) components of the ICAD formula for

establishing a functional Wildlife Management Area. A discrete analysis for WMA and eco-enterprise
operations was conducted for each of the four principal communities in the WMA which includes

Ubaigubi, Herowana, Maimafu and Haia. Each has a unique community profile and history of

involvement in the development and management of the WMA. Due to the informal beginnings of the

Crater Mountain project, indicators of participation were not predetermined and have not been

formally monitored over the entire length of the project as recommended (Oakley 1991). Descriptions

and assessments of participation processes have been derived from the historical trail of written
records, reports, minutes and personal communication from community members and project staff.

Table 2. Indicators of Community Participation

1) Needs assessment / action choice
Indicates the extent of community involvement in determining the need for and the choice of an action in some
aspect of the operation of the Wildlife Management Area
2) Training /Agents of Change

Indicates the extent of technical and management training which the community is receiving to build capacity to
perform the operation of some aspect of the Wildlife Management Area. Agents of change may include any
external field workers from an agency associated with the project area or community members from within the
Wildlife Management Area which have been used to influence the level of the community participation in the
Crater Mountain WMA.

The conceptual framework ranks resident trainers as the most desirable (level 5). While this is seen as desirable
as a ‘means’ to community participation, it should not be viewed as the ‘end’. As the community reaches high
levels of participation in the other indicator areas (action choice, organisation, leadership, etc.), it is expected that
community participation will provide for sustainable operation of the WMA with the need for training
reciprocally declining.

3) Organisational structure / Institution presence

Indicates the extent of development of community organisation and institutions involved in some aspect the
operation of the Wildlife Management Area. Also reflects how well the organisation incorporates and represents
the existing power structure of the community.

4) Leadership

Indicates the extent of community leadership (ranging from none to a few individuals to full community
representation) which is active in the operation of some aspect of the Wildlife Management Area

5) Management

Indicates the extent of management actions (assigning duties or coordinating the work of others) being conducted
by community leaders and / or institutions in the operation of some aspect of the Wildlife Management Area
6) Resource Mobilisation

Indicates the extent of funds or in-kind services which are being contributed by the community, its leaders and / or
institutions to the operation of some aspect of the Wildlife Management Area
7) Monitoring / Evaluation

Indicates the extent of community involvement in the collection, utilisation and dissemination of information to
evaluate the operation of some aspect of the Wildlife Management Area

RESULTS: THE CRATER MOUNTAIN STORY
1. COMMUNITY PARTICIPATION IN DEVELOPMENT OF THE WMA

In this section, the processes for engaging community participation in the development of the Wildlife
Management Area are described. The extent of community participation achieved in each WMA
community by utilising these processes was ranked according to the framework (Table 1) and shown in
Figures 3a-d. Results are shown separately for each WMA village. Years of assessment for each
community begin from the time of arrival of the first field workers/researchers which is depicted in the
lightest color grey with recent years in black.

Training / agents of change in WMA development

From 1982-1992, intermittent field workers and researchers met with communities to introduce the
WMA concept and assist in establishment of WMA committees. David Gillison worked closely with
the Gimi communities in the north, while researchers Andrew Mack and Deb Wright dialogued with
the Pawaians to the south. The first resident field worker, Jamie James, arrived in 1992 to conduct on-
going on-site technical and management training in WMA development. In 1995 and 1996, the first
national resident field workers, John Ericho and Robert Bino, were placed in the WMA communities
of Maimafu, Herowana and Haia. Intermittent assistance from field researchers is still ongoing.
