COMMUNITY NEEDS A MOTIVATING FORCE IN ECO-FORESTRY

Marie Tyler’
Programme Coordinator
Pacific Heritage Foundation
P.O.Box 546, Rabaul, East New Britain Province.

INTRODUCTION

A key role for many extension officers in conservation is that of a researcher, listening to people,
observing, and documenting information about people’s needs within their families, clan and
community. Another important role is the one of educator: encouraging critical thinking, discussing,
and sharing information and skills that may become a part of people’s knowledge (Forest and Baker,
1994).

Generally, extension officers are not members of the communities which they work with, but are
professionals trying to assist groups in managing their resources. What assumptions, knowledge and
skills should they, the extension professionals, have in working with people to investigate their needs,
wants and aspirations in life?

The focus of this paper is to discuss an effective approach in investigating people’s needs within their
land use system. First, I will introduce a livelihood perspective in resource management. Secondly, |
will describe a successful strategy in investigating community needs. Finally, I will look at some
preliminary findings about community needs in eco-forestry projects in the Wide Bay Area, East New
Britain Province.

It is important to emphasise among colleagues who may be ecologists, biologists or agronomists that it
is the people, themselves, in the traditional land use systems in Papua New Guinea who are the users
and guardians of their own resources. They make critical management decisions based on their beliefs,
values, attitudes, practices and common sense.

COMMUNITY NEEDS FROM A LIVELIHOOD PERSPECTIVE

In Melanesian society, communities are made up of individuals, families, clans and communities.
Therefore, identifying people’s needs at each level is important in planning and evaluating the impact
of economic activities in eco-forestry. In turn, there are inevitably social, cultural and political changes
in communities.

Chamber’s and Conway’s (1992) description of livelihood is useful in thinking about needs as a
motivating force in eco-forestry projects. A livelihood is made up of people’s capabilities, their assets
and activities necessary for a means of living. What assets can people claim, gain access to and use
within their land use system? Resources within the land use system are the foundation for rural
people’s lives. The natural environment is the base for people achieving their needs, wants and
aspirations. The land use system includes sub-systems: forest reserves, agroforestry practices, namely
cash crop blocks, shifting cultivation, household gardens and settlement.

Generally, people perceive socio-economic constraints in land use systems rather than focus on
biophysical constraints. Yet conservationists know that the natural environment provides the raw
materials for human-technological activities such as hunting and gathering, food gardening and forestry
(Tyler, 1993)

Over generations, there is an ongoing process of people gaining knowledge and skills to develop their
capabilities to manage their traditional resources. However, in the midst of rapid environmental,

' Presented by Wesley Watt

164

economic and social change in Papua New Guinea how can people meet their changing needs?

Four communities managing their own eco-forestry projects in the Wide Bay Area are an island
surrounded by a sea of large-scale logging activity. Within the last ten years, the scale and speed at
which forest resources are being depleted primarily by foreign owned logging companies is indeed a
shock to the sustainable livelihoods of many people. Surrounded by short-term, easy money from
logging companies people’s needs and aspirations in the project communities are influenced. They
observe and hear stories from relatives living in the logged out areas down the coast. A model of
economic change for many people from the remote area of Wide Bay is the lower Gazelle Peninsula
which has relatively good infrastructure, markets and social services.

Chambers and Conway (1992) stressed that ‘a livelihood is sustainable which can cope with and
recover from stress and shocks, maintain or enhance its capabilities and assets, and provide sustainable
livelihood opportunities for the next generation.’ As well a sustainable livelihood contributes new
benefits in the short and long term to other people’s livelihoods at the local and global levels.

Most rural people participating in the market economy have needs such as food security, housing, and
income generation (Chambers and Leach, 1987; Engberg, 1990; Tyler, 1993). However, it is the drive
for meeting discretionary needs or wants, and aspirations that appears to be a strong motivating force.
Many people have expressed desires to change their daily diet and customary feasting that incorporates
imported foods. People are interested in changing their housing style and furnishings. There are clan
and family obligations in bride-price exchange, school fees, church donations, clothing, transportation
and health care services.

People’s opinions about their needs vary according to their gender, economic activities and size of land
holdings (Engberg, 1996; Tyler, 1993). It is important to listen to and talk with a cross-section of
people, not just clan leaders but both men and women, people with different types of work, the wealthy
and poor in communities. (Chambers and Leach, 1987; Pursley and Firebaugh, 1996)

STRATEGIES FOR INVESTIGATING COMMUNITY NEEDS

With financial and technical support from the U.S. NGO Biodiversity Conservation Network, Pacific
Heritage Foundation (PHF) has tried out several strategies in helping people to identify their needs in
planning and evaluating activities in eco-forestry.

Generally there is an invitation by someone in the community for PHF extension officers to visit and
begin discussion about support for environmental conservation. That key contact person may not bea
recognised leader within the community but has taken the initiative to approach PHF.

The next step is usually to hold individual or group interviews with clan/sub-clan leaders to learn more
about their group, land use system and interests in working with PHF. Much information is usually
collected about general information about the groups that is useful in further investigation with
individuals.

A useful tool for discussing people’s needs is a community mapping exercise. Together, clan leaders
and PHF staff discuss and draw a map outlining their land use system, clan sub-system, settlement,
business enterprises and boundaries. This map is a key tool in selecting clusters of hamlets and
household to conduct individual interviews. This is another way to open dialogue and document
information.

The next step is that individual households are approached and adult males and females who make
decisions about resource management are interviewed individually. In Melanesian society, interviews
seem to be more successful when extension officers interview people of their own gender.

A standard questionnaire is designed to investigate key needs, which were identified in the community
mapping exercise and group interviews. Examples of some key indicators of people’s needs are
housing, food security, income generation and clan participation. Questions are designed to learn more
about current housing and sources of housing materials. Other questions investigate food consumption,
food sources, income sources and income levels, expenditures and people’s economic activities.

PRELIMINARY FINDINGS FROM MONITORING ECO-FORESTRY PROJECTS

Usually, the findings from the group interviews, community mapping exercise and individual
interviews are discussed in community forum, but not before the key themes are discussed with clan
leaders.

From field work in the Wide Bay Area that was carried out from March to August, 1997, people have
been able to identify some of their livelihood needs. They can judge whether those needs have been
met. Often people refer to those achieved needs as benefits. The sawmill enterprise and other small-
scale businesses have been operating for periods of between six months and two and a half years.

A change in housing style is one of the most commonly mentioned benefits of sawmill enterprises in
eco-forestry projects. In many households there are stashes of sawn timber, both first and second
grades, waiting to be used in housing construction. In one community, 40% of the people interviewed
said that they purchased materials for housing, mainly iron roofing. Ten per cent bought sawn timber
from the sawmill business in 1997.

Families continue to produce most food for household consumption, exchange and customary
exchange. Owing to the rotating schedule of four to six people who are employed regularly at the
sawmill, there has not been a shift in labour away from gardening and cash crop production, their
livelihood mainstay. The most common staple foods eaten were sweet potato and cassava, followed by
rice. More store foods were consumed by sawmill employees compared to other households.

Most people earn irregular income from copra sales and mainly women earn a regular small income
from food marketing. The five sawmill employees earning a salary of K40 per fortnight are privileged
wage earners in the community. Most people’s expenditures were necessities such as soap, kerosene,
salt, tin fish and meat and clothing.

Further research and education is needed to learn more about the process of how people’s needs can be
achieved through eco-forestry activities. People in group and individual interviews talked about their
concerns in how the benefits of the sawmill are shared in the clans and communities. There are
questions about who benefits from the sawmill and do people understand how the sawmill enterprise
and other small businesses supported by PHF operate in their community. Generally, there is a
registered landowner group and several key leaders who accept responsibility for the management of
the eco-forestry projects.

CONCLUSION

Pacific Heritage Foundation, a non-government organisation, has worked with people over the last five
years to provide support in conserving their traditional resources and heritage. Our aim is to improve
the welfare of people consistent with improved conservation and managed economic change that will
enhance their livelihoods.

Extension officers in conservation programmes should think about a land use systems approach and be
aware of the pivotal role that livelihood issues have in eco-forestry. We should be working
cooperatively with a cross-section of people in communities to identify the significant clan groups,
families’ and individuals’ needs as all member of the community attempt to earn a livelihood.

‘Families can either damage the earth or conserve and develop resources needed for human survival.’
(Engberg, 1996). There is a need to provide opportunities for extension officers and people in eco-
forestry projects to work together in identifying their needs, wants and aspirations. Realising whether
those needs have been achieved and who has benefited is a powerful motivation. Have people’s
livelihoods been enhanced in conservation practices?

LESSONS FROM CRATER MOUNTAIN ICAD PROJECT
AND SOME SUGGESTIONS.

John Ericho
Project Manager,
Crater Mountain Integrated Conservation and Development Project
Box 1261, Goroka, EHP

OVERVIEW

The Integrated Conservation and Development (ICAD) hypothesis, when introduced in the 1970s, was
an attempt to shy away from the exclusive zone concepts of running wildlife protected areas. The
concept is to include the human component rather than exclude them from the conserved areas. T he
idea is that human beings must be the major actors in conservation from the inside rather than from the
outside in the protected areas. The stake-holders must be trained to manage their resources
sustainably over time. Several ICAD projects have been established and implemented in Papua New
Guinea (PNG) for some time now. The first lessons of success and constraints are becoming clear;
some of those that are obvious from the Crater Mountain ICAD project will be highlighted and
elaborated in this paper. Some suggestions for improvement will be made towards the end of the

paper.

BACKGROUND

The Crater Mountain Wildlife Management Area (WMA) is an upland region of approximately 2700
km, which ranges from lowland rainforest north of Purari River (altitude 100m) to montane cloud
forests along the slopes of Crater Mountain, which is 3118m high. (See Map 1b, in Arlyne Johnson’s
paper, in this volume.) The biodiversity of Crater is high, containing a diverse collection of fauna and
flora, which indicates that it is a natural resource of national importance.

The area is being operated as a WMA by a consortium of non-government organizations (NGOs) in
collaboration with the Crater Mountain landowners. The goals and objectives of the operation are
listed below.

Goal: The Conservation of Biodiversity.

Objectives:

1. Increase the average annual per capita income of clans (land-owning groups) over the next three
years from the establishment of locally-owned research and ecotourism enterprises in the WMA.-

2. Over the next three years, increase the level and range of understanding and skills of community
residents who work in the research and ecotourism enterprises in the WMA.

3. Over the next three years, increase the number of decisions and actions which integrate the results
of enterprise, biological and socio-economic monitoring programs into the working management
plan.

4, Over the next three years, increase national involvement and human resource exchange within the
WMA, as teachers, trainers, and consultants working towards conserving natural resources in the
WMA.

The goals and objectives are in line with the Fourth Goal enshrined in the country’s constitution. This
states that ‘We declare our fourth goal to be for PNG’s natural resources and environment to be
conserved and used for the collective benefit for all of us, and be replenished for the benefit of future
generations’, and further provides that:

e ‘Wise use to be made of our natural resources and environment in and on the land, or sea bed, in
the sea, under the land, in the air, in the interest of our development and trust for future
generations;

e The conservation and replenishment for the benefit of ourselves and posterity, of the environment
and its sacred, scenic, and historic qualities and

e all necessary steps to be taken to give adequate protection to all our valued birds, animals, fish,

168

insects, plants and trees.’

Further to that, the Parliament of this nation went on to put several Acts in place to define the
framework for the implementation of the constitutional goals and objectives, in particular the
Conservation Areas Act (1980, 1992) and the Fauna (Protection and Control) Act (1966).

The provisions of the Conservation Area Acts (1980, 1992) allows for the identification and
management of protected areas through the formation of management committees who are intended to
be the custodians of formation and enforcement of laws and regulations to manage conservation areas.

The provisions of the Fauna (Protection and Control 1966) Act allows for the protection of fauna in
protected areas such as WMAs. The objectives of establishing such areas are to protect subsistence
resources from over-harvesting by customary landholders in order to enhance biodiversity, to gain
formal recognition for tenure and resource ownership, to provide opportunities for income generation,
to protect cultural values, and to provide scientific research and educational opportunities.

There is no doubt that the constitutional framework is well in place, perhaps beyond anything any other
developing countries have by the way of conservation legislation.

The concept of Integrated Conservation and Development (ICAD) fits into the WMA concept readily.
In that sense the ICAD hypothesis is inherent in the WMA concept, and thus the Crater Mountain
WMA is operated as an ICAD project. This is to test the hypothesis that development and conservation
can be linked for the ultimate goal of conservation of biodiversity.

With the able technical assistance of national and overseas NGOs in collaboration with the landowners

in Crater Mountain the people have pledged to declare the area as a WMA. It was gazetted in 1993.
The landowner communities have now established landowners’ committees as stipulated under the
Fauna (Protection and Control Act (1980, 1992). The Research and Conservation Foundation (RCF)
provides technical assistance. The concept of ICAD is being tested under the framework of the WMA.

The Crater Mountain project has been operating for some four years now. Every year since 1993 the
management committees have convened on a rotating basis at each of the sites. At these meetings the
.draft management plans (which includes decision-making in regards to land use practices, laws to
regulate and control the natural resource and fee structures to be charged for all services provided to
visitors are debated and enacted. They review each scheme every second year to see if they are
working or not and make amendments accordingly. The management plan is being crystallized slowly,
but it will be their own when it is completed.

Most of the structures, mechanisms and laws have been put into place and they are now put into
practice. How has the project prospered? Have they all been success stories? What are the constraints
if any? These are important questions and we need to scrutinize them. The project mechanisms and
processes need to be dissected, so that we can have insight into them. ‘The first lessons from Crater are
becoming clear, so I will discuss the success stories first, then the constraints, offering some
recommendations towards the end.

POSITIVE OUTCOMES FROM THE PROJECT

Success is being accomplished, despite the following negative variables:

e The area is vast and remote, not easily accessible.

e It offers only a subsistence living

e There are minimal or no government services, which means literacy level is very low, population
is low, due to the lack of proper health services, and there is no infrastructure.

e The cash economy is low and very new - creating unrealistic expectations.

There has been significant success in seven distinct areas of operation.
1, Management committees have been formed.

The management committees have been organized and are operating, and we are beginning to
appreciate the processes that were employed in soliciting membership for the committees. Though we

169

did not follow the standard procedures, the management committees are functioning. The members are
holding onto the office with enthusiasm and waiting eagerly for things to evolve for them.

2. Laws have been formulated

The people realized that they could not manage a WMA without laws so they formulated and drafted
eighteen laws in the third annual meeting in Maimafu in 1995. (See Table 3 in Arlyne Johnson’s paper
above.) These laws were reviewed at the 1996 annual meeting in Herowana and again at the Haia
annual meeting this year. Some laws originally included as community laws were difficult for the
management committees to enforce so they were deleted. The laws are undergoing the acid test of time
and reality, and as time goes on they will become part of the management plans.

3. Guest houses have been established.

The guest houses could have been built at high costs to be flashy affairs but we are allowing them to
develop at their own pace. They have constructed basic visitor accommodation, with a bare minimum
of amenities to allow them to train themselves in visitor care practices before they enlarge. We are also
teaching them business skills at their own level for reinvestment and putting time and labor into any
venture free before they can appreciate the fruits of their labor. We are getting to shy away from the
free handout mentality. It is a hard process as the ideas of free handouts have become entrenched. We
will continue to work with them.

4, Artifacts shops have been established.

Alongside the guest houses, artifact shops have been established, to serve the visitors who would like to
take a souvenir home. This is the most successful business of the project to date yet, though research is
a close second. A lot more people from the community are earning money through artifacts, as
opposed to all the other ventures. One community earned in a four-year period approximately K35,000
from artifact sales alone, whereas coffee brought in about K30,000. The people now know that their
forest has monetary value as well as aesthetic value. These ideas are imperceptibly taking a foothold.

5. Research stations have been set up.

The other successful enterprise in the Crater Mountain Project is the Research As Business section,
which develops research stations. There is one partially equipped research station, which has most of
what a researcher needs, especially a pristine, less human-impacted area for conducting research. It has
brought in at least five long-term researchers and another five short-term researchers from overseas and
several national researchers. It has enabled the people to earn a substantial amount of money to one
community. There are promises of other researchers coming in the near future that will ensure more
money earning opportunities for some more people. The people are not merely working as equipment
catriers but also as trained assistants for researchers and trained local observers (TLO).

6. People are earning some money.

One of our objective states that the project will increase the clan’s income levels and it is apparent that
at least that objective is being achieved in a small way, with a promise to become more substantial as
the project develops.

7. People’s initial conceptions are changing.

It is becoming obvious to us that the people did not know what they were getting into when they agreed
to enter into the venture but slowly it is getting to them. Even if they knew what they were getting into
they have not been the same things their counterparts had envisioned. But the good that has come is
that they are allowed to think of the consequences of their decisions and what they must do to change it
if it did not meet their expectations.

Having highlighted the positive developments from the project we now focus on the constraints.

PROBLEMS OF THE PROJECT.

The paradigms we have employed to assure that development is slow and sure are:
© No cash handouts.

e A lot of patience and allowing things to develop at their own pace.

e [Educating and developing their skills and capacity to manage.

170

The variables the system is operating under:

e The people did not appreciate the length and breadth of what they were entering into when they
gazetted the area and formed management committees.

e . They expected cargo and other benefits immediately.

e They expected services to come with it.

I will divide constraints into two subsections. These are internal constraints and external constraints.
Internal Constraints.
The internal constraints are:

1. The expectations and perceptions.

The people expected the organization to bring large wholesale changes to their communities and they
have been waiting for these since they started in 1993. This problem stems from historical
associations. The pioneers of the project were white people and the locals associated cargo and change
with white people. Why shouldn’t they? The white man brought the balus, the car, the clothes, the
store goods, everything western was brought in by the white man. So when they started talking about a
conservation area, everyone agreed while all the time thinking about the services like education and
health services, and other money earning opportunities. The expectation was quite high. As the project
has been running for four years people are beginning to be disillusioned.

Field officers living on site and giving continuous education are paying dividends. The western
concept of conservation is dawning on them though and they are integrating their own concepts of
lukautim bus in the new concepts. How far and good that integration will be will unfold as time goes
on.

2. Ownership questions.

The ownership issue is a thing that is not too difficult for them to comprehend, as they know in their

culture that if someone is helping them, the onus is on the person being assisted to feed the person

assisting. The same system can be transferred to the ownership issue. They just refused to believe that
it is their own enterprise. The confusion might stem from several factors:

a) The project proponents have been saying that the project is theirs exclusively, when it really is not
theirs alone. The project is the project proponents’ initiative, while the business enterprises should
not be claimed as theirs. So we should from the outset separate the project from the enterprises.

b) We have been from necessity working with the most prominent and forward individuals. They in
turn have been working very hard assisting with the project, but culturally speaking these
individuals must meet clan and cultural obligations by siphoning money-earning opportunities to
their clans first and more frequently than to other clans. This has created a hierarchy of owners as
perceived by the community at large. They perceive that project proponents run the project,
nurture and train a minority group and establish business enterprises for those few and the
expected dividends do not filter down to the community at large. Hence they do not appreciate the
business enterprises as their own. Until they see direct financial benefit accruing to them
personally, they understand that the enterprise and the project belongs to the project proponent or
‘others’ - bilong narapala lain.

3. Leadership issues

It is not for want of leaders that we face this hurdle, but the integrity and capacity of leadership in the
emerging cash economy that is trying for those individuals we are nurturing in the communities. In the
communities the traditional leader is not an elected office. It is a recognized office due to an
individual’s material possessions, powers of oration, and capacity to move and intimidate and to initiate
and lead. So it seems that the management committees may have been ‘elected’, but not ‘recognized’.
The elected leadership may be superficial and therefore ineffective. The recognized leadership may be
strong and effective in the traditional society but may be not so practical when the cash economy and
formal education is thrown into the picture. The problems we encounter now regarding leadership
issue may be a mixture of both problems. The ‘elected’ leaders are not effective as they do not possess
the leadership qualities, while those ‘recognized’ leaders we have worked with do not possess the
finesse of an informed proper leader with integrity and charisma. . There is a leadership vacuum in a sea
of would-be leaders, and that is our dilemma.

4. The literacy levels.
The low literacy levels in the communities attenuate the leadership problems that we face in the
project. It is so pivotal that it affects all other areas that we discussed earlier and those we will discuss
below. The management skills are directly proportional to the educational level. The reduced capacity
to assimilate and disseminate information is also a factor of low literacy. Though we would like to
inundate the communities with information, only the educated will assimilate that, then they also hoard
information as information is power. Though most of our efforts are lost we continue to disseminate
information, since if one person can be affected positively, it may mean a lot for the future.

We are now targeting three groups in the community. These are the management committees, the
women’s group, and the students at the community schools. If our attempts with the former two groups
give us marginal results then we hope that the next generation, which is being educated, will assimilate
and internalize the conservation issues so that it becomes a way of life for them. While we will
continue to use workshops, meetings, audiovisual means to educate the two older groups, the next
generation is a prime target for conservation education. In that regard we plan to introduce a school
subsidy scheme to assist the top 5 % of the students to further their education. This is or will be
deemed as a conservation investment, for which we will reap a full harvest of dividends in the near
future.

5. The socio-conomic levels

The socioeconomic levels are also related to literacy levels. The community operates a rudimentary
economics that is growing, selling and buying. Individuals in some communities have tried segondary
level of business- retail stores, which involves investment. In one community there were 13 trade
stores for 600 inhabitants. Most of them operate on a seasonal basis, meaning they stock up their stores
with goods only during coffee season, the other nine months most stores close up business. The basic
business concepts are ill understood and therefore overdone or underdone and hence they do not make
business sense.

Our business development officer (BDO) is on site to give technical assistance, but then when literacy
level is low, the job of the BDO is an uphill battle, at the least frustrating, at the most — forget it. But
there are signs that a few individuals are catching up, and if that is any indication, the future looks
bright. When the educated individuals come out and receive the entrepreneur skills by the BDO, the
potential is there. There is saying in the communities that “A tree will not fall unless we continue to
hack at it’. So we will continue to work.

6. The unequal distribution of money earning opportunities. +

While this problem has been alluded to in the section on ownership issues, there are other problems
associated with it. The enterprises are at the best very nebulous. While the artifact and research
businesses earn a small but handsome amount of money for those communities that are advanced, the
ecotourism business is still a dream. It is understood that all enterprise activities will not bring money
to every individual at the same time. But that is the problem for us. Our leaders and managers are
strongly influenced by their cultural upbringing, so that the money-earning opportunities are not evenly
distributed. So there is dissatisfaction from every portion of the community. This evokes traditional
clan-based sentiments, resulting in political alignments in the communities, which in turn destabilizes
the community ownership concepts that we so fervently foster. The project’s tempo and pace can be
measured by the ability of rival clans to work harmoniously for their collective benefit and betterment,
not only for themselves but also for their children.

7. Women’s involvement.

The highlands communities are gender-segregated communities. Thus culturally the women’s tasks
were basically twofold, domestic chores and procreation. Their domestic duties included gardening
and cooking, while their other obligation was to bear and rear children. The men’s duties included
fence-building for gardens and planting large food plants and building houses. Most important of all
was the decision-making in regard to land and use of natural resources on their land. The women
decide the food varieties planted but the men decide where the garden should and should not be.

When donors require that women be on the management committees, red flags go up in the minds of

the men. Of course women can get involved in the project by making bilums and other artifacts and
selling them, be tour guides, cook and wash for visitors but sitting on the committee that makes

172

decisions regarding land is another thing. Only one community is trying out, tentatively, the idea of
women sitting on the committee. The other communities are watching with great interest as to what
will unfold. They all see that their culture is under threat and are just waiting to see what will happen.
It will take some time before gender equality will be seen to come through. This change must not be
forced on them, it must come from within them, and this should be the method for proper change.

External Constraints.

1. Law enforcement

Law enforcement in the communities by the management committees is experiencing problems but it is
developing well. People who are used to obeying unwritten laws in the past are now coming up against
written laws and their punishments. Infringement of unwritten laws had always brought only one
consequence - beating or a fight. These new written rules have varying penalties. It is hard to make
head or tail of, at times, but that is not the major constraint. The biggest constraint is not having
enough money to send those who infringe on the law to the major centers to be dealt with by law. The
other problem is that the law enforcement agencies, when requested by the management committees to
come and assist with the resolution of conflicts, either do not have the money to do so or could not care
less. This is the weakest point in strengthening the management committees. Ifa procedure can be
developed to assist them to link with existing law enforcing agencies that will be good.

2. Government support.

The communities in the WMA would like to see government support on several counts:

1. Funds- they would like to see government putting a bit of money the running of the project. They
ask why people from outside the country give money for them to run this project? The government
should give their share, after all the people are entitled to government funding.

2. Government presence at their annual meetings — to be present would be a show of encouragement
and motivate them to go on from strength to strength. Even if they would not give money their
presence would be sufficient to give the necessary impetus for them to move forward.

3. Policy support. The government must make proper policies to assist the stake-holders to get a
lion’s share of any resource exploitation, and or to put a complete stop to natural resource use by
large-scale developers, if they were to really protect and conserve their natural resources.

4. Law enforcement. As already mentioned above.

3. Inadequate funds compared to Large scale Resource Developers.

The fund the project’s technical proponent has is not enough to offset the money that large-scale
developers bring in. Would it be possible to request donors and governments to chip in and offset or
equalize the money brought in by resource developers?

RECOMMENDATIONS.
I would like to make several recommendations to the government basically to assist if at all possible.

1. The Government must go beyond legislation to assist management committees in law enforcement.
A structure must be set up to strengthen the weak spot in the government’s structure and back it up

with funds.
2. Government services must be encouraged at policy level to filter to the people in remote areas.
3. The relevant government department must be financially capable of assisting such operations.

References

Sekhran, N., and S. Miller (eds). 1996 Papua New Guinea Country Study on Biological Diversity.
Colorcroft Ltd. Hong Kong.

173

THE MANAGEMENT AND IMPLEMENTATION
OF
INTEGRATED CONSERVATION AND DEVELOPMENT PROJECTS:
THE LAKEKAMU EXPERIENCE

Thomas Paka
Foundation of the Peoples of the South Pacific, PNG, Inc.
P. O. Box 1119, Boroko, NCD

ABSTRACT.

Conservation and sustainable development is an issue that is facing the developed and the developing
world alike. Given the current alarming rates of environmental destruction, various conservation tools
have been tried to protect and conserve the environment. But mere conservation cannot occur unless the
people’s social, cultural and economic needs are satisfactorily met. For the developing world in particular
where the economy relies heavily on natural resources, it is either development or conservation, or both.
This dilemma is now being resolved in principle by the now-popular concept ‘Integrated Conservation and
Development’ (ICAD) being tried in most developing countries. The Lakekamu ICAD initiative is no
exception in trying this ICAD concept that highly recognises resource owners' participation. This is not
easy when it comes to the management and implementation of project goals and activities. It involves
people with different levels of interest and understanding, let alone the four major clan groups we are
dealing with in the Lakekamu area.

This paper begins by briefly defining and introducing the ICAD concept and the project overview. It
outlines the approach and activities engaged in implementing the project goals and objectives. Problems,
challenges, lessons learnt and steps taken to avoid such problems are also discussed, in terms of the main
sources and or factors. The project’s achievements to date have also been highlighted.

Since this is a new initiative, much is anticipated to be learnt through the feedback from the participants
depending on individual and general conclusions that can be drawn from this presentation.

INTRODUCTION

Background

Defined as the process of integrating conservation and development to meet resource holders social and
economic needs and aspirations while meeting conservation objectives, ICAD is a fairly recently
introduced concept in PNG. It is a complex approach aimed at releasing tangible social and economic
benefits through sustainable conservation management and resource development incentives.

This concept was first experimented with in some African countries as an alternative to other contemporary
conservation tools such as national parks, zoos, nature reserves, wildlife sanctuaries, etc., which failed in
most instances because the resource owners were not included in the initial decision-making process.
Conservation efforts are certainly more effective if the stakeholders are involved and receive some benefit
from their conservation efforts, be it increased income or better access to resources, leading towards a
better quality of life.

Project Overview

The Lakekamu Basin area is a vast expanse of pristine lowland rainforest with a scattering of small
villages, sparsely settled by four distinct cultural or language groups — the Kunimaipa, the Kamea, the
Biaru and the Kovio. The Basin spans across the Gulf, Central and Morobe provinces and is situated 60km
east of Kerema town (the capital of the Gulf province) and 50km SSE of Wau in the Morobe province
(Fig.1). Supporting fewer than 2000 residents, this 2,500km’ tract is the largest expanse of unbroken

174

humid forest in the southern watershed of peninsular PNG and is remarkably rich in wildlife, especially
species of large and showy birds and mammals that are threatened elsewhere in PNG — the southern
cassowary, Gurney’s eagle, lowland wallaby and great-billed heron to name a few. This was initially
revealed by Dr. Bruce Bechler, then Conservation International’s senior ecologist for the Asia-Pacific
Region, who visited the Basin on several occasions surveying birds and forest ecology between 1978 and

Se corres

1992. He learnt of the biological significance of the area and recommended it for conservation. The Basin
has been designated ‘of very high priority’ by the recently completed ‘Conservation Needs Assessment for
Papua New Guinea’. This revelation prompted a series of discussions seeking ways to conserve the
biological richness of the area, which eventually led to this project.

Despite the ecological richness of the area, the community is disadvantaged in terms of government
services and infrastructural development. The only services in the Basin comprise two airstrips, two
community schools, and a sub-health centre. Health workers, teachers, and patrol officers sent there leave
after a few weeks or months because they claim that the place is remote and there is no incentive and
government support.

In the midst of problems or rather realities, the Lakekamu ICAD project envisions in its activities to
provide the Basin communities with alternatives for sustainable development as opposed to large-scale
logging and mining operations which are currently major resource development threats to the Basin’s
biodiversity, and or any other ecologically destructive resource development projects. The main objective
of this project is to promote the conservation of the pristine lowland forests of the Basin and its upland
watershed. This would be achieved by building the capacity of the local people to understand the linkages
between environment, conservation and development to enable them to lead their own developmental
process and to conserve their natural resources for continued benefits.

This project is a joint initiative of Conservation International (CI) and the Foundation of the Peoples of the
South Pacific (FSP/PNG) in collaboration with the Wau Ecology Institute (WEI). The project is funded
with a three-year (1995 to 1998) implementation grant of US$355,487 made available by the United States
Agency for International Development (USAID) through US-based NGO, Biodiversity Conservation
Network (BCN). The British High Commission provided us with a supplementary fund of K8000 for the
project’s Research facility.

PROJECT MANAGEMENT AND IMPLEMENTATION

Management and implementation of project objectives and activities, for the purpose of this paper is simply
making sure that all components and issues relating to the project are organised and addressed, so that
eventually the project meets the expected outputs in the line with ICAD principles, satisfactorily and within
the given time frame.

Due to the general complexities involved in the management and implementation of ICAD project
objectives, we have based our project activities around two assumptions more common in community-
oriented conservation projects. These are:

1. Income generation from small scale commercial natural resource-based eco-enterprises will lead to
conservation success (chart 1);
2. Environmental education will influence people to act to conserve natural resources (chart 2).

Having in mind these assumptions, the project embarks on three main activities, which include:

Firstly, the development of Science/Research Tourism based on a landowner-managed rainforest field
station for the study and training in ecology, wildlife biology, and conservation science. The Research
Station has finally been set up at Ivimka after the Kamea landowners accepted us on ‘sympathetic’ grounds
after abandoning three sites, Nagore, Sii and Korr (Map 1b) basically due to landowner disputes and
disapproval of our activities because they were suspicious of our presence.

Since the project inception, this enterprise has taken much of our time. Work on the Research building
began in late 1995 after the green light was given by the landowners. The almost-completed field station is

being manage by Kurt Merg, our resident scientist who will be leaving at the end of 1997. A replacement
is being sought.

The materials for the research facility were airlifted, particularly the corrugated galvanised iron and nails

while the timber were cut by ourselves using a chainsaw and an Alaskan mill. Twenty-nine percent (about
K36,000) of the funds spent so far on the project has directly been related to the research building and other
related costs, such as transport, equipment repair, wages, materials etc.

In the process of constructing the building, we have had numerous problems ranging from personnel
getting sick, chronic mechanical problems with the chain saw, attending to other community obligations
and delay in hiring of technical staff. In spite of these problems, we were able to move forward slowly but
steadily to achieving our objectives.

Secondly, the development of adventure tourism featuring the famous Bulldog Trek (Map 2), a
spectacular walking track which is ideal for scenic photographers, bird watchers, backpackers and nature
lovers needs to be fully developed. Seven wayside rest houses have been planned for the Circuit Trek but
to date nothing has been done due to lack of collaborator support and to an extent, staff shortage. On the
other hand the people have taken the initiative to construct lodges in the Basin. The one at the Kakoro
Station was completed and officially opened in May 1997. The other lodge is being built at Tekadu by the
Kamea people. Both are being constructed with bush materials. One more lodge is being proposed for
Okavai near the coast.

Thirdly, the provision of initial education and training for the local communities in the implementation
phase of this project in order to allow the Basin community to ensure that both the enterprise and the
conservation objectives are not compromised by future developments. This is aimed at building the
capacity of the local people so that they can be able to manage their own resource developmental process.
Hence building the knowledge base of the local community is fundamental in achieving conservation
objectives. Apart from continuous awareness work being carried out by the field officers, we conducted
two formal workshops. They are the Literacy Training held in Madang mid-1996, where four landowner
representatives attended. The Madang workshop in particular had a tremendous effect on the community.
Some people who had previously rejected the idea of conservation later pledged their support for the
project. The construction of the lodges serves as evidence of the peoples’ support for Eco-enterprises in the
Basin.

Although training for landowners was the third primary objective of the initial proposal, there is

tremendous work still to be done, and more resources to be allocated to this aspect. This indicates the need
for additional and specialised manpower.

PROBLEMS AND CHALLENGES

In the process of implementing the project objectives, the project staff and the management team (Chart 3)
had encountered a lot of problems and challenges. Lessons learnt from the Lak ICAD project in southern
New Ireland seems almost identical to those that are beginning to unfold in the Lakekamu initiative. These
problems are seen to have been generated from three main sources/factors which include: the project
proponents or NGO partners and collaborators (CI, FSP/PNG and WEI); the community or landowners,
and the government, bureaucrats and/or politicians. These are discussed in detail below.

Chart 3: The Management and Project Staff Structure of the
Lakekamu Initiative.

NGO Partners and Collaborators or Project Proponents

Some of the problems and challenges encountered in the field were caused mainly due to poor planning by
the project proponents. Some of the loopholes and failures of the project implementers have thus been
identified and discussed.

The first failure we have realised was the middle approach we took where we brought the idea to the
people, and at the same time, seeking government support. The village leaders voiced their concerns about
the future of their lands and the communities to Yati Bun (Executive Director - FSP/PNG), Harry Sakulas
(WEI - Director), and Bruce Beehler (Senior Scientist - CI) in three Basin-wide forums, held respectively

180

in November 1992, August 1993 and September 1994, which proposed and developed the Basin’s
development plan. However, in a community having a very high illiteracy level, those general forums were
insufficient to gauge and draw concrete support and conclusion from the community at large. It was learnt
from those forums that the leaders and the Basin’s populace understood the importance of this forest
resource to their material and cultural well-being and showed their support for the proposed initiative, but
were still doubtful as to whether it will ever bring the perceived tangible benefits. The people were also not
sure of who we really were and that built suspicion among the community members. This could mainly be
due to the fact that we entered the community without much groundwork or without following the proper
community entry procedures which is very vital in any community-oriented development projects.
Community readiness was frankly lacking as the substance of what we discussed was totally foreign to
them.

To make the matter worse, we never really identified who to work with from the community level apart
from some leaders identified in principle. That made our project to start on an ad hoc basis. The
publication by Nadine Grant (1996) on community entry explicitly highlights such hindrance. The
community ‘accepted’ the idea perceiving it would generate large sums of money which in turn will bring
material necessities. Our failure to consult the provincial authorities with our proposal with our proposal
until after we moved into the project site, has had a negative impact on the project. We were told to leave
the area by the then Premier for Gulf Province, Mr Paul Apio, who claimed that he was not informed of our
presence and activities and also that we caused division amongst the four ethnic groups. However, he now
fully supports the project after close dialogue has been established.

The other problem encountered was the lack of cooperation between the NGO partners and collaborators
due to their involvement in some other activities. Sometimes for instance, our requests for certain inputs
from CI and WEI have not been attended to for weeks, months and even not at all, because they were doing
other things. It was also noted that while collectively implementing one project by many partner
organisations is an advantage, it also raised some problems. For instance, when the WEI laid off its staff
due to shortage of funds, some of the project activities that were assigned to the institute, such as the water
quality monitoring work scheduled for 1996, could not be carried out. In addition to this, we found it a
problem to compromise divergent and sometimes overlapping work plans for NGO partners and
collaborators. This is a problem which really needs to be addressed.

The other problem we have noticed is the shortage of staff. We have two staff on the ground who have
overstretched themselves doing work that is supposed to be done by various specialised persons such as
health workers, community development officers and so on. Hank Cauley, the Director for BCN
commented that the Lakekamu initiative is one of the most understaffed BCN-funded projects. This is
generally because these very essential community workers were not considered in the initial budget. The
proposal lacked mention of practical social, cultural, spiritual and economic needs and aspirations of the
community, despite their clear importance for such projects, especially where government services are very
limited or non-existent. Some funds have been deviated from other line items to cater for these needs. The
funds estimated for the construction of the Research Station and associated costs were also underestimated.

Because of this gap, the project staff, over and above their already heavy workloads, send out proposals for
community development activities such as health and nutrition and literacy work, since these really need to
be addressed before conservation issues and implications. We have also looked for ways to increase our
staff strength, and as a result we have a US Peace Corps couple in the field working as community
development officers.

Despite these failures and challenges, the two field officers have been doing extensive awareness work on
environmental and related issues to stress our point. The decision to have our field officers living with the
villagers and being part of the community is showing some positive signs in mobilising community
support. Recent plans to transfer the wife of a Village Outreach Officer, Cosmas Makamet, to work in the
community is highly supported and desired by the people, since her skills as a nursing sister are in strong
demand. It is anticipated that such approaches will over-ride our failures over time and we are highly
optimistic that this will in fact come about.

SS

|

The Community and Existing Threats.
Convincing many individuals in a community with varied interests and perceptions is not easy. One of the
greatest problems, or rather challenges, that we had to overcome for the success of this project is ‘
convince everyone to believe and support the concept we are trying in the Basin. Dealing with four ethnic
groups who claim different parts of the Basin and possess different views about development is a headache
for the field staff. Since the project’s inception it has been our aim to see every group and gender
participate in this step towards development. We have identified landowner representatives to work wit)
us and this approach is showing positive signs especially the developments that had occurred after the eco.
tourism workshop we had in Madang as mentioned earlier.

The other main challenge is for project staff to identify strategic ways to divert the people’s interest from
fast money-making options and also from the other activities that constitute their daily lifestyles which had
existed even before we entered the community with our proposal. These comprise the current threats to the
project such as mining, logging, hunting and gardening. The people in the Basin have grown up with these
activities; we can say that they are part of them and it seems hard for them to give in to conservation ideas
easily.

On another note, the community sees this project as a supplementary or additional income source. Two
instances have been observed. Firstly, we have witnessed some young men who worked with us as
assistants for a fortnight or two going back to the creeks and rivers to pan for gold when they were laid off
to give equal opportunities for others to assist us. In the second occasion, some men who played a leading
role in the construction of the Kakoro lodge were seen panning for gold to raise funds to furnish the lodge
and to buy rations for the opening ceremony of the lodge. If what we have seen is likely to continue, there
certainly is a need for more awareness work from the project proponents as well as some commitment from
the landowners to trade off such unsustainable practices for the success of this project.

Additionally, observations have shown that the people are yet to fully understand the project proponents as
non-profit organisations. Many people have taken us for a charity organisation which give free handouts.
They think that we can bail them out from any financial problem, whether it be the need for a plane ticket,
an election nomination feé, a court settlement or whatever. Despite the numerous times we told them that
the project was theirs, they want money for everything they do.

The Government, Bureaucrats and Politicians
The government is indirectly responsible for some of the problems and challenges this project encounters.
Firstly, the people in the Basin have been neglected by successive governments over the years in terms of
delivering basic services. Because of the great need for the basic goods and services, the people are likely
to accept any offer for immediate cash and services to meet their immediate needs and aspirations. It is
believed that the provision of basic goods and services is likely to or can have a greater impact on the
attitudes of the people. Because of the government’s ignorance and failure in delivering the basic needs
due to the people, much of our time has been taken up in trying to play their part.

Secondly, political will and bureaucratic support for such projects is needed to add some weight to
indirectly drive the message of conservation and development home. This would relieve the pressure on
the project staff who are in general over-stretched and exhausted. Support for such projects is relevant to
PNG as a whole as well, as it is enshrined in the Fourth National Goal and Directive Principles of our
national constitution, which basically calls for sustainable use of our limited natural resources for the
benefit of the present and future generations. To give effect to this goal, the government has enacted
numerous comprehensive environmental legislations and developmental policies; however, there are
problems with the implementation of the legislation. While we acknowledge the dilemma the government
is faced with, acting as an environmental protector and resource development proponent, since the
government takes equity in major resource extraction projects, we feel that comparatively very small
conservation efforts being tried in locations like Lakekamu should be given ample support in anyway they
can.

We have been working closely with the Gulf provincial authorities to show them that what we are doing in
the Basin is for the good of their people now and in the future and therefore their support is needed.
Developments in this regard are looking good with constant dialogue being established.

182

ACHIEVEMENTS

Despite the numerous problems and challenges we have encountered, we have been moving forward as a
team from the administration down to the field officers to see the project objectives achieved. The
highlights of our achievements include:

1.

10.

The acquisition of land and the development of the Ivimka Research facility. The land on which the
building now stands was given to the project by the Kamea people of Tekadu who wanted to ‘have a
go’ at this ICAD concept.

The planning and erection of eco-tourism lodges in the Basin.

Despite the absence of initial communication with the Gulf Provincial Authorities, recent dialogue
with them showed positive support for work we are doing in the area. Our activities in this part of the
province has been acknowledged in their draft forest policy.

We now have a Basin Committee in place, comprised of representatives from the four ethnic or
language groups. Work already being done by these representatives in mobilising community support
is having effect.

The formation of two women’s groups, one at Tekadu and one at Kakoro to carry out work related to
women. They are very keen in participating in the project as they have indicated to our field officers.

Work is being underway to gazette this area as a Wildlife Management Area. CI’s in-country director,
Gaikovina Kula, is looking into this matter.

The project has provided employment for the people on short-term basis through; pottery, assistance
and sale of roots and vegetables to project staff and researchers. According to our records, the
community has earned a total amount of K7772.95 between March and December, 1996 in this
manner.

Marketing of the project is looking good. NGO partner network and other overseas contacts are
showing good potential for effective marketing and is likely to attract more scientists in particular.
Our first scientist is here for two months doing work on crown pigeons. More have sent in letters
requesting more information and permission to come.

Identifying, training and establishing butterfly farming in the Basin by our collaborator, the WEI is
progressing well. The local people were trained on the basics of insect collection, butterfly farming
and preservation techniques. From the last report gathered, an amount of k300 was received by an
individual during a single sale of five Birdwing butterfly, Ornithoptera meridianalis. This is likely to
boost support for conservation.

We have successfully negotiated with the Port Moresby General Hospital for medical supplies for the
Kakoro sub health center and Tekadu aid post to be sent via us.

LESSONS LEARNT

From the discussions presented above, the following lessons have been learnt. Nadine Grant (1996) has
explicitly discussed some of these points. Lessons learnt include the following:

1.

Unless all the stakeholders are involved in the planning process from the start, any conservation
attempt is certain to face hectic problems.

183

2. Working with individuals and influential people and building the interest of the people conserved is
very essential in such engagements though a lot of time is required for this.

3. Unless the viability of the project is properly assessed, planned and implemented, large sums of funds
will not have lasting effects.

4. People must be taught from the very start that the project proponents are NGOs who are trying to help
the community to have a better and a sustainable future and not a charity organization to hand out free
cash. People need to be convinced that the project is theirs and the proponents are only helping them
get started. They are not going to take anything away.

5. Liaison with the government and appropriate authorities to channel essential goods and services will at
least ease the pressure on the people, reducing their need to go for fast cash-earning options such as
large-scale mining and logging operations. The government needs to play its required role in
delivering goods and services to the people.

6. Unless there is enough experienced and specialised project personnel on the ground, nothing much will
be accomplished.

7. Cooperation and NGO partner support is a must. Coordinated and well-fitted schedules and work
plans for partners and strict implementation of these is essential if planned activities are to be done on
time.

8. Proposal submission for conservation projects as such must equally and thoroughly identify, address
and budget for every issue that one way or another affects the people’s well being which is likely to
affect the project. Otherwise much fund deviation will occur with little or no lasting results.

9. Marketing of the product is the single most important activity that will determine whether or not such
projects continue or survive. This project will be wasteful if the products are not sold.

10. Facilitating individuals and families taking initiatives in engaging themselves in small income-
generating activities is one issue that surfaced which needs to be addressed.

11. Our performance seems to have been judged by the achievements of other projects which have
different cultural, social, spiritual and economic settings; this is potentially dangerous.

CONCLUSION

ICAD is a fairly new concept and a complex approach to conservation. It is being tried in PNG on an
experimental basis, including the Lakekamu initiative. Trying the concept in a socially and culturally
diverse society is a battle the implementers had to fight.

Many problems, challenges and lessons learnt have been discussed. Three sources of problems have been
highlighted and for any project to be successful, those problems have to be addressed problem by problem.
Careful and long term planning with input from all stakeholders and parties involved and those to be
involved is very essential in any ICAD initiative. Such planning must address issues ranging from funds,
man-power commitment and dedication, collaborator and government support, local or host community
education, awareness, participation and so on.

Despite the problems and challenges encountered, the project proponents have been very patient and
persistent which resulted in achieving results as highlighted in the discussion. However hard this initiative
may seem, the project staff and the management team will continue to work at their own pace to see the
fruits of their labor, even if it takes a thousand years.

Conclusions and lessons learnt from this experience and other similar projects in PNG are envisaged to

184

form the basis for better and more workable plans for future projects as well as adjusting and refining
current projects for desired results or output.

References

Grant, N. 1996. Community Entry for ICAD Projects - The Participatory Way. Department of
Environment & Conservation/United Nations Development Programme - OPS - PNG/93/G31 -
PNG Biodiversity Conservation & Resource Management Programme, Port Moresby.

Salufsky, Nick and Maroluis, R., 1966, ‘Measures of Success: a systematic approach to designing,
managing and monitoring community-oriented conservation projects’, Circulated draft paper.

THE ORO BUTTERFLY PROJECT EXPERIENCES

Ishmael Koneha and Fabian Tambari
Department of Environment and Conservation
P.O. Box 647, Popondetta
Oro Province

INTRODUCTION

There are three proponents of the Oro Butterfly project, which include the Department of Environment
and Conservation (DEC), AusAID, and Oro Provincial Government (OPG). The office for the project
was set up in 1991 by the Nature Conservation Branch of DEC. In 1995 it was incorporated together
with OPG and AusAID.

The objective of this project is to maintain a viable population of the Queen Alexandra Birdwing
Butterfly (QABB) in its natural habitat.

The need for QABB conservation stem from three major reasons: QABB is a nationally protected
fauna under the Fauna Protection and Control Act of 1976, it is an endangered species, and as such is
placed under Appendix 1 of CITES (Convention in Trade of Endangered Species), and lastly, since
QABB is the largest birdwing butterfly in the world and only found in Oro Province, it has
considerable cultural value and is used in the provincial flag as an emblem.

HOW IS ICAD ADDRESSED IN THIS BUTTERFLY PROJECT ACTIVITY?

The important major underlying factor contributing to the project process relates to the landowner
participation and what the project can do to assist in a micro-economic level in maintaining the interest
of the landowners in conservation.

The project’s targeted economic incentives

I. Eco-tourism

One landowner within the Popondetta Plain area has already had his guesthouse completed, fully
funded by the Oro Conservation Project. The QABB population in this area is very healthy and has
been the centre of attraction. Other landowners within the adjacent areas will be encouraged to have
their areas conserved.

2. Water well
Another landowner within the same area (i.e. Popondetta Plains) has been assisted with a water well.
His area has been having a lot of problem with safe drinking water.

3. Cash incentives for landowner conservationist

The project has engaged twelve landowner conservationists from the Popondetta Plain and the Afore
Plateau who have decided to conserve their land, and collect general data on QABB occurrences in
their respective areas. The data collected are presented during the monitors’ workshop held once every
six months. During this workshop information on the progress of the project and other related issues
are discussed. At the end of such meetings, each landowner conservationist is paid a small allowance
as a token for his/her efforts in collecting the data.

4. Purchase of chillies

The project has gone in for small-scale chilli buying at Afore Station on the Managalase Plateau to
generate income for the people in the area. Coffee and chillies grow extremely well at Afore, but
marketing, especially for chillies is quite difficult. Transportation is one major problem hindering the
continuing coffee and chilli-growing activities. The road conditions to Afore are very bad and present
another compounding problem for the villagers in the area.

186

5. Rabbits for nutrition

Rabbit farming has just been introduced into the Afore area on a trial basis involving landowner
conservationists. The rabbits are bred and reared by the participating landowners to supplement the
lack of protein in the plateau area.

6. Training for insect traders

Identified insect collectors have been sent to Lae for insect trade course. It includes those identified
people from the QABB habitat areas. By trading other insects in the area, they may be able to conserve
the QABB.

Problems encountered regarding these economic incentives
The Oro Butterfly project cannot provide all landowners with guesthouses within the QABB ‘hot spot’
areas, since this over-servicing would not be viable.

As is the case throughout the country, land ownership is through traditional custom and hence poses
problems, especially in identifying the rightful owners of the land. The problems are left to the
disputing parties to resolve before approaching the Oro Butterfly Project Management Office.

Cargo cult mentality - there are perceptions within the communities that this project is a multi-million
kina project operated by a multi-national company. As such people think it must provide large-scale
investments overnight, especially on the Managalase Plateau area.

Manpower - lack of manpower to continually monitor the QABB. Many more landowners are needed
to register their interest to be involved in conservation activities.

AWARENESS-RAISING AS A TOOL FOR CONSERVATION

Awareness is one such tool used by the project to advocate conservation ideas and the economic
incentives within QABB conservation project. In this project awareness is being conducted through:

1. Radio broadcast - through Radio Northern, which has allocated 30 minutes air-time every Tuesday
evening at 8.00pm for the project. In the programme, project happenings and/or activities are
relayed to the people during such times;

2. Education Video Shows - Videos are shown to schools, villages and other interested parties.
Video contents include forest regeneration after logging activities and other educational materials
related to conservation;

3. School visits - Several schools within the Popondetta Plain area and the Managalase Plateau area,
especially at Afore have had nursery settings and habitat creation;

4. Provincial Authorities are normally being informed about the project’s activities.

THREATS TO THE SURVIVAL OF QABB

There are two major threats to QABB conservation efforts: the expansion of the oil palm plantations
within the Popondetta Plain and subsistence agriculture.

The Oil Palm Industry Corporation has introduced the concept of village oil palm or VOP to the
surrounding villages within the plain, which encourages villagers to provide blocks of their land for oil
palm cultivation. This is a popular approach and increasingly, a number of villagers have cleared their
forested areas for oil palm, despite the fact that much of the forests in the area are QABB habitats.

Although subsistence agriculture has been recognized, the human activities on available land have
raised concerns over QABB habitats. Subsistence agriculture is seen both at the Popondetta plains and
Afore areas to have slowly, but increasingly, reducing the QABB Habitats, especially regarding the
butterfly food plant and the butterfly populations.

187

SUSTAINABLE DEVELOPMENT PLANNING AND ICADS:
SOME IDEAS FROM THE OUTSIDE

Elspeth Young,
National Centre for Development Studies,
Australian National University,
Canberra, ACT 0200, Australia.

INTRODUCTION

ICAD projects, broadly defined as undertakings to, under agreement with traditional owners, manage
specific areas for biodiversity conservation and also for the achievement of the development objectives of
the local population, are complex operations. They demand a delicate balancing of environmental care and
understanding, set within a framework in which the people’s needs to participate in the cash economy are
seen as equally if not more important. Such a balance can only be achieved through effective consultation
and negotiation between all those with a stake in the project — the traditional owners and others with
interests in the use of the natural resources of the region; government and non-government agencies
concerned about conservation, economic development and service provision for the ICAD area; and donor
agencies providing financial and technical support for the project. Forging effective partnerships between
these groups is an essential mechanism for achieving results, which provide tangible benefits for the local
group and also meet biodiversity conservation goals. Not only do such approaches accord with those
generally recognised as most useful in implementing the ideals of sustainable development, a process
commonly defined as ‘development that meets the needs of the present without compromising the ability of
future generations to meet their own needs.’ World Commission on Environment and Development
(WCED, 1987); they are also, in the case of ICADs, occurring at the local level, where, because the
integration of environmental, economic and social elements of people’s lives is more readily understood,
sustainable development may be more effectively implemented.

WCED and others (e.g. Barbier, 1987) not only emphasise the need to balance and integrate environmental,
economic and social considerations in considering the desirability of a development project (see Fig. |
below); they also stress that the outcomes must be assessed in terms of both short-term and long-term costs
and benefits. This means that planning, whereby guidelines are established to help participants in a
development project move towards the goals they set for their present and future, is a vital process in
aiming for sustainable development. But while planning obviously plays a key role in the operation of
ICADs in Papua New Guinea it has not always occupied the central position. This paper, which briefly
considers ICAD type projects established in collaboration with indigenous peoples living in remote parts of
Australia, outlines how planning for sustainable development might provide an organisational framework
useful for improving the coordination and long-term definition of ICAD objectives.

188

Fig.1 The Sustainable Development Model
(adapted from Barbier, 1987)

PLANNING FOR SUSTAINABLE DEVELOPMENT

Sustainable development requires, as implied above, thinking about how environmental, economic and
social needs can be combined into a ‘win-win’ situation. Actual definition in advance of what that
situation might be is difficult and perhaps unrealistic. Achieving greater sustainability is more likely to
consist of progressive gains, which reflect the ability to constantly adapt to changing circumstances. It is
thus more useful to consider sustainable development as a way of thinking, a philosophy, rather than a
model, which can be systematically followed under many different circumstances. Planning for sustainable
development therefore needs to adopt a flexible approach, process rather than goal oriented. This type of
planning, sometimes called appropriate planning or action planning, is generally initiated by the local
community, involves a high degree of participation between the ‘outsiders’ and the community, and is
supportive of local ‘empowerment’. It contrasts markedly with conventional planning which is usually
externally initiated and organised, non-participatory, of limited value for local empowerment and aims at
the production of a clearly defined plan rather than the establishment of a process upon which local
communities can continue to build in the future.

AUSTRALIAN INDIGENOUS COMMUNITY PLANNING FOR SUSTAINABLE
DEVELOPMENT

The Issue

In the traditional lifestyle of indigenous Australians, as with other indigenous groups, society was
inextricably combined with its sustaining economy in an interdependent relationship with natural resources
derived through customary rights to use lands and seas. In many ways it accorded closely with the
contemporary ideals of sustainable development (Young, 1995). However, because of changes in
population size and distribution, alienation from their customary lands and their changing priorities for
contemporary and future lifestyles indigenous Australians (Aborigines and Torres Strait Islanders) can no
longer follow the same paths as before. As a result, while their culture has proved surprisingly resilient and
much knowledge has been passed on from older people to today’s younger generations, many elements of
their traditional systems of ecological knowledge (TEK) have already disappeared. These losses are highly
significant in undermining overall biodiversity. Alternative approaches to resource management, using a

189

community development are needed. Appropriate planning is likely to be a fundamental process in such
approaches. The following example examines research ‘in planning for sustainable development in
Australian Aboriginal communities. Co-researchers on this project are Drs Helen Ross (CRES) and
Jocelyn Davies (University of Adelaide).

The setting

Five rural indigenous groups, socio-economically and environmentally diverse and located in different
parts of Australia, agreed to participate in the study because they believed that an appropriate planning
exercise would be useful to them. They included the following (see Fig.2): -

2. Wallaga Lake Koori Village, a diverse community largely living on a small area of land on the south
