Contrasts between these case-studies include:-

Environmental - coastal, temperate and tropical (Wallaga Lake and Broome); inland arid/semi-arid
(Irrwanyere, Warupunju); inland tropical savanna (Narinyin);

Social and Cultural - Single language group, residential community (Warupunju); single language group,
multiple communities (Irrwanyere, Narinyin); multiple language groups, single community (Wallaga
Lake); multiple language groups, urban community (Broome);

Economic base/support - Cattle Station/ Community Development Employment Project (CDEP)!
(Narinyin, Warupunju); CDEP/local government services (Wallaga Lake); urban (including some CDEP)
Irrwanyere, Broome;

Political/Institutional (including customary resource tenure, State/Territory) - Northern Territory,
Aboriginal freehold land (Warupunju, some Irrwanyere); South Australia, negotiated land management
agreement (Irrwanyere); Western Australia, pastoral lease, excision (Narinyin); Western Australia urban,
Native Title negotiations (Broome); New South Wales Aboriginal land (Wallaga Lake);

Political/Institutional (Aboriginal organisation) - Regional Land Councils (Warupunju, Narinyin,
Broome); local Aboriginal organisation (Irrwanyere, Wallaga lake); and combination of regional and local
organisation (all groups);

Human resources available to enable meaningful participation in planning process - Well developed
education/skill and exposure to understanding of issues (Irrwanyere); limited formal education/skill and
exposure to understanding of issues (Narinyin, Warupunju).

The Research Process

Each case-study followed a loosely-defined common research plan which was designed to leave scope for
variations suited to the particular circumstances of each group. It included the following elements, all
aimed at identifying community aspirations, resources, strategies and options (see Fig.3):-

« determination of the community’s willingness to be involved in the study and discussion on how more
appropriate planning might best assist people’s needs (negotiation, choice etc);

= workshops and discussions to determine the present needs and future goals of different groups in the
community (participation, vision, values); ASPIRATIONS

= compilation of information on the resources available in the community to enable these goals to be
attained (long-term strategies); RESOURCES, STRATEGIES

» identification of key resources not available, and how to obtain these (medium-term strategies),
RESOURCES, STRATEGIES, OPTIONS

«  jdentification of goal priorities - which goals to aim for first, and hence which resources to seek
immediately (short-term strategies). OPTIONS

' Community Development Employment Projects established in indigenous communities in all parts of
Australia, use bulk funding from normal social security entitlements (unemployment benefit) to provide
wage jobs for people who would otherwise be without work. Actual jobs and decisions on who will hold
them are determined by community consultation groups.

ASPIRATIONS

RESOURCES

STRATEGIES

OPTIONS

Fig.3 The Planning Framework

The characteristics of these components of the planning process varied markedly between the five groups.
Irrwanyere and Wallaga Lake, for example, followed the above plan fairly systematically. But the
Warapunju mob, faced with finding practical ways of reoccupying their customary lands to which they had
recently received legally recognised tenure, were interested in a more fundamental process — visiting sites
which were sufficiently rich in natural resources (water, bush foods, wildlife) to provide at least semi-
permanent support for camping groups; and deciding on how their interests in natural biodiversity
conservation could be balanced with economic development through cattle enterprises and also with social
development through improved access to schools, health services and communication systems. Unlike
Irrwanyere and Wallaga Lake they did not see the production of a written plan as a priority; nor did they,
with their more limited levels of formal education, want to be directly involved in data collection and
processing. The Narinyin also participated differently. While they were interested in reaching the goals set ©
by each of their small communities they also had broader concerns. They also wanted the wider
community to openly accept their claims as prime stakeholders and participants in overall planning for this
physically remote region.

Some Findings

Despite these differences the study identified some important common elements which provide a basis for
more widely applicable models of appropriate sustainable development planning for indigenous people.
These included the following:-

1. All groups agreed that development should be approached holistically, as a process, which integrates
people’s natural resource base (e.g. land), with their socio-cultural background and their economic
base. Planning must occur within such a framework. Irrwanyere’s plan, for example, clearly
illustrates this perception (Fig.4).

2. Planning appropriate to such development must be based on community participation and focus on
people’s visions for the future and their ideas of how to realise these. This ‘bottom-up’ approach must be
combined with understanding of the broader framework of resource allocation and market system, which
affects them (e.g. through relevant government institutions and private sector).

heakhn and wetara

Fig.4 Irrwanyere people’s relationship to country

193

3. Appropriate planning must deal not only with short-term needs but must set these within a longer-term
context. Planning solely as a reaction to immediate circumstances and political expediency will not benefit
people in the long run.

4, Planning which is undertaken with full participation by the community, both in determining what it will
cover, how and by whom it will be carried out is more likely to be of lasting benefit because people are
then able to continue the process themselves. Plans produced under such a participatory system, and
owned and controlled by the community are much more likely to be effective.

5. Successful community-based planning requires a straightforward system or framework with which the
people can readily identify, and which they find convenient to use. The four-step process we developed
(aspirations, resources, options, and strategies) works well, and is far simpler than the conventional
planning systems generally promoted in indigenous communities.

IS APPROPRIATE SUSTAINABLE DEVELOPMENT PLANNING USEFUL FOR ICADS IN
PAPUA NEW GUINEA?

The methodology adopted in this study could be relevant for indigenous resource management in ICAD
projects in Papua New Guinea. The broad organisational framework, involving the identification of each
group’s aspirations, resources, strategies and options, could provide a setting for better coordination of
more specific efforts aimed at plant, insect, bird and wildlife conservation; or the development of
commercial enterprise development from local resources. Thus, for example, this type of approach
explicitly links the important scientific research undertaken in the Crater Mountain and Oro Butterfly
ICAD projects with the socio-economic development aspirations of the local people; similarly the emphasis
on participatory approaches adopted in the Bismarck-Ramu ICAD can, under such a planning framework,
overtly incorporate both biodiversity conservation and socio-economic needs. And, because adaptability,
both in time and space, is a key feature of this planning process it can be applied in very diverse situations.
Ultimately the advantage of this alternative approach is that it is driven by the local communities and can
give people the opportunities to enhance whatever skills they feel are needed to take greater control over
their own futures.

ICAD MANAGEMENT AND IMPLEMENTATION:
THE KAMIALI EXPERIENCES

Sasa Zibe-Kokino & Bill Girard
Village Development Trust,
P. O. Box 2397, Lae, Morobe Province

INTRODUCTION

In this paper the Village Development Trust (VDT) will discuss lessons learnt and experiences gained in
establishing the Kamiali ICAD Project. There are two parts in this paper. I will present the first, dealing
with some background on the area and some comments on conservation in the PNG context, and our
Director of Planning and Development, Bill Girard, will present an overview of VDT’s strategies and
initiatives.

Let me begin by saying that there is no blueprint available to establish a successful ICAD (Integrated
Conservation and Development) Project in Papua New Guinea. That is the reason why we are here today
to share experiences of the Kamiali ICAD with you.

BACKGROUND TO THE KAMIALI ICAD

Geographical Information

The Kamiali Conservation Project (KCP) is located on the Huon Gulf coast, 60 kilometres south of Lae
past the historical site of Salamaua. KCP is located within a high priority zone as identified in the PNG
Conservation Needs Assessment. The Project involves 47,000 hectares consisting of 29,000 hectares of
terrestrial area and 18,000 hectares of marine area. This is part of a larger region (approximately 250,000
hectares) starting from the Lasanga group of Islands on the Huon Gulf and extending to Lake Trist 1800 m
above sea level. It comprises many different vegetation types: lowland tropical rain forest and tree
swamps, low and high montane forest, Savannah grassland and dipterocarp forests, There are extensive
areas of approximately 250,000 hectares defined what was originally known as the Lasanga Project and
included the five villages of Buso, Kui, Siboma, Paiewa, and Lababia. However, the task of working with
this much area and this many villages became unmanageable and the project was later scaled down to its
present size of 47,000 hectares and the single village of Lababia.

Large Scale Forestry Development

The neighboring villages of Lababia (Buso, Siboma & Paiewa) have been subjected to conventional
logging for the last three decades. One would ask why the villagers allow the loggers to continue to exploit
their resources? Research and analysis have demonstrated that timber provides the major current projected
use value of Papua New Guinea’s biodiversity. Alternatives to timber are currently small ticket items,
although there is potential to find new uses for existing products, and to establish markets both locally and
internationally for new products. The major problem is that conservation initiatives are in competition with
the exploitative development alternatives. This reinforces the need to present a balance perspective and
provide adequate information to land owner, on the options available to them for the developments of their
forest resources. The major conceptual message is the sustainability of forest resources whilst considering
the impacts of environment, social and economic development issues.

We know that, landowners would like to have their forest resources developed. They would like to gain
economic benefits (change in Socio-economics) from their land and resources. We know that, they are also
concerned about the protection and care for their land, resources and environment. However, they do not
have the necessary information on the best options and technical know-how to do it, that is, most forms of

195

development. This is the dilemma in which the landowners are caught. The need to provide balance
information in order to make an informed decision is vital.

CONSERVATION IN THE PNG CONTEXT

Protected areas for conservation purposes initiated by the state of PNG in the sixties and seventies have
failed to achieve their objectives. Land holders were not adequately consulted and did not participate in the
decision making process; thus the concept was indeed difficult for land holders to grasp. As a consequence
most protected areas in the country were abandoned due to land holders moving into the protected areas for
traditional uses of their land.

The PNG way of protecting resources is different from the western concept, and must be consistent with
Papua New Guinea ways of using the land and forests. The planning process must begin with a full
consultation and bottom-up planning where all segments of the community are involved. In the awareness
process the concept of Conservation in Development must be thoroughly defined.

I understand there are various NGOs and government agents undertaking some good models. Some are
successful and others have failed. These can be seen as good lessons learnt and successful models can be
duplicated elsewhere in the country. However, the local people must take initiatives themselves and seek
alternative avenues to develop and manage their natural resources sustainably. Most of the time land
owners expect development/change to come to them rather than going out and seeking it.

We have found that, conservation or ICAD concepts and programmes are being received with mixed
reactions. Although, villagers express concern about the destruction of their rain forest, they still would
like to have their forest resources developed. They would like to receive economic benefits from their land
and resources. Thus we have to provide balanced information to enable them to choose the type of
development that is appropriate for them.

Long-term conservation cannot be achieved unless landowners see conservation to be in their interest (by
meeting their demands for social and economic development). The challenge is to reconcile development
objectives with conservation management. As is argued repeatedly in many studies, unless landowners
receive tangible benefits from conservation interventions, they are unlikely in the long run to allocate land
to conservation purposes; and they will often opt for exploitative forms of land use that threaten
biodiversity. Papua New Guinea’s conservation drive is currently aimed at developing a nexus between
development and conservation objectives, by harnessing direct productive use values. Thus, the strategy
aims at integrating conservation and development objectives to attain a more secure conservation footing
but most of all bring a tangible and long-term benefit to the land owners.

Developing a Management Ethic

Much of VDT’s work over the last three years has focused on developing ‘management’ as a discipline,
which is a prerequisite to effectively achieving any stated objectives. I think we would all agree that
without a firm foundation in the distinctions and practices of management most desired objectives
(regardless of the field of endeavor) would elude the individual or group intending the particular outcome.
And with development work, even that of NGOs, good intentions simply do not ‘cut it? any more, (and
probably good intentions never have, although we may have been guilty of kidding ourselves and donors
that noble and worthy objectives are enough). VDT has certainly found this to be true with donors and
other supporters who are feeling their own pressures of finance and the need to demonstrate results. This is
also true in our association with villagers who are demanding tangible benefits from their work with
outside organisations.

We have found that management skills are best developed by ‘stepping into the field’ with something
ultimately at stake. Our experience has shown that people have to learn to be innovative, to think
creatively, and to seize opportunities that present themselves — especially when they are in the midst of
breakdowns. We must confess that this adopted strategy of VDTs — focusing on the development of
personal and organisational management, wasn’t a function of a lengthy analysis and strategic planning and

196

much of what we are sharing with you today is coming from the benefit of hindsight. Our focus on
management was really adopted because we recognised a state of emergency — ‘either swim or sink’. In
early 1993 VDT found itself in what some may have considered the enviable position of having the two-
year support of a major international funder to engage in sustainable development initiatives within an
ecologically important region of the country. Although we were appreciative of the opportunity and the
support being provided it was quickly recognised that we were in a very precarious position. VDT was
now involved with five villages throughout a region of some two hundred thousand hectares, supposedly
accountable for delivering many facets of a very large project, having as its overall objective:

The protection of endangered wildlife habitats and natural, forest and marine resources by providing
sustainable economic development alternatives to large-scale logging

Needless to say, a huge task for any organization, especially an emerging (three year old) NGO. No one is
to blame for the breakdown that followed. These were simply a function of taking on something large and
unprecedented; quite frankly the breakdowns ‘come with the territory’. I personally want to acknowledge
Sasa, Bing, other individuals within VDT and WWF who were the driving forces behind staying with this
project in the early years when the going was particularly tough. Sasa has explained to you that the project
was eventually scaled down to include one village Lababia, and an area of approximately 47, 000 hectares
that is now referred as Kamiali. The scaling down was initiated primarily by WWF, and came after the
recognition of two vital facts: VDT had a very limited organisational capacity and there had been a
misinterpretation of the commitment of four of the original villages in the project. It was later discovered
they were engaged in unsustainable logging activities. The village of Lababia as far as we have been able
to tell (despite some misinformation to the contrary) never received serious overtures from large-scale
logging enterprises. So there was not the presence of a logging company to deal with at Kamiali; and the
villagers said they ‘wanted development’ and were prepared to look closely at what VDT had to offer. This
gave VDT and its partner a special opportunity even though work with the other four villages did not
appear to be workable — at least for the immediate future.

After the scaling down it became apparent that we needed to make a concerted effort to develop a strong
management ethic and build capacity within the organization and within our full range of projects
(including the Kamiali Project). Without doing this, even a downsized conservation project would result in
another misused opportunity. In short, VDT had to get its own house in order and work better as a team.
As progressive steps were taken in this we then had to discover methods of working more effectively with
outside groups such as the villagers and other organisations. We had to undertake to support villagers to
managing their own activities within their own groups, organisations, and community. It has certainly not
been an easy task and the work has really barely begun. However, what is significant is that we have begun
addressing these micro issues and did not overlook them in the interest of the more widely discussed and
popular macro issues of science, biodiversity, and sustainable development. We have accomplished some
things in this process but there is still breakdown — which is evidence that we are in fact working on the
important issues. Misunderstandings still need to be addressed and big challenges lie ahead of us. I would
like to share some of the accomplishment, breakdown, and challenges with you in short time that we have
remaining.

THE KAMIALI ICAD

Emphasising short term projects

Rather than developing and implementing a comprehensive land use plan or conservation and development
strategies we have undertaken a series of short-term projects, most of them relatively small. They have
allowed us to begin cultivating some of the skills of effective management. The project has given us
forums for working with others (including the villagers) and they have offered us experience at planning a
project and seeing it through to completion. They have also offered people the opportunity to experience
the satisfaction that comes from setting out to do something, and then actually doing it. We have had the
opportunity to confront the unexpected challenges — those things that could only become apparent while
working towards a particular objective—and to learn that we can deal with them effectively to resolve
them. These projects include awareness workshops, working with village committees, the initiating of the

197

ranger training programme, creating a buffer zone to help protect the leatherback turtles, field trips to other
guesthouses and conservation areas, a drum oven project, a fishing project, a portable sawmill project,
getting the area declared as Wildlife Management Area, a village water system, and the building of VDT’s
new Kamiali Training Centre and Guest House. It could be argued that this patchwork approach might not
be ideal for implementing a comprehensive plan that ensures conservation. But we feel that we have built
some valuable foundations from which future work can now be launched.

Demonstrating Trust and a Commitment to Results

The villagers made it clear to us that they were looking for ways to generate cash income. Lababia, as with
many villages of Papua New Guinea, could be said to be subsistence wealthy but cash poor. They have
access to good land for growing vegetables, their fishing practices assure them adequate protein, there are
minimal population pressures and there is a dependable supply of water. However, their ability to earn
cash is very limited due in great part to the transport and logistical challenges of living 60 km down the
coast from Lae. We had the need of the villagers to earn cash and undertook to find ways of working with
them that would result in this happening. Not all income they subsequently earned could be linked directly
with conservation practices but we didn’t feel that this was the most important issue at first. What we
wanted to do was build trust with them and demonstrate that working with our organization and taking a
sustainable approach to development could put cash into their hands; not as handouts or cargo but as
payments for the goods and services they provide. This did pose some challenges. Many villagers felt that
by allowing VDT to come into the area we were simply going to ‘bring development to them’ which meant
we were going to bring cargo. When they saw and heard that we were getting funding to carry out our
work, some people were very upset upon realising that they weren’t simply going to get a piece of the
overall funding package because they let us come onto their land. We did have many conversations about
this — some of them very heated.

AILS BNET

We kept demonstrating that we would give money in exchange for valuable services provided to VDT and
its partners AND that we were not going to pay them for doing things that would be considered part of their
own community projects. For instance we do pay villagers for assisting in the construction of VDT’s
training centre and guest house but we do not pay them to build their own village water supply system. We
do not pay villagers to be rangers and construct a walk track through the WMA because this is part of their
training to ultimately manage the WMA, but people who are in the ranger training programme are some of
the people we employ when VDT needs assistance in conducting one of its trainings or when a visitor to
the area wishes the services of the guide for a few days.

Other Forms of Integration

The term ICAD emphasises the desire to preserve biodiversity on the one hand, while embracing the need
to promote development, that contributes to the income level and quality of life of the residents, on the
other. Such a commitment to integration serves as a foundation of VDT’s actions at Kamiali. This has
resulted in our taking steps towards introducing two potential long-term sustainable development

possibilities namely eco-forestry and eco-tourism.

However, we have also been committed to integration in another fashion — namely integrating VDT’s
other activities as an NGO with the development of income-earning opportunities for the residents of
Kamiali. For instance as early as 1994 we had already secured funding from the National Forestry and
Conservation Action Plan (NFCAP) for a training centre to be built somewhere in Morobe Province. When
our original site at Bukawa village was abandoned after torrential floods in September 1994 washed away
the initial construction there, we decided to construct the facility within the Kamiali area as a complement
to the ICAD project. We entered into a 30-year lease with the villagers, paying them an annual rental. We
employed them in the construction of the building, and we are employing them in the operation of the
facility. In short, VDT’s ‘business’ of providing eco-forestry training to villages, other NGOs, and other
organisations, has presented new income-earning possibilities for the people of Lababia. This type of
integration is part of our eco-tourism plans. Rather than relying totally upon income derived from eco-
tourists and researchers visiting the facility, the numerous courses delivered by us (with their associated
budgets for lodging and part time labor required to conduct these courses) will complement the other room
revenues; this should make financial viability more attainable.

198

The commitment to integration will probably continue with VDT’s plan to use the Kamiali site as a base
for carrying out eco-forestry extension work and maybe as a storage yard for eco-timber bought from
villages along the Morobe coast; this will probably create a need for further services and employable labour
that could be provided by Kamiali residents.

Consistent with integrating the different initiatives of VDT into the Kamiali Project has been the utilisation
of multiple donors. We have often secured funding for specific activities with the ICAD. Some of VDT’s
donors have preferred to fund education and awareness, others capacity-building, some are interested in
eco-forestry, and still others prefer more conventional projects such as village water supply systems. The
multiple donor approach can have its disadvantages especially when it comes to covering certain costs that
can’t be nicely slotted to specific short-term projects. However, since 1995 we have quite frankly
experienced challenges in securing multi-year funding that could include the cost of scientific staff and the
carrying out of an extensive biodiversity survey. Therefore, developing creative ways of blending VDT’s
different funding sources has allowed us to generate some consistency of effort over time within Kamiali.
This has enabled us to stay true to the long-term commitment we made to the community. Fortunately, we
have recently obtained three-year funding that is allowing us to hire the much needed scientific staff,
conduct the biodiversity survey, and undertake a more comprehensive approach in the future.

More on the Training Centre and Guest House

The significance of this new facility at Kamiali and the size of this particular initiative warrant a few
additional comments. As mentioned earlier the buildings have been constructed on land leased by VDT
from the villagers for a 30-year period. This may be an unconventional approach for an NGO. It reflects,
in part, our long-term commitment to provide eco-forestry training services and the multi-year funding we
have secured from donors to provide this training. WDT is responsible for running the facility and is
training village residents — both men and women, to take on progressively more responsibility for its
operation. Certainly the concepts of a land lease and VDT owning a building that sits on village land was
not easy to convey to the villagers. But we feel the landowners have become comfortable with the ideas
and despite some people still not being sure what this all means, we have been able to continue to maintain
the support of the clan that has traditional use rights of the land being leased. We feel we have been able to
get this support because they are receiving economic benefits through both the lease payments and the
presence of an operating facility. For instance over the last eighteen months approximately K10, 000 has
been paid to village residents. This has probably involved more than 100 different individuals and has
included payments for sawn timber and other materials for the building; wages to people assisting in the
construction, and payments for some of the miscellaneous services required by VDT and visitors to the
area. In a village of approximately 500 people (around 70 families) with an average household income of
no more than K500 per year, this figure of K10, 000 could be considered a significant injection of cash into
the village economy. Furthermore, our assistance in establishing the first drum oven in the village — a
small project with some of the women about a year ago — is an example of how some of the residents can
further share in the flow of cash after the payments have been made by VDT.

The significance of VDT’s presence at Kamiali and the benefits being experienced by the villagers was
highlighted when disputes with some members of the building crew resulted in construction being halted on
a number of occasions throughout the last eighteen months. The sense of urgency expressed by the
villagers each time for us to resume construction, not just from those directly working on the site, reflected
the impact of the temporary delays on village cash flow. Now of course, with much of the construction
completed we and they face the challenge of maintaining the cashflow that they have become accustomed
to. But we are confident that we can match and perhaps even surpass these income levels by paying
villagers to work in the facility, buying vegetables and fish to feed our trainees and guests, and by our
agreement to pay K2 per guest per night into a community fund that the villagers can use for special
projects they want to undertake. Also, now that our organisation is better established physically at Kamiali,
we are entering into discussions with some of the residents to cut and sell sawn timber on a continuing
commercial basis. VDT plans to stockpile the purchased timber at our training centre site and then ship
economical quantities up to our eco-timber yard that has been established with Timber Industry Training
College in Lae.

199

We now stand on the threshold of a new future for the community. We have built some foundations on
which to undertake more comprehensive and long term initiatives, especially those linked directly to
conservation. We have built a physical and psychological base from which to work; and we have
assembled a good team of villagers, technical and scientific staff, and this exists all within an atmosphere
of cooperation and realistic excitement. And over the years we have been fortunate to receive the valued
support of outside resource experts. Our presentation today has focused upon VDT’s internal
developments as they relate to the work at Kamiali. However, it would be a mistake to suppose that we did
it all ourselves. Over the years we have relied upon the continued support, recommendations, and input of
many NGOs, government organisations, overseas donors and technical experts, and other individuals who
have a genuine commitment to empowering the progress of sustainable development within Papua New
Guinea. Many of you are here with us today; so in closing may we take this opportunity to thank you for
your contributions and we look forward to its continuing in the years ahead.

DESIGN AND IMPLEMENTATION OF AN INTERDISCIPLINARY MONITORING PROGRAM
IN THE
CRATER MOUNTAIN WILDLIFE MANAGEMENT AREA ICAD PROJECT

Paul Igag, Paul Hukahu and Robert Bino,
Research and Conservation Foundation of Papua New Guinea
P.O. Box 1261, Goroka
Eastern Highlands Province, Papua New Guinea

INTRODUCTION

The Crater Mountain Wildlife Management Area (CMWMA) is an integrated conservation and
development (ICAD) project, which is testing the hypothesis that the establishment of viable business
enterprises which depend on the site biodiversity will lead to conservation of biodiversity and
sustainable use of the natural resources by the landowners who occupy the management area. The
project is a collaborative effort between the Research and Conservation Foundation of PNG (RCF), the
Wildlife Conservation Society (WCS) of the New York Zoological Society and the Kimi and Pawaian
landowners of the Crater Mountains. The Kimis are a highland group of people, dwelling in static
communities with activities based around gardening and coffee production (Pearl 1994). They go on
occasional hunting forays into their traditional hunting grounds. In contrast the Pawaians are a lowland
group of people who lead a semi-nomadic existence. They thrive on sago harvests with minimal
subsistence agriculture and extensive hunting practices.

Location and Site Features

The CMWMA straddles three provincial boundaries namely Eastern Highlands, Simbu and Gulf
Provinces, and encompasses approximately 2700 square kilometres in total area (see Johnson’s Fig.1b
at page 137 in this volume). The area accommodates a steep altitudinal gradient (range: 70m - 3000+
m) making it incredibly rich in biological diversity ie. 221 species of birds, with 59 endemics and 84
mammal species with 15 endemics. The PNG Conservation Needs Assessment has identified the
CMWMaA as a priority area for conservation (Beehler 1992). Major vegetation types include: primary
rainforest, subalpine scrub forest, lowland alluvial rainforest and upland grassland areas.

Conceptual Model

The conceptual model (see Fig. 1, p. 202) is a gross representation of what we perceive as constituting
the conditions, situations and ‘pressure systems’ which drive the mechanics of the on-the-ground
scenario which ultimately influences the biodiversity in the CMWMA. The conceptual model is not a
final product and it is subject to change. Take note that the target condition of the conceptual model is
biodiversity conservation. The conceptual model has been an indispensable tool in that it assists
project implementation to be more focussed because there are innumerable things to pursue. With the
conceptual model, we identify where our intervention points are and that is where we place our
priorities, so that staff know exactly what to do come Monday morning, so to speak. Without this,
there is an tendency to become disillusioned by the vast array of opportunities available to the project.
In the course of duty, one may be inclined to attempt everything, resulting in the end in the
achievement of nothing.

Objectives of the Project

The CMWMA project has four principal objectives, which govern its implementation. These
objectives are derived from the intervention factors in the conceptual model known by the acronym
SMART, which means that each of the objectives should be Specific, Measurable, Achievable, Realistic
and Timebound. The CMWMA project realizes and values the importance of moving in rhythm with
the pace and ability of the landowners, who are the key players in the game. We are working with
communities who possess very low literacy levels. Therefore we do not push the landowners but we
utilize these objectives for the effective governance of our project’s activities in relation to our major
donor’s (Biodiversity Conservation Network) time-frame requirements, i.e. a three-year period.

BIODIVERSITY

202

Objective 1: Increase the average annual per capita income of clans over the next three years from the
establishment of locally owned research and eco-tourism enterprises in the WMA

Objective 2: Over the next three years, increase the level of understanding and skills of community
residents who work in the research and eco-tourism enterprises in the WMA

Objective 3: Over the next three years, increase the number of decisions and actions which integrate
the results of enterprise, biological and socio-economic monitoring programs into the working
management plan.

Objective 4: Over the next three years , increase national involvement and national human resource
exchange within the WMA as teachers, trainers and consultants working towards conservation natural
resources in the WMA.

IMPORTANCE OF MONITORING

The ICAD concept assumes that by integrating conservation and development in close collaboration
with the landowners who inhabit and utilize the management area, the state of the biological diversity
can be managed or improved. The CMWMA project is devising and implementing specific monitoring
tools in the areas of socio-economic management and biological circumstances in order to measure the
merits of this assumption.

It is hoped that after a valid period of monitoring, the project can be able to assess whether its
facilitated interventions have improved, regulated or degraded the biological diversity of the CMWMA.
Only then can we legitimately prove or disprove the hypothesis that ‘the establishment of viable
business enterprises that depend on the site biodiversity will lead to the conservation of biodiversity’.
The state of the biological diversity as a product of the ICAD process will be determined through the
biological monitoring devices.

With the intervention of economic incentives, which have direct linkages to biodiversity conservation,
a risk factor immediately comes into play as well. That is the potential that excessive and unguided
economic developments would result in a gross divergence from the intended goal of the project. The
socio-economic monitoring protocol is also aimed to meter the progress of the project’s
implementation with respect to the project’s first and second objectives, given above.

Not only are we intervening with economic incentives, but we are also embarking on the instillation of
management stewardship instincts into the nucleus of the local population with each CMWMA
community we are working with. This is accomplished through workshops, management plans,
formulation of management area laws, and other, informal means. The management monitoring regime
is devised to register landowners decisions made independently of the external collaborators which
favour conservation. This is consistent with the project’s third objective, and would also reflect the
bounds of the project’s impact on decision-making at the local level.

Constraints

There are several constraints in implementing the interdisciplinary monitoring program. In particular,
we might note difficulties raised by low literacy levels, traditional rivalry within clans, and culturally
taboo areas.

BIOLOGICAL MONITORING

Who does the Monitoring

The project’s field biologists based at each field site take the lead role in implementing the biological
monitoring program. Emphasis is also placed on training local observers who are interested and show
potential in becoming engaged in the monitoring program. Since the literacy level is low, we focus on
the limited output of community school leavers and people who attend our literacy training courses.
The locals who are trained are referred to as Trained Local Observers (TLOs) and when they get past a
satisfactory level of proficiency they are certified.

Information on the Biological Monitoring Tools
Below is an overview of the nine biological monitoring tools currently in active use or planned for the
CMWMaA project. They were assembled as a result of consultation with various experts.

|

Bio 1: Bird Flyover Counts. We record from permanent stations which have a respectable opening in
the canopy (e.g. airstrip, playground or river bank) exposing an adequate field of view of the sky.
There are two monitoring sessions a week in which we conduct an early morning (6:00am - 7:00am)
bout and a late afternoon (5:00pm - 6:00pm) bout. These bouts are selected randomly and can be on
the same day or on different days of the week. Long range movements are targeted as opposed to local
movements. We record flight direction, number of birds and height of birds in flight from the point of
observation. The reasoning is that:
1. This will provide a means of standardised monitoring continuum to periodically update our bird
species checklist.
2. It will record daily and seasonal long-rage movements of birds so we can document where such
species are heading to feed and to roost, and over the long term establish such movement patterns.
3. The records will provide a basis for analytical comparisons.

Bio 2: Bird Point Count. Two systems of transects are set up, one in a disturbed habitat and one in an
intact and pristine habitat. The transect systems are set up such that they diverge from each other so
that in the process of conducting surveys, data is not duplicated. The system of transects are 1.5 km
long with 10 stations at 150m intervals. The idea is to walk to each station and spend about 10 minutes
at each station doing an audio censusing and identification of birds i.e. do a bird count and
identification by bird calls. This survey is meant to be performed twice a year. The reasoning behind
this decision is that such a survey provides:

1. Another means of a standardized continuum to periodically update our bird species checklist.

2. Acontrol and an impact site to draw analytical comparisons after a valid period of monitoring.

Bio 3: Hunting Card. Accompany hunting parties to the bush (without arousing suspicion) and
documenting what species of animals are being hunted, how many and for what purposes/reasons
specific forms of wildlife are being hunted for example, bride price, ceremonies, customary
obligations, etc.. A difficult survey to conduct without causing suspicion and dismantling the trust and
cooperation of hunters and clans. This is conducted for the following reasons:

1. Asameans to continually update the mammal species checklist.

2. Asa way to gauge the hunting pressure on specific faunal taxa to provide basis for management

plans with the landowners themselves and for analytical comparisons for the project.

Bio 4: School diet survey. Interact casually with students at CAWMA community schools with
assistance from teachers to find out what house-holds are eating in terms of hunted wildlife. A difficult
survey to conduct without arousing suspicion and losing the cooperation of students. The reasons
being:

1. Another means (but less direct) to document the hunting pressure.

2. Records will provide a basis for analytical comparisons.

Bio 5: Wildlife export. Work with airline agent based at each village airstrip in recording the species
and quantities of wildlife going out of the villages via the airstrips for sale at town markets or for other
purposes, e.g., gifts or bride price, compensation, etc.. The airline agent is expected to fill out a form
for every plane departure from each respective village airstrip. The reason for this is:

1. To provide a means to gauge and monitor the pressure by wildlife trade.

2. Records will also provide a basis for analytical comparisons.

Bio 6: Captive Wildlife. Conduct quarterly house-hold surveys at village hauslains on wildlife kept as

pets or reared for purposes such as subsistence consumption, bride price, gifts, etc. Once registered

each animal item is tracked till it is killed, sold or given away. The reasons are:

1. A means to gauge and monitor the pressure on the hunting and removal of cassowary chicks in
particular from the wild for domestication.

2. Records will provide a basis for analytical comparisons.

Bio 7: Use of Plant Species. A responsive survey is conducted every time a new house is constructed
or when an old house is repaired. The survey tracks the quantities and types (species) of vegetative
materials used in house construction and repairs. The reasons for this are:

1. A means to monitor and gauge the pressure on targeted plant species for construction purposes.

2. Records will provide a basis for management plans with landowners and for analytical

comparisons.

204

/
|
|
|
|

Bio 8: Mega Fauna Transect This survey is designed to be conducted on the same transects used in
Bio 2 (Bird Point Counts). After the bird point count at the final station at 1.5 km the monitor would
walk the transect back to the starting point. During this walk the monitor will record actual sightings or
look out for signs and evidences of species of mega fauna ie cassowary, wallabies, tree kangaroos,
cuscuses, rodents etc. The reason for this is:

1. A means to gauge animal abundance/presence mostly by an indirect method.

2. Records to provide the basis for analytical comparisons with pertinence to a control site versus an

impact site.

Bio 9: Incidental Wildlife Observation. This survey is meant to record any wildlife sighting by

anyone. A form is deposited at CMWMA guest houses and other such places so visitors, project

personnel and landowners alike are encouraged to record any interesting observation on wildlife. The

reason for this is:

1. An extra means to capture data utilizing visitors to the CMWMaA or landowners who may have an
eye or ear for wildlife.

ENTERPRISE DEVELOPMENT OR SUSTAINABLE INCOME GENERATING ACTIVITIES
IN A WILDLIFE MANAGEMENT AREA (WMA)

In the Crater Mountain Wildlife Management Area the people, with assistance from RCF and US Peace
Corps volunteers, have set up three types of business enterprises. They are tourism, handicrafts and
research. Tourism includes development of guesthouses and trails for adventure and nature seekers
while artifacts production is geared for all types of visitors. Research includes the development of
research stations to accommodate researchers who go onto the Crater Mountains to study various fauna
and flora.

These businesses though in their initial stage are doing well. Handicraft sales, being one of the major
sources of income in the Crater Mountain, bagged K35,000 for Herowana village in four years ending
last year, 1996, whilst Haia and Maimafu villages sold K800 and K1400 respectively in the first six
months of this year.: There is opportunity to maintain this at a rate that will ensure that the artists get a
continuous income from handicrafts.

For the research business, Haia village got more than K6000 for the first six months of this year. The
Management Committee gets 10% of the fees, while the rest goes to individual workers. The workers
include carriers, guides and maintenance workers. Money for the Committee is used to pay for their
allowances, stationery and reinvestment in their businesses.

The tourism business, though not as polished as yet, has received very good comments from a handful
of tourists who have gone into the Crater Mountains.

In addition, the people have started and are interested to venture into other businesses. Some of these
businesses are coffee, peanuts, livestock and spices. Although coffee is growing well in the area,
especially Herowana and Maimafu, and there is fertile land available to raise other cash crops, there are
still some constraints to successful business development in the Crater Mountains.

RCF is now assisting people with these problems by providing capital for infrastructural development,
find markets for their products and provide technical advice for efficient management of their
businesses.

On the whole the people's income has been raised to a reasonable level, resulting in some spin-off
businesses. For example, in the case of a landowner clan business group of Haia, they have reinvested
money from the guest house sleep fees to start other businesses. To date they have started a trade store
and have put some money aside to start a fish project. They started the store business with K330 in
February 1997 and at the end of April 1997 they had made a net profit of K21.70, bringing their current
assets to K611.70. They decided to use the profits from the trade store and the guest house to expand
their current businesses and diversify into related businesses.

i
|
i
|

Another illustration of the increase in the level of people's income is the increase in the rate of bride
price payments. In Haia before the project, people paid K200 to K500 for a bride. Today they are

paying K1000 to K1500.

Whilst money is flowing into the community from these enterprises as well as other sources, it is quite
difficult to monitor or evaluate the effect of the rise in income and training on the people's lifestyle and
therefore their activities on the environment. As such, we have come up with a monitoring system.

Socio Economic Monitoring Tools:

SE1: Business Register. This sheet is for listing the details of a business.

SE2A: Income statement. The income statement is usually done at the end of the month for the
purpose of working out the month's profit. Basically, it's income minus expenses.

SE2A: Balance Sheet. Usually done once a year to find the business's total net worth.

SE3: Evaluation of a business. This is done once a year or more often to remind the business owner
or manager about how well he/she should run his/her business.

SE4: Cash crop sales. Monitoring sales of cash crops, especially coffee.

SES: _ Bride Price Survey. This is done when a bride price is paid. The Bride Price Survey monitors
the change in the paying of bride price relative to the changes in the level of income.

SE6: Training Record. To try to provide some feedback on the skills acquired.

SEArt: To monitor artifacts sales.

Man 2: Research Station employment records.

Man 3: Tourism Receipt Book.

We are training the locals to monitor the socio-economic activities so that when we leave they will be
able to continue monitoring for their own benefit. However, one problem in this regard is that the local
level of literacy is very low, and it will take them a lot of training before they can be able to monitor
the activities themselves. Thus we are trying to train them through literacy classes (USPC) leadership
and TLO workshops, and other, informal training.

Transparency:

Successes of establishing business enterprises:

1. The businesses are increasing the income level of the community.

2. The people are trained by volunteers and project staff to successfully run those businesses.
Training includes basic book keeping, sales and marketing.

Constraints:

1. People believe that the businesses will make fast money overnight.

2. Ownership issue -- people prefer clan-based businesses to community-based because of their
traditional organisations.

3. The people’s literacy level is very low, hindering business progress.

4. The project is threatened by mining and logging agreements.

To summarise, the Crater Mountain Wildlife Management Area has three types of business enterprise
operating: research, tourism and artifacts. These are boosting the local community's income level. By
monitoring income and expenditure of the community w aim to evaluate the benefits/losses of setting
up the enterprises. Although at this stage we have realised some successes we still have some
constraints hindering successful business operations. However, at this stage it is quite difficult to give
a clear cut conclusion of the effects of introducing the enterprises to the local communities in the Crater
Mountain Wildlife Management Area. The data collected from our monitoring may be able to offer
more concrete evidence to either prove or disprove our hypothesis.

MANAGEMENT

The project’s third objective states that over the next three years it is intended to increase the number of
decisions and actions which integrate the results of enterprise, biological and socio-economic
monitoring programs into the working management plan. Crater Mountain ICAD has set this objective
as well as the other three and the project goal to achieve. Hence indicators were chosen from activities
to guide the project design.

206

In ‘management’ we are really talking about decisions made and actions taken in the overall
management of the WMA. For instance in management, decisions are made on distributions of
benefits derived from eco-enterprise activities in terms of training and revenue-earning and
enforcement of laws and rules.

Monitoring

Monitoring management practices in the CM ICAD project is done through monitoring forms
developed as was done for the biological and socio-economic monitoring forms. These forms are used
in collecting data for the management committee (MC) to use to guide their management decisions to
manage the WMA. The forms are:

MAN-1: This form is used to document the resolutions made in the MC meetings on issues pertaining
to the WMA. The assumption is that in the long run decision and actions taken will be used as
a guide to manage the operations of the eco-enterprise activities in the WMA.

MAN-2: This form will be used to records the conservation areas within the WMA. The assumption
is that the number of conservation areas would indicate that the people are becoming aware of
the implications of destruction of the land and forest, including its wildlife resource in terms
of allowing large-scale development to happen.

MAN-3: This form is used to document the suggestions and comments from the visitors. The MC
would be able to use these as information to improve services for the benefit of their
customers and themselves.

An example of the use of the MAN-1 form is the case of the MC 1995 decision that the old pay rate
schedule would be reviewed every two years. In August 1997 the pay rate schedules were reviewed as
well as the laws of the WMA. These changes were done according to the wish of the MC with some
guidance from the field staff to ensure that changes made are appropriate for the sustainability of the
project.

SUMMARY

The entire interdisciplinary monitoring plan had been designed to monitor the biological, socio-
economic and management activities. The process in designing the monitoring program did not
involve local participants, but implementation of the monitoring program do involve the local
participants. Thus the forms were designed to cater for the local participants’ use after appropriate
training is given. Currently few monitoring activities in three sites are off the ground, with the locals
that are carrying it out are doing the job with little assistance from the staff.

The forms used are competent to collect scientific data. The information collected will be used as a
tool to test the project effectiveness, that is measure whether the project is achieving its goal and its
four objectives or not.

In the near future the management plan will be drawn up by integration of the information collected
from the interdisciplinary monitoring program.

References

Beehler, B. 1992. ‘Mapping PNG’s Biodiversity’ in: Papua New Guinea Conservation Needs
Assessment, edited by J.B. Alcorn, Vol.1., 193-209. Corporate Press Inc., Maryland

Pearl, M. 1994. Local initiatives and the rewards for biodiversity conservation: Crater Mountain
Wildlife Management Area, Papua New Guinea. in: Natural Connections: Perspectives in
Community-based Conservation, edited by D. Western and R.M. Wright, 193-214. Island
Press, Washington, DC.

UTILIZING TRAINED LOCAL PEOPLE
IN LONG-TERM BIOLOGICAL MONITORING
AT
CRATER MOUNTAIN WILDLIFE MANAGEMENT AREA (CMWMA) ICAD PROJECT.

David Bickford,
Resident Scientist,
Research and Conservation Foundation
P.O. Box 1261, Goroka, EHP

ABSTRACT

Utilizing trained local people to effectively monitor biological indicators is an effective method which
saves money, empowers and educates locals, and fosters a true sense of stewardship while generating
a modest economic incentive for local people. Since 1995, the Pawaia-Piotura people of the Crater
Mountain Wildlife Management Area (CMWMA) ICAD project have been involved in training at the
Wara Sera biological research field site. Training was commenced on a long-term basis in small
groups of two to five men with intensive field practice in simple biological methodology. Another
training technique was to conduct a short term, week-long workshop.

A week-long Trained Local Observer (TLO) workshop was held in November, 1995, at Wara Sera
research station in CMWMA. The training course included specific instruction about measurement
devices used in field methods and taxon-specific field methods. Trainees were tested initially to gauge
the level of skills and knowledge while final testing was conducted on the last day of the workshop.
Although, in some cases training helped participants improve specific skills, the post-training skill
level in the field was not effective for any scientific methodology.

Results of the 1995 TLO training workshop and long term training efforts point in the direction of more
effective training to be focused on the long term with small groups in the field, mixing older and
younger participants with varying formal education levels. Although results vary, individuals who
show promise are selected after about a one-month period of intense field instruction and hands-on
learning for further training. These individuals are indoctrinated and groomed as leaders for further
field work. This seems to be even more effective when done in pairs or one-on-one with a scientist. I
have begun including women in the training program, but because of cultural issues and
communication barriers, this is a slow process. The ultimate goal is for local trainers to be able to
train fellow landowners in the area, thus both continuing the training and empowering local people
through conservation.

INTRODUCTION

To facilitate further biological research and also to as part of the interdisciplinary monitoring program
of biological, socio-economic, and management indicators, I began training local people for
independent field work in 1995. Although my work has focused primarily on frogs, two other RCF
staff using the long-term training methods have focused on birds. Working with Research and
Conservation Foundation (RCF) staff and other NGOs, we also set up a short training workshop in
1995. The eventual goal of this training program is for empowered local people to monitor their own
biological, socio-economic, and management indicators, making decisions for conservation based on
these data, and ultimately wisely managing their natural resources.

METHODS

Two approaches have been used for training local people in Crater Mountain. Because these
approaches can be easiest to visualize as two extremes of the training continuum, I have termed them,

208

sari ci

‘short, fast, and hard’ or ‘long, slow, and easy’. The two approaches are strikingly different in many
ways.

The ‘short, fast and hard’ method incorporated a one-day pre-workshop discussion to decide on
training subjects and methods in Port Moresby. Experts were invited from many agencies and NGOs
in PNG to discuss training and assist in building a training plan. Then many of these experts were
flown by helicopter into the study site to begin the week-long Trained Local Observer (TLO)
workshop in November, 1995, at Wara Sera research station in CMWMA. Twelve participants paid a
minimal entrance fee (5 Kina) and were chosen primarily for political reasons, not on the basis of merit
or past work experience or skill. To begin the workshop, we tested the knowledge and skill levels of
the participants in a comprehensive examination. The exam was a day-long event with stations for
different activities (e.g. weighing objects, measuring objects with different tools, etc.). We then split
up into groups based on interest of study/training. We had a trainer in each of three groups: plants,
frogs, and birds. Each group had intensive training in field and classroom over three days. The
training course included specific instruction about measurement devices used in field methods (ruler,
tape measure, a scale, compass, etc.) and taxon-specific field methods for birds (identification, mist-
netting, binocular use, point-count and transect methods), plants (identification, DBH tape reading, plot
methods), and frogs (identification, plot and transect methods). We also allowed for people to try other
groups on the fourth day so that people could practice other skills. On the fifth day, we had another
test - very similar to the first day’s exam, but a bit more rigorous - incorporating more specific skills
from the training.

The ‘long, slow, and easy’ method is quite different. It is based more on a trusted long term presence
on the ground. I hike in and out of the site, I eat and spend a lot of time with the local people while
training them as well. I hire local people to work as research assistants and train them on-the-job. In
this capacity, they can earn wages while getting trained. This method has only been initiated for frog
and bird research thus far. This is due primarily to the constraint of not having trainers in the field for
long periods of time. The frog research has been on-going and continuous since 1995 and most of this
report is based on this work and my personal experience. Hands-on training over an extended period
of time (one month minimum) with both day and night methods and classroom sessions when needed
is the approach I have adopted after two years of experimenting with training techniques and
intensities.

Classroom training has included basic biological background, use of rulers and other measurement
devices, basic numeracy and accuracy skills, data sheet review, the significance of amphibian declines,
frog taxonomy, and review of ideas about long term monitoring and conservation. The classroom
work is balanced with homework assignments and the day-to-day field work. Putting the work in
context while having a firm understanding about how the classroom work and field work fit into the
larger picture of conservation is a priority. I also encourage them to try new methods, to let them make
mistakes and do things on their own. If constraints or mistakes occur, I conduct both on-the-spot and
later-on classroom training to alleviate the problem. | facilitate the sharing of knowledge gained from
these mistakes by asking direct questions.

RESULTS

In the case of the ‘short, fast, and hard’ method, results did not meet expectations. Final testing was
condicted on the last day of the workshop with varying results. Workshop attendees did not have
significantly higher scores after one week of training. Constraints were primarily based on lack of
formal education and poor understanding in basic scientific methods. The feasibility of real training to
happen in one week is questionable because of the large gap between current level of knowledge and
skills and the level needed for scientific research. Results of the 1995 TLO training workshop and
experience since then points in the direction of more effective training to be focused on long-term
training.

The ‘long, slow, and easy’ method seems to be the most productive and conducive method for

effective training. Results have been most effective with small groups in the field, mixing older and
younger participants with varying formal education levels. Although results vary, individuals who

209

show promise are selected after at least one month period of intense field instruction and hands-on
learning for further training. These individuals are then indoctrinated and groomed as leaders for
further field work. This seems to be even more effective when done in pairs or one-on-one with a
scientist. These individuals can then lead small training sessions and more effectively communicate
complex ideas or techniques in tok ples.

CONCLUSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS

The obvious conclusion based on these experiences is that the ‘long, slow, and easy’ method is much
more conducive to effective training than the ‘short, fast, and hard’ at least in the PNG context of the
Crater Mountain ICAD. A trusted, long term presence on the ground is crucial to the success of any
training program. No surrogate can replace it.

Because it is more effective to combine elders with more natural history knowledge and skills with
younger school-educated men and boys, I recommend that this template be followed in other training
programs. Mixing generations facilitates communication in fok ples, makes the training a cultural
experience, and makes training more meaningful for trainees.

DISCUSSIONS AND RECOMMENDATIONS

During the last two days of the conference, the participants were divided into five groups to discuss and
make recommendations on the five topics, chosen as being the most problematic issues, needing urgent
attention. These were: the problems associated with economic incentives for ICAD projects; the
integration of western, traditional and Christian values, taboos and concepts in conservation; regulation
of Protected Areas; landownership and community participation in conservation; and ICAD project
implementation and management. The followings are brief summaries of these discussions and the
recommendations of the groups.

GROUP 1: ECONOMIC INCENTIVES IN ICAD PROJECTS

In addressing this topic, the group discussed the issues of sustainable immediate gains (kwik mani)
versus unsustainable immediate gains from the economic incentives for ICAD projects as perceived by
the local communities. Sustainable immediate gain (kwik mani) that supports conservation, and
sustainable immediate gain that supports true development for the whole community is the aim. But
kwik mani also brings problems, and the group discussed how to manage these problems. We know
what all the economic incentives are, but the question is ‘why do we need them and how quickly do we
need to obtain such benefits and how much?’ Further, where does the desire for kwik mani come from?
The group identified four reasons why communities wanted quick benefits:

® logging, mining, etc. offer Kwik mani, and NGO proposes alternatives;

® as compensation for giving up hunting, etc.

e the need for school fees, medicine, etc.

e to satisfy ‘bigman syndrome’.

So what is a good project, that will supply or provide kwik mani?

The group identified a number of criteria, which were seen as being the ingredients for good projects,

and these were:

The project gives kwik mani (not necessarily big money)

It benefits the whole community; facilitates education

The whole community contributes to, participates in the project

The project provides jobs (paid or unpaid)

It starts small and locally controlled, but can grow to be big

It is structured to give community control over and access to, all needed information (give people

the means to get that information., e.g., a telephone)

It does not deplete resources

8. It builds trust between stakeholders (landowners, NGOs, DEC, local level government, men and
women; must be honest with them); and builds patience

9. It does not build dependence

10. It builds on local skills

11. It must build up, expand on, those local skills and existing business opportunities

12. It can last a long time

13. It must have a market

AA Pw See

n

The group noted that too much information could also raise expectations and thus, information must be
given at a time when the community is able to absorb it.

Problems with I and 5. Enterprises that bring kwik mani can be in different forms. The group sees that
economic incentives result from threats. Kwik mani means in a few months, instant gratification.
Projects must give people a reasonable benefit that comes back reasonably quickly, but not a handout.
It must be something that they themselves do to give them an immediate return.

Approaches are lacking and likewise, the methodology. One of the failures is “how do we do it?’ How

do we promote that incentive? The above list is a criteria list, it is just a list to determine whether one
has a good project or not. Each of the criteria will have its own approach or process.

211

GROUP 2

Topic: Integration of western, traditional and Christian values, taboos and concepts in
conservation.

What are values? The importance one places on things around oneself, beliefs and customs, how one
thinks and how one feels. The group looked at relationship between humanity and nature, rather than
specifically at conservation, and came up with the following characteristics of different sets of values:

Traditional: strong spirituality between man and nature, use of rituals and sacred places. Close
connection with environment for survival and identity. Strong sense of community, interdependence
between people and between people and environment. Conservation for feasts and cultural purposes.

Western: man has power over environment and environment is separate. Western society feels that it
is losing something; value of the natural world is therefore based on scarcity. More individualism.

;
/
|

Christianity. man is guardian of God’s creation; each person has a responsibility for others and for
nature.

4

What good lessons can we learn from each other?
1. Christian theology and traditional values imply that everything is connected and that man is part of
the environment.

2. Traditional beliefs suggest that the environment is sacred and should be treated with respect.

3. Inthe traditional view the well-being of the community and its resources are central.

4. Likewise, traditional beliefs see nature and culture as two sides of the same coin.

5. From the west, we have learned that we can destroy nature and let us add something positive; we
are learning that we may be able to conserve it.

Recommendations

1. Use local traditional practices and organisations as a basis for conservation; use them to move
toward general biodiversity conservation

2. Try to use ideas instead of terms or concepts. Use examples, stories, symbols to get message across

3. Use the extensive network of churches in the work of conservation. Christian view of conservation
bridges the traditional and western understanding of the environment

4, Learn lessons from other parts of PNG and elsewhere. What seems so large can be easily destroyed
and is limited.

5. Pass on the history, values and practices relating to the interconnectedness between man and

environment and the responsibility for the past, present and future generations

We must educate the children.

7. Women are key stakeholders in the use and management of resources and therefore, should also
play a key role in conservation activities.

2

At this point of discussions, questions arose, in particular about the relationship between traditional
views and Christianity. Discussion covered the following points:

e Some people’s beliefs in Christianity think there is no reason to take care of the environment
because the world is going to end. Also, the bible says they should have dominion over the
environment, so they can take the trees, blow up the reefs, etc. Christianity has two opposing views,
both supportable by the bible. The traditional world-view is more conservation-related.

e Weare conflicting today, regarding the traditional idea of belonging to the environment. The
western concept is completely different. We do not hold on that strongly to our traditional beliefs
now; we still seem to be trying to bend sideways to the western ideal.

e A removal of our value system has happened. The traditional system says all the spirits live in the
bush. Then the church came in and said there is no evil spirit in the bush, or that it has been removed,
and replaced by the big God spirit who will not hurt you, is benign. People are not really afraid of the
bush anymore. Now you can do things you never would have done, can go places you never would
have gone, because the good spirit is with you. Values have changed. So conservation is really a
way of life. We should go back to the hausman idea, a place where people in common got educated
in community values, such as not to go into another person’s place. In that form of education,

conservation ideas are integrated into the whole way of life, and practised without any separation.
Western idea separates conservation off. Here we create a park, where we keep out people. Life and
conservation are separated, there is a boundary. Traditional thinking integrated conservation as a way
of life. We need to go back to the rubbish bin where the church has thrown the past and bring it back
into our lives.

e Traditional thinking is a circle, the interconnectedness of man and environment doesn’t change.
Western thinking with man over environment could change and environment could dominate man.
Christianity is triangle, can stand on any point, man can be most important, God can be most
important, or environment. We have to balance, or find a balance between all three (or two) angles of
the triangle.

The group agreed that they accepted the recommendations, and decided to move on with the
discussion. Further discussions covered the points that the mainstream churches have failed the
environment, and have failed the people. The churches are remaining silent about the destruction of the
environment. Logging agreements are a sin, since they are stealing the future of our children.

Further recommendation:

8. We must start lobbying the churches and force them to be strong. A mechanism to do this must be
developed, and people to undertake this work must be found.

GROUP 3
Topic: Regulation of Protected Areas.

Discussion covered protection under both customary law and common law, (including all Government
laws, e.g., various Conservation Acts). A major question discussed was: How do we establish, manage
and regulate Protected Areas in light of the breakdown of Customary laws?

Strengths of customary law: \andownership; recognised in the constitution; can be used to establish
Protected Areas; flexible (and inherent, especially in regard to social interaction, less conflicts in
absence of new money ventures); community enforcement.

Weaknesses: breaking down (customary laws are disappearing); not practiced by people though
recognised by Constitution; unwritten law, flexible for depletion of resource; difficult for conservation
purpose; not institutionalized.

Strengths of common law: strengthens customary law; convenient; enforceable and strong; written
laws, insitutionalised in courts, police, Department of Environment and Conservation.

Weaknesses: Too specific; inappropriate and out of context; out of date; not practiced/implemented;
difficult to enforce, with complicated requirements.

Types of protected areas:

e National Parks/Nature Reserves/Sanctuaries/Historical Walking Trails/Provincial Parks.
Regulated under the Lands Act, National Parks Act — land is leased, custom rights are not allowed,
land is alienated.

e® Wildlife Management Areas (WMAs)/Sanctuaries/Protected Areas; Fauna Protection and Control
Act, Regulations: establishment of committee; community law making and enforcement;
customary land; area managed by landowners; community benefits (money/community use
allowance)

e Cultural Sites: National Museum and Cultural preservation; Nature Protection Area (NPA);
Conservation Area (CA), Environment Planning (EP)

e Conservation Areas: Conservation Areas Act (No areas have been declared under this Act).

e RAMSAR (RAMSAR Convention) - UNESCO: Tonda WMA is a RAMSAR site. To protect
wetlands and important migratory flyway birds (e.g., S/E flyway, Russia - Indonesia) - Indonesia,
Australia and PNG are now working together to protect wetlands. International convention.

e@ World Heritage Site (UNESCO): Archeological sites. We should be looking at two sites currently
(Kuk Archaeological site and Lake Bakatawa). International convention.

e Sacred Sites: protected under customary law and practices.

Discussing points: What impact do regulations on protected areas have on ICAD process? What is the
objective of ICAD? What is the end product of ICAD? Is it for:

e protected areas?

e areas outside protected areas?

e biodiversity conservation?

e sustainable development?

ICAD is therefore defined as a process for building capacity to ‘manage’ resources sustainably, and it
involves economic, social and ecological parameters of the environment.

Recommendations:

1. Encourage Conservation Areas Act (CAA) to be implemented and effectively enforced.

2. Agree on the definition of ICAD

3. All types of protected areas should be seen as equally important

4. Traditional type protected area systems should be recognised by various Acts

5. Direct current funding facilities to be linked and targeted to current ICAD process to build the

capacity to manage resources sustainably in protected areas and also outside of protected areas.

A marine act (including watershed to protect water quality, coral health, and fish spawning

grounds), a species act, and an act protecting habitat are missing and need to be addressed. New

laws need to be passed to address these gaps. Any such legislation must be carefully framed, so as
not to take away community control and sovereignty.

7. Conservation Areas Act and Fauna Act need to be amended. CAA needs flexibility and
community control as in the WMAs; WMaAs need protection available under CAA. (Some
participants expressed the hope that much of these two points would be addressed by the AusAID
institutional strengthening.)

8. The Forestry and Agriculture Acts need to be integrated.

9.

ICAD practitioners need to work with technical people for assistance, like DEC

10. There should be effective communication between national and provincial departments, e.g.

working together to protect watersheds which cross provincial boundaries.

In the case for Marine Conservation, a number of issues were raised and discussed, including the
following points:

What are the possibility of WMAs in Marine Conservation? Do we need legislation for marine
ICAD? The need for enabling legislation for conservation marine management.

The aim is for resource owner participation without management/exploitation. Thought must be
given to how far the process is externally driven, leading to marginalisation. The aim is for
enabling of communities, a process of facilitation, gradual transfer of skills, knowledge, etc.

How does customary management relate to marine conservation? Customary management case
study in Jdaul Island, New Ireland Province. Problems - threat from fishing vessels, community
conflicts vice ownership and benefits. How to manage marine WMAs.

Consideration must be given to raising awareness in terms of rights, legislation, continuity,
effectiveness.

An inventory of threatened marine resources is needed: there is a need for a resource data base,
and an inventory of marine information. ICAD practitioners are called on to consider what action
can be taken to bring this about.

Marine conservation, like any other conservation, can be threatened by conflicts within
communities and power struggles. We need to work on processes of resolving such conflicts.

Threats to Marine resources: fishing methods, such as the use of dynamite; development
economy, such as coastal shipping, tourism, mining, logging; and traditional economy, including
human settlements, land claims, and agriculture
