PLENARY AND ACTION PLANS

Discussion following the return from the small group discussion was lively, and covered all the issues
raised throughout the conference, with the benefit of the new insights gained from each other’s
experiences and from the group discussions. It was also directed especially at action plans.

It was agreed that PNG ICAD practitioners could send their CVs to the National Volunteer Service, to
allow a better use of national skills and experience. NVS also suggested that a capacity statement for
each of the NGO groups would be very helpful. NVS runs an upper level capacity building program,

217

but what is missing is middle level, lower level capacity building (writing reports, writing proposals,
compiling and analysing data, etc.)

The idea of a co-ordinator for ICAD was discussed; it was suggested that this should be someone at
DEC (perhaps even the Secretary of DEC) to ensure that ICAD practitioners had input to conservation
legislation. The formation of an NGO coordinating body was raised, and also the use of electronic
networking, an ICAD-NET.

Smaller working groups to deal with the five issues covered in the group discussions were
provisionally organised. Persons offering to be part of these were:

Economic Group: Mike Parsons, Stanley, Simon P., John.

Church Lobby Group: Elizabeth, Leo

Regulation of Protected Areas: Leslie, John, Matros, Sasa, June

Landownership and Community Participation:

Project Management and Conservation: Max, Kelly

The value of the workshop itself, as a way to reflect on issues raised during ICAD work, was strongly
endorsed, and it was thought that further such workshops would be valuable. The problem of which
agency would be able to run such a workshop was discussed, given the expense of the exercise. (The
Conservation Resource Centre program is due to finish up in April, 1998.) As an alternative, the idea
of better developing links between NGO ICAD Practitioners was discussed.. CRC offered to act as the
server for this network. Participants asked that CRC also assisted them in accessing email, that it
create a real email network to share information. It was agreed further that one member from each
NGO group be designated as to be the established link between that group and CRC and all other
groups.

It was stated that NGO ICAD practitioners must have practice guidelines, since a failure by one will be
a bad example for all.

One staff member of UPNG noted that there are about 40 students at UPNG interested in conservation,
etc. and that ICAD practitioners should feel free to utilize them.

The drafting of a new mission statement was discussed, as the new Minister had expressed interest in
seeing a draft from the ICAD practitioners. The task force designated for this work was also given the
responsibility of drawing up specific recommendations for DEC, and it was suggested that the task
force should act as a continuous linkage between DEC and NGOs, since there was no point in making
suggestions and then ‘dropping the ball’.

WRAP UP SESSION

The closing session was addressed by Finn Reske-Nielsen, Deputy Resident Representative of the

United Nations Development Programme,which had sponsored the conference, and general discussion

followed. This session was well-attended, reflecting the value of the conference overall. There were

two chief conclusions during this final discussion.

e ICAD is about putting people’s development goals at the heart;

e@ the community development approaches used by groups have a lot in common but are based on the
conditions that prevail locally.

The role of UNDP in ICAD work was discussed. There has been some criticism as a result of the Lak
ICAD exercise, as not using the community development approach. Some problems are genuine, but
they have been a good learning experience. UNDP members present pointed out that they had been
willing to share openly these experiences so others might learn from them and avoid the same mistakes,
and that the Bismarck-Ramu ICAD hopefully signifies a more aware and balanced approach. UNDP
want to give grants out to the benefit of existing projects, occasionally to establish projects. UNDP is a
development partner with the PNG Government, but increasingly also with NGOs and the private
sector. It does not have vast resources to implement large projects, and so is directing its funds to
piloting certain activities, such as the eco-forestry program.

218

Statement and Recommendations by the NGOs to the Minister for Environment
and Conservation

The following is an abstract of an NGO-CRC meeting held on the 9" September 1997 at the
Conservation Resource Centre office, Hohola, at 9.00. am. The object of this meeting was to consider
the request from the then Minister for Environment and Conservation, the Hon. Minister Robert Sakias,
to the NGOs to provide some mission statements about conservation and the environment in general.
This meeting eventuated from the discussions held during the Second ICAD Practitioners held at
Motupore Island from the 1‘ - 5" September 1997, following the announcements made by Mr Kembi
Watoka (A/Deputy Secretary, DEC) and Dr Brian Brunton (Director, Greenpeace (PNG) about the
Minister’s request.

In Attendance

Gaikovina Kula (Conservation International), Celine Beaulieu (Foundation of the People of the South
Pacific, Papua New Guinea Inc.), Max Kuduk (Village Development Trust), Robert Bino (Research
and Conservation Foundation), Stanley Kundal (World Wildlife Fund, Kikori), Brian Brunton
(Greenpeace), Mike Parsons and Simon Saulei (Conservation Resource Centre/DEC).

ISSUES DISCUSSED

The group reviewed and discussed the issues raised during the ICAD Practitioners Conference held at
Motupore Island and listed the following issues, which were considered as particularly important and
thus, in need of immediate attention from the Minister for possible action:

Legislation

There is a need for the government to provide firm commitments on statutory legislation to give ICAD
stakeholders security of tenure. [CAD NGOs currently invest millions of kina in ICAD areas. Security
of this investment is inadequately provided by current legislation. It was recommended that the
Minister should address this as a priority issue in current reviews of the legislation.

The Conservation Areas Act (CAA) and Wildlife Management Area (WMA) provisions under the
Fauna Protection Act were discussed. The Private Contractual Agreements model as suggested by
Brian Brunton, CITES, and the Fauna Protection and Control Act were also discussed. The CAA
provides for protection. The issues were related to the problems posed in regard to the implementation
of ICAD projects in declared WMAs, the implementation of other land-uses such as mining and
forestry within the same WMA, as is the case in the Crater Mountain, and the whole conservation issue.
It is important to note that CAA, WMA provisions of the Fauna Protection and Control Act and CITES
only protect the species, not the habitat.

Another issue raised was the problem of implementing species protection, which does not include the
protection of the habitats of the species concerned, e.g., the birdwing butterfly project in Popondetta.
In this case the intrusion of both estate and village oil palm projects are increasingly reducing the
birdwing butterfly habitat in the Popondetta plains. Conservation is one legitimate land-use and thus,
must be entertained in any land-use planning and allocation.

It was recommended that the Minister should review the Environmental Plan Act, especially the
requisition in Sect.7.1b to allow NGOs involvement. Also Sect.10.2a.i Exemptions and 10.2b Notices
to be implemented, circulation should be to NGOs too.

Marine Conservation

The NGOs are requesting that some actions be taken to looking into the problem of lack of proper
legislation for Marine Ecosystem Conservation. There are currently no laws that prohibit cyanide
fishing. Dynamite fishing is also a problem area that requires some vigorous regulatory enforcement.
An issue was raised about the impacts of terrestrial development affecting marine environment,
especially those related to soil erosion, sedimentation and siltation. It was recommended that DEC
looks at the possibility of including the Total Catchment Area approach as one way of incorporating
both terrestrial and marine ecosystems conservation.

219

Co-ordination
The issue of co-ordination was raised in relation to DEC and NGOs in their respective approaches to

implementing ICAD and other conservation matters. It was recommended that CRC act as a bridge
between the NGO groups and DEC.

Inter-sectoral co-ordination and dialogue

This issue was found to have been very problematic over the past twenty to thirty years or so,
especially when dealing with projects that have multi-sectoral inputs from relevant Government
agencies, coupled with their respective legislative and policy requirements. This issue becomes very
relevant particularly in relation to the new organic law on provincial reforms and since the ICAD
initiatives take place at the district level and sometimes cross provincial boundaries, e.g. Lakekamu and
Crater Mountain, it is important that such co-ordination and dialogue be established and maintained.
The issue of cross provincial co-ordination of development planning is therefore tricky to address, as it
appears to relate back to the concept of ‘Total Catchment Area’. It was recommended that DEC or
the Minister looks at this issue and discusses with concerned agencies/Ministers to allow for multi-
sectoral approach to project development. This would hopefully remove the current isolation and
independent approaches being taken by DEC, Forest Authority, etc., in implementing projects that
require cross-provincial boundaries.

Options and/or alternative economic incentives for conservation

During the Motupore ICAD conference a number of development options were suggested such as
bioprospecting, eco-tourism, greenhouse gas mitigation, and non-timber forest products. The most
favoured income-generating option Pacific-wide is eco-tourism. It was recommended that the
Minister prepares a National Eco-tourism Policy in conjunction with the Minister for Tourism as well
as the NGOs and that this be implemented jointly by the two Ministries and ICAD NGOs.

International Commitments/Obligations

The meeting endorsed that DEC should be reminded that it should take up with the Minister the
commitments that the department has taken on behalf of the state such as the UNFCCC, BCD, and
Ramsar (e.g. Tonda). Security of tenure should be confirmed for conservation activities in these sites.

At the end of the meeting, the NGO group then drew up the mission statement and the summary briefs
of the recommendations it wished to deliver to the Minister as provided below.

_ MISSION STATEMENT

ICADs in Papua New Guinea are an educational as well as human development process. It is through
this process that the chance for survival of native flora and fauna is enhanced, and at the same time
bringing improvements to the livelihood of the local people. ICADs must have a good scientific (social
and biological) basis and should be put in place with appropriate community interaction, and not
simply donor-driven. This would ensure that conservation is implemented and sustained.

Conservation must always be seen as the ultimate goal of ICAD. However, this goal will only be
achieved through rural development. By referring to integrated conservation and development,
however, we recognised that rural development cannot occur at the expense of the ecosystem in which
we live. We recognised that there is a very real danger that an improved rural standard of living
involving the introduction of new technology and increased harvest capacity may lead directly to
depletion of biodiversity. In depleting, rather than sustaining biodiversity, landholders trade the option
of long-term rural development for ephemeral short-term gain. Assisting landholders to identify and
meet their real short-term development needs is a challenge that all ICAD practitioners must endeavour

to meet.

ICAD is in the business of behaviour modification. Our ever-increasing population, accessing new
technologies, must learn new modes of behaviour in order not to destroy their life support systems.
Education, and not just awareness, therefore, must be a major thrust in the ICAD process.

220

SUMMARY OF RECOMMENDATIONS

It was recommended that the Minister prepares a National Ecotourism Policy in conjunction with the
Minister for Tourism, and that this be implemented jointly by the two Ministries and ICAD NGOs.
These NGOs are involved in implementing the concept of ‘integrated conservation and development’.

It was recommended that the Minister should address the issue of security of tenure for ICAD
projects. Like mining and forestry companies, [CAD NGOs currently invest millions of kina in ICAD
areas, but unlike mining and forestry companies, they are given no security of tenure.

It was recommended that the Minister should address the issue of habitat protection in legislation
dealing with protection of endangered species. The species cannot be protected unless its habitat is
also protected. Additionally, we could look at the concept of ‘Ecological Communities’ as now being
implemented in Australia.

It was recommended that the Minister should include NGOs in the requisition in Sect.7.1b of the
Environmental Planning Act, and also in Sect.10.2a.i Exemptions and Sect 10.2b Notices to be
implemented.

It was recommended that the Minister address the issue of legislation for Marine Conservation.

It was recommended that the Minister address the issue of dialogue on integrated conservation and
development with other government Departments and agencies, e.g., Department of Agriculture &
Livestock, PNG Forest Authority, Fisheries, Mining & Petroleum, Health and so on.

It was recommended that the Minister should visit some ICAD projects and see for himself some of
the conservation initiatives already being implemented by some NGOs.

It was recommended that the Minister redirect his Department to take a more active part in the
drawing up of both the National and Provincial Forest Plans to ensure that conservation areas are
secured in such plans.

It was recommended that the Minister direct his Department into looking at identifying lost and
existing famous national heritage items in conjunction with the Tourist Promotion Authority and the
National Museum.

The above mission statement and the recommendations were then submitted to the Minister through the
Departmental Secretary.
